# Theatre, Puppetry and Performance Traditions - Live Learning Session

> **Learner-first lock:** This session teaches you to hear any claim about an Indian performance tradition as six separable things - a **text or script** (what is performed, and whether it is written, sung, recited or improvised), a **stage relation** (where the performer stands with respect to the audience, the screen, the curtain, the courtyard or the temple), a **mechanism** (what physically produces the performance: a human body, a masked body, a strung figure, a rod-held figure, a leather cut-out against light, or a hand inside a sleeve), a **carrier** (the community, caste, troupe, monastery, family or institution that holds and transmits the skill), an **occasion** (the temple festival, harvest, spring festival, Dussehra, wedding, night-long vigil, courtyard or proscenium booking at which it happens), and a **status** (what a named body has formally said about it, and when). It runs from the treatise that supplied India's dramatic vocabulary to the recognition decisions still being taken, and it keeps five habits throughout: name the discriminating feature rather than the shared one; keep a prescriptive text separate from a record of what was actually performed; never let a state boundary become an ownership claim over a form; never rank a folk form below a classical one; and never convert a form's present difficulty into a general story about dying traditions. Dance grammar belongs to the dance topic; raga and tala theory belong to the music topic; the authorship and literary history of Sanskrit plays belong to the language and literature topic; the administration of awards, heritage lists and cultural funding belongs to the heritage and institutions topic; sport policy and the circus industry belong elsewhere again. What is owned here is **dramatic composition, enactment, the stage relation, the puppet mechanism, the folk and ritual performance field, the modern theatre movement, and the martial and storytelling traditions in so far as they are performed**.

## How to read this session

Work down the lessons in order. Each lesson opens with the question a learner actually has at that point, draws or tabulates before it names, and hands over the technical word only after the idea it names has been established. Five habits are built deliberately, because these are the five places this topic is lost.

1. **Answer an identification demand with the feature no other form shares.** "A devotional folk theatre of eastern India performed in the open air" fits Jatra, Bhaona, Ankia Naat and Daskathia equally. Chaitanya Mahaprabhu's use of the medium, the *Brajavali* language, the mask, the *kathia* instrument and the two named narrators do not. Almost every objective failure in this topic is a candidate reaching for a shared feature when a discriminating one was available.
2. **Keep a prescription apart from a performance.** A treatise that says a stage should hold four hundred people, be two-storeyed and use no masks is telling you what its author prescribed. It is not a survey of every stage ever built in India, and it says nothing at all about what a Kerala temple courtyard or a Manipuri open yard did four centuries later. The single most expensive error in this topic is quoting the prescription as though it were a census.
3. **Treat a state name as an address, not a title deed.** Many forms in this topic are recorded across two states, in two languages, or under two names. Tal-Maddale is recorded in coastal Karnataka *and* Kerala. Villu Paatu is recorded in Kerala *and* parts of Tamil Nadu. Theyyam is performed in Kerala and has a Karnataka counterpart. Dashavatar belongs to the Konkan region across a modern border. Writing "X is the folk theatre of state Y, exclusively" converts a living region into an administrative unit and is wrong more often than it is right.
4. **Do not build a ladder.** In this topic *classical*, *folk*, *ritual* and *tribal* are descriptions of setting, transmission and repertoire. They are not grades. The same chapter that records a Sanskrit dramaturgic treatise also records a village ritual in which the entire community participates, and neither is the superior case of the other. A session that treats folk theatre as simple entertainment has already made the error the examiner is testing for.
5. **Do not decorate.** Costume colours, origin centuries, performer honours and audience sizes carry marks only where the record supplies them. Where this session does not state an origin date, an award or a founder, that silence is deliberate and should be reproduced in your answer.

Everything is drawn in text. No photograph, video, mask plate or puppet diagram is needed to follow any lesson here.

**Boundary map.** The dance topic owns dance grammar - *nritta*, *nritya*, *natya*, *abhinaya*, the nine *rasas*, *hasta* and *mudra*, the eight recognised classical forms and the folk *dance* field; here a dance-drama is owned when its dominant grammar is enacted or narrated text, and it is routed out when its dominant grammar is codified danced movement. The music topic owns raga, tala, the two classical systems and instruments as sound-producing objects; here an instrument is named only when it identifies a performance form - the *khol* and cymbals of the *Gayan-Bayan Mandali*, the *Nagada* of Nautanki, the *kathia* of Daskathia, the *Mizhavu* and *Edakka* of Kutiyattam, the *Burra* of Burra Katha, the *Oggu* of Oggukatha. The language and literature topic owns the authorship, text history and literary criticism of Sanskrit and modern plays; the core record for this chapter itself routes its list of ancient Sanskrit dramas to that owner, and this session follows that routing. The sculpture and painting topics own iconography and pictorial technique; here a painted picture-scroll or a leather cut-out appears as a performance object, not as a painting tradition. The heritage, conservation and institutions topic owns the administration of heritage lists, academies and awards, and every changeable status total; this session records the dated inscriptions that belong to its own forms, states exactly one current record, and asserts nothing beyond it. The society papers own the livelihoods of puppeteer and folk-performer communities as a social question; here the community is named as the carrier of a skill and its recorded condition is stated, and the livelihood debate itself is not adjudicated. Sport policy, sport federations and the circus industry are not owned here at all; where the core record carries them alongside this chapter's material, they are routed out and declared, not improvised into the teaching.

Read the diagram from left to right and notice its shape. The first six questions are answerable from evidence. The seventh is not, and it is placed last and marked as answering nothing, because an answer that opens "Indian theatre is an eternal and sacred art that has flowed unbroken since antiquity" has spent its first sentence in the only column that carries no marks.

### 1. The exact scope, stated once

This topic owns **dramatic composition and enactment, the relation between performer and audience, puppet mechanism and manipulation, the regional folk and ritual performance field, the modern theatre movement and its institutions, and martial and narrative-storytelling traditions in so far as they are performed**. Stated as a checklist of what must be answerable by the end of this session:

- the treatise foundation: the *Natya Shastra*, its authorship, its bracketed date, the *Natya Veda* conception, and the four terms used for drama;
- the earliest performance spaces claimed for India, and the exact status of that claim;
- the two dramatic modes, *Lokadharmi* and *Natyadharmi*;
- the ten play-types, their names, and the two that the treatise itself develops;
- the rigid conventions of the Sanskrit play, in the source's own terms, and their evidentiary limit;
- the ritual progression of a performance and the role of the *Sutradhar*;
- the recorded stage: its capacity, its two storeys, its curtains and its mask statement;
- the three character categories, their sub-types, and the language register attached to each;
- the recorded reasons for the decline of Sanskrit theatre;
- Kutiyattam in full: region, antiquity, adherence, castes, languages, instruments, the *Nirvahana* convention, its commentary function and a named exponent;
- folk theatre: its definition against urban Sanskrit theatre, its three-stage thematic arc and every named regional form with its state, theme, narrator convention, language, instrument and community;
- modern theatre: colonial origin, the proscenium, commercial themes, the 1876 Act, the earliest named venues, Parsi theatre, literary and nationalist drama, organised political theatre, the touring and permanent theatre, the post-1947 institutions, regional revival and the practitioner ledger;
- puppetry: its Indian antiquity and textual references, the philosophical framing, the four-category classification by mechanism, the construction and control of each mechanism, every named tradition with its region, material, community and repertoire, and the forms that mix two mechanisms;
- martial and ritual performance: the named traditions with weapon, movement, community and performance context, and the boundary at which sport recognition takes over;
- the mask, the narrator and the cross-art frontier with dance, music, sculpture and painting;
- the safeguarding record: dated inscriptions that belong to this topic's own forms, the academy and drama-school layer, and the two structurally different kinds of decline;
- the attribution, chronology, region and terminology discipline that keeps an answer defensible.

### 2. The six questions, each with its technical vocabulary

**Question one - text.** What is performed, and in what language? The answers in this topic are startlingly varied and the variation is examinable. A Sanskrit play is a written literary composition in a high register with a second register assigned to one character. An Assamese one-act play is written in a mixed language made for the purpose. A Malwa folk theatre delivers its dialogue in couplets. A Goan form is performed in a particular Konkani dialect. A Kerala shadow-puppet form performs a named Tamil recension of the Ramayana. Nothing about "an Indian play" predicts any of this; the record does.

**Question two - stage relation.** Where does the performer stand with respect to the audience? The possibilities in this topic are: behind a curtain that is later withdrawn; on a two-storeyed stage; in an open courtyard; in front of a village temple; on a special raised platform attached to a temple; behind a white screen lit from the rear; hidden behind a curtain with only rods visible; seated, without costume, dance or acting at all; and on a raised picture-frame stage with the audience in a darkened house. Each relation carries its own consequences for what can be shown, and identification questions are often built directly on it.

**Question three - mechanism.** What physically produces the performance? An unmasked human body, a masked human body, a body in elaborate headgear and face paint, a strung wooden figure, a rod-held figure, a leather cut-out interrupting light, a hand inside a cloth sleeve, or a painted picture used as a narrative prop. Mechanism is the axis on which Indian puppetry is classified, and it is the reason the classification is not regional.

**Question four - carrier.** Who holds the skill and passes it on? The record names specific carriers again and again: two Kerala castes with a traditional privilege; a Muslim community of impersonators; a Brahmin community that narrates with painted pictures; a community that performed a Karnataka form under royal patronage; pastoral communities of the Deccan; a Kerala community associated with shadow puppetry; another associated with a Karnataka shadow form; a tribal community of Maharashtra; a community associated with an Odisha shadow form; a caste that wears a particular sacred mask in Garhwal. A form whose carrier you cannot name is a form you have not identified.

**Question five - occasion.** When and where does it happen? The record ties performance to the period before Dussehra, to the Manasha festival, to Holi celebrated as the spring festival of the Konkan, to Baisakhi, to the harvest season of a rice-growing tract, to the achievement of agricultural prosperity or the fulfilment of a personal wish, to eight consecutive nights at a temple, to an overnight vigil, and to a worship of the local deity. Occasion is the most neglected question in this topic and the one that turns a folk-theatre list into an analytical answer.

**Question six - status.** Who has formally said what about it, and when? Four kinds of body matter here and they are not interchangeable: an international intergovernmental list; a national academy that confers awards and holds fellowships; a national training school; and a colonial-era statute that regulated performance rather than recognising it. Status is dated, institutional and revisable. It is never evidence about antiquity, and a statute that restricts a form is as much a "status" fact as an award that honours one.

### 3. The vocabulary fixed here, used without pause later

These terms recur constantly across the session. Each is developed properly in its own lesson; fix the working sense now.

| Term | Working definition, fixed here |
|---|---|
| **Natya Veda** | The conception of drama as a distinct body of knowledge created by Brahma from the four Vedas, recorded at the opening of the treatise tradition |
| **Rupaka** | The general word for a dramatic composition; ten types are named, of which the treatise develops two |
| **Lokadharmi** | The mode of realistic depiction of daily life |
| **Natyadharmi** | The conventional mode, with more stylised narration and overt symbolism |
| **Sutradhar** | Literally the holder of the thread or string. In the Sanskrit system, the stage manager and director who performs the preliminaries and introduces the play; in several folk forms, the narrator who carries the performance; in puppetry, the puppeteer-narrator, where the same word is read as "holder of strings" |
| **Purva-raga** | The pre-play ritual performed behind the curtain before a Sanskrit play begins |
| **Nirvahana** | The Kutiyattam convention by which each character begins with a recollection of the past events of the story before the story itself unfolds |
| **Vesham** | Disguise or role-appearance; the word names the principal character in one Telangana and Andhra folk tradition |
| **Marionette** | A figure suspended and manipulated by strings - the English term for the string-puppet mechanism |

Two of these are already enough to prevent a standard failure. If *Sutradhar* names a stage manager in one system, a narrator in another and a puppeteer in a third, then "the Sutradhar is the stage manager of Indian theatre" is a false generalisation built out of a true particular. The word travels; the job description does not.

### 4. The three clocks this topic keeps apart

An examiner can test a date here in three different senses, and merging them is expensive.

1. **The clock of a text.** The *Natya Shastra* is a datable work with a broad bracket, and the safest formulation is that bracket rather than a single year. This clock is the oldest in the topic and also the least informative about practice.
2. **The clock of a practice.** When was a form performed, by whom, under whose patronage, in which language, and when did any of that change? Most folk theatre in this topic begins on this clock in the fifteenth and sixteenth centuries; a Kerala dance-drama tradition on this clock begins in the mid-seventeenth century; a Kerala glove-puppet tradition begins in the eighteenth; a Manipuri puppet tradition dates to the nineteenth. This clock is regional and social and runs quite independently of the text clock.
3. **The clock of an institution.** When was a form legislated against, recognised, inscribed, funded or taught in a school? This clock begins for this topic in 1876 with a restrictive statute, continues through 1952 with a national academy, and reaches an international list only in the twenty-first century. A form's history does not begin when this clock starts, and confusing the two is the most frequently tested chronological error in the topic.

### 5. The boundaries, stated so they never have to be re-argued

- **Dance** owns the grammar of codified movement, the eight recognised classical forms and the folk-dance field. Here a dance-drama is owned when what identifies it is the enacted or narrated text and the stage relation, and routed out when what identifies it is a codified movement vocabulary. Where a form sits on that frontier - and several do - this session says so and gives the reason rather than pretending the boundary is clean.
- **Music** owns raga and tala theory, the two classical systems, and instruments as sound-producing objects. Here an instrument is named only as an identifier of a performance form, and a song-genre only where a form is defined by it.
- **Language and literature** owns the authorship, dating, text history and criticism of Sanskrit and modern plays. The core record for this chapter routes its own list of ancient Sanskrit dramas there, and this session does not reproduce that list or attribute plays to playwrights beyond what is needed for an owned question.
- **Sculpture and painting** own material, iconography and pictorial technique. Here a painted scroll or a coloured leather figure is a performance object with a manipulation method.
- **Heritage, conservation and institutions** owns list administration, award administration and every changeable total. This session records dated inscriptions for its own forms and one current status record, and claims nothing beyond them.
- **Society** owns the socio-economic circumstances of performing communities as a livelihood question in depth. Here the community is named as the carrier, the recorded condition of the art form is stated, and the policy debate is not settled.
- **Sport and industry** are not owned here. The core record carries a circus history and an indigenous-sports list alongside this chapter's material; those are declared and routed out rather than improvised into a performance-traditions answer.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Explain what this topic owns within Indian performance, and why its boundaries with dance, music and literature must be settled before an answer begins. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model

The topic owns dramatic composition and enactment, the relation between performer and audience, puppet mechanism and manipulation, the regional folk and ritual field, the modern theatre movement and its institutions, and martial and narrative traditions in so far as they are performed.

Boundaries matter because the vocabulary is shared while ownership is not. Dance owns codified movement grammar and the recognised classical forms; here a dance-drama is owned when enacted or narrated text identifies it. Music owns raga and tala; here an instrument appears only as an identifier - the *khol*, the *Nagada*, the *kathia*, the *Mizhavu* and *Edakka*. Literature owns the authorship and text history of plays, and the classical account itself routes its list of dramas there.

Settling this first prevents two failures: teaching dance grammar under a theatre question, and attributing a play to the wrong owner.

Verdict: name the boundary once, then never re-argue it.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the owned scope stated as a list rather than a slogan 3 + three boundaries each with a concrete instance 4 + the two failures the boundary prevents 2 + a verdict that turns boundary-setting into a procedure 1.**

### Examination use

For Prelims, this lesson supplies the habit rather than the facts: read every stem for which of the six questions it is actually asking. A stem that names a community is asking about the carrier; a stem that names an instrument is asking for an identifier; a stem that names a festival is asking about occasion; a stem that names a control method is asking about mechanism. The commonest objective failure in this topic is answering a mechanism question with a regional fact.

For Mains, the lesson supplies the opening move. A directive that asks you to *discuss*, *examine* or *elucidate* an Indian performance tradition is asking for an analytical frame, and the six questions are that frame. An answer structured as text, stage relation, mechanism, carrier, occasion and status is already doing what a list of names cannot do, and it leaves a natural place for the qualification that ends a good answer.

### Traps introduced here

- Treating *Sutradhar* as one job across the Sanskrit stage, the folk stage and the puppet stage.
- Assuming that a state name in a table is an exclusive ownership claim.
- Beginning an answer with the value question instead of the evidence questions.
- Treating "classical" and "folk" as a ranking rather than as descriptions of setting and transmission.
- Importing the circus or indigenous-sport material into a performance-traditions answer because it shares a chapter.

### Revision notes

1. This topic owns **dramatic composition, enactment, stage relation, puppet mechanism, the folk and ritual field, modern theatre and its institutions, and martial and storytelling traditions as performance**.
2. Six evidence questions: **text, stage relation, mechanism, carrier, occasion, status**. The seventh - value - carries no marks.
3. ***Sutradhar*** = stage manager and director in the Sanskrit system; **narrator** in several folk forms; **puppeteer-narrator**, read as "holder of strings", in puppetry.
4. **Lokadharmi** = realistic depiction of daily life; **Natyadharmi** = conventional, stylised, overtly symbolic narration.
5. ***Rupaka*** = a dramatic composition; **ten types** named; **two** developed in the treatise.
6. ***Purva-raga*** = the pre-play ritual behind the curtain. ***Nirvahana*** = the Kutiyattam recollection of past events with which each character begins.
7. Three clocks: **text**, **practice**, **institution**. Do not merge them.
8. Institutional clock for this topic begins with a **restrictive statute in 1876**, not with an award.
9. Boundaries: dance grammar, music theory, play authorship, list and award administration, community livelihood, and sport or industry policy are each owned elsewhere.
10. A form whose **carrier** you cannot name is a form you have not identified.

### Concept check

**Question:** The word *Sutradhar* appears in three different places in this session - in the Sanskrit play, in more than one folk theatre, and in puppetry. Why is this an advantage for an examiner, and what is the one sentence about the word that is safe to write in every case?

**Model answer:** It is an advantage because a single shared word across three systems is exactly the material from which a close-option item is built. A candidate who has learned "Sutradhar means stage manager" will accept a statement that attaches the stage-manager job to a folk narrator or to a puppeteer, and a candidate who has learned "Sutradhar means narrator" will make the opposite error on the Sanskrit stage. The safe sentence is about the **function the word names rather than the office it fills**: *Sutradhar* names the figure who holds the performance together and delivers it to the audience - who, in the Sanskrit system, enters dressed in white with assistants, offers prayers, invokes blessings, summons the leading lady and announces the time and place; who, in an Assamese or Gujarati folk form, narrates the play and sings the verses, and is even known by a different name in one of them; and who, in puppetry, is the manipulator-narrator, with the word itself read as "holder of strings". The common element is *holding and delivering the performance*; the specific duties are form-specific and must be taken from the record of that form. Write the function, then the particular; never generalise the particular.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding that because the word travels, the traditions are historically connected in a single lineage. Nothing in the record establishes that the Sanskrit stage manager, the Assamese narrator and the Rajasthani puppeteer belong to one descent. A shared technical vocabulary across Indian performance is evidence of a shared conceptual world, not of a genealogy, and turning the first into the second is precisely the kind of unsupported claim that an *examine* directive is designed to catch.

## Lesson 2 - Where India's dramatic vocabulary came from, and what the earliest performance spaces can actually prove

Progress: 2/23 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: the Natya Shastra and its bracket, the Natya Veda conception, the four words for drama, and the disputed amphitheatre claim

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried - the chapter's opening statement on Sitabena and Jogimara, the Natya Shastra sentence with its date bracket and Natya Veda derivation, and the opening paragraph of the Classical Sanskrit Theatre section
CA search: "Not applicable - textual-foundation lesson with no genuine current linkage"
CA found: None found; none claimed
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: two different kinds of evidence, and what each can carry

```text
   THE QUESTION: how old is Indian theatre, and how do we know?

   ARCHAEOLOGICAL EVIDENCE            |   TEXTUAL EVIDENCE
   ---------------------------------- | ----------------------------------
   SITABENA and JOGIMARA CAVES        |   NATYA SHASTRA
   often known as the world's oldest  |   Bharat Muni
   amphitheatres; cited as showing    |   200 BC - 200 AD
   India's ancient theatre tradition  |   the FIRST TREATISE on dramaturgy
                                      |
   WHAT IT CAN CARRY:                 |   WHAT IT CAN CARRY:
   that a space existed which some    |   that a codified theory of drama
   scholars read as a performance     |   existed and was written down;
   space                              |   that it named the elements, the
                                      |   play-types and the conventions
   WHAT IT CANNOT CARRY:              |
   a settled world record; a proven   |   WHAT IT CANNOT CARRY:
   performance repertoire; a date for |   proof that every prescription was
   any named form                     |   followed on every stage, in every
                                      |   region, in every century
   >> THE LABEL IS DISPUTED. Say so.  |   >> IT IS A PRESCRIPTION. Say so.
```

The diagram is the lesson. Two different evidence types are available at the start of this topic, and each is routinely over-read in exactly one direction: the cave is over-read into a world record, and the treatise is over-read into a description of practice. Both over-readings are cheap to make and expensive to write.

### 1. The learner's actual problem

Almost every account of Indian theatre opens with the same two sentences - an ancient cave described as the world's oldest amphitheatre, and a treatise described as the first work on dramaturgy - and a learner reasonably concludes that Indian theatre has a fixed beginning and an unbroken record. Neither sentence supports that conclusion, and the way they fail is different in each case. The cave claim fails because the **label is contested**. The treatise claim fails because a **prescription is not a survey**. Understanding both failures now prevents five separate errors later in this session.

### 2. The caves, stated exactly and no further

The record notes that the **Sitabena and Jogimara caves**, often known as the **world's oldest amphitheatres**, show India's ancient theatre tradition. That is the claim as it is made, and it should be reproduced with its hedge intact.

⚠️ The popular "world's oldest amphitheatre" label is **disputed**. The arrangement at the site has been read by some as tiered audience seating and by others as serving a quite different function, such as a rest house on a route; and the inscriptional material at the neighbouring cave has been read in more than one way as well. Nothing follows from this for the rest of the topic except a discipline: **state the claim as a claim**. The formulation that survives scrutiny is "the caves are *often described* as the world's oldest amphitheatres and are cited as evidence of an ancient performance tradition, though the label is contested" - not "India has the world's oldest theatre, built at Sitabena".

Why does this matter for marks? Because a *discuss* or *critically examine* directive rewards exactly this move. A candidate who can say what a piece of evidence is usually taken to prove, and then say what it can actually carry, has demonstrated the historical judgement the directive asks for. A candidate who reproduces the slogan has demonstrated only that they read a summary.

### 3. The treatise, and the four terms it stands behind

**Bharat Muni's *Natya Shastra*** is recorded as the **first treatise on dramaturgy**, with the bracket **200 BC - 200 AD**. Its founding statement is theological rather than technical: **Brahma created the *Natya Veda* from the four Vedas**, and the treatise **details ten types of Sanskrit plays**.

Three things deserve attention in that sentence, and each is separately examinable.

1. **"First treatise on dramaturgy."** Dramaturgy is the theory and craft of dramatic composition and staging - not the writing of plays, but the rules by which plays are made and mounted. The claim is therefore about the codification of theatre, not about its beginning. Theatre existed before it was theorised; the treatise records the moment it acquired a formal grammar.
2. **The bracket, not a year.** *200 BC - 200 AD* is a four-century range. Write the range. A candidate who converts it into a single year has manufactured precision the record does not offer, and the treatise is in any case the kind of work that accumulates over time rather than appearing on a date.
3. **The *Natya Veda* derivation.** Locating drama's origin in a creation by Brahma out of the four Vedas is a **sacralising move**: it places theatre inside the authoritative knowledge system rather than outside it, and it is the reason the Sanskrit play is later described as an amalgamation of entertainment and religious traditions. The claim is doctrinal, and you should present it as the tradition's own account of itself rather than as a historical statement about origins.

### 4. The four words for drama, and why four words exist

Theatre in India **began as a narrative art form blending music, dance and acting**, and four terms are recorded for drama:

| Term | What the word emphasises |
|---|---|
| ***Nataka*** | From ***nata***, meaning **dancer** - the performer's body is in the root of the word for a play |
| ***Rupaka*** | Form or figure - drama as the *making visible* of a story in a shaped representation |
| ***Drishyakavya*** | Literally, **poetry that is seen** - the composition classed as a visible rather than a merely recited literary work |
| ***Preksakavya*** | Literally, **poetry for spectators** - the composition classed by the presence of an audience |

Why does one thing need four names? Because the four names are four different claims about what drama *is*. One derives it from dance. One derives it from representation. Two derive it from a contrast with ordinary poetry - one by the sense used, one by the audience required. Together they record a tradition that treated the play as a composite art in which music, dance and acting are blended rather than separated, and that classified it by asking what made it different from a poem.

This is also the cleanest way to answer a "what is the relationship between Indian theatre and the other arts" demand. You do not need a general statement about Indian holism. You have four terms, each of which encodes a specific relation: to dance, to visual form, to sight, and to spectatorship.

### 5. What the foundation does and does not license

The foundation licenses three things: that a codified dramatic theory existed early and was written down; that the tradition classified drama as a seen and attended composition distinct from recited poetry; and that it described drama as a blend of music, dance and acting.

It does not license: a proven continuous performance history; a claim that any named surviving form descends unbroken from the treatise; a world record for any Indian site; or the assumption that later regional theatre grew out of the treatise. The folk field taught from Lesson 9 onward has its own beginnings in the fifteenth and sixteenth centuries, and the record does not derive it from Bharat Muni. Only one living form in this topic is recorded as completely adhering to the treatise's rules, and that is the subject of Lesson 8.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**The classical tradition used four different words for drama. Elucidate what each of them claims. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model

Four terms are recorded, and each encodes a different claim about what drama is.

*Nataka* derives from *nata*, meaning dancer, so the performer's body sits inside the word for a play. *Rupaka* means form or figure, and treats drama as the making visible of a story in a shaped representation. *Drishyakavya* means poetry that is seen, classing the composition by the sense it addresses. *Preksakavya* means poetry for spectators, classing it by the audience it requires.

Read together they show a tradition defining drama against recited poetry twice - once by sense, once by spectatorship - while deriving it from dance and from representation in the other two cases. That matches the opening statement that theatre in India began as a narrative art form blending music, dance and acting.

Verdict: four names, four claims - cite the derivation, not the translation alone.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = all four terms with their exact derivations 4 + the two contrasts with recited poetry identified 3 + the link to the composite-art statement 2 + a verdict rewarding derivation over translation 1.**

### Examination use

For Prelims, the testable core is small and exact: the **author**, the **bracket 200 BC - 200 AD**, the description **first treatise on dramaturgy**, the ***Natya Veda* created by Brahma from the four Vedas**, the statement that it **details ten types of Sanskrit plays**, and the **four terms** *nataka* (from *nata*, dancer), *rupaka*, *drishyakavya* and *preksakavya*. Matching and statement items in this area are typically built on the derivation of one of the four terms or on a manufactured single-year date.

For Mains, this lesson supplies the **evidence-handling paragraph** that lifts an answer out of narration. An answer on Indian theatre's antiquity that distinguishes a contested archaeological label from a datable prescriptive text, and then says what each can carry, is doing source criticism. That is worth more than an extra named form.

### Traps introduced here

- Writing that Sitabena is the world's oldest amphitheatre as a settled fact.
- Converting the four-century bracket of the treatise into a single year.
- Treating "first treatise on dramaturgy" as "the beginning of Indian theatre".
- Taking the *Natya Veda* creation account as a historical statement rather than as the tradition's own self-description.
- Deriving the regional folk field from the treatise because both are Indian and one is older.

### Revision notes

1. **Sitabena and Jogimara caves** - often known as the **world's oldest amphitheatres**; cited as showing India's ancient theatre tradition. ⚠️ The label is **disputed**; reproduce the hedge.
2. ***Natya Shastra*** - by **Bharat Muni**; **200 BC - 200 AD**; the **first treatise on dramaturgy**.
3. It records that **Brahma created the *Natya Veda* from the four Vedas**, and it **details ten types of Sanskrit plays**.
4. Theatre in India began as a **narrative art form blending music, dance and acting**.
5. Four terms for drama: ***nataka*** (from ***nata***, dancer), ***rupaka***, ***drishyakavya*** (poetry that is seen), ***preksakavya*** (poetry for spectators).
6. **Dramaturgy** = the theory and craft of dramatic composition and staging, not the writing of plays.
7. A **prescription is not a survey**: the treatise tells you what was required, not what every stage did.
8. The foundation does **not** establish an unbroken performance history or derive the regional folk field.
9. Only **one living form** in this topic is recorded as completely adhering to the treatise's rules.
10. Safe formulation for any antiquity claim here: state what the evidence is usually taken to prove, then what it can actually carry.

### Concept check

**Question:** A stem asks you to describe the significance of the *Natya Shastra* for Indian theatre. A weak answer says it is the oldest and greatest work on Indian drama. What would a strong answer say instead, using only what this lesson has established?

**Model answer:** A strong answer would say that the treatise matters because it **codified** drama rather than because it is old. Three specific claims are available. First, it is recorded as the first treatise on dramaturgy - that is, on the theory and craft of composition and staging - which means Indian drama acquired a formal grammar at the point this text was written, within the bracket 200 BC to 200 AD. Second, it supplies the tradition's own account of drama's authority: Brahma created the *Natya Veda* from the four Vedas, which places theatre inside the recognised body of knowledge rather than outside it, and which is why the Sanskrit play is later described as an amalgamation of entertainment and religious traditions. Third, it supplies the classification that every later discussion uses, detailing ten types of plays and developing two of them, and standing behind the vocabulary in which drama was defined - *nataka* from *nata*, meaning dancer; *rupaka*; *drishyakavya*, poetry that is seen; *preksakavya*, poetry for spectators. The answer would then add the qualification that gives it its edge: a prescriptive treatise records what the tradition required, not what every region and century performed, and the folk field of this topic has beginnings of its own that the treatise does not explain.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that a strong answer must therefore *downgrade* the treatise. It should not. The text is genuinely foundational - it supplies the modal distinction, the play-type classification, the convention set, the character categories and the staging prescription that the next five lessons are built from. The move is not scepticism about the treatise; it is precision about what kind of statement a treatise makes.

## Lesson 3 - Lokadharmi, Natyadharmi, and the ten play-types: the two classifications that decide most Sanskrit-theatre questions

Progress: 3/23 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: the two dramatic modes; the ten rupakas, their names, the two the treatise develops, and the terminology risk attached to the list

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried - the Lokadharmi and Natyadharmi definitions, the Sanskrit Plays sub-section with its list of ten types, and the statement that the treatise describes only two of them
CA search: "Not applicable - classification lesson with no genuine current linkage"
CA found: None found; none claimed
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: two classifications that answer two different questions

```text
   A SANSKRIT PLAY ARRIVES. TWO QUESTIONS CAN BE ASKED OF IT.

   QUESTION 1: HOW is it represented?        QUESTION 2: WHAT kind of play is it?
   ----------------------------------        ---------------------------------
   THE TWO MODES                             THE TEN RUPAKAS
                                             Nataka       Prakarana
   LOKADHARMI                                Bhana        Prahasana
   realistic depictions of daily life        Dima         Vyayoga
        |                                    Samavakara   Vithi
        |  the axis is                       Ihamrga      Utsrishtikanka (Anka)
        |  REPRESENTATION                         |
        |                                         |  the axis is GENRE
   NATYADHARMI                                    |
   conventional plays; more stylised         >> THE NATYA SHASTRA DESCRIBES
   narration; overt symbolism                   ONLY TWO IN DETAIL:
                                                NATAKA and PRAKARANA
   >> A play is placed on this axis.         >> A play is placed in this list.
   >> Two modes, not two genres.             >> Ten genres, not two modes.

   THE ERROR THE EXAMINER WANTS:
   answering a MODE question with a GENRE answer, or reporting that the
   treatise describes ten types when it names ten and develops two.
```

### 1. The learner's actual problem

Two classifications sit within a few lines of each other in every account of Sanskrit theatre, and they are constantly fused. One sorts plays by **how reality is represented**; the other sorts them by **what kind of play it is**. They are independent: a play of any of the ten types could in principle lean realistic or stylised. Keeping them apart is the whole of this lesson, and it converts a memorised list into a usable distinction.

### 2. The two modes, stated exactly

In ancient India, plays were generally of two types.

| Mode | Definition as recorded | What it actually describes |
|---|---|---|
| ***Lokadharmi*** | **Realistic depictions of daily life** | The mode in which the stage tries to look like the world - behaviour, speech and action approximate ordinary life |
| ***Natyadharmi*** | **Conventional plays with a more stylised narration and overt symbolism** | The mode in which the stage substitutes an agreed theatrical language for ordinary behaviour, and expects the audience to read it |

The word *dharmi* in both terms means *practice* or *manner*, so the pair reads literally as "the manner of the world" against "the manner of the drama". That etymology is the fastest way to hold the distinction: one takes its rules from life, the other from the theatre itself.

**Why the distinction earns marks.** Natyadharmi is the mode that makes the rest of Indian performance legible. When a Kerala theatre carries commentary on social, philosophical and political behaviour through an elaborated convention, when a Manipuri puppet tradition presents demon characters by costume masks, when a Karnataka shadow theatre uses **puppet size to show social status** - large figures for kings and deities, smaller for commoners - these are all Natyadharmi solutions: an agreed symbolic language doing work that realistic depiction cannot do. A candidate who understands Natyadharmi as *convention that the audience is trained to read* can use the term across the entire topic. A candidate who has memorised it as "stylised" can use it nowhere.

### 3. The ten play-types, and the two that are developed

In the classical Sanskrit tradition, plays were categorised into **ten types**. The standard set, with the spellings that survive scrutiny, is:

| # | Type | Character of the type |
|---:|---|---|
| 1 | **Nataka** | The major heroic play, on a well-known theme with an exalted hero |
| 2 | **Prakarana** | A play on an invented plot, typically more domestic in setting |
| 3 | **Bhana** | A one-act piece carried by a single performer |
| 4 | **Prahasana** | The farce - comic and satirical |
| 5 | **Dima** | A play involving supernatural or divine agency |
| 6 | **Vyayoga** | A single-episode heroic piece, generally martial |
| 7 | **Samavakara** | A complex piece with many characters, gods and demons |
| 8 | **Vithi** | A slight, single-thread piece |
| 9 | **Ihamrga** | A piece turning on a fantastic or unattainable pursuit |
| 10 | **Utsrishtikanka**, also given simply as **Anka** | A serious piece built on loss or abandonment |

Two facts about this list carry more weight than the list itself.

**First: the treatise names ten and describes only two.** The *Natya Shastra* **describes only *Nataka* and *Prakarana***. This is the fact most often tested and most often stated wrongly. "The *Natya Shastra* describes ten types of plays" is a sentence that fails on its own terms; the ten are *named* in the classification and *two* are developed. If you are asked what the treatise contains, the answer has both halves.

**Second: the spellings are unstable, and the instability is examinable.** ⚠️ Printed and digitised lists of the ten types circulate with corrupted forms - *Ihamgra* for **Ihamrga**, *Vyayog* for **Vyayoga**, *Svakarna* for **Samavakara**, and *Anka* standing alone where the fuller **Utsrishtikanka** is meant. This session prints the standard forms above and records the variants here rather than silently choosing. The practical rule for an answer is simple: **write the names you are confident of, and do not reconstruct an unfamiliar spelling from a half-remembered list**. Naming six types correctly is worth more than naming ten with three corruptions, because a corrupted term signals to an examiner that the list was memorised rather than known.

### 4. How the two classifications are actually used together

Take the pair in practice. *Prahasana*, the farce, is a genre; it says nothing by itself about mode. A farce performed with broadly realistic behaviour is Lokadharmi in mode and Prahasana in genre. A farce performed through an elaborated symbolic convention - an impersonator using exaggerated types to deliver a social message, as in more than one folk form later in this session - is Natyadharmi in mode and comic in genre. Genre answers *what kind of story*; mode answers *what kind of stage language*.

That combination is also the most economical way to compare Sanskrit theatre with the folk field without ranking them. The folk field of this topic is overwhelmingly Natyadharmi in mode - masks, stock characters, impersonation, lyrical dialogue delivery, narrators who step outside the action - while carrying genres that the classical list never anticipated, such as a colonial-era maritime Christian form, a bow-song narration and a mimicry-based social satire. Same axis, different fillings. That is a comparison; "folk theatre is simpler than Sanskrit theatre" is not.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Distinguish the two dramatic modes of Sanskrit theatre from its ten play-types, and show why the distinction decides how a question should be answered. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

They answer two different questions and are independent of one another.

The **modes** answer *how reality is represented*. *Lokadharmi* is the realistic depiction of daily life; *Natyadharmi* is the conventional play, with more stylised narration and overt symbolism. *Dharmi* means manner, so the pair reads as the manner of the world against the manner of the drama.

The **play-types** answer *what kind of play it is*. Ten are named - Nataka, Prakarana, Bhana, Prahasana, Dima, Vyayoga, Samavakara, Vithi, Ihamrga and Utsrishtikanka - and the treatise develops only *Nataka* and *Prakarana*.

Independence is the point. A farce is a genre; it may be staged realistically or through an elaborated symbolic convention. Genre fixes the story, mode fixes the stage language.

The distinction decides answers in three ways. A stem asking what the treatise contains requires both halves - ten named, two described. A stem naming a symbolic device, such as a mask, a stock disguise or a size-coded shadow figure, is a mode question answered by *Natyadharmi*, not by a genre label. And a comparison with the folk field is possible only on the mode axis, because that field carries genres the classical list never anticipated.

⚠️ Two cautions: do not swap the definitions, and do not reconstruct an unfamiliar play-type spelling from memory, since the forms in circulation include *Ihamgra*, *Vyayog* and *Svakarna*.

Verdict: identify the axis a question sits on before answering it.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = both modes defined exactly with the *dharmi* etymology 4 + all ten play-types with the two-described qualification 4 + independence demonstrated on a single genre 3 + three distinct answering consequences 3 + the swap and spelling cautions 1.**

### Examination use

For Prelims, four items are directly testable: the **definition pair** with *Lokadharmi* as realistic depiction of daily life and *Natyadharmi* as conventional, stylised, overtly symbolic narration; the **number ten**; the **two developed types, Nataka and Prakarana**; and the names in the list. Expect a statement item that swaps the two definitions, or that claims the treatise describes all ten.

For Mains, the mode pair is the single most portable pair of terms in this topic. It licenses an analytical sentence about any performance tradition in the session - a mask, a stock disguise, a size-coded shadow puppet, a narrator who announces the time and place - as an instance of convention doing work that realism cannot. Use it once, precisely, rather than four times as decoration.

### Traps introduced here

- Swapping the two mode definitions, which is the cheapest error in this area.
- Writing that the *Natya Shastra* describes ten types of plays.
- Reproducing a corrupted spelling from a memorised list.
- Treating the two modes as two genres, or the ten genres as a spectrum of realism.
- Using *Natyadharmi* as a synonym for "artistic" rather than as a technical term for agreed convention.

### Revision notes

1. **Two modes**: ***Lokadharmi*** = realistic depictions of daily life; ***Natyadharmi*** = conventional plays, more stylised narration, overt symbolism.
2. *Dharmi* = manner or practice: the **manner of the world** against the **manner of the drama**.
3. **Ten play-types** (*rupakas*): Nataka, Prakarana, Bhana, Prahasana, Dima, Vyayoga, Samavakara, Vithi, Ihamrga, Utsrishtikanka (Anka).
4. The ***Natya Shastra* describes only two** of them - **Nataka** and **Prakarana**.
5. **Nataka** = major heroic play on a known theme; **Prakarana** = invented, more domestic plot.
6. ⚠️ Corrupted spellings in circulation: *Ihamgra*, *Vyayog*, *Svakarna*, bare *Anka*. Do not reconstruct an unfamiliar name.
7. The two classifications are **independent**: mode answers *how*, genre answers *what kind*.
8. **Natyadharmi** explains masks, stock disguises, size-coded shadow figures and stepping-out narrators across the whole session.
9. The folk field is predominantly **Natyadharmi in mode** while carrying genres the classical list never anticipated.
10. Six correct names beat ten names with three corruptions.

### Concept check

**Question:** A Karnataka shadow-puppet tradition uses large figures for kings and deities and smaller figures for commoners. Which of the two dramatic modes does that device belong to, and what does your answer show about how far a classical term can travel?

**Model answer:** It belongs to **Natyadharmi**. The device is not an attempt to show what kings and commoners actually look like; it is an agreed symbolic code in which a physical property of the figure - its size - stands for social status, and it works only because the audience has been trained to read it. That is precisely the definition of the conventional mode: stylised narration with overt symbolism, as against the realistic depiction of daily life. What the answer shows about the term's reach is important and easy to overstate in both directions. The term travels **analytically**: it describes a solution to a representational problem, and that solution recurs across the topic - in the mask that marks a special expression, in the disguise that defines a role-playing tradition, in the demon presented by a costume mask, in the narrator who announces time and place from outside the action. It does **not** travel **historically**: calling the shadow tradition Natyadharmi does not claim that it descends from the treatise, that its practitioners used the word, or that it belongs to the Sanskrit system. Use the term as an analytical tool and say so; do not let it smuggle in a lineage.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming that because Natyadharmi is conventional and Lokadharmi is realistic, the first is "higher art" and the second is "plain". The record defines them as two kinds of representation, not two levels of achievement, and an answer that grades them has imported a value judgement the source does not make - the same error, in miniature, as grading folk theatre below classical theatre.

## Lesson 4 - What the Sanskrit convention actually required, and what a convention proves

Progress: 4/23 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: the recorded rigid conventions - act structure, endings, protagonist, the five-part shape - and the evidentiary status of a prescriptive rule

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried - the four recorded conventions of classical Sanskrit plays, the sentence contrasting them with Greek tragedy, and the statement that the play had a defined opening, progression, development, pause and conclusion
CA search: "Not applicable - dramaturgic convention lesson with no genuine current linkage"
CA found: None found; none claimed
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the convention set, and the shape it produces

```text
   CLASSICAL SANSKRIT PLAYS WERE BOUND BY A FEW RIGID CONVENTIONS

   1. LENGTH        generally FOUR to SEVEN ACTS
   2. ENDING        ALWAYS HAPPY ENDINGS - unlike the Greek tragedies -
                    where the hero WINS or DOES NOT DIE
                    "portrayal of tragedy was almost rare"
   3. PROTAGONIST   MALE, and he ALWAYS ACHIEVES THE OBJECT OF HIS DESIRE
                    at the end
   4. SHAPE         a well-defined five-part progression:

        OPENING --> PROGRESSION --> DEVELOPMENT --> PAUSE --> CONCLUSION
           |             |               |            |           |
        the play     the action      complication   a held      restoration
        is set in    advances        deepens        moment      and close
        motion

   WHAT THIS IS:  a set of rules the tradition accepted
   WHAT IT IS NOT: a description of what every performance in every region
                   and every century actually did
```

### 1. The learner's actual problem

The convention set is short, vivid and easy to memorise, which is exactly why it is misused. Two misuses are common. The first is **comparative**: a candidate reads "unlike the Greek tragedies" and writes a paragraph asserting that Indian drama could not represent suffering. The second is **evidentiary**: a candidate treats four prescriptive rules as a report on the history of Indian performance. This lesson gives you the rules exactly, and then gives you the two sentences that stop both misuses.

### 2. The conventions, stated exactly

Classical Sanskrit plays were bound by a few **rigid conventions**:

1. They were **generally four- to seven-act plays**.
2. They **always had happy endings - unlike the Greek tragedies - where the hero wins or does not die**. **Portrayal of tragedy was almost rare.**
3. The **protagonist was male**, who would **always achieve the object of his desire at the end**.
4. The plays had a **well-defined opening, progression, development, pause and conclusion**.

Note the internal hedging in the record itself. "Always had happy endings" sits beside "portrayal of tragedy was **almost** rare" - the second sentence quietly concedes that tragedy was not wholly absent. Reproduce both. A candidate who writes only the absolute has made the record stronger than it is; a candidate who writes both has reproduced a tradition's dominant convention together with its recorded margin.

### 3. What the "happy ending" convention actually says

It is worth being precise about what the second convention claims, because it is narrower and more interesting than "Indian plays were cheerful".

The formulation is that the hero **wins or does not die**. That is a statement about **outcome**, not about **content**. A play may contain loss, exile, injustice, separation and grief and still satisfy the convention, provided the close restores the hero rather than destroying him. Indeed one of the ten play-types in the previous lesson - the serious piece built on loss or abandonment - exists precisely because the classification anticipated grave material. The convention constrains where a play may end, not what it may traverse.

That reading also explains the comparison in the record. Greek tragedy is invoked as a contrast because the Greek form permits the catastrophic close; the Sanskrit convention does not. The contrast is about **closure**, and an answer that says so is doing comparative dramaturgy. An answer that says Indian drama "avoided serious themes" has misread the convention and can be refuted from the treatise's own list of play-types.

### 4. The five-part shape, and why it is a craft rule

The fourth convention - opening, progression, development, pause and conclusion - looks decorative and is not. It is a **craft prescription about pacing**. The presence of an explicit **pause** before the conclusion is the giveaway: a tradition that names a held moment as a structural stage is a tradition thinking about how an audience's attention is managed across a four- to seven-act evening.

This is also the point at which the Sanskrit system connects to the rest of the session. Several traditions taught later have their own explicit staged structures: a Tamil Nadu string-puppet tradition whose show has **four named stages**; a Konkan drama divided into an initial part and a second, main part; an Assamese narrative form with **three named divisions**. The habit of naming the parts of a performance is widespread in Indian practice, and the classical five-part shape is the earliest recorded instance of it in this topic.

### 5. The two sentences that stop the misuse

⚠️ **Sentence one, on evidence.** These are the **conventions recorded by a prescriptive tradition**. They tell you what the tradition required of a play. They do not establish that every stage in every region and century obeyed them, and they should not be written as though they were a survey of performance history. The safe wording is "the convention recorded in the classical tradition was X", not "Sanskrit plays were always X".

⚠️ **Sentence two, on comparison.** The Greek comparison is in the record and may be used, but it is a comparison of **closure conventions**, not of emotional range or artistic seriousness. Do not extend it into a claim that Indian drama lacked tragic capacity, and do not extend it into a claim that Indian drama was morally superior for restoring order. Both extensions go beyond the evidence in opposite directions.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"The classical Sanskrit convention constrained where a play could end, not what it could contain." Examine. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model

The formulation is exact, and the record supports it on its own wording.

The convention is that plays always had happy endings, unlike the Greek tragedies, where the hero wins or does not die, and that the portrayal of tragedy was almost rare. Both clauses concern **outcome**. A play may traverse loss, exile, injustice and separation and still satisfy the rule, provided the close restores the hero rather than destroying him.

The classification corroborates it. One of the ten play-types is built on loss or abandonment, which a tradition that forbade grave material would have had no reason to name.

The Greek contrast is therefore a contrast of **closure conventions**, not of emotional range.

⚠️ Reproduce the hedge: *almost* rare is not absent.

Verdict: the rule governs the last act, and reading it as a ban on serious subjects is refutable from the treatise's own list.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the convention quoted with both of its clauses 3 + the outcome-versus-content distinction argued 3 + the play-type built on loss used as internal corroboration 2 + the Greek contrast confined to closure, with the hedge preserved 2.**

### Examination use

For Prelims, the four conventions are directly testable and so are their exact numbers: **four to seven acts**; **happy endings**; **male protagonist attaining his desire**; and the **five-part progression**. Expect statement items built on the Greek comparison or on the "almost rare" hedge.

For Mains, this lesson supplies the best available answer to a "conventions of classical Sanskrit theatre" demand and, more valuably, the qualification that converts a description into an argument. The structure that works is: state the convention set; show that the conventions are about *outcome and pacing* rather than about subject matter; then qualify the evidentiary status of a prescriptive rule. That is a complete ten-mark answer without a single decorative sentence.

### Traps introduced here

- Writing that Sanskrit plays regularly ended in tragedy, in the manner of Greek drama.
- Writing the reverse over-statement, that Indian drama could not represent suffering.
- Dropping the recorded hedge "portrayal of tragedy was almost rare".
- Presenting a prescriptive convention as a historical survey of performance.
- Forgetting the **pause** when reproducing the five-part shape, which is the part most often lost.

### Revision notes

1. Classical Sanskrit plays were bound by a few **rigid conventions**.
2. **Generally four- to seven-act plays.**
3. **Always happy endings - unlike the Greek tragedies - where the hero wins or does not die**; **portrayal of tragedy was almost rare**.
4. The **protagonist was male** and **always achieved the object of his desire at the end**.
5. Five-part shape: **opening → progression → development → pause → conclusion**.
6. The ending convention is about **closure**, not about content: loss may be traversed, not concluded with.
7. One of the ten play-types is built on **loss or abandonment**, which is why "no serious themes" is refutable from the classification itself.
8. The **pause** is a pacing device and the clearest sign that the rules are craft rules.
9. ⚠️ A prescriptive convention is **not a survey** of what every stage did.
10. The Greek contrast compares **closure conventions**, not emotional range or artistic worth.

### Concept check

**Question:** The record says Sanskrit plays "always had happy endings" and, two lines later, that "portrayal of tragedy was almost rare". Those two sentences are not identical in force. How should a candidate handle the difference, and what does the handling demonstrate?

**Model answer:** The candidate should **reproduce both and name the difference**, rather than choosing the stronger sentence because it is easier to write. The first sentence states an absolute convention; the second states a strong tendency with an explicit margin - *almost* rare, not rare. Read together, they describe a tradition in which the catastrophic close was ruled out as a convention while grave material was neither impossible nor absent, and that reading is independently supported by the classification itself, which contains a play-type built on loss or abandonment. The correct formulation for an answer is therefore something like: "the classical convention required the hero to win or at least not to die, and the portrayal of tragedy was accordingly almost rare - a constraint on the ending rather than on the subject matter." What the handling demonstrates is source discipline. An examiner reading a sentence that preserves an internal hedge can see that the candidate has read the record rather than a slogan derived from it, and that is exactly the quality a *discuss* or *critically examine* directive is scanning for.

**Misconception to avoid:** Turning the qualification into scepticism about the convention. The convention is real, it is recorded as rigid, and it genuinely distinguishes the Sanskrit system from the Greek. The move is not to doubt it but to state it at the right strength - and to keep it as a statement about what the tradition required rather than a claim about what every performance did.

## Lesson 5 - The stage and the ritual opening: Sutradhar, Purva-raga, two storeys, curtains - and the sentence about masks that is misused more than any other

Progress: 5/23 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the ritualistic progression of a Sanskrit performance, the recorded physical stage, and the exact scope of the mask statement

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried - the ritualistic progression sequence, the Sutradhar's entry and functions, the recorded capacity and two-storeyed stage, the curtain statement and the mask statement
CA search: "Not applicable - staging lesson with no genuine current linkage"
CA found: None found; none claimed
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the performance as a sequence, and the house as a diagram

```text
   A. THE RITUAL PROGRESSION - what happens before the story starts

   [1] PURVA-RAGA                pre-play rituals performed BEHIND THE CURTAIN
            |
            v
   [2] SUTRADHAR ENTERS          dressed in WHITE, with ASSISTANTS
            |
            v
   [3] PRAYERS OFFERED           blessings invoked
            |
            v
   [4] LEADING LADY SUMMONED     by the Sutradhar
            |
            v
   [5] TIME AND PLACE ANNOUNCED  by the Sutradhar
            |
            v
   [6] ------------------------ THE PLAY BEGINS ------------------------

   B. THE HOUSE, AS RECORDED BY BHARATA

        +---------------------------------------------------+
        |   UPPER STOREY  =  THE CELESTIAL SPHERE           |
        +---------------------------------------------------+
        |   LOWER STOREY  =  THE TERRESTRIAL SPHERE         |
        +---------------------------------------------------+
        |   CURTAINS used to INTENSIFY THE IMPACT of the play|
        |   MASKS  NOT  USED                                 |
        +---------------------------------------------------+
              capacity, as per Bharata: AROUND 400 PERSONS
```

Read the first diagram as a sequence and notice where the story is. It is at step six. Five complete stages of performance happen before any narrative begins, and four of the five are the business of one figure. That is the lesson's first point: in this system, **performance begins as ritual, not as narrative**.

### 1. The learner's actual problem

A learner who knows that a Sanskrit play opened with a Sutradhar usually knows it as a piece of trivia. It is not trivia; it is the structural fact that explains what kind of event a Sanskrit performance was. And a learner who knows that masks were not used usually over-generalises that sentence across the whole of Indian theatre, which is the single most expensive error available in this topic. Both problems are handled here.

### 2. The ritual progression, stated exactly

Sanskrit plays followed an **almost ritualistic progression**:

1. Plays began with **pre-play rituals (*Purva-raga*) performed behind the curtain**.
2. The ***Sutradhar*** - the **stage manager and director** - **dressed in white**, **entered with assistants**, **offered prayers** and **invoked blessings**.
3. After that, the **leading lady was summoned** and the **time and place of the play were announced by the Sutradhar**.

Three observations convert this from a list into understanding.

**The curtain is doing work before it is doing anything visible.** The pre-play ritual happens *behind* the curtain, which means the curtain is already a functional part of the theatre before the audience has seen a performer. A concealed preliminary that the audience knows is happening is a way of marking the space as consecrated.

**White is a signal, not a costume note.** The Sutradhar enters dressed in white and performs priest-like functions - prayer and the invocation of blessings. The figure who manages the stage is presented in the visual language of ritual officiation. That is why the play is later described as an **amalgamation of entertainment and religious traditions**: the description is not a generalisation about Indian religiosity but a report of what the opening sequence literally does.

**Announcing time and place is a technical solution.** A stage with no scenery must locate its action verbally. The Sutradhar's announcement of time and place is the device that does it, and it is the same solution that reappears across this topic wherever a narrator stands outside the action - the Assamese *Sutradhar* who narrates the play and sings verses from holy texts, the Odia narrator-pair of a chief singer and a co-narrator, the Andhra main artist supported by two co-artists. A stage that cannot build a place must speak one.

### 3. The house, as recorded

| Element | As recorded | What it implies |
|---|---|---|
| **Capacity** | The theatre, **as per Bharata**, could accommodate **around 400 persons** | A substantial but bounded audience - large enough for public performance, small enough for subtle facial and vocal work to register |
| **Storeys** | Stages were **two-storeyed**: the **upper for the celestial sphere**, the **lower for the terrestrial sphere** | Vertical space is semantically assigned. Height is not decoration; it is the location of a category of being |
| **Curtains** | **Curtains were used to intensify the impact of the play** | The curtain is an expressive instrument - concealment and revelation used for effect - not merely a boundary |
| **Masks** | **Masks were not used** | In this system, the face itself carries expression |

The two-storeyed stage is the most analytically useful of the four, because it shows that the classical stage encoded meaning in **architecture**. If the upper level is the celestial sphere and the lower the terrestrial, then a change of level is a change of world, achieved without a word of dialogue. That is Natyadharmi convention built into the building.

⚠️ **The evidentiary caution.** All four are recorded as **Bharata's prescription**. They are not an archaeological description of every Sanskrit stage ever built. Write "as per Bharata, the theatre could accommodate around four hundred persons" rather than "Sanskrit theatres held four hundred people". The record itself supplies the attribution; keep it.

### 4. The mask sentence, and the exact limit of its scope

Here is the sentence that causes more damage than any other in this topic: **masks were not used**.

It is true, and it is true **of the classical Sanskrit staging system described in that passage**. It is not true of Indian theatre generally, and the same chapter that records it also records, within a few pages, the following:

- an **Assamese one-act form** in which one of the **unique features is the use of masks to depict special expressions**;
- a **Garhwal ritual theatre** in which people of a named caste **wear the sacred mask symbolising Narasimha**, the half-man half-lion;
- a **Manipuri puppet tradition** in which **demon characters are presented by costume masks**;
- a **mask-making practice attached to Chhau**, recognised as a distinct field of activity in its own right;
- a **Kerala open-air ritual theatre** whose actors wear **elaborate headgear and colourful costumes**;
- a **Kerala glove-puppet form** whose figures have **colourful headgear, feathers and face paint**.

So the correct formulation is narrow and must stay narrow: **in the classical Sanskrit staging system as recorded, masks were not used; mask use elsewhere in Indian performance is form-specific and must be taken from the record of that form**. A candidate who writes "Indian theatre did not use masks" can be refuted six times over from the same chapter, and a candidate who writes "Indian theatre is famous for its masks" has lost the one precise fact the classical passage supplies.

This is a good moment to name the general discipline, because it recurs: **a statement about one system is not a statement about a tradition**. The mask sentence, the four-hundred-person capacity, the two-storeyed stage and the happy-ending convention are all statements about the classical Sanskrit system. Each of them is true there and portable nowhere.

### 5. What the whole staging apparatus adds up to

Put the pieces together and a coherent theatre emerges. It is a **consecrated** space, opened by ritual and by a white-clad officiant. It is **verbally located**, because the stage supplies no scenery and the announcement supplies the setting. It is **vertically semantic**, with the divine assigned upward. It is **expressively curtained**, using concealment for impact. And it is **unmasked**, which means the actor's own face is the instrument of expression, which in turn explains why the character categories of the next lesson are defined by temperament and type rather than by external emblem.

That last link matters. An unmasked theatre must classify its characters **internally** - by personality, social role and language register - because it cannot classify them externally by mask. Lesson 6 is the direct consequence of the last row of the table above.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Discuss the recorded staging of classical Sanskrit theatre as a system in which ritual, architecture and speech do the work that scenery does elsewhere. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

Three devices carry the load, and none of them is scenery.

**Ritual opens the event.** Pre-play rituals, *Purva-raga*, are performed behind the curtain; the *Sutradhar*, dressed in white, enters with assistants, offers prayers and invokes blessings; only then is the leading lady summoned. Five stages of performance precede any narrative, and four belong to one figure presented in the visual language of priestly officiation - which is why the Sanskrit play is described as an amalgamation of entertainment and religious traditions.

**Architecture carries meaning.** As per Bharata the theatre could accommodate around four hundred persons, and stages were two-storeyed, the upper for the celestial sphere and the lower for the terrestrial. A change of level is a change of world, achieved without a word. Curtains, used to intensify the impact of the play, apply the same principle horizontally.

**Speech supplies the setting.** The *Sutradhar* announces the time and place. A stage that cannot build a location must state one - the solution that reappears wherever a narrator stands outside the action elsewhere in Indian performance.

Masks complete the design by their absence: in this system they were not used, so the face carries expression and character must be classified internally, by temperament, station and language register.

⚠️ These are Bharata's prescriptions, not excavated buildings; keep the attribution.

Verdict: ritual consecrates, architecture signifies, speech locates - and the actor's own face does the rest.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the five-stage ritual opening in order with the Sutradhar's recorded actions 5 + capacity, two storeys and curtains read as architectural meaning 5 + the verbal location device and its wider recurrence 3 + the mask absence linked to internal character classification 1 + the prescription attribution preserved 1.**

### Examination use

For Prelims, the testable set is exact and compact: ***Purva-raga*** performed **behind the curtain**; the ***Sutradhar*** as **stage manager and director**, **dressed in white**, entering **with assistants**, offering **prayers**, invoking **blessings**, **summoning the leading lady** and **announcing time and place**; **around 400 persons** as per Bharata; **two-storeyed** stages with **upper celestial** and **lower terrestrial**; **curtains used to intensify impact**; **masks not used**. Every one of these is a plausible statement-item component, and the mask item is the most likely to be built as a trap by pairing it with a masked folk form.

For Mains, this lesson supplies a complete "conventions and staging" block and, more importantly, the transition sentence that a good answer needs: the classical stage is ritual before it is narrative, and its architecture carries meaning. Add the evidentiary caution and the answer is already qualified.

### Traps introduced here

- Extending "masks were not used" to Indian theatre as a whole.
- Dropping "as per Bharata" and presenting the capacity and storeys as archaeological fact.
- Attributing the summoning of the leading lady or the announcement of time and place to someone other than the Sutradhar.
- Reversing the two storeys - assigning the celestial sphere to the lower level.
- Treating the curtain as a mere boundary rather than as a device used to intensify impact.

### Revision notes

1. **Ritualistic progression**: *Purva-raga* behind the curtain → **Sutradhar** in white with assistants → prayers and blessings → **leading lady summoned** → **time and place announced** → play begins.
2. ***Sutradhar*** here = **stage manager / director**.
3. Capacity **as per Bharata**: **around 400 persons**.
4. Stages were **two-storeyed**: **upper = celestial sphere**, **lower = terrestrial sphere**.
5. **Curtains** were used **to intensify the impact of the play**.
6. **Masks were not used** - ⚠️ in the **classical Sanskrit system only**.
7. Masks appear elsewhere in this very topic: an **Assamese one-act form** (special expressions), a **Garhwal ritual theatre** (Narasimha mask, named caste), a **Manipuri puppet tradition** (demon characters), **Chhau mask making**, and headgear-and-face-paint conventions in Kerala forms.
8. The Sanskrit play is described as an **amalgamation of entertainment and religious traditions** - a report of the opening sequence, not a generalisation.
9. Announcing time and place is the **verbal solution** to a stage without scenery; narrators elsewhere in the topic solve the same problem.
10. An **unmasked** theatre must classify characters **internally** - which is why the next lesson is about type and register.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why does it matter, analytically rather than merely factually, that the classical Sanskrit stage was two-storeyed with the upper level assigned to the celestial sphere?

**Model answer:** It matters because it shows that the classical theatre encoded meaning in **architecture**, and therefore that its conventional mode operated at the level of the building and not only at the level of acting. On a stage with no scenery, where the setting has to be announced verbally by the Sutradhar, a permanently available vertical distinction gives the performance a second signifying system that costs nothing to deploy: a character who appears above is, by the convention of the house, of the celestial sphere, and a shift of level is a shift of world without a line of dialogue. That is Natyadharmi convention made structural. It also connects to two other recorded features. The curtain, used to intensify impact, is the same principle applied horizontally - concealment and revelation as expressive resources built into the fabric of the theatre. And the absence of masks tells you where the remaining expressive load falls: on the face and body of the performer. A stage that carries cosmology in its levels and emphasis in its curtain can leave character entirely to the actor. Read together, the four staging facts describe a coherent design rather than four pieces of trivia.
