**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the two-storeyed stage as an archaeologically attested building type. The record attributes these features to Bharata's prescription, and the correct wording preserves that attribution. The analytical point about architectural meaning holds as an account of what the tradition prescribed and why; it does not license a claim about excavated theatres.

## Lesson 6 - Nayaka, Nayika, Vidusaka: character type, and the moment language itself becomes a stage device

Progress: 6/23 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the three character categories with their sub-types, the Vidusaka's function, and the Sanskrit-Prakrit register split

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried - the three-fold character classification, the Nayaka personality types and the Pratinayaka, the Nayika categories, the Vidusaka description and the language-register statement
CA search: "Not applicable - character-system lesson with no genuine current linkage"
CA found: None found; none claimed
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: three categories, their sub-types, and the register that cuts across them

```text
   CHARACTERS IN SANSKRIT PLAYS - BROADLY CLASSIFIED INTO THREE KINDS

   NAYAKA (hero, protagonist)          NAYIKA (heroine)        VIDUSAKA (clown)
   played by MALES                     played by FEMALES       the comic character
        |                                   |                       |
   personality types:                  categories:              WHO HE IS:
     LALITA   - kind-hearted             queens                 noble and
     SHANTA   - calm and composed        friends                good-hearted;
     UDDHATA  - agitated or arrogant     courtesans (GANIKA)    often a FRIEND
        ...and others                    divine lady (DIVYA)    OF THE HERO
        |                                                           |
   may also be a                                                WHAT HE DOES:
     PRATINAYAKA (antagonist)                                   QUESTIONS THE
     e.g. RAVANA, DURYODHANA                                    PREVAILING SOCIAL
                                                                NORMS THROUGH
                                                                SATIRE
   ------------------------------------------------------------------------
   THE REGISTER LINE, CUTTING ACROSS ALL THREE:

        VIDUSAKA spoke in PRAKRIT        |        OTHERS spoke in SANSKRIT
   ------------------------------------------------------------------------
   >> the one character licensed to criticise is the one who speaks the
      everyday language. The stage encodes social position IN LANGUAGE.
```

### 1. The learner's actual problem

The three character categories are usually learned as a vocabulary list - hero, heroine, clown - and the list is then useless, because every dramatic tradition on earth has heroes, heroines and comic figures. What makes the Sanskrit system examinable and analytically interesting is not the three names but the **sub-types**, the **function assigned to the clown**, and the **language rule**. Those three are where the marks are.

### 2. The three categories, stated exactly

Characters in Sanskrit plays **were important** and were **broadly classified into three kinds**: ***Nayaka*** (hero or protagonist), ***Nayika*** (heroine) and the ***Vidusaka*** (clown).

**The Nayaka.** Played by **males**, and recorded as being of **different personalities**:

| Type | Recorded sense |
|---|---|
| ***Lalita*** | kind-hearted |
| ***Shanta*** | calm and composed |
| ***Uddhata*** | agitated or arrogant |

The record gives these as examples rather than as a closed set, and the phrasing should be preserved: these are types "like" *Lalita*, *Shanta* and *Uddhata*. A hero may also be a ***Pratinayaka*** - an **antagonist** - such as **Ravana** or **Duryodhana**.

Two things are worth noticing. First, the typology is **temperamental**, not moral: kind-hearted, calm, agitated. A classification of protagonists by disposition is what you would expect in a theatre whose emotional apparatus is developed in detail. Second, the *Pratinayaka* category is genuinely unusual and often misread. It does not simply mean "villain"; it is presented within the discussion of the *Nayaka*, as a variant of the protagonist category. The named instances - Ravana, Duryodhana - are figures who occupy the structural position of the hero in their own frame of the story while opposing the hero of the play.

**The Nayika.** Played by **females**, and recorded as being **queens**, **friends**, **courtesans (*Ganika*)** and **divine lady (*Divya*)**. This is a classification by **social station** rather than by temperament - which is a real asymmetry with the *Nayaka* typology and worth stating if an answer has room for it.

**The Vidusaka.** The **comic character**, who **plays a vital role in the plays**. He is **noble and good-hearted**, **often a friend of the hero**, and he **questions the prevailing social norms through satire**.

### 3. Why the Vidusaka is the most important of the three

If a stem asks about any one character category in this system, the *Vidusaka* is the likeliest, and he deserves the space.

He is not simply comic relief. Three recorded attributes define him and each carries weight.

1. **He is noble and good-hearted.** The clown is not a figure of contempt. The tradition assigns him moral standing.
2. **He is often a friend of the hero.** He is inside the protagonist's circle, not outside the action - which is what makes his criticism audible within the play rather than merely addressed to the audience.
3. **He questions the prevailing social norms through satire.** This is a licensed critical function. The play contains a character whose job is to interrogate the norms the play otherwise operates within.

Put those three together and the *Vidusaka* is the classical theatre's **built-in critical voice**: morally credible, socially proximate to power, and licensed to satirise. That is a claim with real analytical value in a Mains answer about theatre and social comment, and it is drawn entirely from the record rather than from interpretation.

### 4. The register rule, and what it encodes

**Traditionally, the *Vidusaka* spoke in Prakrit while others spoke in Sanskrit.**

This one sentence is the most analytically productive line in the classical section, and it is worth unpacking carefully.

Sanskrit is the high, learned register. Prakrit denotes the more everyday languages in use. Assigning them to different characters on the same stage means that **social position is marked by language** in performance. The audience hears who someone is before it is told. The stage becomes bilingual, and the bilingualism is a dramatic device rather than an accident of composition.

Now notice what is being encoded. The character who is **licensed to criticise** is also the character who speaks the **everyday language**. Whether or not the tradition intended the connection, the structure is striking: critique enters the classical stage in the vernacular register and is placed in the mouth of a morally sound insider. An answer that observes this - carefully, as an observation about the structure rather than as a claim about anyone's intention - is doing precisely the analytical work that distinguishes a fifteen-mark answer from a ten-mark one.

The register rule also has a long reach across this session. Kutiyattam, the surviving classical lineage, is performed in **Sanskrit, Prakrit and Malayalam** - three registers on one stage, and the persistence of exactly the classical bilingualism plus a regional language. An Assamese one-act tradition is written in a purpose-made **mixed Assamese-Maithili language called *Brajavali***. A Goan form is performed mainly in the **Roman Konkani dialects**. A Malwa form delivers its dialogue in **couplets called *Rangat Dohas***. Language choice as a performance decision is a thread that runs from the classical stage through the whole regional field.

### 5. The amalgamation statement

The section closes by recording that the Sanskrit play **became an amalgamation of entertainment and religious traditions**, and it routes its list of ancient Sanskrit dramas to the literature owner. Both halves matter for an answer. The first is the summary judgement the record itself offers, and it is well earned by the material: a ritual opening with a white-clad officiant offering prayers, a divine derivation of the art form, a cosmologically divided stage - and, simultaneously, a comic satirist, a courtesan character category and a closure convention designed to satisfy an audience. The second is a boundary: the plays themselves, their authors and their texts belong to the literature topic, and this session does not reproduce that list.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Examine the character system of classical Sanskrit drama as a means of marking social position on stage. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The system marks position twice - once by category and once by language.

**By category.** Characters are broadly classified into three kinds. The *Nayaka*, played by males, is typed by temperament - *Lalita*, kind-hearted; *Shanta*, calm and composed; *Uddhata*, agitated or arrogant - and may be a *Pratinayaka*, an antagonist such as Ravana or Duryodhana. The *Nayika*, played by females, is classified by **station** rather than temperament: queens, friends, courtesans or *Ganika*, and the divine lady or *Divya*. The asymmetry is itself informative - men sorted by disposition, women by place.

**By language.** The *Vidusaka* traditionally spoke in Prakrit while others spoke in Sanskrit. Register is therefore audible social position, and the stage is deliberately bilingual.

The two markings meet in one figure. The *Vidusaka* is the comic character with a vital role: noble and good-hearted, often a friend of the hero, and he questions the prevailing social norms through satire. The character licensed to criticise is also the one who speaks the everyday register, so critique enters in the vernacular from a morally sound insider.

⚠️ Two limits. This describes the **structure** of a convention, not anyone's intention; and it is not evidence that Sanskrit drama was a reform vehicle. That claim belongs to the colonial stage, which named dowry, caste and religious hypocrisy directly.

Verdict: read category and register together, or the system's sharpest feature disappears.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the three categories with all named sub-types 5 + the temperament-versus-station asymmetry identified 3 + the register rule stated exactly 3 + the interaction argued in the Vidusaka 3 + two limits separating structure from intention and from reform 1.**

### Examination use

For Prelims, the exact testable set is: **three categories**; **Nayaka played by males** with **Lalita (kind-hearted)**, **Shanta (calm and composed)**, **Uddhata (agitated or arrogant)** and the **Pratinayaka** with **Ravana and Duryodhana** as instances; **Nayika played by females** as **queens, friends, courtesans (Ganika), divine lady (Divya)**; **Vidusaka** as the **noble, good-hearted comic friend who questions social norms through satire**; and the **Prakrit-Sanskrit register split**. The register line is the single most likely component of a statement item here.

For Mains, this lesson gives you two distinct usable arguments. The first is the *Vidusaka* as a licensed critical voice - deployable in any answer about theatre and social commentary, including the modern-theatre material of Lessons 14 and 15. The second is language as a stage device - deployable in any answer about theatre and society, and extendable across Kutiyattam's three languages, *Brajavali*, Roman Konkani and *Rangat Dohas*.

### Traps introduced here

- Treating *Pratinayaka* as a separate fourth category rather than as a variant within the *Nayaka* discussion.
- Assigning Prakrit to the heroine or to the hero rather than to the *Vidusaka*.
- Describing the *Vidusaka* as a low or contemptible figure; the record calls him noble and good-hearted.
- Confusing the temperamental *Nayaka* typology with the social-station *Nayika* categories.
- Attributing named plays or playwrights to this topic instead of routing them to the literature owner.

### Revision notes

1. **Three character kinds**: ***Nayaka*** (hero/protagonist), ***Nayika*** (heroine), ***Vidusaka*** (clown).
2. **Nayaka**: played by **males**; types like ***Lalita*** (kind-hearted), ***Shanta*** (calm and composed), ***Uddhata*** (agitated or arrogant).
3. A hero may also be a ***Pratinayaka*** (**antagonist**), such as **Ravana** or **Duryodhana**.
4. **Nayika**: played by **females**; **queens, friends, courtesans (*Ganika*), divine lady (*Divya*)**.
5. **Vidusaka**: comic character with a **vital role**; **noble and good-hearted**; **often a friend of the hero**; **questions prevailing social norms through satire**.
6. **Register rule**: the **Vidusaka spoke in Prakrit**; **others spoke in Sanskrit**.
7. The *Nayaka* typology is **temperamental**; the *Nayika* categories are by **social station**.
8. The play became an **amalgamation of entertainment and religious traditions**.
9. Named plays and playwrights of the classical period belong to the **literature owner**, not here.
10. Language-as-device recurs across the session: **Kutiyattam's Sanskrit, Prakrit and Malayalam**; ***Brajavali***; **Roman Konkani**; ***Rangat Dohas***.

### Concept check

**Question:** The *Vidusaka* is noble and good-hearted, is often the hero's friend, questions prevailing social norms through satire, and speaks Prakrit while everyone else speaks Sanskrit. Assemble those four facts into a single analytical claim, and then state the limit of the claim.

**Model answer:** The claim is that the classical Sanskrit stage contained a **structurally licensed critical voice, and located it in the everyday register**. Each of the four facts contributes a necessary element. Nobility of character gives the critic moral credibility, so his satire cannot be dismissed as the malice of a base figure. Friendship with the hero places him inside the circle of the play's power, so his criticism is delivered at close range and must be heard by those it concerns. The explicit function of questioning prevailing social norms through satire makes the critique institutional rather than incidental - it is what the character is for. And the register split means the criticism arrives in the language of ordinary use while the rest of the stage speaks the learned language, so the critical voice is audibly marked as coming from outside the high register even though it is spoken by an insider to power. That is a precise, evidenced account of how a dramatic tradition builds dissent into its own form. The **limit** is equally important: this is a statement about the *structure* of the convention, not about anyone's intentions, and not a claim that Sanskrit drama was a vehicle of social reform. The material for a genuine reform claim appears much later in this topic, in the colonial and post-colonial stage that addressed dowry, caste and religious hypocrisy directly and was legislated against for its political content.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading the Prakrit assignment as a mark of the *Vidusaka*'s inferiority and stopping there. The register does mark social position on stage - that much is the device - but the same character is recorded as noble and good-hearted with a vital role. An answer that treats the language marker as a simple demotion has missed the tension that makes the convention interesting: the tradition gave its most socially penetrating lines to a figure it deliberately made both lower in register and higher in character.

## Lesson 7 - Why Sanskrit theatre declined, and what kind of decline it was

Progress: 7/23 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the three recorded causes, the mechanism behind each, and the discipline of naming a decline type

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried - the sub-section on reasons for the decline of Sanskrit theatre, with its three listed causes, read together with the enrichment treatment of decline as an analytical category
CA search: "Not applicable - historical-causation lesson; the one genuine current linkage in this session is placed where it belongs, in Lesson 22"
CA found: None found; none claimed
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: three causes, three different kinds of cause

```text
   REASONS FOR THE DECLINE OF SANSKRIT THEATRE - AS RECORDED

   CAUSE 1 : AUTHORIAL
   "Writers moved from writing plays to writing poems and songs."
        -> the SUPPLY of new work contracts
        -> a form starved of new composition lives on revival alone

   CAUSE 2 : LINGUISTIC + FORMAL
   "Strict rules and difficult Sanskrit made theatre less popular,
    while Pali and Prakrit became common."
        -> TWO mechanisms inside one sentence:
              (a) the rules were demanding  -> a barrier to making
              (b) the language grew distant -> a barrier to receiving
        -> the AUDIENCE contracts at the same time as the makers

   CAUSE 3 : PATRONAGE
   "Under Muslim rulers, dance and music were patronised more than theatre."
        -> RESOURCES move to neighbouring arts
        -> not suppression: REDIRECTION within the performing arts

   +--------------------------------------------------------------+
   |  THREE DIFFERENT KINDS OF CAUSE, NOT THREE VERSIONS OF ONE.   |
   |  production  |  reception  |  patronage                       |
   |  They are NOT mutually exclusive and they reinforce each other|
   +--------------------------------------------------------------+
```

### 1. The learner's actual problem

"Why did Sanskrit theatre decline" is the single most likely Mains-shaped demand in the classical half of this topic, and the weak answer to it is a paragraph about the general decay of ancient Indian culture. The record offers something much better: **three specific, differently-typed causes**, each of which names a mechanism. A candidate who can sort them into production, reception and patronage has an argument rather than a lament.

### 2. The three causes, stated exactly

The reasons for the decline of Sanskrit theatre include the following:

1. **Writers moved from writing plays to writing poems and songs.**
2. **Strict rules and difficult Sanskrit made theatre less popular, while Pali and Prakrit became common.**
3. **Under Muslim rulers, dance and music were patronised more than theatre.**

That is the list. Notice how much is packed into it and how little of it is vague.

### 3. What each cause actually claims

**Cause one is about production.** A dramatic tradition needs a continuous supply of new compositions. If the literary energy of a culture migrates into other genres - poems and songs - then theatre is not attacked; it is *abandoned by its makers*. This is a supply-side explanation and it is the most easily overlooked. It also has a tidy connection to Lesson 3: the tradition's own classification recognised ten play-types, several of them highly specific; a form with that much internal specialisation depends on writers willing to work inside it.

**Cause two is about reception, and it contains two mechanisms.** Read the sentence again: "**strict rules and difficult Sanskrit** made theatre less popular, while **Pali and Prakrit became common**." The first clause names two separate barriers - the formal difficulty of the conventions and the linguistic difficulty of the medium. The second clause names the alternative: other languages were in common use. So the audience is not merely shrinking; it is shifting to a linguistic environment in which the form's medium is no longer the ordinary one. This is the most powerful of the three causes and the one that connects best to the rest of the session, because it explains why the regional field of Lesson 9 onwards is composed in regional languages and registers.

**Cause three is about patronage, and it must be stated carefully.** The record says that under Muslim rulers, **dance and music were patronised more than theatre**. Two disciplines apply. First, this is a statement of **comparative patronage within the performing arts** - a redirection of resources towards neighbouring forms - and not a statement about suppression or prohibition. Write it as the record writes it. Second, it is one of three causes listed together, and the record does not rank them or claim that any one is sufficient. ⚠️ Present the three as the reasons the record supplies, note that they are **not mutually exclusive**, and resist converting a comparative patronage observation into a single-cause political explanation.

### 4. The discipline: name the kind of decline

Here is the move that lifts this lesson out of the classical section and makes it useful across the whole topic.

"Decline" is not one thing. There are at least two structurally different kinds in this session, and they require different analysis and different responses.

| | **Sanskrit court theatre** | **Puppetry, as recorded now** |
|---|---|---|
| **When** | A historical process, already worked through | A **current** condition, described in the present tense |
| **Stated causes** | Authorial migration; formal and linguistic difficulty; comparative patronage shift | **Lack of funding and captive audience** |
| **Evidence type** | Textual and historical | Living-practice evidence, including a named and dated recognition |
| **What survives** | A living lineage - Kutiyattam - plus the texts and the theory | The skills and the traditions, with named practitioners still working |
| **What a response would mean** | Documentation, transmission, training, performance spaces for the surviving lineage | Funding and audience: the two things the record names as lacking |

⚠️ Two cautions on this table, both of which an examiner can test. First, do **not** write that Sanskrit theatre died out. It did not: the oldest continuing form of theatre in India is a Sanskrit-adhering lineage that has survived since the tenth century AD, and it is the subject of the next lesson. Historical contraction and living continuity coexist. Second, do **not** generalise the puppetry condition into "traditional arts are dying". The record names two specific causes for one art form; a general lament is not an argument and cannot be acted on.

This two-decline distinction is the highest-value analytical move available in this topic and it is developed fully in Lesson 22, where the safeguarding material sits.

### 5. What the decline did not touch

One more qualification, because it is the kind that examiners reward. The contraction of court Sanskrit theatre did not empty the Indian stage. The folk field recorded in this chapter **begins in the fifteenth and sixteenth centuries** - that is, on a clock that runs alongside and after the classical contraction, not out of it. Performance in India did not stop and restart; a court form contracted while a regional field developed with its own devotional beginnings, its own languages and its own carriers. An answer that says "Sanskrit theatre declined and Indian theatre revived in the colonial period" has deleted four centuries of the record.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Sort the recorded causes of the decline of Sanskrit theatre by the kind of cause each one names, and state what follows. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model

Three causes are recorded and they are of three different kinds.

**Production.** Writers moved from writing plays to writing poems and songs - a supply failure, in which a form is abandoned by its makers rather than attacked.

**Reception.** Strict rules and difficult Sanskrit made theatre less popular, while Pali and Prakrit became common. One clause names two barriers, formal and linguistic, and the second names the alternative environment.

**Patronage.** Under Muslim rulers, dance and music were patronised more than theatre - a redirection of resources within the performing arts, not suppression.

Three things follow: the causes reinforce one another rather than compete; the record ranks none of them; and the reception cause explains why the later regional field composes in regional languages.

Verdict: name the mechanism, not the mood - and never write that the form became extinct.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = three causes reproduced exactly 3 + each sorted to production, reception or patronage 3 + the two mechanisms inside the reception clause 2 + the non-suppression reading and the non-extinction caution 2.**

### Examination use

For Prelims, the three causes are directly testable and the likeliest trap is a statement item that converts the patronage clause into a claim about prohibition, or that adds a fourth cause such as foreign invasion or the loss of temples.

For Mains, this lesson is the spine of any decline-and-survival demand. The structure that works is: three causes, sorted by type; the observation that they reinforce one another; the refusal to declare the form extinct, evidenced by the surviving lineage; and the distinction between a historical contraction and a current, documented pressure. That is a full fifteen-mark answer whose entire content is recorded fact plus one analytical distinction.

### Traps introduced here

- Writing that Sanskrit theatre died out or became extinct.
- Converting "dance and music were patronised more than theatre" into suppression or prohibition of theatre.
- Adding unrecorded causes to the list of three.
- Treating the three causes as competing explanations rather than as reinforcing ones.
- Collapsing the classical contraction and the present-day puppetry pressure into one generic decline narrative.

### Revision notes

1. **Three recorded causes**: writers moved from plays to **poems and songs**; **strict rules and difficult Sanskrit** reduced popularity while **Pali and Prakrit became common**; under **Muslim rulers**, **dance and music were patronised more than theatre**.
2. The causes are of **three different types**: **production**, **reception**, **patronage**.
3. Cause two contains **two** mechanisms: formal difficulty and linguistic distance.
4. Cause three is **comparative patronage within the performing arts**, not suppression.
5. The three are **not mutually exclusive** and the record does not rank them.
6. ⚠️ Sanskrit theatre is **not extinct**: **Kutiyattam** is India's **oldest continuing form of theatre**, surviving since the **10th century AD**.
7. **Two kinds of decline**: a **completed historical contraction** against a **current, documented** pressure.
8. Puppetry's recorded present-day causes are **lack of funding and captive audience** - specific, and therefore actionable.
9. Most **folk theatre begins in the 15th-16th century**, on a clock beside the classical contraction, not out of it.
10. Do not write a generic "traditional arts are dying" conclusion; name the mechanism the record names.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate writes: "Sanskrit theatre died out because of foreign invasions, and Indian theatre was only revived under the British." Identify every error in that sentence and give the corrected version.

**Model answer:** There are four errors. First, **"died out"** is false: the oldest continuing form of theatre in India is a Sanskrit-adhering tradition that has survived in Kerala since the tenth century AD, completely adhering to the treatise's rules, and it is performed to this day. Second, **"foreign invasions"** is not among the recorded causes; the record names authorial migration to poems and songs, the twin barrier of strict rules and difficult Sanskrit alongside the commonness of Pali and Prakrit, and a comparative patronage shift under Muslim rulers towards dance and music. A patronage shift within the performing arts is not an invasion, and substituting one for the other converts a specific cultural-economic mechanism into a political slogan. Third, **"only revived under the British"** deletes the entire folk field, most of which begins in the fifteenth and sixteenth centuries with devotional themes and later adds love stories and local heroes - a field with its own languages, communities and occasions that owes nothing to colonial revival. Fourth, the sentence assumes a **single Indian theatre** with one life-cycle, when the record describes several traditions on different clocks. The corrected version is: "Court Sanskrit theatre contracted for three recorded reasons - the migration of writers to poems and songs, the formal and linguistic difficulty of the medium as Pali and Prakrit became common, and a patronage shift under Muslim rulers towards dance and music - while a Sanskrit-adhering lineage continued unbroken in Kerala and an extensive regional folk field developed from the fifteenth and sixteenth centuries onward; the colonial city then added a further, distinct layer of proscenium and commercial theatre."

**Misconception to avoid:** Over-correcting into the claim that nothing declined at all. Something real did contract - a court-based, Sanskrit-medium, rule-bound dramatic tradition lost its writers, its audience and its comparative share of patronage, and the record says so in its own words. The discipline is to be exact about *what* contracted and *how*, not to deny that a contraction occurred.

## Lesson 8 - Kutiyattam: the lineage that never stopped, and the first thing on this topic's recognition clock

Progress: 8/23 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the surviving Sanskrit-adhering theatre of Kerala - antiquity, castes, languages, instruments, the Nirvahana convention, its commentary function and its named exponent

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried - the boxed treatment of Kuttiyatam/Koodiyattam within the decline sub-section, read in full, together with the enrichment treatment of living transmission
CA search: "Not applicable - the single current linkage claimed by this session is placed in Lesson 22, where the safeguarding material sits"
CA found: None found; none claimed
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: one form, nine identifying facts, no shared ones

```text
   KUTIYATTAM  (also given as KOODIYATTAM, KUTTIYATAM)

   +-------------------------------------------------------------------+
   | STATUS   India's OLDEST CONTINUING FORM OF THEATRE                |
   |          a LIVING TRADITION that has SURVIVED SINCE 10th c. AD    |
   +-------------------------------------------------------------------+
   | REGION   KERALA                                                   |
   +-------------------------------------------------------------------+
   | RULE     COMPLETELY ADHERES to the rules laid down in the         |
   |          NATYA SHASTRA                                            |
   +-------------------------------------------------------------------+
   | CARRIER  the traditional PRIVILEGE of the CHAKYAR and NAMBIAR     |
   |          castes of Kerala                                         |
   +-------------------------------------------------------------------+
   | LANGUAGE performed in SANSKRIT + PRAKRIT + MALAYALAM              |
   +-------------------------------------------------------------------+
   | MUSIC    MIZHAVU and EDAKKA provide the background music          |
   +-------------------------------------------------------------------+
   | OPENING  ALL characters begin with NIRVAHANA - a recollection of  |
   |          the PAST EVENTS of the story - and only then does the    |
   |          story unfold, in a LEISURELY fashion                     |
   +-------------------------------------------------------------------+
   | CONTENT  commentaries on SOCIAL, PHILOSOPHICAL and POLITICAL      |
   |          behaviour                                                |
   +-------------------------------------------------------------------+
   | EXPONENT MARGI MADHU CHAKYAR                                      |
   +-------------------------------------------------------------------+

   NOT ONE of these nine rows is shared with any other form in this session.
   That is what makes this box the densest identification unit in the topic.
```

### 1. The learner's actual problem

Lesson 7 ended with a contraction. This lesson answers the question that contraction immediately raises: if court Sanskrit theatre lost its writers, its audience and its share of patronage, did the Sanskrit stage end? The answer is no, and the counter-example is unusually well documented - so well documented that it supplies nine separate identifying facts. The difficulty for a learner is not understanding the form; it is **remembering the right nine facts and not importing a tenth from a neighbouring Kerala tradition**.

### 2. The form, stated exactly

**Kutiyattam** - also given as **Koodiyattam** and **Kuttiyatam** - is **India's oldest continuing form of theatre** and a **living tradition that has survived since the 10th century AD in Kerala**.

It **completely adheres to the rules laid down in the *Natya Shastra***, and it is the **traditional privilege of the Chakyar and Nambiar castes of Kerala**.

The play is **performed in Sanskrit, Prakrit and Malayalam**, with the musical instruments ***Mizhavu*** and ***Edakka*** providing the background music.

**All the characters begin the play with *Nirvahana*, a recollection of the past events of the story.** After this, the story **unfolds in a leisurely fashion**, with **commentaries on social, philosophical and political behaviour**.

**Margi Madhu Chakyar** is a leading exponent of this art form.

### 3. Reading the nine facts as a single argument

Taken individually these are nine things to memorise. Taken together they make an argument, and the argument is what an examiner rewards.

**The adherence claim explains the antiquity claim.** A form described as completely adhering to the treatise's rules and as surviving since the tenth century is a form whose identity is defined by conformity to a text. That is an unusual survival strategy: most living traditions survive by adapting. This one is recorded as surviving by **not** adapting - which is exactly why it can be described as the oldest continuing theatre rather than merely as an old theatre.

**The caste privilege explains the transmission.** A tradition held as the traditional privilege of two named castes has a **built-in transmission mechanism**: the skill passes within the communities that hold the right to perform it. That is why it did not depend on the court patronage whose redirection is named among the causes of the classical contraction. Embedding in a regional community and its institutions is a different survival base from embedding in a court.

**The three languages explain the register logic.** Lesson 6 established that the classical stage marked social position by assigning Sanskrit and Prakrit to different characters. Kutiyattam performs in **Sanskrit, Prakrit and Malayalam** - it keeps the classical pair and adds the regional language. That is a precise, evidenced instance of a classical convention surviving inside a regional setting, and it is a far stronger sentence than any general claim about continuity.

**The *Nirvahana* convention explains the pacing.** Every character opens with a recollection of past events, and only afterwards does the story unfold leisurely. This is a form that puts **retrospect before narrative**, for all characters, every time. The consequence is a theatre in which an evening's performance may be occupied largely by the elaboration of what has already happened - which is why "leisurely" is the record's own word for how the story then proceeds.

**The commentary function explains why it is not a museum piece.** A form whose performance carries commentaries on **social, philosophical and political behaviour** is doing live interpretive work, not reciting a fixed text. Note how this connects back to the *Vidusaka* of Lesson 6: a classical theatre that built in a satirical critical voice, surviving in a lineage whose performances carry social, philosophical and political commentary.

### 4. The four Kerala confusions, settled now

Kerala supplies more named performance traditions than any other region in this session, and four of them are routinely fused with Kutiyattam. Settle them here.

| Form | What it is, in one line | Why it is not Kutiyattam |
|---|---|---|
| **Kutiyattam** | The oldest continuing theatre; Sanskrit-adhering; Chakyar and Nambiar privilege; Mizhavu and Edakka; *Nirvahana* | The Sanskrit-adhering theatre proper |
| **Krishnanattam** | A colourful dance-drama tradition of Kerala, originating in the **mid-17th century**, based on the works of **Krishna Geethi**; a carnival lasting **eight days**, performed on **eight consecutive nights at temples**, narrating the life story of Lord Krishna | A seventeenth-century dance-drama with a fixed eight-night cycle, not a Sanskrit-adhering theatre |
| **Chavittu Natakam** | An **old maritime theatre form of coastal Kerala**; a **colourful Christian art form** with elements borrowed from **both European and Kerala art forms**; origin traced to the **advent of the Portuguese**; themes from the **Bible** and the heroic exploits of **legendary Christian warriors** | A colonial-contact Christian form; nothing to do with Sanskrit dramaturgy |
| **Theyyam** | Performed in Kerala; **similar to Bhuta Kola in Karnataka**; an **open theatre** performed **in front of local temples**, mainly to honour the **spirit of the ancestors** besides gods; **Vaishnavism, Shaktism and Shaivism** themes are now common; actors wear **elaborate headgear and colourful costumes** | A ritual open theatre of ancestor and deity honouring, with a Karnataka counterpart |
| **Mudiyettu** | A Kerala **ritual theatre and dance drama**, inscribed on the UNESCO Representative List of the Intangible Cultural Heritage of Humanity in **2010** | A ritual theatre with its own inscription, distinct from the Sanskrit lineage |

Four of these five are developed in their own place in Lesson 13; they are listed here so that the Kutiyattam box does not absorb them. ⚠️ **Mudiyettu** is named in an owned Main Examination item of this topic but is absent from the folk-theatre classification used elsewhere in this session, so only three facts are stated for it anywhere here - that it is a Kerala ritual theatre and dance drama, and that it was inscribed in 2010.

### 5. The recognition clock starts here

Kutiyattam is also the point at which this topic's **institutional clock** begins in an international sense.

📰 **Kutiyattam, Sanskrit theatre, was inscribed on the UNESCO Representative List of the Intangible Cultural Heritage of Humanity in 2008** - the earliest and most theatre-specific Indian inscription relevant to this session. Three further inscriptions belong to forms taught in this session: **Ramman**, the ritual theatre of the Garhwal Himalayas, in **2009**; **Mudiyettu**, the ritual theatre and dance drama of Kerala, in **2010**; and **Chhau**, in **2010**, which this session names as a mask-and-martial-linked form while routing its dance classification to the dance owner.

⚠️ Two disciplines apply and both are examinable. First, **the list administration, any running total of Indian elements and every current award position belong to the heritage and institutions owner**, which holds its own verification date; this session records only the four inscriptions that attach to forms it teaches. Second, **an international inscription is not a classical designation, an antiquity certificate or a quality ranking**. It records that a state party nominated an element and that an intergovernmental committee inscribed it in a stated year. Reading it as anything else is the commonest status error in the whole art-and-culture syllabus.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Discuss Kutiyattam as evidence against the claim that Sanskrit theatre is extinct, and state the limits of that evidence. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

It is decisive evidence for a narrow claim and no evidence at all for a wider one.

**What it establishes.** Kutiyattam is India's oldest continuing form of theatre and a living tradition surviving since the tenth century AD in Kerala. It completely adheres to the rules laid down in the *Natya Shastra*. It is the traditional privilege of the Chakyar and Nambiar castes. It is performed in Sanskrit, Prakrit and Malayalam with *Mizhavu* and *Edakka*; every character opens with *Nirvahana*, a recollection of past events, before the story unfolds leisurely with commentaries on social, philosophical and political behaviour; and Margi Madhu Chakyar is a leading exponent. A rule-governed Sanskrit repertoire is therefore still performed.

**Why it survived.** The facts explain one another: a text fixes the standard, two named castes hold the transmission right, and a regional setting supplies instruments, languages and occasion. That base is independent of the court patronage whose redirection is named among the causes of the contraction.

**The limits.** It does not show that court Sanskrit drama continued; that contraction is real and separately caused. It does not license "unchanged since antiquity", because adherence to a rule-set is not a claim that nothing has altered. And the 2008 inscription is a twenty-first-century administrative act, not the source of the antiquity claim.

Verdict: say the lineage survives and how, without saying the tradition never declined.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the nine recorded identifiers used as the evidential base 6 + the survival mechanism argued from text, caste and region 4 + three separate limits on the inference 4 + a verdict that keeps survival and contraction simultaneously true 1.**

### Examination use

For Prelims, Kutiyattam is one of the densest identification units in the topic and nearly every row is a plausible item component: **oldest continuing theatre**; **10th century AD**; **Kerala**; **complete adherence to the *Natya Shastra***; **Chakyar and Nambiar** castes; **Sanskrit, Prakrit and Malayalam**; ***Mizhavu*** and ***Edakka***; ***Nirvahana***; **social, philosophical and political commentary**; **Margi Madhu Chakyar**; and the **2008** inscription. Expect items that swap the instruments with those of another Kerala form, or that attach the *Nirvahana* convention to a dance-drama.

For Mains, this is the evidence that refutes "Sanskrit theatre is dead" in a single sentence, and the evidence that explains **why** it survived: a text-adhering repertoire, a community-held transmission right and a regional institutional base rather than a court one. Both halves are needed - the fact of survival and the mechanism of survival.

### Traps introduced here

- Writing that Sanskrit theatre is extinct, or that Kutiyattam is a revival rather than a continuing tradition.
- Attaching *Mizhavu* and *Edakka* to another Kerala form, or Kutiyattam's languages to a dance-drama.
- Fusing Kutiyattam with Krishnanattam, Chavittu Natakam, Theyyam or Mudiyettu.
- Reading the UNESCO inscription as a statement about antiquity, classical status or quality.
- Reproducing a running total of Indian inscriptions, which belongs to a different owner with its own verification date.

### Revision notes

1. **Kutiyattam / Koodiyattam** - India's **oldest continuing form of theatre**; a **living tradition since the 10th century AD**; **Kerala**.
2. **Completely adheres** to the rules laid down in the ***Natya Shastra***.
3. Traditional **privilege of the Chakyar and Nambiar castes** of Kerala.
4. Performed in **Sanskrit, Prakrit and Malayalam**.
5. Instruments: ***Mizhavu*** and ***Edakka***, providing background music.
6. **All characters begin with *Nirvahana*** - a recollection of past events; the story then unfolds **leisurely**.
7. Carries **commentaries on social, philosophical and political behaviour**.
8. Leading exponent: **Margi Madhu Chakyar**.
9. 📰 Inscribed on the **UNESCO Representative List in 2008**; **Ramman 2009**; **Mudiyettu 2010**; **Chhau 2010**.
10. Survival mechanism: **text-adhering repertoire + caste-held transmission + regional base**, not court patronage.

### Concept check

**Question:** Kutiyattam is described as completely adhering to the rules of the *Natya Shastra* and as India's oldest continuing theatre. Why are those two facts connected rather than merely both true, and what risk does the connection create for a careless answer?

**Model answer:** They are connected because the adherence is the **identity condition** of the continuity claim. To say that a form has continued since the tenth century, you need a criterion for saying that what is performed now is the same thing as what was performed then; in this case the criterion is supplied by conformity to a codified text. A form that changed freely could still be old, but it could not be described as the *oldest continuing* form in the same sense, because there would be no fixed standard against which the continuity is measured. The adherence also explains how the continuity was practically achieved: a rule-governed repertoire, held as the traditional privilege of two named castes and embedded in a regional setting with its own instruments and languages, has a transmission mechanism that does not depend on the court patronage whose redirection the record names among the causes of the classical contraction. The **risk** is that a careless answer converts "adheres completely to the rules" into "unchanged since antiquity" and then into "the pure and original form of Indian theatre". None of those follow. The record states adherence to a rule-set and survival as a living tradition; it does not claim that no element of the performance has altered in a thousand years, and it does not rank this form above any other. Write the adherence and the survival; do not write purity.

**Misconception to avoid:** Using Kutiyattam's 2008 inscription as the evidence for its antiquity. The inscription is a twenty-first-century administrative act on the institutional clock; the antiquity claim comes from the practice clock and is stated independently. Fusing the two produces the most common status error in this syllabus - the belief that an international list certifies how old or how classical something is.

## Lesson 9 - Folk theatre: the three-stage arc, and a four-question frame that reads any regional form

Progress: 9/23 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the definition of folk theatre against urban Sanskrit theatre, its recorded thematic arc, and the reading frame used for the next four lessons

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried - the Folk Theatre introduction in full, including the contrast with sophisticated urban Sanskrit theatre, the rural-roots statement and the three-stage thematic sequence that opens the classification table
CA search: "Not applicable - definitional lesson introducing the regional field"
CA found: None found; none claimed
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the arc, and the frame

```text
   A. THE RECORDED THEMATIC ARC OF INDIAN FOLK THEATRE

   15th-16th CENTURY          LATER                    AFTER INDEPENDENCE
   most folk theatres     ->  added LOVE STORIES   ->  spread SOCIAL LESSONS
   began with                 and LOCAL HEROES         as well as
   DEVOTIONAL THEMES                                   ENTERTAINMENT
        |                          |                        |
   the sacred occasion        the secular subject      the didactic purpose
   supplies the audience      widens the audience      supplies a new function

   B. THE FOUR-QUESTION FRAME - use this on EVERY form in Lessons 10-13

   +-----------------+-------------------------------------------------+
   | 1. OCCASION     | when and where: festival, harvest, night vigil, |
   |                 | temple front, courtyard, wedding, spring        |
   +-----------------+-------------------------------------------------+
   | 2. CARRIER      | who performs: a caste, a community, a troupe,   |
   |                 | a named founder's lineage, a gendered convention|
   +-----------------+-------------------------------------------------+
   | 3. MEANS        | what carries it: narrator, mask, instrument,    |
   |                 | painted picture, couplet, dialect, mimicry      |
   +-----------------+-------------------------------------------------+
   | 4. THEME        | what is performed: epic, Purana, saint's life,  |
   |                 | local hero, romance, satire, social message     |
   +-----------------+-------------------------------------------------+

   >> Any form you can answer on all four is a form you can identify,
      compare and place in an answer. A form you can only place on a
      map is a form you have merely listed.
```

### 1. The learner's actual problem

The folk-theatre field in this topic is large - close to forty named forms - and the usual response to it is rote memorisation of a form-to-state table. That approach fails for two reasons. First, several forms are recorded across two states, so a strict one-to-one map is wrong on its own terms. Second, a table of names cannot answer a Mains question, and it cannot survive a Prelims item built on a community, an instrument or an occasion rather than on a state. The remedy is to learn each form on **four axes** instead of one. The next four lessons do exactly that, region by region, and this lesson establishes the frame and the general facts that apply to the whole field.

### 2. What folk theatre is, stated exactly

India has a **rich tradition of folk theatre** which **reflects local lifestyle, social norms, beliefs and customs**. **Unlike the sophisticated urban Sanskrit theatre, folk theatre had rural roots and a rustic style.**

Read that contrast precisely, because it is easy to misuse in both directions. The record places the two on a **sociological** axis - *urban and sophisticated* against *rural and rustic* - which is a statement about setting and idiom. It is not a statement about artistic worth, technical difficulty or antiquity. A form whose performance runs all night, requires a community of narrators and chorus, uses masks, sustains a three-part narrative division and is delivered in a purpose-built mixed language is not "simple"; it is differently sited. The word to hold is **rustic**, meaning of the countryside, and not "crude".

Equally, do not over-correct. The record does draw a real distinction, and folk theatre genuinely does what the definition says: it reflects local lifestyle, social norms, beliefs and customs. That is a **functional** definition and it is the reason the four-question frame works. A performance form that exists to express local life will encode its occasion, its community, its means and its themes in ways that a court form need not.

### 3. The three-stage arc, and how to use it

**Most folk theatres began in the 15th-16th century with devotional themes, later adding love stories and local heroes; post-independence, they spread social lessons as well as entertainment.**

That single sentence is the most useful causal structure in this half of the session, and it is worth naming its three stages.

**Stage one - devotional.** A beginning in the fifteenth and sixteenth centuries with devotional themes. The field is dense with instances: an Assamese one-act form started by a named Vaishnava saint and his disciple in the fifteenth-sixteenth century; an Assamese presentation form introduced by the same saint in the early sixteenth century; an eastern Indian open-air form initiated by a named Vaishnava saint who used the medium to propagate the teachings of Lord Krishna; a Karnataka form strongly influenced by the Vaishnava Bhakti movement; a Konkan drama to honour the ten incarnations of Vishnu. The devotional stage is not an atmosphere; it is a set of named saints, named deities and named occasions.

**Stage two - romance and local heroism.** Love stories and local heroes are added. Again the instances are specific: a Gujarat form whose theme is generally romantic; a Madhya Pradesh form that added romantic folk tales to a repertoire that began with the epics; a Maharashtra operatic ballad tradition that arose from a play written lauding the heroic deeds of Shivaji; a Tamil Nadu form built around a love-struck heroine and a fortune-teller.

**Stage three - social message.** After Independence, folk theatre spreads social lessons alongside entertainment. Here the field connects directly to the modern stage of Lessons 14 and 15, where the same impulse is institutionalised - a political theatre association writing on social themes, a street-theatre movement built on small, socially conscious plays with minimalist staging, and a commercial stage addressing dowry, caste and religious hypocrisy.

**How to use the arc.** It converts any "folk theatre as a vehicle of social communication" demand into a ready causal spine: a devotional origin supplies the occasion and the audience; a secular widening supplies the subject matter; and Independence supplies the didactic purpose. Each stage can then be evidenced with named forms. That is an argument with a chronology, and it beats a list of regional names every time.

### 4. The recurring devices, named once

Before the regional lessons begin, it is worth naming the devices that recur across them, so that each lesson can use them without re-explaining.

| Device | What it does | Where it recurs |
|---|---|---|
| **The narrator who stands outside the action** | Supplies setting, links episodes, sings scripture, comments | The Assamese *Sutradhar* with his *Gayan-Bayan Mandali*; the Gujarat *Sutradhar* known as *Nayaka*; the Odia *Gayaka* and *Palia*; the Assamese *Ojha* and *Palis*; the Karnataka *Bhagavata* with his *Arthadharis*; the Andhra main artist with two co-artists |
| **The mask** | Marks a special expression, a deity or a demon | An Assamese one-act form; a Garhwal ritual theatre; a Manipuri puppet tradition; the mask-making practice attached to Chhau |
| **The named instrument as identifier** | Gives the form its signature sound, and often its name | *khol* and cymbals; *Nagada*; *kathia*; *dhol* and *mohuri*; *Burra*; *Oggu*; *Jhanjhar* and *dholak*; *ektara*, harmonium, *sarangi*, *dholak* and *khartal*; *Tal* and *Maddale* |
| **The painted picture as prop** | Carries the narrative visually while the narrator speaks | The Gujarat Brahmin community form that uses painted pictures to narrate stories of romance and valour |
| **Impersonation and disguise** | Builds the performance around role-play rather than plot | The mimicry-based north Indian form with its *behrupiya* or *naqalchi*; the Telangana and Andhra tradition centred on a principal *vesham*; the Manipuri form in which men play women's roles as *Nupi Shabis* |
| **The gendered convention** | Assigns or reverses performing roles by gender | The Uttar Pradesh epic enactment generally performed by male actors, including the role of Sita; the Manipuri form with men playing women; the Maharashtra form whose unique feature is the presence of **female actors, who even play male roles** |
| **The dialect or purpose-built language** | Marks the form's audience and region | *Brajavali*; *Rangat Dohas*; Roman Konkani dialects |

That last row of the gendered-convention line deserves a note now, because it is a genuine analytical asset. Within a single field, the record contains a form in which men play women, a form in which women play men, and a form in which male actors perform female roles in an epic enactment. Any answer tempted to generalise about gender and Indian folk performance has three mutually complicating instances in front of it, and the honest sentence names the variation rather than the rule.

### 5. What the folk field is not

Three disciplines, stated once and applied in every lesson that follows.

⚠️ **It is not a hierarchy.** The contrast with urban Sanskrit theatre is sociological. Do not write that folk theatre is a simplified or degraded version of classical theatre; the record does not derive one from the other, and most of the field begins on a different clock.

⚠️ **It is not a clean state map.** Several forms are recorded across two states or regions, and this session prints the record's own dual attributions rather than forcing a single state.

⚠️ **It is not undated where it is dated.** Where the record supplies a century - a fifteenth-sixteenth-century founding, an early-sixteenth-century introduction, a mid-seventeenth-century origin, a form originating around three hundred years ago - the century is examinable and must be reproduced. Where it supplies none, none should be invented.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Explain the recorded thematic arc of Indian folk theatre and the analytical use it can be put to. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model

Most folk theatres began in the fifteenth and sixteenth centuries with devotional themes, later added love stories and local heroes, and after Independence spread social lessons as well as entertainment.

Each stage is instantiated. The devotional stage names saints and deities - an Assamese one-act play begun by a Vaishnava saint and his disciple, an eastern open-air form used by a saint to propagate Krishna's teachings, a Konkan drama honouring ten incarnations. The second names romance and heroism - a Gujarat form generally romantic, a Malwa form that added romantic folk tales, a ballad tradition arising from a play lauding a ruler. The third names purpose - a Manipuri form that promotes humanism, tolerance and justice.

The analytical use is causal: devotion supplies the occasion, secular widening the subject, Independence the didactic function.

⚠️ The arc is recorded for *most* forms; a maritime Christian form sits outside it.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the three stages reproduced with the recorded century 3 + at least two named instances per stage 4 + the causal reading that turns the arc into an argument 2 + the *most* hedge with a named exception 1.**

### Examination use

For Prelims, the three general facts are directly testable: the **definition** of folk theatre as reflecting local lifestyle, social norms, beliefs and customs; the **contrast** with sophisticated urban Sanskrit theatre as rural and rustic; and the **three-stage arc** with its **15th-16th century** devotional beginning and its post-Independence social turn. Regional items are handled form by form in the next four lessons.

For Mains, this lesson supplies the causal spine for every folk-theatre demand, and the four-question frame supplies the internal structure of any answer that has to cover several forms without turning into a list.

### Traps introduced here

- Treating "rural roots and rustic style" as a judgement of quality.
- Deriving folk theatre from Sanskrit theatre because one is older.
- Forcing every form into exactly one state when the record gives two.
- Generalising about gender in folk performance when the record contains three contrary conventions.
- Losing the century where the record supplies one, or inventing one where it does not.

### Revision notes

1. Folk theatre **reflects local lifestyle, social norms, beliefs and customs**.
2. **Unlike the sophisticated urban Sanskrit theatre, folk theatre had rural roots and a rustic style** - a **sociological** contrast, not a ranking.
3. **Most folk theatres began in the 15th-16th century with devotional themes**.
4. **Later** they added **love stories and local heroes**.
5. **Post-Independence** they spread **social lessons as well as entertainment**.
6. Four-question frame: **occasion, carrier, means, theme**.
7. Recurring devices: the **outside narrator**, the **mask**, the **named instrument**, the **painted picture**, **impersonation**, the **gendered convention**, the **dialect or purpose-built language**.
8. Gender conventions vary in **three contrary directions** within this one field.
9. Several forms are recorded across **two states or regions**; reproduce the dual attribution.
10. Reproduce a recorded century; never invent an unrecorded one.

### Concept check

**Question:** How does the three-stage thematic arc of Indian folk theatre let you answer a "folk theatre as a vehicle of social communication" question without listing forms, and what is the one qualification the answer needs?

**Model answer:** The arc gives the answer a **causal chronology**, which is what such a question actually wants. Stage one explains why the form had an audience at all: most folk theatres began in the fifteenth and sixteenth centuries with devotional themes, which means performance was attached to an occasion the community already attended - a saint's teaching propagated through an open-air medium, a Vaishnava presentation form, a drama honouring the incarnations of a deity. Stage two explains how the audience widened: love stories and local heroes entered the repertoire, so the form acquired secular subject matter without losing its occasion, and a heroic ballad tradition could grow directly out of a play written to laud a ruler's deeds. Stage three explains how the communicative capacity was redirected: after Independence, folk theatre spread social lessons as well as entertainment, using a delivery system - assembled audience, trusted performers, familiar idiom - that had already been built for devotion and entertainment. The argument is therefore that folk theatre communicates well because its **infrastructure of attention predates its message**, and the named forms become evidence for the stages rather than a substitute for the argument. The **qualification** is that the arc is a general pattern recorded for "most folk theatres" and not a law: the field contains a colonial-contact Christian maritime form with biblical themes, a ritual theatre attached to a serpent-goddess festival, a satirical mimicry tradition and a harvest-season commercial-folk blend, and not all of them travel the three stages in order. State the pattern, evidence it, and note the exceptions rather than smoothing them away.

**Misconception to avoid:** Converting "post-Independence social lessons" into a claim that folk theatre became a government communication tool. The record says these forms spread social lessons as well as entertainment - a statement about their own repertoire, not about state sponsorship. The organised, explicitly political use of theatre in this topic has its own named history in the modern-theatre lessons and should be cited there, with its own dates, rather than read backwards into the village stage.

## Lesson 10 - The eastern and north-eastern field: Assam, Bengal, Odisha, Manipur

Progress: 10/23 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: Ankia Naat, Ojhapali, Bhaona, Jatra and Sahi Jatra, Daskathia and Chaiti Ghoda, Thok Lila and Shumang Leela - each on occasion, carrier, means and theme

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried - the classification table entries for Assam, Uttar Pradesh's eastern neighbours, West Bengal and eastern India, Odisha and Manipur, read in full with their state and theme columns
CA search: "Not applicable - regional field lesson; the session's single current linkage is placed in Lesson 22"
CA found: None found; none claimed
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: one region, one shared founder-figure, four distinct solutions

```text
   THE EASTERN AND NORTH-EASTERN FIELD

   ASSAM                                    BENGAL / EASTERN INDIA
   ANKIA NAAT   one-act play                JATRA   open-air performance
     started by SANKARDEV + MADHAVDEV         initiated by CHAITANYA
     15th-16th century AD                     MAHAPRABHU, who used the
     performed in the style of an OPERA       medium during travels through
     depicts incidents from KRISHNA's life    rural Bengal to propagate the
     SUTRADHAR + GAYAN-BAYAN MANDALI          teachings of LORD KRISHNA
       playing KHOL and CYMBALS                 -> later RAMA JATRA,
     UNIQUE: use of MASKS for special              SHIV JATRA, CHANDI JATRA
       expressions                              -> modern: SECULAR,
     written in BRAJAVALI (Assamese-               HISTORICAL, PATRIOTIC
       Maithili mixed language)               SAHI JATRA - street theatre
                                                form in ODISHA
   BHAONA       a PRESENTATION of Ankia Naat
     introduced by SANKARDEVA, early 16th c  ODISHA
     Vaishnavite themes common               DASKATHIA  two narrators:
     SUTRADHAR narrates and SINGS VERSES       GAYAKA (chief singer)
       FROM HOLY TEXTS                         PALIA  (co-narrator)
     songs and music a part of it              instrument: KATHIA (wooden)
     purpose: RELIGIOUS AND MORAL MESSAGES     theme: LORD SHIVA
       through entertainment and drama         variant: CHAITI GHODA -
                                                 DHOL + MOHURI, THREE narrators
   OJHAPALI     narrative theatre form
     festival of MANASHA, the Serpent Goddess
     THREE parts: BANIYA KHANDA |            MANIPUR
       BHATIYALI KHANDA | DEVA KHANDA        THOK LILA  satire, wit, comedy
     OJHA = main narrator                    SHUMANG LEELA  'play of the open
     PALIS = members of the chorus             courtyard'; women's roles played
                                               by MEN called NUPI SHABIS
```

### 1. The learner's actual problem

Assam alone supplies three forms, two of them attached to the same founder, and they are constantly fused. Bengal and Odisha supply a form whose name means "journey" and which recurs as a style label inside puppetry two lessons later. Manipur supplies two forms whose names are easily reversed. This lesson separates all of them on the four axes and then names the one link that makes the region analytically coherent.

### 2. Assam: three forms, one saint, three different jobs

**Ankia Naat.** A **traditional one-act play**, **started by the famous Vaishnava saint Sankardev and his disciple Madhavdev in the 15th-16th century AD**. It is **performed in the style of an opera** and **depicts incidents from the life of Lord Krishna**. The ***Sutradhar*** or **narrator** is accompanied by a group of musicians known as the ***Gayan-Bayan Mandali***, who play the ***khol*** and **cymbals**. **One of the unique features of this form of theatre is the use of masks to depict special expressions.** Plays are **written in an Assamese-Maithili mixed language called *Brajavali***.

Five identifying facts, none of them shared: the one-act structure, the operatic style, the named musician group with its two instruments, the mask device, and the purpose-built mixed language. Note in particular the mask: this is the form that refutes any attempt to extend the Sanskrit stage's no-mask statement across Indian theatre.

**Bhaona.** **The idea is to spread religious and moral messages to people through entertainment and drama.** It is a **presentation of Ankia Naat** and **the use of Vaishnavite themes is common**. The ***Sutradhar*** (narrator) **narrates the play and sings verses from holy texts**. **Songs and music are also a part of it.** This folk theatre was **introduced by Sankardeva in the early 16th century**.

The relation between the two is the examinable point, and it is stated in the record itself: **Bhaona is a presentation of Ankia Naat**. One is the composition; the other is the performance event. A candidate who writes "Ankia Naat and Bhaona are two Assamese folk theatres" has stated something true but has missed the relation that a matching item is most likely to test. Note also the second Sutradhar function here - he **narrates and sings verses from holy texts**, which is a different job description from the Assamese *Sutradhar* of the first form and from the Sanskrit stage manager of Lesson 5.

**Ojhapali.** A **unique narrative theatre form primarily associated with the festival of Manasha, or the Serpent Goddess**. The **narration is a prolonged affair, with three distinct parts - *Baniya Khanda*, *Bhatiyali Khanda* and *Deva Khanda***. The ***Ojha*** is the **main narrator** and the ***Palis*** are the **members of the chorus**.

Three facts do all the identification work: the goddess-festival occasion, the three named divisions, and the narrator-chorus pair whose names give the form its own name. The structural point is worth noticing: this is the second named instance in this session of a performance with formally named parts, after the classical five-part shape of Lesson 4.

### 3. Bengal and eastern India: Jatra, and a name that travels

**Jatra** is a **popular folk theatre of eastern India**. It is **generally an open-air performance** that was **initiated by the Vaishnava saint Chaitanya Mahaprabhu**. **During his travels through rural Bengal, he used the medium of Jatra to propagate the teachings of Lord Krishna.** **Later, variants such as *Rama Jatra*, *Shiv Jatra* and *Chandi Jatra* also came into existence, narrating stories of Puranic legends.** **In modern times, Jatra is used to narrate stories of secular, historical and even patriotic themes.** **In Odisha, a popular form of street theatre known as *Sahi Jatra* is prevalent.**

This entry is the single clearest instance of the three-stage arc from Lesson 9 inside one form. It begins devotional, with a named saint using the medium deliberately for propagation. It broadens into Puranic variants named for other deities. And it reaches secular, historical and patriotic themes in modern times. If a question asks you to demonstrate the arc with one example, this is the example - the whole sequence is recorded for a single form.

⚠️ **The name travels.** *Jatra* recurs later in this session as a **style label inside puppetry**: the West Bengal, Odisha and Assam rod-puppet tradition uses figures described as **Jatra style**, and an Odisha rod-puppet tradition uses **60 cm Jatra style puppets in full dress**. A form name that becomes a style descriptor for puppet figures is exactly the kind of cross-reference an objective item exploits. Read the stem for whether it is asking about the theatre or about the puppet.

### 4. Odisha: two narrators, one instrument, one variant

**Daskathia** is a form of folk theatre **popular in Odisha**. In this form, there are **two narrators - *Gayaka*, who is the chief singer, and *Palia*, who is the co-narrator**. The narration is **accompanied by dramatic music composed using a wooden musical instrument called *kathia***. The **theme mainly revolves around Lord Shiva**.

**A close variant of this form is *Chaiti Ghoda*, which uses two musical instruments - *dhol* and *mohuri* - and three narrators.**

The pair is a clean comparison and therefore a likely item: same region, related forms, but **two narrators and one instrument** against **three narrators and two instruments**. Note also the instrument-to-name relation, which recurs across the field: *kathia* is a wooden instrument and it names the form, exactly as *Burra* and *Oggu* will name two Andhra forms in Lesson 12 and as *kathi*, the wooden rod, will name an Odisha puppet form in Lesson 19.

### 5. Manipur: satire and the open courtyard

**Thok Lila** is a **folk theatre which involves satire, wit and even comedy**.

**Shumang Leela** is a **traditional form of theatre where the roles of women are played by men, called *Nupi Shabis***. ***Shumang*** means **open courtyard** and ***Leela*** means **play**, so the literal meaning of *Shumang Leela* is the **play of the open courtyard**. It **attempts to preserve and promote humanism, brotherhood, tolerance, confidence, devotion, truth and justice through its performances**.

Two observations. First, the etymology is the identification: a form whose name literally means the play of the open courtyard is a form defined by its **stage relation**, and it belongs beside Jatra's open-air performance and Theyyam's open theatre in front of local temples as an instance of Indian performance that does not require a built house. Second, the value list - humanism, brotherhood, tolerance, confidence, devotion, truth and justice - is an explicit statement of the form's own didactic purpose, and it is the clearest single illustration of the third stage of the folk arc: a form stating that it performs in order to promote social values.

⚠️ Manipur's gendered convention here - **men playing women's roles as *Nupi Shabis*** - should be held alongside two others in this session: the Uttar Pradesh epic enactment **generally performed by male actors, who perform the role of Sita as well**, and the Maharashtra form whose **unique feature is the presence of female actors, who even play male roles**. Three conventions, three directions, one field. Never generalise.

### 6. What links the region

Two threads run through all four sub-regions and both are usable in an answer.

**The Vaishnava thread.** Assam's one-act play and its presentation form are both attached to Sankardev; Bengal's open-air form is attached to Chaitanya Mahaprabhu; and both saints used performance deliberately to propagate teaching. A single sentence covers it: **in the eastern field, folk theatre is recorded as an instrument of Vaishnava propagation before it is anything else**, and the devotional stage of the folk arc is therefore especially well evidenced here.

**The narrator-plus-chorus thread.** The east is the region of the named narrator and his accompanying group: *Sutradhar* with *Gayan-Bayan Mandali*; *Sutradhar* narrating and singing holy verses; *Ojha* with *Palis*; *Gayaka* with *Palia*. Where the Sanskrit stage put one officiant in front of the play, the eastern folk field puts a narrator and a chorus inside it.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**In the eastern and north-eastern field, folk theatre is recorded first as an instrument of devotional propagation. Discuss, with attention to what the region also contributes structurally. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

Both halves are evidenced, and the second is the less obvious.

**Propagation.** An Assamese one-act play was started by the Vaishnava saint Sankardev and his disciple Madhavdev in the fifteenth to sixteenth century AD, performed in the style of an opera, depicting incidents from the life of Lord Krishna. Its presentation form was introduced by Sankardeva in the early sixteenth century, explicitly to spread religious and moral messages through entertainment and drama. In Bengal, Jatra was initiated by Chaitanya Mahaprabhu, who used the medium during his travels through rural Bengal to propagate the teachings of Lord Krishna, and later produced *Rama Jatra*, *Shiv Jatra* and *Chandi Jatra*. Two saints, two regions, one deliberate use of performance.

**Structure.** The same field supplies the topic's densest set of narrator conventions: a *Sutradhar* with the *Gayan-Bayan Mandali* on *khol* and cymbals; a *Sutradhar* who narrates and sings verses from holy texts; the *Ojha* with his *Palis*; the *Gayaka* with the *Palia*, and a close variant with three narrators. Where the classical stage placed one officiant in front of the play, the eastern field places a narrator and a chorus inside it.

Two further contributions: masks used to depict special expressions, and a purpose-built Assamese-Maithili language, *Brajavali*.

⚠️ Devotion is not the whole field: Jatra now carries secular, historical and patriotic themes, and one Manipuri form is defined by satire.

Verdict: the east supplies the clearest propagation evidence and the narrator grammar together.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = two named saints with their forms, centuries and deity 6 + four narrator conventions named exactly 4 + the mask device and the purpose-built language 2 + the secular-turn and satire qualifications 2 + a verdict joining propagation to structure 1.**

### Examination use

For Prelims, the high-value items are: **Ankia Naat** - Sankardev and Madhavdev, 15th-16th century, opera style, Krishna, *Gayan-Bayan Mandali* with *khol* and cymbals, **masks**, ***Brajavali***; **Bhaona** - a **presentation of Ankia Naat**, Sankardeva, **early 16th century**, Sutradhar sings verses from holy texts; **Ojhapali** - **Manasha** festival, three *Khandas*, *Ojha* and *Palis*; **Jatra** - Chaitanya Mahaprabhu, open-air, the three named variants, the modern secular turn, and **Sahi Jatra in Odisha**; **Daskathia** - *Gayaka* and *Palia*, *kathia*, Shiva, and the **Chaiti Ghoda** variant with *dhol*, *mohuri* and three narrators; **Thok Lila**; **Shumang Leela** - open courtyard, *Nupi Shabis*.

For Mains, this region supplies the best single evidence block for two claims: theatre as an instrument of devotional propagation, and the complete three-stage folk arc demonstrated within one named form.

### Traps introduced here

- Treating Ankia Naat and Bhaona as unrelated, or reversing which is the presentation of which.
- Attributing Jatra to Sankardev or Ankia Naat to Chaitanya Mahaprabhu.
- Confusing the *Ojha*-and-*Palis* pair with the *Gayaka*-and-*Palia* pair.
- Giving Daskathia three narrators, or Chaiti Ghoda two.
- Reading "Jatra style" in a puppetry stem as a reference to the Bengal theatre form.
- Generalising the Manipuri men-play-women convention across Indian folk theatre.

### Revision notes

1. **Ankia Naat** (Assam) - one-act play; **Sankardev and Madhavdev**, **15th-16th century AD**; **opera style**; **life of Lord Krishna**; ***Sutradhar*** with the ***Gayan-Bayan Mandali*** playing ***khol*** and **cymbals**; unique feature **masks for special expressions**; written in ***Brajavali***, an **Assamese-Maithili mixed language**.
2. **Bhaona** (Assam) - a **presentation of Ankia Naat**; **Sankardeva, early 16th century**; **Vaishnavite themes**; *Sutradhar* **narrates and sings verses from holy texts**; religious and moral messages through entertainment and drama.
3. **Ojhapali** (Assam) - narrative theatre of the **Manasha / Serpent Goddess** festival; three parts ***Baniya Khanda, Bhatiyali Khanda, Deva Khanda***; ***Ojha*** main narrator, ***Palis*** chorus.
4. **Jatra** (West Bengal / eastern India) - **open-air**; initiated by **Chaitanya Mahaprabhu** to propagate the teachings of **Lord Krishna**; later ***Rama Jatra, Shiv Jatra, Chandi Jatra***; modern **secular, historical, patriotic** themes.
5. ***Sahi Jatra*** - street theatre form of **Odisha**.
6. **Daskathia** (Odisha) - **two narrators**: ***Gayaka*** chief singer, ***Palia*** co-narrator; wooden instrument ***kathia***; theme **Lord Shiva**.
7. ***Chaiti Ghoda*** - close variant; ***dhol*** and ***mohuri***; **three narrators**.
8. **Thok Lila** (Manipur) - **satire, wit, comedy**.
9. **Shumang Leela** (Manipur) - **play of the open courtyard**; women's roles played by men called ***Nupi Shabis***; promotes **humanism, brotherhood, tolerance, confidence, devotion, truth and justice**.
10. Regional threads: **Vaishnava propagation** (Sankardev, Chaitanya) and the **named narrator with a chorus**.

### Concept check

**Question:** The record says that Bhaona "is a presentation of Ankia Naat". Why is that phrasing more useful to you than simply listing both as Assamese folk theatres, and what second relation in this lesson has the same shape?

**Model answer:** It is more useful because it tells you that the two entries are on **different levels**: one names a composition and the other names the performance event in which that composition is presented.
