That converts a pair of names into a structure you can use. It explains why both are attached to the same founder and why both carry Vaishnavite themes without being duplicates; it explains why the narrator's job is described differently in each - accompanied by a named musician group in the one, narrating and singing verses from holy texts in the other; and it gives you the correct answer to a matching item that tries to present them as parallel alternatives. The second relation with the same shape in this lesson is the **Daskathia and Chaiti Ghoda** pair, where the record explicitly calls the second a **close variant** of the first and then distinguishes it by exactly two countable features: two musical instruments instead of one, and three narrators instead of two. In both cases the record has done the comparative work for you by naming the relation, and reproducing that relation is worth more than reproducing either entry alone.

**Misconception to avoid:** Extending the composition-and-presentation relation to every pair of forms in the same state. Assam's third form is not a presentation of anything - it is an independent narrative theatre attached to a goddess festival with its own three-part structure and its own narrator-and-chorus pair. Named relations should be reproduced where the record supplies them and not constructed where it does not.

## Lesson 11 - The northern and western field: Uttar Pradesh, Punjab, Kashmir, Himachal, Uttarakhand, Rajasthan, Madhya Pradesh and Gujarat

Progress: 11/23 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: Ramlila, Naqal and Bhand, Nautanki, Bhand Pather, Kariyala, Ramman, Swang, Khyal, Maach, Raslila, Garoda and Bhavai - each on occasion, carrier, means and theme

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

CA found: None found; none claimed

### Visual: twelve forms, sorted by what identifies each one

```text
   (Raj / MP-Malwar;    HARMONIUM, SARANGI, DHOLAK, KHARTAL; also involves

    and Haryana)

                        MUSIC and DANCE; at FESTIVALS and FAIRS; humour and
                        social messages

                        MYTHOLOGICAL themes (Mahabharata, Ramayana), later
                        ROMANTIC FOLK TALES; unique feature: dialogues
                        delivered as COUPLETS called RANGAT DOHAS

                        of KRISHNA and RADHA

                        PLAYS; theme generally ROMANTIC; performers BALANCE
                        EARTHEN POTS or BRASS PITCHERS; semi-classical music
                        in folk style with JHANJHAR and DHOLAK; the
                        SUTRADHAR is known as NAYAKA
```

### 1. The learner's actual problem

The northern and western field contains the highest concentration of **name collisions** in this topic. *Bhand* names a mimicry form in Uttar Pradesh and also appears inside the Kashmiri form *Bhand Pather*. *Swang* is a form and also the parent of *Nautanki*. *Khyal* names a Rajasthani folk theatre and is also a well-known term in the music topic. *Nayaka* names the hero category on the Sanskrit stage and also the *Sutradhar* in a Gujarat form. This lesson teaches the forms and, in the same movement, disarms each collision.

### 2. The occasion-defined forms

**Ramlila** (Uttar Pradesh) is an **enactment of the Ramayana using songs, dances and dialogues, mainly during the period before Dussehra**. It is **generally performed by male actors, who perform the role of Sita as well**.

Three identifying elements: a **named epic**, a **named calendar window**, and a **gendered performing convention**. The last is the one most likely to be tested and the one that must be held against the contrary conventions elsewhere in the field.

**Ramman** (Uttarakhand) is a **ritualistic theatre of the Garhwal region of Uttarakhand**, **dedicated to *Bhumiyal Devta*, the local deity**. It is **listed in UNESCO's Representative List of the Intangible Cultural Heritage of Humanity**. **People of the *Bhandari* caste wear the sacred mask symbolising Narasimha, the half-man and half-lion.** **Dances are performed and various songs are sung**, and **stories of Lord Rama are also narrated**.

This is the densest entry in the northern field and every clause is examinable: the **region**, the **named local deity**, the **inscription**, the **named caste**, the **mask and its iconographic identity**, and the presence of Rama narration alongside the local-deity dedication. 📰 The inscription year is **2009**; note that the form's own description states the listing without giving the year, so the year has to be held separately.

**Kariyala** (Himachal Pradesh) is a folk theatre form **related to worshipping the local deity *Bijeshwar*** of the **Solan, Shimla and Sirmour districts**. It is **practised mainly by the communities in the region** and is **performed when agricultural prosperity is achieved in the region or personal wishes are fulfilled**.

The occasion here is the analytically interesting part and it is unusual in this topic: performance is **triggered by an outcome** - a good agricultural year, a fulfilled wish - rather than fixed to a calendar date. That is a votive logic, and it belongs beside the Garhwal form's local-deity dedication as evidence that the northern hill field ties theatre to the deity's relationship with the community rather than to a festival slot alone.

### 3. The technique-defined forms, and the two Bhands

**Naqal or Bhand** (Uttar Pradesh / Punjab) is a **mimicry-based performance**, from **Punjab (*Naqal*) and Uttar Pradesh (*Bhand*)**. The performing artist is often called ***behrupiya*** or ***naqalchi*** - **impersonator**. He uses **comedy, absurdities, wit and humour to get across the social or political message**. It is **performed by artists from the Muslim Bhand Community**.

**Bhand Pather** (Jammu and Kashmir) is a folk theatre of Jammu and Kashmir. It **involves contemporary social satire or even mythical stories**. It is **secular in outlook, although it is performed by Muslims**. It **has music, dance and drama**.

⚠️ **These are two different entries and the difference matters.** The first is a mimicry and impersonation tradition of Punjab and Uttar Pradesh carried by a named community; the second is a Kashmiri form combining social satire with mythical stories, described as secular in outlook and performed by Muslims, and containing music, dance and drama. What they share is a **carrier community** and a **satirical function**; what they do not share is region, repertoire or technique. The safest discipline is to attach the word *mimicry* and the words *behrupiya* and *naqalchi* to the first, and the words *contemporary social satire*, *secular in outlook* and *music, dance and drama* to the second.

Both entries together make a point worth stating in an answer about theatre and society: the record shows a **Muslim community carrying satirical performance traditions in three northern states**, and describes one of those traditions as secular in outlook. A generalisation about religion and Indian folk performance is refuted by this pair alone.

**Garoda** (Gujarat) is a **popular art form of the *Garoda* Brahmin community of Gujarat**. It **uses painted pictures to narrate stories of romance and valour**.

The identifying feature is the **prop**: a narrative carried by painted pictures. This is the only picture-narration entry in the folk table, and it is the natural bridge to the puppetry lessons, where the performance object rather than the performer's body carries the image.

### 4. The musically and verbally defined forms

**Nautanki** (North India) is an **offshoot of Swang** and is **one of the most popular forms of theatre in north India**, which **finds mention in Abul Fazl's *Ain-i-Akbari***. The **plays are themed around historical, social and folk tales** and are **delivered through dance and music**. **Dialogues are delivered in a lyrical fashion, accompanied by the beats of a drum called *Nagada*.** **In the later period, two schools of Nautanki seated around Kanpur and Lucknow gained importance.**

Four testable elements: the **parent form**, the **textual mention** in a named Mughal-period work, the **named drum**, and the **two later schools** with their cities. The *Ain-i-Akbari* mention is particularly useful, because it is one of the very few instances in the folk field where a form is attested in a datable text rather than only in practice - a genuine bridge between the text clock and the practice clock of Lesson 1.

**Swang / Saang** (Rajasthan / Madhya Pradesh, Malwar region) - **Swangs are another popular source of entertainment in the region of Rajasthan and Haryana**. They are **mainly musical dramas, sung through verses, accompanied by the music of *ektara*, *harmonium*, *sarangi*, *dholak* and *khartal***. It **also involves mimicry accompanied by dialogue**.

⚠️ Note the internal geography of this entry: the table places it under **Rajasthan / Madhya Pradesh (Malwar region)** while the text says the forms are popular in **Rajasthan and Haryana**. Reproduce both attributions rather than choosing one; this is a recorded discrepancy, not an error to be tidied away. Note also that *Swang* carries **mimicry with dialogue** - the same technique that defines the *Naqal* and *Bhand* entry - which is a real connection across the northern field and not a coincidence of vocabulary.

**Khyal** (Rajasthan) is a **North Indian folk theatre combining storytelling, music and dance**, **performed during festivals and fairs**, **often with humour and social messages, reflecting local culture**.

⚠️ **The collision to disarm:** *Khyal* is also a major vocal genre in the Hindustani system, which belongs to the music owner. In this session the word names a **Rajasthani folk theatre** performed at festivals and fairs. Read the stem: if it asks about a vocal form, gharanas or a musical structure, the music topic owns it; if it asks about a folk theatre combining storytelling, music and dance, this entry owns it.

**Maach** (Madhya Pradesh) is the **folk theatre of the region of Malwa in Madhya Pradesh**. **Initially, it was based on mythological themes such as the Mahabharata and Ramayana. Later, romantic folk tales were included in its repertoire.** **The unique feature of this form is the dialogues, which are delivered in the form of couplets known as *Rangat Dohas*.**

This entry is a miniature of the folk arc: mythological first, romantic later. And its unique feature is **verbal form** - dialogue as couplet - which places it beside the lyrical delivery of Nautanki and the verse-singing of Swang as the third northern instance of a form identified by *how the words are delivered* rather than by what they say.

### 5. Gujarat's three, and a Sutradhar with another name

**Raslila** (Gujarat) is a **dance drama enactment of the adolescent love stories of Krishna and Radha**.

**Bhavai** (Gujarat) **incorporates an extensive use of dance to narrate a series of small plays**. The **theme of the play is generally romantic** and **the performers balance a number of earthen pots or brass pitchers**. The play is **accompanied by semi-classical music, played in a distinct folk style with instruments such as *Jhanjhar* and *dholak***. **The *Sutradhar* is known as *Nayaka* in the Bhavai theatre.**

Bhavai is the most heavily featured Gujarat entry and each feature identifies it: the **series of small plays** rather than one continuous drama; the **romantic theme**; the **balanced earthen pots or brass pitchers**, which is a physical property-feat and the kind of detail objective items are built on; the **semi-classical music in folk style** with two named instruments; and the **renaming of the Sutradhar as Nayaka**.

⚠️ **The collision to disarm:** *Nayaka* on the Sanskrit stage is the **hero or protagonist** (Lesson 6). In Bhavai it is the name of the ***Sutradhar***. These are two different uses of one word, and an item that places them side by side is testing whether you know that Indian performance vocabulary is shared across systems while job descriptions are not - the same discipline established for *Sutradhar* itself in Lesson 1.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"Satire in the northern folk field is an institutionalised function with a named carrier." Examine. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model

The claim holds on two recorded entries and needs one boundary.

A mimicry-based form of Punjab and Uttar Pradesh is performed by artists of the Muslim Bhand community; its performer is called the *behrupiya* or *naqalchi*, the impersonator, and he uses comedy, absurdities, wit and humour to carry a **social or political message**. Satire is the form's purpose, not its tone.

A Kashmiri form involves contemporary social satire or mythical stories, has music, dance and drama, and is described as **secular in outlook although performed by Muslims**.

Institutionalisation is visible in three features: a defined technique, a named carrier community and a stated communicative purpose.

⚠️ The boundary: this evidences a regional performance function, not an ideology, and the two entries must not be merged merely because both contain the word *Bhand*.

Verdict: name the technique, the carrier and the purpose - then stop.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = both entries with their exact technique and carrier 4 + satire identified as purpose rather than tone 2 + three features of institutionalisation 2 + the ideology boundary and the non-merger caution 2.**

### Examination use

For Prelims, the highest-value items in this lesson are the ones with named particulars: **Ramman** (Bhumiyal Devta, Bhandari caste, Narasimha mask, UNESCO listing); **Kariyala** (Bijeshwar; Solan, Shimla, Sirmour; performed on agricultural prosperity or fulfilled wishes); **Nautanki** (offshoot of Swang, *Ain-i-Akbari*, *Nagada*, Kanpur and Lucknow schools); **Maach** (Malwa, *Rangat Dohas*); **Bhavai** (pots and pitchers, *Jhanjhar* and *dholak*, *Sutradhar* called *Nayaka*); **Garoda** (Brahmin community, painted pictures); **Naqal/Bhand** (*behrupiya*, *naqalchi*, Muslim Bhand community); **Bhand Pather** (secular in outlook, performed by Muslims); **Ramlila** (before Dussehra, male actors playing Sita).

For Mains, this region supplies two distinct arguments. The first is **theatre as licensed social and political criticism**: a mimicry tradition using comedy and absurdity to deliver a social or political message, and a Kashmiri tradition of contemporary social satire described as secular in outlook - which connects directly back to the *Vidusaka* of Lesson 6 and forward to the political theatre of Lesson 15. The second is **occasion as the engine of folk performance**: a Dussehra window, a local-deity dedication, and a votive performance triggered by agricultural prosperity or a fulfilled wish.

### Traps introduced here

- Fusing *Naqal / Bhand* with *Bhand Pather*, or transferring *behrupiya* and *naqalchi* to the Kashmiri form.
- Treating *Khyal* in this topic as the Hindustani vocal genre.
- Treating *Nayaka* in Bhavai as the hero rather than as the *Sutradhar*.
- Reversing the parentage of Nautanki and Swang.
- Assigning the *Nagada* to Swang or the *Jhanjhar* to Nautanki.
- Forgetting that the record gives Swang **two** regional attributions.
- Reproducing Ramman without the caste, the deity or the mask - the three features that actually identify it.

### Revision notes

1. **Ramlila** (UP) - **Ramayana** in songs, dances and dialogues, **mainly before Dussehra**; **male actors**, who also **perform Sita**.
2. **Naqal / Bhand** (Punjab / UP) - **mimicry**; ***behrupiya*** or ***naqalchi***; comedy, absurdities, wit, humour for a **social or political message**; **Muslim Bhand community**.
3. **Nautanki** (North India) - **offshoot of Swang**; in **Abul Fazl's *Ain-i-Akbari***; historical, social and folk tales; **lyrical dialogue**; drum ***Nagada***; later **two schools, Kanpur and Lucknow**.
4. **Bhand Pather** (J&K) - contemporary **social satire** or **mythical stories**; **secular in outlook**, performed by **Muslims**; music, dance and drama.
5. **Kariyala** (HP) - local deity ***Bijeshwar***; **Solan, Shimla, Sirmour**; performed on **agricultural prosperity** or **fulfilled personal wishes**.
6. **Ramman** (Uttarakhand) - **Garhwal**; ***Bhumiyal Devta***; on **UNESCO's Representative List** (📰 inscribed **2009**); **Bhandari caste** wears the **sacred Narasimha mask**; stories of Rama narrated.
7. **Swang / Saang** (Rajasthan / MP-Malwar; popular in **Rajasthan and Haryana**) - **musical dramas sung through verses**; ***ektara, harmonium, sarangi, dholak, khartal***; **mimicry with dialogue**.
8. **Khyal** (Rajasthan) - folk theatre of **storytelling, music and dance** at **festivals and fairs**; humour and social messages. ⚠️ Not the Hindustani vocal genre.
9. **Maach** (MP) - **Malwa**; mythological first, **romantic folk tales** later; dialogues as **couplets - *Rangat Dohas***.
10. **Raslila** (Gujarat) - **dance drama** of the **adolescent love stories of Krishna and Radha**. **Garoda** (Gujarat) - **Garoda Brahmin** community; **painted pictures**; romance and valour. **Bhavai** (Gujarat) - **series of small plays**; romantic; **earthen pots / brass pitchers balanced**; **semi-classical music in folk style**, ***Jhanjhar*** and ***dholak***; ***Sutradhar* known as *Nayaka***.

### Concept check

**Question:** Within one region this lesson records a mimicry tradition carried by a named Muslim community that delivers social and political messages through comedy, and a Kashmiri satirical tradition described as secular in outlook although performed by Muslims. What analytical claim can you build from the pair, and what must you avoid claiming?

**Model answer:** The claim is that in the northern folk field, **satire is an institutionalised performance function with an identifiable carrier community, and its content is not determined by the religious identity of its performers**. Both halves are evidenced. The function is institutional rather than incidental: one tradition is defined by mimicry and impersonation - its performer is called the impersonator - and its stated purpose is to get a social or political message across through comedy, absurdities, wit and humour; the other is defined by contemporary social satire alongside mythical stories. The carrier is named in both cases. And the independence of content from performer identity is stated in the record itself, which describes the Kashmiri form as secular in outlook although performed by Muslims, and elsewhere records a satirical mimicry tradition performed by artists of a Muslim community that draws on general social and political material. That pairing is genuinely useful in a Mains answer about theatre as a vehicle of social communication, because it gives a named, communal, regionally specific mechanism instead of a general assertion. What must be **avoided** is the leap from these two entries to a claim about religious communities and Indian performance as a whole, or to a claim that these traditions were consciously secularist projects. The record describes an outlook and a repertoire; it does not supply an ideology, a manifesto or a national pattern.

**Misconception to avoid:** Merging the two traditions because both contain the word *Bhand* and both involve satire. They differ in region, in technique and in repertoire, and the words *behrupiya*, *naqalchi* and *mimicry* belong to one of them while *contemporary social satire*, *secular in outlook* and *music, dance and drama* belong to the other. A shared syllable is the weakest possible reason to merge two entries, and it is exactly the reason an item-setter would offer you.

## Lesson 12 - The Deccan and the west coast: Maharashtra, Goa, Chhattisgarh, Telangana and Andhra Pradesh

Progress: 12/23 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: Tamasha, Powada, Zadipatti, Dashavatar, Ranmale, Tiatr, Nacha, Pagati Veshaalu, Burra Katha and Oggukatha - each on occasion, carrier, means and theme

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

CA search: "Not applicable - regional field lesson"
CA found: None found; none claimed
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: ten forms, grouped by what the performance is organised around

```text
   ORGANISED AROUND A PERSON OR A ROLE
   PAGATI VESHAALU    Telangana + Krishna district of Andhra Pradesh;
                      primarily a ROLE-PLAYING ACT, revolving around a
                      principal character known as VESHAM (disguise) and
                      other sub-characters
   TAMASHA            Maharashtra; known for HUMOUR and EROTIC CONTENT;
                      unique feature: the presence of FEMALE ACTORS, WHO
                      EVEN PLAY MALE ROLES; generally accompanied by
                      LAVANI songs

   ORGANISED AROUND AN INSTRUMENT
   BURRA KATHA        Andhra Pradesh; storytelling tradition named from
                      BURRA, the percussion instrument used extensively;
                      a MAIN ARTIST or NARRATOR + TWO CO-ARTISTS who
                      provide the RHYTHM and the CHORUS
   OGGUKATHA          Andhra Pradesh; from OGGU, a SMALL HAND DRUM
                      associated with LORD SHIVA; literally OGGU-TALES;
                      performed by PASTORAL COMMUNITIES - KURUMA and
                      GOLLA (YADAVS) of the DECCAN PLATEAU; troupes MOVE
                      FROM PLACE TO PLACE narrating stories of their
                      CASTE GODS

   ORGANISED AROUND AN EVENT OR A DEITY CYCLE
   POWADA             Maharashtra; when SHIVAJI killed AFZAL KHAN a play
                      was written lauding his HEROIC DEEDS, later known
                      as Powada; OPERATIC BALLADS of VALOUR sung by folk
                      musicians called GONDHALIS and SHAHIRS
   DASHAVATAR         Maharashtra / Goa; by the FARMERS of the KONKAN
                      region, notably SINDHUDURG and NORTH GOA; to honour
                      the TEN INCARNATIONS OF LORD VISHNU; TWO PARTS -
                      POORVA-RANGA (initial) and UTTARA-RANGA (second and
                      MAIN act, based on MYTHOLOGICAL STORIES)
   RANMALE            Goa; RITUALISTIC and FOLK theatre on mythological
                      stories from the RAMAYANA and MAHABHARATA; performed
                      during HOLI, celebrated as SHIGMO (spring festival)
                      in Goa and the Konkan region

   ORGANISED AROUND A SEASON OR A COMMUNITY
   ZADIPATTI          Maharashtra; name from the local word ZADI for RICE;
                      performed in the RICE-CULTIVATING region during the
                      HARVEST SEASON; a BLEND OF COMMERCIAL AND FOLK
                      theatre
   NACHA              Chhattisgarh; performed by the RURAL COMMUNITIES;
                      FOUR forms - GANDAWA NACHA, KHARE SAAJ NACHA,
                      DEWAR NACHA, BAITHE SAAJ NACHA; usually held at
                      NIGHT; COMEDY is an essential aspect
   TIATR              Goa; mainly in the ROMAN KONKANI dialects; music,
                      dancing and singing; social, religious and political
                      themes; considered a 'MIRROR OF GOAN CULTURE'
```

### 1. The learner's actual problem

This region contains the two entries most likely to be reversed in an objective item - two Andhra storytelling forms each named after a different drum - and the one entry whose name collides with a famous temple in another topic altogether. It also contains the field's clearest instance of a performance tradition arising from a datable political event. The lesson teaches all ten and settles each collision as it arises.

### 2. The role and the person: Pagati Veshaalu and Tamasha

**Pagati Veshaalu** is the **folk tradition popular in the Telangana region as well as the Krishna district of Andhra Pradesh**. It is **primarily a role-playing act, revolving around a principal character known as *vesham* (disguise) and other sub-characters**.

The identifying idea is that the performance is built around **disguise as its organising principle** rather than around a plot. Note the vocabulary: *vesham* means disguise, and it is the same conceptual family as the impersonation traditions of the north, where the performer is called the impersonator. Two regions, two names, one technique - and an answer that observes this has made a genuine cross-regional connection rather than a list.

**Tamasha** is a form of folk theatre of **Maharashtra known for its humour and erotic content**. **The unique feature of Tamasha is the presence of female actors, who even play male roles.** **Tamasha performances are generally accompanied by *Lavani* songs.**

Three facts, all examinable. The **content** description is the record's own and should be reproduced as recorded rather than softened or sensationalised. The **gendered convention** is the field's mirror image of the Manipuri form in which men play women, and of the Uttar Pradesh epic enactment in which male actors perform Sita. The **accompanying song genre** - *Lavani* - is the identifier most likely to appear in a matching item, and it is the point at which this form touches the music and dance owners; here it is named only as the accompaniment that identifies the theatre.

### 3. The instrument as name: Burra Katha and Oggukatha

These two are the most confusable pair in the region and the most efficiently separated.

| | **Burra Katha** | **Oggukatha** |
|---|---|---|
| **Region** | Andhra Pradesh | Andhra Pradesh |
| **Named from** | ***Burra*** - the **percussion instrument** used extensively during the performance | ***Oggu*** - a **small hand drum** associated with **Lord Shiva** |
| **Literal sense** | A storytelling tradition deriving its name from the instrument | Literally **oggu-tales** |
| **Personnel** | A **main artist or narrator** and **two co-artists** who provide **the rhythm as well as the chorus** | Performed by **pastoral communities** |
| **Carrier** | Not separately named | ***Kuruma*** and ***Golla* (Yadavs)** of the **Deccan plateau** |
| **Mobility** | Not separately recorded | Troupes **move from place to place** |
| **Theme** | A popular storytelling tradition | Narrating the stories of **their caste gods** |

Read down the two columns and the discrimination is easy: **Burra Katha is identified by its three-person structure**, and **Oggukatha is identified by its carrier communities, its mobility and its deity association**. If a stem names a small hand drum associated with Shiva, or pastoral communities of the Deccan plateau, it is the second. If a stem names a main narrator supported by two co-artists providing rhythm and chorus, it is the first.

Oggukatha also carries an analytical point worth a sentence in a Mains answer: **itinerant troupes narrating the stories of their own caste gods** is a precise description of performance functioning as community self-representation. The tradition is not performing a national epic to a general public; it is carrying a specific community's deities across a region.

### 4. The event and the deity cycle: Powada, Dashavatar and Ranmale

**Powada** - **when Shivaji killed his adversary Afzal Khan, a play was written lauding the heroic deeds of Shivaji, which later came to be known as Powada.** They are **operatic ballads depicting stories of valour** and are **sung by the folk musicians known as *Gondhalis* and *Shahirs***.

This is the field's clearest instance of a performance genre arising from a **specific political event**. It belongs to the second stage of the folk arc - local heroes entering the repertoire - and it is the best single example of it, because the record names the event, the protagonist, the adversary and the carrier musicians. Note the two named carrier categories, *Gondhalis* and *Shahirs*, which are likely item components.

**Dashavatar** is a **popular form of drama by the farmers of the Konkan region, notably Sindhudurg (Maharashtra) and North Goa**. It is **to honour the ten incarnations of Lord Vishnu**. It has **two parts: *poorva-ranga* (initial part) and *uttara-ranga* (second part)**. **The second session is the main act and is based on mythological stories.**

Four points. The **carrier is occupational** - farmers - which is unusual and examinable. The **region crosses a state border**, and the record names both sides. The **theme is a deity cycle**, the ten incarnations. And the **two-part structure** with the named second part as the main act is the fourth instance in this session of a performance with formally named divisions, after the classical five-part shape, the Assamese three *Khandas* and the Tamil Nadu puppet show's four stages still to come.

⚠️ **The collision to disarm:** *Dashavatara* also names a **temple at Deogarh**, which belongs to the temple-architecture owner and has appeared in recent objective papers in that capacity. In this session the word names a **Konkan folk drama of the ten incarnations performed by farmers**. Read the stem: a *shikhara* style or a temple location is not this entry.

**Ranmale** is a **ritualistic and folk theatre form** of **Goa**, **based on mythological stories from the popular Indian epics, the Ramayana and the Mahabharata**. It is **performed during the Holi festival which is celebrated as *Shigmo* (spring festival) in Goa and the Konkan region**.

The identifier is the **named festival under its regional name**: Holi as *Shigmo*. A form tied to a named regional spring festival belongs beside the Dussehra window of the northern epic enactment and the Baisakhi timing of a Himachali martial tradition later in this session; occasion is the axis that these three share.

### 5. Season, community and dialect: Zadipatti, Nacha and Tiatr

**Zadipatti** **derives its name from the local name *zadi* for rice**. **Performed in the rice-cultivating region of Maharashtra during the harvest season**, it is a **blend of commercial and folk theatre form**.

Two things make this entry unusually interesting. First, the name is **agricultural**: a theatre named after a crop, sited in the tract that grows it and timed to the harvest. Second, the record calls it a **blend of commercial and folk theatre** - which is the only entry in the whole folk table that explicitly names a commercial dimension, and therefore the natural bridge to the commercial proscenium stage of Lesson 14. Folk and commercial are not opposites in this record; one named form is recorded as both.

**Nacha** (Chhattisgarh) is **performed by the rural communities there** and **involves four different forms of Nacha: *Gandawa Nacha*, *Khare Saaj Nacha*, *Dewar Nacha* and *Baithe Saaj Nacha***. **Usually held at night, comedy is an essential aspect of this folk theatre.**

The four named sub-forms are the identifier, together with the **night** timing and the **essential** role of comedy. Note the last word: the record does not say comedy is present, it says it is essential - a statement about the form's constitution, not its atmosphere.

**Tiatr** (Goa) **is performed mainly in the Roman Konkani dialects and includes music, dancing and singing**. **Tiatr revolves around social, religious and political themes.** **It is considered to be a 'Mirror of Goan Culture'.**

Three identifiers: the **named dialect group**, the **three-fold thematic range** and the **epithet**. Tiatr is also the western coast's clearest instance of the third stage of the folk arc - a form whose stated subject matter is social, religious and political - and it should be cited beside the Manipuri courtyard form's value list when an answer needs evidence that folk theatre states its own social purpose.

### 6. What links the region

Three threads, each usable.

**The instrument names the form.** Two Andhra traditions are named for their drums, and a third connection runs to the Odisha form named for its wooden instrument in Lesson 10 and to the Odisha puppet form named for its rod in Lesson 19. In the Deccan and the east, the object that makes the sound frequently supplies the name.

**The occupational and community carrier is explicit.** Farmers of the Konkan; pastoral Kuruma and Golla communities of the Deccan plateau; rural communities of Chhattisgarh; the *Gondhali* and *Shahir* musicians of Maharashtra. This region names *who performs* more consistently than any other in the table, which makes it the strongest evidence block for a "performance and community" argument.

**Commercial and folk are not opposed.** One Maharashtra form is recorded as a blend of commercial and folk theatre. That single entry prevents the easy but false opposition between a commercial urban stage and a non-commercial village stage, and it should be held ready for the modern-theatre lessons.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**In the Deccan and on the west coast, a performance form is frequently named by its instrument or produced by an event. Elucidate. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model

Both naming logics are recorded, and each is a ready identifier.

**Named by instrument.** Burra Katha derives its name from the *Burra*, the percussion instrument used extensively in performance, and is carried by a main artist with two co-artists supplying rhythm and chorus. Oggukatha derives its name from the *Oggu*, a small hand drum associated with Lord Shiva, and means oggu-tales; it is performed by the pastoral Kuruma and Golla communities of the Deccan plateau. A third case names a crop instead: Zadipatti, from *zadi*, the local word for rice.

**Produced by an event.** Powada began when a play was written lauding Shivaji's heroic deeds after he killed Afzal Khan, and became operatic ballads of valour sung by the *Gondhalis* and *Shahirs*.

Verdict: where a form's name is a noun you can identify, the identification question is already half answered.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = two instrument-named forms with their exact instruments and personnel 4 + the crop-named third case 2 + the event-produced form with its occasion and carriers 3 + a verdict converting etymology into an examination tactic 1.**

### Examination use

For Prelims, the highest-value discriminations are: **Burra Katha** (percussion instrument *Burra*; main artist plus two co-artists providing rhythm and chorus) against **Oggukatha** (*Oggu*, small hand drum associated with Shiva; Kuruma and Golla pastoral communities; itinerant; caste gods); **Tamasha** (female actors playing male roles; *Lavani* accompaniment); **Powada** (Shivaji and Afzal Khan; *Gondhalis* and *Shahirs*); **Dashavatar** (Konkan farmers; Sindhudurg and North Goa; *poorva-ranga* and *uttara-ranga*); **Ranmale** (Holi as *Shigmo*); **Zadipatti** (*zadi* = rice; harvest; commercial-folk blend); **Nacha** (four named sub-forms; night; comedy essential); **Tiatr** (Roman Konkani; Mirror of Goan Culture); **Pagati Veshaalu** (*vesham*; Telangana and Krishna district).

For Mains, the region supplies the best community-and-performance evidence in the topic, and one clean instance of a genre arising from a dated political event.

### Traps introduced here

- Reversing *Burra* and *Oggu*, or attaching the Shiva association to the wrong one.
- Attaching the Kuruma and Golla communities to the wrong Andhra form.
- Reading *Dashavatar* in a stem as the Deogarh temple.
- Assigning *Lavani* to a form other than Tamasha.
- Reversing *poorva-ranga* and *uttara-ranga*, or forgetting that the second is the main act.
- Treating Zadipatti as purely folk when the record calls it a blend with commercial theatre.
- Losing the four named sub-forms of the Chhattisgarh entry, which is the only thing that identifies it.

### Revision notes

1. **Tamasha** (Maharashtra) - **humour and erotic content**; unique feature **female actors who even play male roles**; accompanied by ***Lavani*** songs.
2. **Powada** (Maharashtra) - written lauding **Shivaji's** heroic deeds after he killed **Afzal Khan**; **operatic ballads of valour**; sung by ***Gondhalis*** and ***Shahirs***.
3. **Zadipatti** (Maharashtra) - from ***zadi***, rice; **rice-cultivating region**, **harvest season**; **blend of commercial and folk theatre**.
4. **Dashavatar** (Maharashtra / Goa) - by the **farmers of the Konkan**, notably **Sindhudurg** and **North Goa**; honours the **ten incarnations of Vishnu**; two parts ***poorva-ranga*** and ***uttara-ranga***, the **second being the main act**, based on mythological stories.
5. **Ranmale** (Goa) - **ritualistic and folk**; Ramayana and Mahabharata; performed during **Holi, celebrated as *Shigmo***, the spring festival of Goa and the Konkan.
6. **Tiatr** (Goa) - **Roman Konkani dialects**; music, dancing, singing; **social, religious and political themes**; **'Mirror of Goan Culture'**.
7. **Nacha** (Chhattisgarh) - **rural communities**; four forms: ***Gandawa Nacha, Khare Saaj Nacha, Dewar Nacha, Baithe Saaj Nacha***; **night**; **comedy essential**.
8. **Pagati Veshaalu** (Telangana / Krishna district, AP) - **role-playing act** around a principal ***vesham*** (disguise) with sub-characters.
9. **Burra Katha** (AP) - from ***Burra***, the percussion instrument; **main artist/narrator + two co-artists** giving **rhythm and chorus**.
10. **Oggukatha** (AP) - from ***Oggu***, a **small hand drum associated with Lord Shiva**; **oggu-tales**; **Kuruma and Golla (Yadavs)** pastoral communities of the **Deccan plateau**; itinerant troupes; **caste gods**.

### Concept check

**Question:** Oggukatha is performed by itinerant pastoral communities narrating the stories of their own caste gods. What does that single entry establish about the function of folk theatre that a devotional-origin statement alone would not?

**Model answer:** It establishes that folk performance can function as **community self-representation** rather than as the transmission of a shared religious repertoire. A general devotional-origin statement tells you that many forms began with devotional themes; it leaves open the assumption that the devotion in question is a common, pan-regional one - the epics, the Puranas, a major deity. This entry blocks that assumption in three moves. The repertoire is specified as the stories of **their caste gods**, which makes the content community-specific rather than universal. The carriers are specified as **pastoral communities**, named as Kuruma and Golla of the Deccan plateau, which ties the repertoire to an occupational and social group. And the troupes are specified as **moving from place to place**, which means the performance carries that community's deities outward into a wider region rather than only reproducing them internally. Put together, the form is a mobile assertion of a particular community's sacred world, and that is a different social function from a village enactment of a national epic. In an answer on performance and community, this is the entry that makes the point concretely, and it pairs well with the Konkan farmers' drama and the Chhattisgarh rural communities' night theatre to show that the record names carriers specifically rather than speaking of "villagers".

**Misconception to avoid:** Turning the observation into a claim about caste hierarchy or exclusion. The record names the communities and their repertoire; it does not describe their social standing, their relations with other groups or any restriction on audience. The livelihood and social-structure question belongs to the society papers, and an answer that crosses into it without evidence has left this topic's boundary.

```text
   B. THE REST OF THE SOUTHERN FIELD

   KARNATAKA    BHUTA ARADHANA / BHUTA KOLA - BHUTA means SPIRIT; worship
                of divine beings (ancestors, avatars of Hindu gods, local
                folklore heroes) mainly by the TULU-SPEAKING population;
                prevalent in COASTAL KARNATAKA; KOLA is the chief form of

   KERALA       CHAVITTU NATAKAM - old MARITIME theatre of coastal Kerala;
                colourful CHRISTIAN art form; elements from EUROPEAN and
                KERALA art forms; origin traced to the advent of the
                PORTUGUESE; themes from the BIBLE and heroic exploits of
                legendary CHRISTIAN WARRIORS
                KRISHNANATTAM - colourful DANCE-DRAMA; MID-17th CENTURY;
                based on KRISHNA GEETHI; a carnival lasting EIGHT DAYS,
                performed on EIGHT CONSECUTIVE NIGHTS AT TEMPLES,
                narrating the life story of Lord Krishna

                Representative List, 2010

                BOW-SONG; musical theatre in Kerala and parts of Tamil
                Nadu; stories of the RAMAYANA narrated using BOW-SHAPED
                INSTRUMENTS
   TAMIL NADU   BHAGAVATA MELA - traditional folk theatre performed mainly
                IN VILLAGES; combines dance, drama and music; stories from
                Hindu epics, especially the BHAGAVATA PURANA; traditionally
                during RELIGIOUS FESTIVALS; elaborate costumes, devotional
                songs, stylized acting; moral and spiritual lessons
                KURUVANJI - originated around 300 YEARS AGO; classical
                TAMIL POETRY and songs; first Kuruvanji composed by
                TIRIKOODA RASAPPA KAVIRAYAR; theme revolves around a
                LOVE-STRUCK HEROINE; KURUVANJI literally means
                'FORTUNE-TELLER' who predicts the fate of the heroine;
                performed in a DANCE BALLET form with BHARATANATYAM as the
                principal dance form in Tamil Nadu
                TERUKKUTTU / THERUKOOTHU - STREET THEATRE of Tamil Nadu
                and TAMIL-SPEAKING REGIONS OF SRI LANKA; theme mainly the
                MAHABHARATA and, in some instances, the RAMAYANA; a
                combination of song, music, dance and drama with 'CLEVER
                STAGE TRICKS'; actors wear COLOURFUL COSTUMES
```

### 1. The learner's actual problem

The southern field contains the topic's single most connected entry. Yakshagana appears four separate times in the record - as a folk theatre in its own right, as the parent of a Karnataka open-air tradition, as the successor of a seated narration form, and as the model for a string-puppet tradition - and it influences a fifth, a coastal ritual theatre. A learner who treats these as five unrelated names will lose all five; a learner who holds the cluster will gain a cross-referenced block that answers questions in three different lessons. This lesson builds the cluster first and then teaches the rest of the field.

### 2. The Yakshagana cluster, built in order

**Tal-Maddale** (Karnataka / Kerala). ***Tal*** is a **kind of cymbal** and ***Maddale*** is a **kind of drum**. It is **generally considered as the predecessor of Yakshagana**. **The play is performed while being seated and without any costume, dance or acting.** The **narration is carried out by a *Bhagavata* who is aided by a group of *Arthadharis***. It is **mainly performed in coastal Karnataka and Kerala**.

Read the second sentence again, because it is one of the most striking in this topic. A theatre performed **seated, without costume, dance or acting** is a theatre reduced to **speech and music alone**. It is the extreme case on the stage-relation axis of Lesson 1: the performer does not embody the character at all, and the whole performance is carried by narration and argument between a lead narrator and a group. Naming that as the **predecessor** of a fully costumed dance-drama is a claim about how one kind of performance grew out of another - a claim you can use whenever a question asks how Indian performance forms develop.

**Yakshagana** (Karnataka, and parts of Kerala). It is **perhaps one of the oldest theatre traditions, which is prevalent till date in Karnataka and parts of Kerala**. It **originated in the royal courts of the Vijayanagara empire** and was **performed by a particular community known as *Jakkula Varu***. **Originally, it was largely a descriptive dance-drama enacted by a single artist. Later forms adopted further variations and became a typical dance drama.** It is **strongly influenced by the Vaishnava Bhakti Movement.**

Five examinable facts: the **antiquity claim with its hedge** - *perhaps* one of the oldest; the **court origin** in a named empire; the **named performing community**; the **single-artist origin** and its later expansion into a full dance-drama; and the **Bhakti influence**. Note that this is one of the few folk-table entries with a **court origin**, which complicates any neat division between courtly and rural theatre - and it should be cited whenever an answer needs to show that the urban-rural contrast of Lesson 9 is a general description with real exceptions.

**Bayalata** (Karnataka). It is an **open-air theatre tradition of Karnataka that is performed during the worship of the local deity**. **The stories are also based on the love of Radha-Krishna.** **It is a form of Yakshagana.**

The record explicitly makes this a **form of** the parent tradition, so the relation is given rather than inferred. Its own identifiers are the **open-air staging**, the **local-deity occasion** and the **Radha-Krishna material**.

**The two external links.** Outside the folk table, Yakshagana reaches into two further entries: the Karnataka **string-puppet tradition whose puppets are styled and designed based on the various characters of the Yakshagana theatres** - taught in Lesson 17 - and the coastal Karnataka **ritual theatre of spirit worship, which is recorded as influenced by the Yakshagana form** - taught immediately below. A tradition that supplies a puppet tradition's character designs and shapes a ritual theatre's style is doing regional work well beyond its own performance, and saying so is the single strongest sentence available about Karnataka in this topic.

### 3. Karnataka's ritual theatre

**Bhuta Aradhana or Bhuta Kola** (Karnataka). ***Bhuta***, which **means spirit**, is a **traditional practice of worshipping divine beings** - **ancestors, different avatars of Hindu gods, local folklore heroes** and similar - **mainly by the Tulu-speaking population**, and it is **prevalent in coastal Karnataka**. ***Kola* is the chief form of Bhuta worship** and it is a **ritualistic theatre, influenced by Yakshagana form**.

Four identifiers: the **meaning of the name**, the **three categories of divine beings** honoured, the **linguistic community**, and the **Yakshagana influence**. The three categories deserve attention because they are precise and unusual: ancestors, avatars of Hindu gods, **and local folklore heroes** - a ritual system that admits a local hero into the same frame as a deity. That is worth a sentence in any answer on the relation between religion and performance.

The record also pairs this form explicitly with a Kerala tradition: **Theyyam is similar to a practice in Karnataka, called Bhuta Kola**. Two coastal ritual theatres across a language boundary, recorded as similar - a genuine cross-regional pairing given by the source rather than constructed.

### 4. Kerala's four, and the maritime outlier

**Chavittu Natakam** is an **old maritime theatre form of coastal Kerala**. It is a **colourful Christian art form** and **has elements borrowed from both European and Kerala art forms**. **Its origin can be traced with the advent of the Portuguese to this region.** **The themes are stories from the Bible and heroic exploits of legendary Christian warriors.**

This is the outlier of the whole folk table, and it is valuable precisely because it is one. Every generalisation about Indian folk theatre as devotional-Hindu in origin, rural in setting and indigenous in idiom fails against a **coastal Christian maritime form of European contact origin with biblical themes**. Cite it whenever an answer needs to show that the field is not homogeneous - and cite it accurately, with the contact origin and the two-source borrowing both named.

**Krishnanattam** is a **colourful dance-drama tradition of Kerala that originated in the mid-17th century**. **Based on the works of *Krishna Geethi*, it is a carnival that lasts for eight days, with performances in eight consecutive nights at temples narrating the life story of Lord Krishna.**

The identifiers are the **century**, the **named textual base**, and the **eight-day, eight-night temple cycle**. The last is the most distinctive scheduling fact in the entire folk field: a performance structured as a fixed multi-night cycle at temples.

**Theyyam** is **performed in Kerala**. It is **similar to a practice in Karnataka, called Bhuta Kola**. It is an **open theatre** and is **performed in front of the local temples to mainly honour the spirit of the ancestors besides gods**. **Themes of Vaishnavism, Shaktism and Shaivism are now common.** **The actors wear elaborate headgear and colourful costumes.**

Note the word **now** in the themes sentence: the record is describing a repertoire that has changed, with the major sectarian themes becoming common over time alongside the ancestral core. That is a recorded change in a living form and it is exactly the kind of detail that distinguishes a careful answer from a static one.

**Mudiyettu** is a Kerala **ritual theatre and dance drama**, inscribed on the **UNESCO Representative List of the Intangible Cultural Heritage of Humanity in 2010**. ⚠️ Those three facts - form-type, region and inscription year - are the whole of what this session holds for it, and nothing further should be supplied in an answer.

**Villu Paatu / Villadi Champaatu** (Kerala / Tamil Nadu). The term ***Villu Paatu*** means **bow-song**. It is a form of **musical theatre popular in Kerala and some parts of Tamil Nadu**, in which **stories of the Ramayana are narrated using bow-shaped instruments**.

The identifier is the **instrument that gives the name**, and the form belongs in the same analytical family as the two Andhra drum-named traditions of Lesson 12 and the Odisha *kathia* tradition of Lesson 10.

### 5. Tamil Nadu's three

**Bhagavata Mela** is a **traditional folk theatre, performed mainly in villages**. It **combines dance, drama and music to depict stories from Hindu epics, especially the *Bhagavata Purana***. **Traditionally performed during religious festivals, it involves elaborate costumes, devotional songs and stylized acting to convey moral and spiritual lessons.**

The identifiers are the **named Purana**, the **religious-festival occasion** and the explicitly **stylized acting** - which is a direct instance of the *Natyadharmi* mode of Lesson 3 operating in the folk field.

**Kuruvanji** **originated around 300 years ago** and is **characterised by classical Tamil poetry and songs**. **The first Kuruvanji was composed by *Tirikooda Rasappa Kavirayar*.** **The basic theme revolves around a love-struck heroine.** ***Kuruvanji* literally means 'fortune-teller'**, who **predicts the fate of the heroine**. It is **performed in a dance ballet form with Bharatanatyam being the principal dance form in Tamil Nadu**.

This is the folk table's most literary entry: a named first composer, a classical poetic medium, a defined plot situation and a title that names a character function rather than the form's technique. The **fortune-teller etymology** is the single most likely item component here, because the name appears to describe the heroine and in fact describes the figure who predicts her fate.

**Terukkuttu / Therukoothu** is a **street theatre form practised in the state of Tamil Nadu in India and Tamil-speaking regions of Sri Lanka**. **The theme mainly focuses on epics like the Mahabharata and, in some instances, the Ramayana.** **The plays are a combination of song, music, dance and drama along with 'clever stage tricks'.** **The actors wear colourful costumes.**

Two features do the identification: the **transnational Tamil-speaking reach**, which is the only cross-border attribution in the folk table, and the **clever stage tricks**, which is the record's own phrase and marks the form as one in which spectacle and stage effect are constitutive rather than incidental.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**One Karnataka tradition is recorded as shaping a puppet tradition, a ritual theatre and an open-air form, and as having a predecessor of its own. Critically examine what this tells us about how Indian performance forms develop. (20 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

It shows development by **local dominance** rather than by descent from a treatise.

**The cluster.** Yakshagana is perhaps one of the oldest theatre traditions, prevalent till date in Karnataka and parts of Kerala; it originated in the royal courts of the Vijayanagara empire, was performed by the community known as *Jakkula Varu*, began as a descriptive dance-drama enacted by a single artist, later became a typical dance drama, and is strongly influenced by the Vaishnava Bhakti Movement. Its recorded predecessor, Tal-Maddale, is performed seated, without any costume, dance or acting, narrated by a *Bhagavata* aided by *Arthadharis*. Bayalata is recorded as a form of it, performed open-air during the worship of the local deity. Bhuta Kola, a ritual theatre of the Tulu-speaking coast, is influenced by it. And a Karnataka string-puppet tradition's figures are styled and designed on its characters.

**What this establishes.** A dominant regional form supplies design, style and repertoire to neighbours using entirely different mechanisms - a seated narration, a ritual theatre, an open-air deity performance and a strung wooden figure.

**The critical limits.** Influence is not genealogy: an influence is recorded, not a common ancestor. The direction is not universal, since elsewhere a dance form shapes a puppet tradition and a martial art shapes a masked dance. And a court origin inside a folk classification shows those categories are descriptive, not derivational.

Verdict: development here is neighbourhood-led, and should be written as influence, never as descent.

**Unique scoring note:** **20 marks = the parent tradition with origin, community, development and influence 6 + all four recorded relations named separately 6 + the cross-mechanism generalisation stated precisely 4 + three critical limits on influence, direction and category 3 + a verdict distinguishing influence from descent 1.**

### Examination use

For Prelims, the southern field's highest-value discriminations are the Yakshagana cluster - **Tal-Maddale** as the seated, costume-free predecessor with its *Bhagavata* and *Arthadharis*; **Yakshagana** with Vijayanagara courts, *Jakkula Varu*, single-artist origin and Bhakti influence; **Bayalata** as an open-air form of Yakshagana during local-deity worship - together with **Bhuta Kola** (*Bhuta* = spirit; Tulu-speaking; coastal Karnataka; Yakshagana-influenced; similar to Theyyam), **Chavittu Natakam** (maritime, Christian, Portuguese advent, biblical themes), **Krishnanattam** (mid-17th century, *Krishna Geethi*, eight days and eight nights at temples), **Theyyam** (open theatre before local temples, ancestral spirits, elaborate headgear), **Villu Paatu** (bow-song, Ramayana, bow-shaped instruments), **Bhagavata Mela** (*Bhagavata Purana*, villages, religious festivals), **Kuruvanji** (300 years ago, Tirikooda Rasappa Kavirayar, fortune-teller, Bharatanatyam ballet form) and **Therukoothu** (street theatre, Tamil Nadu and Sri Lanka, Mahabharata, clever stage tricks).

For Mains, the south supplies the topic's best evidence for three claims: that a single regional tradition can seed a puppet tradition and shape a ritual theatre; that the folk field includes court-origin and colonial-contact forms and is therefore not reducible to a rural-devotional type; and that the dance-theatre frontier is genuinely contested, since several southern entries are recorded as dance-dramas while sitting inside a theatre classification.

### Traps introduced here

- Treating Tal-Maddale as a variant of Yakshagana rather than as its recorded predecessor.
- Attributing Yakshagana's origin to a temple or a village rather than to the royal courts of the Vijayanagara empire.
- Forgetting the *Jakkula Varu* community, which is the form's named carrier.
- Confusing Bhuta Kola with Theyyam, when the record calls them similar practices in two states.
- Confusing Krishnanattam with Kutiyattam because of the name, or with Chavittu Natakam because both are Kerala and both are called colourful.
- Reading *Kuruvanji* as the heroine rather than as the fortune-teller.
- Dropping the Sri Lanka attribution from the Tamil street-theatre entry.

### Revision notes

1. **Tal-Maddale** (Karnataka / Kerala) - ***Tal*** cymbal, ***Maddale*** drum; **predecessor of Yakshagana**; performed **seated, without costume, dance or acting**; ***Bhagavata*** narrates, aided by ***Arthadharis***; coastal Karnataka and Kerala.
2. **Yakshagana** (Karnataka + parts of Kerala) - **perhaps one of the oldest** theatre traditions, **prevalent till date**; originated in the **royal courts of the Vijayanagara empire**; community ***Jakkula Varu***; **originally a single-artist descriptive dance-drama**, later a typical dance drama; **Vaishnava Bhakti** influence.
3. **Bayalata** (Karnataka) - **open-air**; during **worship of the local deity**; **Radha-Krishna** stories; **a form of Yakshagana**.
4. Yakshagana's reach: **Gombeyatta** puppets are **styled on Yakshagana characters**; **Bhuta Kola** is **influenced by the Yakshagana form**.
5. **Bhuta Aradhana / Bhuta Kola** (Karnataka) - ***Bhuta*** = **spirit**; worship of **ancestors, avatars of Hindu gods, local folklore heroes**; **Tulu-speaking** population; **coastal Karnataka**; ***Kola*** is the chief form; **ritualistic theatre**.
6. **Chavittu Natakam** (Kerala) - **maritime**, **Christian**; **European + Kerala** elements; origin with the **advent of the Portuguese**; **Bible** and **legendary Christian warriors**.
7. **Krishnanattam** (Kerala) - **mid-17th century**; based on ***Krishna Geethi***; **eight days**, **eight consecutive nights at temples**; life of Krishna.
8. **Theyyam** (Kerala / Karnataka) - **open theatre** in front of **local temples**; **spirit of the ancestors** besides gods; **Vaishnavism, Shaktism, Shaivism** now common; **elaborate headgear**; similar to **Bhuta Kola**. **Mudiyettu** (Kerala) - ritual theatre and dance drama; **UNESCO 2010**.
9. **Villu Paatu / Villadi Champaatu** (Kerala / TN) - **bow-song**; **Ramayana**; **bow-shaped instruments**.
10. **Bhagavata Mela** (TN) - villages; **Bhagavata Purana**; religious festivals; stylized acting. **Kuruvanji** (TN) - **300 years ago**; **Tirikooda Rasappa Kavirayar**; **fortune-teller**; love-struck heroine; **dance ballet with Bharatanatyam**. **Therukoothu** (TN + **Tamil-speaking Sri Lanka**) - **street theatre**; **Mahabharata**, sometimes Ramayana; **clever stage tricks**; colourful costumes.

### Concept check

**Question:** Yakshagana is recorded as originating in the royal courts of the Vijayanagara empire, yet it appears in a table of folk theatre. What does that tension tell you about the urban-rural contrast established earlier, and how should you state it in an answer?

**Model answer:** It tells you that the contrast is a **general description of a field, not a rule that classifies every form**. The definitional statement is that folk theatre reflects local lifestyle, social norms, beliefs and customs and, unlike the sophisticated urban Sanskrit theatre, has rural roots and a rustic style. That holds for the great majority of the field. But this entry is recorded as originating in the royal courts of a named empire, performed by a named community, beginning as a single-artist descriptive dance-drama and later expanding into a full dance-drama - a court beginning with a subsequent regional life. The honest statement is therefore: folk theatre in India is predominantly rural in roots and rustic in idiom, but the category as recorded also contains court-origin traditions that later became regionally embedded, and at least one colonial-contact Christian maritime form whose idiom borrows from European as well as local art. Writing that sentence does three things at once - it reproduces the definition, it demonstrates that you have read the field rather than the heading, and it pre-empts the examiner's own counter-example. The same move is available with the Maharashtra entry recorded as a blend of commercial and folk theatre, which similarly complicates a clean folk-versus-commercial division.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding that the folk category is therefore meaningless or that the classification is faulty. The category does real work: it groups traditions by rural siting, community transmission, occasion-bound performance and regional idiom, and the overwhelming majority of the field fits. A good answer states the general pattern, names the exceptions precisely and moves on. It does not abandon the classification because two entries strain it.

## Lesson 14 - Modern theatre Part A: the colonial city, the proscenium, the commercial stage and the Act that tried to control it

Progress: 14/23 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the colonial origin of modern Indian theatre, the proscenium form, commercial themes, the Dramatic Performances Act of 1876, the earliest named venues and Parsi theatre

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried - the Modern Indian Theatre section's opening paragraphs, the boxed venue notes for Calcutta, Madras and Shimla, and the Parsi theatre paragraph, read in full
CA search: "Not applicable - colonial-period institutional lesson"
CA found: None found; none claimed
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: a new theatre, and the four things that made it new

```text
   INDIA'S MODERN THEATRE GREW DURING THE COLONIAL ERA

   INPUT 1 : SOURCES                 INPUT 2 : SITE
   influenced by SANSKRIT            URBAN CENTRES - CALCUTTA and MADRAS
   *AND* WESTERN texts               led to PROSCENIUM-STYLE THEATRE
        \                                   /
         \                                 /
          v                               v
        A BLENDED STYLE: "during the British era, Indians created a
        theatre style blended with Indian and Western elements"
                          |
          +---------------+---------------+
          |                               |
   INPUT 3 : ECONOMY               INPUT 4 : SUBJECT MATTER
   THEATRES BECAME COMMERCIAL      plays addressed SOCIAL EVILS -
   (tickets, houses, companies)    DOWRY | CASTE | RELIGIOUS
                                   HYPOCRISY | POLITICS
                          |
                          v
   +--------------------------------------------------------------+
   |  THE DRAMATIC PERFORMANCES ACT, 1876                          |
   |  "imposed to curb political themes"                           |
   |  >> the state legislates against a STAGE. That is the clearest|
   |     single proof that this theatre was politically consequential|
   +--------------------------------------------------------------+

   THE EARLIEST NAMED VENUES
   CALCUTTA   THE CALCUTTA THEATRE / THE NEW PLAYHOUSE - founded 1775,
              active until 1808
              First motion pictures in Bengal, by HIRA LALA SEN, screened
              at THE STAR THEATRE, MINERVA THEATRE and THE CLASSIC THEATRE
   MADRAS     GAIETY THEATRE - the first PERMANENT CINEMA THEATRE set up in
              the then Madras, by MR RAGHUPATHY VENKAIAH
   SHIMLA     a GAIETY THEATRE since 1887

   PARSI THEATRE  famous in WESTERN INDIA, 1850s-1920s
                  plays in GUJARATI and MARATHI
                  COLOURFUL BACKDROPS and MUSIC essential
                  themes: ROMANCE, HUMOUR, MELODRAMA
                  FROM THE 1930s, with the advent of cinema, many Parsi
                  producers WENT INTO FILM-MAKING
```

### 1. The learner's actual problem

The modern stage looks, at first glance, like a discontinuity - as though Indian theatre stopped after the classical contraction and restarted under the British. Lessons 9 to 13 have already disproved that. What this lesson has to explain is something more specific: **what was actually new** about the colonial-era stage, given that India had a large and active regional theatre field throughout. The answer is four things, and they are the four inputs in the diagram: a **blended source base**, an **urban proscenium site**, a **commercial economy** and a **directly social and political subject matter**. The fifth item - the statute - is the state's response to the fourth.

### 2. The four new things, stated exactly

**India's modern theatre grew during the colonial era, influenced by Sanskrit and Western texts.** **Urban centres like Calcutta and Madras led to proscenium-style theatre.**

**During the British era, Indians created a theatre style blended with Indian and Western elements.** **Theatres became commercial, and plays addressed social evils like dowry, caste, religious hypocrisy and politics.** **The Dramatic Performances Act (1876) was imposed to curb political themes.**

Take the four inputs one at a time.

**The blended source base.** The record is explicit that the influence runs from **Sanskrit *and* Western texts**. This is not a story of Western imposition on a blank field, and it is not a story of indigenous continuity either. It is a recorded **blend**, and the word appears twice in the passage. An answer that describes modern Indian theatre as simply derivative of the Western stage has contradicted the source; so has an answer that describes it as a continuation of the classical tradition.

**The proscenium.** A proscenium stage is a picture-frame stage: the audience sits in front of a framed opening and looks in at the action, rather than surrounding it or sharing its space. Compare that with the stage relations catalogued in Lessons 10 to 13 - open air, open courtyard, in front of a local temple, seated narration without costume, a street. The proscenium is a **spatial revolution** in this topic, and it is directly tied to the urban centre: the record links Calcutta and Madras to the emergence of the form. A framed, ticketed, enclosed house is what makes the next input possible.

**The commercial economy.** **Theatres became commercial.** That single clause changes the entire basis of performance. In the folk field, performance is funded by occasion and community: a festival, a harvest, a votive fulfilment, a temple cycle. In the classical system it was tied to patronage. A commercial theatre is funded by an **audience that pays to enter**, which means the audience's preferences become the form's economic constraint. Hold this beside the Maharashtra form recorded as a **blend of commercial and folk theatre** from Lesson 12: commerce is not exclusively urban in this record, but the urban house institutionalises it.

**The social and political subject matter.** Plays addressed **dowry, caste, religious hypocrisy and politics**. Note what kind of list this is. These are not mythological themes reinterpreted; they are **named contemporary social questions**. The colonial-era stage is the point in this topic at which theatre stops encoding social comment inside a character type - the satirical *Vidusaka* of Lesson 6, the impersonator of Lesson 11, the satirical Kashmiri form - and starts naming its targets directly.

### 3. The 1876 Act, and what it proves

**The Dramatic Performances Act (1876) was imposed to curb political themes.**

This is the most analytically valuable single fact in the modern half of this topic, and it is frequently reduced to a date. It deserves three sentences of thought.

**What it is.** A colonial statute directed at performance, whose stated purpose in this record is to **curb political themes** on the stage.

**What it proves.** A government does not legislate against an art form that is inconsequential. The existence of the Act is therefore **independent evidence** that the colonial-era Indian stage had become politically significant enough to require control. That inference is stronger than any assertion about theatre's importance, because it comes from the state's own behaviour rather than from the theatre's self-description.

**How to use it.** In a Mains answer, the Act is the hinge between the commercial stage and the political theatre of Lesson 15. The sequence is: a commercial urban stage acquires a mass audience; that stage begins to address dowry, caste, religious hypocrisy and politics; the colonial state legislates to curb the political element; and organised political theatre nevertheless emerges in the twentieth century as the cultural wing of a party, writing on social themes. That is a complete causal narrative built entirely from recorded facts.

⚠️ **The discipline.** State the Act's year and its recorded purpose. Do not extend it into claims about specific prosecutions, plays banned or provinces covered unless a source supplies them; this session does not hold that material and does not supply it.

### 4. The named venues, and why three cities appear

The record supplies a small set of named, dated venue facts. They matter as **anchors**: an examiner can build a matching item from them, and an answer that carries two of them is visibly grounded.

| City | Venue fact as recorded |
|---|---|
| **Calcutta** | The **first theatre in Calcutta** was **The Calcutta Theatre or The New Playhouse**, **founded in 1775 and active until 1808** |
| **Calcutta** | The **first motion pictures in Bengal**, made by **Hira Lala Sen**, were **screened at The Star Theatre, Minerva Theatre and The Classic Theatre** |
| **Madras** | The **first theatre in Madras** - the **Gaiety Theatre** was the **first permanent cinema theatre** set up in the then Madras, by **Mr Raghupathy Venkaiah** |
| **Shimla** | There is **also a Gaiety theatre in Shimla, since 1887** |

Two observations. First, **two of the four entries are already about cinema**, not about stage drama: motion pictures screened in named theatres, and a permanent cinema theatre. The theatre building is the physical infrastructure that early cinema inherited, and this record shows the transition happening inside the venue itself. ⚠️ The **cinema history proper - its milestones, its personalities, its institutions and its regulation - belongs to the cinema owner**, and this session names these venue facts only because they are recorded as theatre facts. Second, the **name Gaiety appears twice**, in Madras and Shimla, with only the second carrying a date. That is a planted confusion waiting to be used, and the discipline is to attach **1887** to Shimla and **Raghupathy Venkaiah and the first permanent cinema theatre** to Madras.

### 5. Parsi theatre: the bridge between stage and screen

**Parsi Theatres were famous in western India during the 1850s-1920s, featuring plays in Gujarati and Marathi with colourful backdrops and music.** **Colourful backdrops and music were essential.** **The themes were romance, humour, melodrama, etc.** **From the 1930s, with the advent of cinema, many Parsi producers went into film-making.**

Five facts and one argument.

The facts: the **region** (western India); the **period** (1850s-1920s); the **two languages**; the **essential** backdrops and music; and the **themes**.

The argument: Parsi theatre is the clearest instance in this topic of a **commercial popular form migrating into a new medium**. A theatre defined by colourful backdrops, music, romance, humour and melodrama is a theatre whose strengths are exactly the strengths early commercial cinema needed, and the record states the migration directly: from the 1930s, many of its producers went into film-making. Notice how this completes the venue observation above - the buildings were already being used for motion pictures, and now the producers follow. That is the whole transition, stated in two recorded sentences.

⚠️ The word **essential** in the record's description of backdrops and music should be preserved. It marks them as constitutive features of the form rather than decorative ones, in the same way that the record calls comedy an essential aspect of the Chhattisgarh folk form.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"The Dramatic Performances Act of 1876 tells us more about the condition of the Indian stage than any contemporary description of it." Examine. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model

The claim holds, because the Act is behavioural evidence rather than testimony.

What a description asserts, a statute demonstrates. The record describes a blended Indian and Western style, a proscenium stage led by Calcutta and Madras, commercial theatres, and plays on dowry, caste, religious hypocrisy and politics. The Dramatic Performances Act of 1876, imposed to curb political themes, is the state acting on that condition.

A state legislates only against what it finds consequential. Three conditions follow: the stage reached an audience large enough to matter politically; its political content was specific enough to be targeted; and existing controls were judged insufficient.

The qualification: the Act evidences official perception, not artistic quality or audience size, and it addressed one urban segment of a much wider field.

Verdict: as proof of political consequence the Act is decisive; as evidence of the whole Indian stage it is partial.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the behavioural-evidence distinction stated in the opening 2 + the four recorded descriptive facts used as the baseline 2 + three inferences drawn specifically from the act of legislating 3 + a two-part qualification separating official perception from artistic reach, and the urban segment from the whole field 2 + a verdict that accepts the claim in its strong sense and limits its scope 1.**

### Examination use

For Prelims, the dated set is the examinable core: **Dramatic Performances Act, 1876**; **The Calcutta Theatre / The New Playhouse, 1775-1808**; **Gaiety Theatre, Shimla, since 1887**; **Gaiety Theatre, Madras, first permanent cinema theatre, Raghupathy Venkaiah**; **Hira Lala Sen** and the three named Calcutta theatres; **Parsi theatre, 1850s-1920s**, **Gujarati and Marathi**, **from the 1930s into film-making**. Expect a matching item built on the two Gaiety theatres or on the venue-to-city pairs.

For Mains, this lesson supplies the first half of the modern arc and the strongest single argument in it - that legislation against a stage is evidence of that stage's political weight.

### Traps introduced here

- Describing modern Indian theatre as purely Western-derived, when the record names Sanskrit and Western influence and calls the result a blend.
- Treating the colonial stage as the revival of a dead Indian theatre, ignoring the regional field.
- Attaching 1887 to the Madras Gaiety rather than to Shimla.
- Extending the 1876 Act beyond its recorded purpose into unrecorded prosecutions or bans.
- Importing cinema history into this topic beyond the venue facts the record supplies as theatre facts.
- Treating Parsi theatre's backdrops and music as decorative when the record calls them essential.

### Revision notes

1. **Modern Indian theatre grew during the colonial era**, influenced by **Sanskrit *and* Western texts**.
2. **Urban centres like Calcutta and Madras led to proscenium-style theatre** - a picture-frame stage, against the open-air, courtyard, temple-front and street relations of the folk field.
3. Indians created a style **blended with Indian and Western elements**.
4. **Theatres became commercial**; plays addressed **dowry, caste, religious hypocrisy and politics**.
5. **Dramatic Performances Act, 1876** - **imposed to curb political themes**. Legislation is evidence of political consequence.
6. **The Calcutta Theatre / The New Playhouse** - **founded 1775**, **active until 1808**; first theatre in Calcutta.
7. **Hira Lala Sen's** first motion pictures in Bengal screened at **The Star Theatre, Minerva Theatre and The Classic Theatre**.
8. **Gaiety Theatre, Madras** - **first permanent cinema theatre** there, by **Mr Raghupathy Venkaiah**. **Gaiety theatre, Shimla - since 1887.**
9. **Parsi Theatres** - **western India, 1850s-1920s**; **Gujarati and Marathi**; **colourful backdrops and music essential**; **romance, humour, melodrama**; **from the 1930s** many producers **went into film-making**.
10. The venue record shows theatre buildings hosting early cinema **before** the producers migrate - the transition happens in the house first.

### Concept check

**Question:** The record says modern Indian theatre was influenced by "Sanskrit and Western texts" and that Indians created a style "blended with Indian and Western elements". Why is the word *blended* doing more work than it appears to, and what would be lost by replacing it with *influenced by the West*?

**Model answer:** The word is doing categorical work: it makes the modern stage a **third thing** rather than a local version of a foreign form. If the sentence read only "influenced by the West", the modern stage would be a derivative and its Indian content would be residual - a matter of setting and language layered over an imported structure. *Blended*, together with the explicit naming of Sanskrit alongside Western texts as sources, makes both inputs constitutive. That matters for three specific claims later in the arc. First, it explains why the same period produces a stage that is simultaneously proscenium-framed and commercially organised on one side and drawing on indigenous dramatic material on the other. Second, it makes sense of the later, deliberate return to traditional form by named modern institutions - a Manipur institution formed to keep traditional theatre alive and a repertory theatre working in that idiom - which would be inexplicable as a reversal if the modern stage had been simply Western. Third, it protects the answer from a nationalist over-correction in the other direction, because the Western input is named in the record and cannot be deleted. What would be lost by the substitution is therefore both the accuracy of the source and the explanatory power of the sentence.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating "blended" as a comfortable, contentless word that lets you avoid saying anything. It has specific content here and should be given specific evidence: the two named source traditions, the proscenium form adopted from the urban Western model, the commercial organisation, and a subject matter - dowry, caste, religious hypocrisy, politics - that is neither Sanskrit nor Western but contemporary Indian.

## Lesson 15 - Modern theatre Part B: literary and political theatre, institutions, regional revival, and the practitioners who must be named

### Visual: a century, read as a sequence of answers to one question
