### 1. The learner's actual problem

This lesson contains more proper names than any other in the session, and a learner meeting them as a list will retain none. The remedy is the question at the top of the diagram. Every entry here is an answer to **who pays for and who authorises a performance** - a professional company, a literary author, a party's cultural wing, a touring enterprise, a state academy, a regional institution, or a movement that deliberately needs almost no money at all. Learn the sequence of answers and the names attach themselves.

### 2. The professional company, and the two Bengali names that must be known

**Girish Chandra Ghosh (1844-1912)** was a **Bengali actor, director and writer**. He was **largely responsible for the golden age of Bengali theatre**. He **cofounded the Great National Theatre, the first Bengali professional theatre company, in 1872**, and **wrote nearly 40 plays and acted in and directed many more**.

**Binodini Dasi (1863-1941)**, popularly known as ***Notee Binodini***, was a **Bengali actress and theatre artist**. She was the **prima donna of Bengali theatre**, and **the first woman in India to become a notable stage performer from the Bengal Presidency in the 1870s**. She **began her career in acting at the age of 12 and ended by the time she was 23**, as she later recounted in her noted **autobiography, *Amar Katha* (The Story of My Life), which was published in 1913**. She **also made contributions in founding the Star Theatre in Kolkata in 1883**.

These two entries belong together because they describe the same institutional moment from two sides. The first Bengali professional theatre company in 1872 creates a **salaried, organised stage**; within that stage, within the same decade, a woman becomes a notable performer for the first time in India from that presidency, and by 1883 is contributing to the founding of a theatre house. A professional company is what makes a professional performer possible.

The Binodini Dasi entry is also the densest single-person entry in the topic and every element of it is examinable: the **dates**, the **epithet**, the **first-woman claim with its exact scope**, the **career span from twelve to twenty-three**, the **autobiography with its title, English rendering and publication year**, and the **Star Theatre, Kolkata, 1883**.

⚠️ **A recorded discrepancy, preserved rather than resolved.** This session's own reproduced question material states that she **serialised her autobiography between 1910 and 1913**, while the chapter's prose states that it **was published in 1913**. Serialisation over a period and publication in a year are not contradictory descriptions, but they are different claims and this session records both rather than choosing. The safe formulation in an answer is "*Amar Katha*, published in 1913".

### 3. The literary author

**Rabindranath Tagore was a celebrated playwright.** He **wrote his first play at the age of 20 - *Valmiki Pratibha***. His **famous works are *Raktakarabi* (Red Oleanders), *Chitrangada*, *Post-Office*, etc.** His **themes included nationalism, spirituality, the socio-political situation, etc.** **Other noted personalities were Prasanna Kumar Thakur, Girishchandra Ghosh and Dinabandhu Mitra (*Nildarpan*).**

Three examinable specifics: the **first play at twenty** with its title; the **three named works** with the English rendering of the first; and the **three-fold thematic range**. The associated names matter too, and one of them carries a title - **Dinabandhu Mitra (*Nildarpan*)** - which is the only play attributed to a named author in this part of the record and therefore the most likely matching item.

⚠️ **Boundary note.** Play authorship and literary analysis belong to the language and literature owner. The names and titles above are carried here because the record places them inside the theatre chapter as part of the modern stage's institutional history, and because one owned Main Examination item of this topic asks for a play's author. Nothing further about these texts is claimed here.

### 4. The organised political movement

**In 1943, the Indian People's Theatre Association was formed as a cultural wing of the Communist Party.** **Although it was disbanded in 1947, it played an important role in the further development of theatre.** **Its theatre-artists wrote plays on various social themes such as the Bengal famines.** **Notable personalities associated with it were Balraj Sahni, Prithviraj Kapoor, Bijon Bhattacharya, Ritwik Ghatak and Utpal Dutt.** **It now exists in Chhattisgarh, Punjab and West Bengal.**

This is the clearest instance in the topic of **theatre as organised political mobilisation**, and four elements make it so: the **date**, the explicit description as the **cultural wing of a political party**, the **subject matter** - social themes such as the Bengal famines - and the **named personalities**. The fifth element, its **disbandment in 1947** followed by the statement that it **now exists** in three named states, is the one most often dropped and the one an item is most likely to use.

Note the direct connection to Lesson 14. A colonial statute of 1876 is imposed to curb political themes on the stage; sixty-seven years later a political party forms a theatre association that writes on social themes. The line between them is not a single causal chain, and it should not be drawn as one - but the sequence does show that theatre remained the site where political content and official concern met.

### 5. The mobile enterprise, and the state institutions

**Prithvi Theatre was established in 1944 by Prithviraj Kapoor.** It was a **moving theatre with an entourage of 150 artists** and **held more than 2000 plays**. **It was only in 1978 when a permanent theatre was opened in Mumbai, which still exists today.**

Read the three numbers together - **1944**, **150 artists**, **more than 2000 plays**, **permanent house only in 1978** - and the entry makes an argument. For thirty-four years, this was a theatre that **travelled to its audience** rather than waiting for an audience to come to a house. That is a **distribution strategy**, and it is the exact opposite of the fixed proscenium house of Lesson 14. It also places Prithviraj Kapoor in two of this lesson's entries at once, since he is also among the personalities associated with the political theatre association of the previous year.

**In 1952, the Sangeet Natak Akademi was established for promoting the performing arts including theatre, giving further impetus to the development of theatre in India.** **The National School of Drama also contributed in producing great theatre personalities.**

This is the arrival of the **state institution** as the authorising body. Two chronological refinements are worth holding, because both are examinable and neither cancels the other.

⚠️ **The academy's own date is double.** The academy was **created by a Government of India resolution in 1952** and **formally inaugurated on 28 January 1953**. Accounts that say 1952 and accounts that say 1953 are therefore describing **two different acts** - a creating resolution and an inauguration - and the academy's own award series is counted from **1952**. The safe formulation in an answer is "created by resolution in 1952 and inaugurated in 1953"; the unsafe one is to assert either year alone as *the* founding date and to treat the other as an error.

⚠️ **The drama school's relation to the academy is also dated.** It was **set up under the Sangeet Natak Akademi in 1959** and **became an autonomous body in 1975**. The parent-to-independence relation is the examinable part: a school born inside the academy and later separated from it.

The academy's award administration, its fellowship structure and the school's detailed governance belong to the heritage and institutions owner; this session states the establishment years and purposes because they are part of the modern stage's history, and places the one current record it claims in Lesson 22.

### 6. The regional return, and the minimalist alternative

**Kalakshetra Manipur was formed by Heisnam Kanhailal in 1969 in Manipur to keep the traditional theatre alive.** **Ratan Thiyam established the famous Chorus Repertory Theatre in 1976.**

Two Manipuri institutions, seven years apart, both modern in form and both oriented towards traditional theatre. This is the point at which the modern stage turns back towards the field of Lessons 10 to 13 - not by abandoning institutional form but by using it deliberately to sustain traditional performance. In an answer this is the counter-weight to any claim that modernisation simply displaced tradition: the record shows modern institutions created **for the purpose of** keeping traditional theatre alive.

**Badal Sircar (1925-2011)** was a **pioneer of modern Indian theatre and street theatre**. He **developed the 'Third Theatre' movement, which focused on small, experimental and socially conscious plays**. He **used theatre as a tool for social change, addressing issues like poverty, injustice and political oppression**, and **encouraged minimalist staging and direct actor-audience interaction, making theatre accessible to all**.

This is the lesson's final answer to its opening question, and it is an answer by **subtraction**. If a theatre needs almost no set, no house and no ticketed enclosure, then the question of who pays for and authorises it changes shape entirely. Minimalist staging plus direct actor-audience interaction plus accessibility is a design that removes the proscenium, removes the distance and removes the cost barrier in one move. Set it against the 1775 playhouse and the 1978 permanent house and the arc of the whole lesson becomes visible: from the built house, to the travelling company, to the state institution, to the street.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Discuss the emergence of the professional stage performer in modern India, using only what is recorded of the Bengali theatre. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The professional performer appears when a professional structure appears, and the record dates both.

**The structure.** Girish Chandra Ghosh (1844-1912), a Bengali actor, director and writer largely responsible for the golden age of Bengali theatre, cofounded the Great National Theatre - the first Bengali professional theatre company - in 1872, and wrote nearly forty plays while acting in and directing many more. Theatres had by then become commercial.

**The performer.** Binodini Dasi (1863-1941), popularly known as *Notee Binodini*, was the prima donna of Bengali theatre and the first woman in India to become a notable stage performer from the Bengal Presidency in the 1870s. She began acting at twelve and ended by twenty-three; she contributed to founding the Star Theatre in Kolkata in 1883; and her autobiography, *Amar Katha*, was published in 1913.

**The relation.** A salaried company creates a career; a career creates a performer whose standing is public; and a performer of standing helps found a house. The sequence runs from 1872 to 1883 in one city.

Two qualifications. The career is strikingly short - eleven years - and no explanation is recorded, so none should be invented. And the autobiography's dating appears in two forms, published in 1913 and serialised between 1910 and 1913; state the publication year.

Verdict: professionalisation is the condition of this career, not its consequence.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the company with its founder, its first-ness and its date 4 + the performer with dates, epithet, exact first-woman scope, career span, theatre founding and autobiography 6 + the structural relation argued as a dated sequence 3 + the unexplained career length and the dating variant 1 + a verdict reversing the intuitive causal order 1.**

### Examination use

For Prelims, the dated and named set is large and every element is a plausible item component: **Great National Theatre, first Bengali professional theatre company, 1872**; **Girish Chandra Ghosh 1844-1912, nearly 40 plays**; **Binodini Dasi 1863-1941, *Notee Binodini*, first woman in India to become a notable stage performer from the Bengal Presidency in the 1870s, career from 12 to 23, *Amar Katha* 1913, Star Theatre Kolkata 1883**; **Tagore's first play at 20, *Valmiki Pratibha***; ***Nildarpan* by Dinabandhu Mitra**; **IPTA 1943, cultural wing of the Communist Party, disbanded 1947, Bengal famines, now in Chhattisgarh, Punjab and West Bengal**; **Prithvi Theatre 1944, 150 artists, 2000+ plays, permanent theatre 1978**; **Sangeet Natak Akademi 1952**; **Kalakshetra Manipur 1969, Heisnam Kanhailal**; **Chorus Repertory Theatre 1976, Ratan Thiyam**; **Badal Sircar 1925-2011, Third Theatre**; **K.V. Subbanna, NINASAM, Ramon Magsaysay**.

For Mains, this lesson is the whole second half of the modern arc and supplies two of the topic's strongest units: **gender and the professional stage**, evidenced by a named first woman performer with a published autobiography and a theatre founding; and **theatre as a deliberate tool of social change**, evidenced by an organised political association and a named minimalist movement with a stated method.

### Traps introduced here

- Merging the 1872 professional company with the 1883 theatre house, or the 1944 moving theatre with the 1978 permanent house.
- Dropping IPTA's 1947 disbandment or its present existence in three named states.
- Attributing *Nildarpan* to Tagore, or *Valmiki Pratibha* to another author.
- Inventing awards, honours or founding dates for any named practitioner.
- Describing Third Theatre as merely "street theatre" without its stated method - small, experimental, socially conscious plays, minimalist staging, direct actor-audience interaction, accessibility.
- Treating the Manipur institutions as traditional bodies rather than as modern institutions founded to sustain traditional theatre.

### Revision notes

1. **Girish Chandra Ghosh (1844-1912)** - Bengali actor, director, writer; **golden age of Bengali theatre**; cofounded the **Great National Theatre**, the **first Bengali professional theatre company, 1872**; **nearly 40 plays**.
2. **Binodini Dasi (1863-1941)**, ***Notee Binodini*** - **prima donna of Bengali theatre**; **first woman in India to become a notable stage performer from the Bengal Presidency in the 1870s**; career from **12 to 23**; ***Amar Katha (The Story of My Life)*, published 1913**; contributed to founding the **Star Theatre, Kolkata, 1883**.
3. **Tagore** - first play at **20**, ***Valmiki Pratibha***; ***Raktakarabi* (Red Oleanders), *Chitrangada*, *Post-Office***; themes **nationalism, spirituality, socio-political situation**. Also **Prasanna Kumar Thakur**, **Girishchandra Ghosh**, **Dinabandhu Mitra (*Nildarpan*)**.
4. **IPTA, 1943** - cultural wing of the **Communist Party**; **disbanded 1947**; plays on social themes such as the **Bengal famines**; **Balraj Sahni, Prithviraj Kapoor, Bijon Bhattacharya, Ritwik Ghatak, Utpal Dutt**; **now exists in Chhattisgarh, Punjab and West Bengal**.
5. **Prithvi Theatre, 1944**, by **Prithviraj Kapoor** - **moving theatre**, **150 artists**, **more than 2000 plays**; **permanent theatre in Mumbai only in 1978**.
6. **Sangeet Natak Akademi, 1952** - for promoting the performing arts **including theatre**. ⚠️ Created by **resolution in 1952** and **inaugurated on 28 January 1953**; both years are current in different accounts because they name different acts. **National School of Drama** produced great theatre personalities; set up under the academy in **1959** and **autonomous from 1975**.
7. **Kalakshetra Manipur, 1969**, **Heisnam Kanhailal** - to **keep traditional theatre alive**. **Chorus Repertory Theatre, 1976**, **Ratan Thiyam**.
8. **Badal Sircar (1925-2011)** - **Third Theatre**: small, experimental, socially conscious; **minimalist staging**, **direct actor-audience interaction**, **accessible to all**; issues of **poverty, injustice, political oppression**.

Ledger: **Samasa (1898-1939)**, **Adya Rangacharya (1904-1984)**, **Kuvempu (1904-1994)**, **Subramanya Bharathiar** (TN), **Veeresalingam Pantulu** (AP), **Sreekandan Nair** (Kerala), **Bhartendu Harishchandra**, **Jaishankar Prasad**, **B.V. Karanth (1929-2002)** (AP, Karnataka, MP), **K.V. Subbanna (1932-2005)** (**NINASAM**, **Ramon Magsaysay**), and the further set including **Girish Karnad, Habib Tanvir, Vijay Tendulkar, Vijaya Mehta, Dharamvir Bharati, Mohan Rakesh, Chandrashekhar Kambar, P. Lankesh**.

### Concept check

**Question:** Arrange the modern-theatre entries of this lesson as a sequence of answers to the question "who pays for and authorises a performance", and say what Third Theatre does to that question.

**Model answer:** The sequence has six answers. **The professional company** answers it commercially and organisationally: the first Bengali professional theatre company of 1872 creates salaried, organised production, and within that structure a professional performer becomes possible, with a theatre house founded in 1883. **The literary author** answers it by reputation: a celebrated playwright's name authorises the work, and the repertoire follows his themes of nationalism, spirituality and the socio-political situation. **The party** answers it politically: the theatre association of 1943 is the cultural wing of a political party, and its plays on social themes such as the Bengal famines are authorised by a movement rather than by a market. **The touring enterprise** answers it by mobility: a moving theatre with a hundred and fifty artists and more than two thousand plays carries production to audiences for thirty-four years before acquiring a permanent house in 1978. **The state** answers it institutionally: a national academy created by resolution in 1952 to promote the performing arts including theatre, and a national drama school producing practitioners. **The region** answers it by deliberate return: two Manipuri institutions, founded in 1969 and 1976, use modern institutional form to keep traditional theatre alive. What **Third Theatre** does is change the question rather than answer it. By adopting minimalist staging, direct actor-audience interaction and a commitment to accessibility, it reduces the material requirements of performance to the point where the house, the set and the ticket largely disappear - and with them, much of the leverage that a payer or an authoriser holds. That is why the record describes it as using theatre as a tool for social change: the form's economics are part of its politics.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading the sequence as a progression in which each stage replaces the last. It does not. The professional stage, the literary repertoire, the political association, the touring company, the state institution, the regional revival institution and the minimalist movement coexist, and at least one person - a founder of the touring theatre who is also named among the political association's personalities - belongs to two of them at once. The sequence organises the answers; it does not date their disappearance.

## Lesson 16 - Puppetry: why mechanism is the classification axis, and the claim that puppetry is an archive

Progress: 16/23 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: puppetry's definition and economics, its Indian antiquity and textual references, the philosophical framing, the four-category classification and its present recorded condition

### Visual: one art, four mechanisms, and the reason the axis is not regional

```text
   WHY CLASSIFY PUPPETRY BY MECHANISM AND NOT BY REGION?

   IF YOU CLASSIFY BY REGION:            IF YOU CLASSIFY BY MECHANISM:
   ODISHA has string (Kundhei),          STRING  -> a suspended figure,
     shadow (Ravanchhaya) and rod                  controlled from above
     (Kathi Kandhe) traditions           SHADOW  -> a flat cut-out
   KARNATAKA has string (Gombeyatta)               interrupting light
     and shadow (Togalu Gombeyatta)      GLOVE   -> a body worn on the hand
   KERALA has shadow (Tolpava Kuthu)     ROD     -> a figure pushed from
     and glove (Pavakoothu)                        below or behind
   >> the state tells you NOTHING        >> the mechanism predicts the
      about how the figure moves            CONSTRUCTION, the SCALE, the
                                            NUMBER of puppeteers, the
                                            STAGE RELATION and the kind
                                            of story that can be told

   THE FOUR CATEGORIES, AS RECORDED
   "Puppetry in India can be broadly classified into four categories"
        STRING  |  SHADOW  |  GLOVE  |  ROD

   AND THE TWO THINGS THAT COMPLICATE A CLEAN FOUR-WAY SPLIT
        BOMMALATTAM (Tamil Nadu) MIXES ROD AND STRING puppetry
        ROD puppets are described as LARGER VARIATIONS OF GLOVE PUPPETS
```

### 1. The learner's actual problem

Puppetry is usually memorised as a form-to-state table, which is the least useful way to hold it. The record itself supplies a better axis: it classifies **by mechanism**, and it then gives, for each mechanism, a set of **construction and control features** that apply across the traditions in that category. Learn the mechanism first and each named tradition becomes a short list of departures from a known baseline. Learn the states first and you will have fourteen names and no structure.

### 2. What puppetry is, and its recorded economics

**Puppetry is one of the oldest art forms that captivates audiences through the master's control over puppets.** It has **low cost and creative freedom in design, colour and movement**, which make it a **popular and ingenious form of entertainment**.

Two phrases in that definition do analytical work.

**"The master's control over puppets"** locates the art in the **relation between manipulator and figure**, not in the figure itself. That is why every tradition below is described by how it is controlled - strings through holes in the hands, head and back; an iron ring on the head; rods at the waist; an index finger in the head; light behind a screen - and why the control method is the first thing to learn about any named tradition.

**"Low cost and creative freedom in design, colour and movement"** is an economic and aesthetic claim together. A form that is cheap to mount and unconstrained in design can be made and remade by small companies without patrons or houses. Hold this beside the present-condition statement in section 5 below, because the two sit in tension: an art form described as low-cost is also described as declining for lack of funding, and an answer that notices the tension and resolves it - cheapness of production is not the same as viability of livelihood - is doing real analysis.

### 3. Antiquity, textual reference and the archive claim

**India has a long history of puppetry, as evidenced by the Harappa and Mohenjo-daro periods.** **The Mahabharata, the Tamil text *Silappadikaram*, and marionette theatre from 500 BC all make reference to it.**

**It also has a philosophical side.** **The narrator is known as *Sutradhar*, or the 'holder of strings', whereas in the *Bhagavad Gita*, God is described as a puppeteer.**

**Each of India's numerous and varied puppetry traditions has its own distinct style, frequently drawing inspiration from local legends, mythology and folklore.** **It is a multifaceted art form that incorporates dance, music, theatre, sculpture and painting.**

Four distinct claims are packed in here and they should be separated.

**The material claim** is that puppetry's Indian history is evidenced from the Harappan periods. ⚠️ Treat this as the record's statement about evidence; do not convert it into a claim that a named tradition existed then.

**The textual claim** names three references: the Mahabharata, the Tamil *Silappadikaram*, and marionette theatre from 500 BC. Note that the same Tamil text appears again in Lesson 20 as the source of a reference to the trade of staves, swords and armour - one text, two different uses in one chapter, and a good instance of why a source reference must be read for what it actually supports.

**The philosophical claim** is a double etymology. The word ***Sutradhar*** is read here as **holder of strings**, and a scriptural image describes God as a puppeteer. Put together, they give the art form a metaphysical resonance: the manipulator-and-figure relation is used as an image of divine agency. This is what makes the "puppetry is not mere children's entertainment" argument evidenced rather than asserted.

**The integration claim** is that puppetry **incorporates dance, music, theatre, sculpture and painting**. This is the single most useful sentence for the cross-art lesson later, and it has a double edge worth stating: integration is a **strength**, because the form carries several arts at once, and a **vulnerability**, because sustaining it requires several linked skills to survive together - the figure has to be carved and painted, the story performed, the music played.

**Reading the four together: the archive claim.** A form referenced in the epic and in early Tamil literature, drawing on local legends, mythology and folklore, and carrying the imagery of scripture in its own vocabulary, is functioning as a **transmission vehicle for textual and mythological material** across regions and centuries. That framing - puppetry as an archive, not merely an entertainment - is the strongest analytical claim available in this half of the topic, and every named tradition in the next three lessons supports it: a Kerala shadow form performing a named Ramayana recension; an Andhra shadow theatre on the epics and Puranas; a Maharashtra leather form on the Ramayana and a Draupadi story; an Odisha rod tradition on Hindu mythology; a Manipuri string tradition on the *Raas Leela*; a Kerala glove tradition on the Ramayana and Mahabharata.

### 4. The four categories, and the two complications

**Puppetry in India can be broadly classified into four categories.** They are **string**, **shadow**, **glove** and **rod**, and each is taught with its construction, control and named traditions in Lessons 17 to 19.

Two recorded facts complicate any attempt to treat the four as watertight, and both are examinable.

⚠️ **A named tradition mixes two mechanisms.** The Tamil Nadu tradition **Bommalattam mixes rod and string puppetry**. It is placed among the string examples in the record and is genuinely both. An item that asks you to classify it has a correct answer only if you reproduce the mixture.

⚠️ **One category is defined by reference to another.** **Rod puppets are larger variations of glove puppets**, controlled by rods from behind a screen. So the four-fold classification is not four independent types; it contains one derivational relation and one hybrid. The right formulation is that puppetry in India is **broadly** classified into four categories - reproducing the record's own hedge - and that the boundaries between them are crossed in practice.

### 5. The present condition, stated exactly

**However, puppetry is currently on decline because of lack of funding and captive audience.**

**Anupama Hoskere was awarded the Sangeet Natak Akademi Puraskar in 2020 in recognition of her efforts to revive puppetry through her organization, 'Dhaatu'.**

Three disciplines apply to this pair, and they are the most important in the topic's safeguarding half.

**First, the tense.** The decline is recorded in the **present tense** and with **two named causes**: lack of funding and lack of a captive audience. That makes it a different kind of statement from the historical decline of Sanskrit theatre in Lesson 7, and the difference is developed fully in Lesson 22.

**Second, the specificity.** Two named causes are far more useful than a general lament. A funding shortage and an audience shortage are different problems with different responses, and an answer that says so is offering analysis rather than sentiment.

**Third, the counter-evidence.** The decline statement sits immediately beside a **named person, a named organisation and a dated award for reviving the art**. The record therefore documents a condition and a response in the same breath. An answer that reports the decline without the response has reproduced half the record; an answer that reports the response without the condition has reproduced the other half.

⚠️ Two further cautions. **Do not generalise the single named revival case across all four mechanisms**; the record names one effort, and the four categories have different construction, scale and skill requirements and therefore different needs. And **do not multiply awards**: this session records exactly two puppetry-related honours from the core record - the 2020 academy award to the named reviver through her organisation, and the **2004 Sangeet Natak Akademi Award to Maguni Charan Kuanr for his contribution to *Kathi Kundhei Nacha* and to Indian puppetry**, taught in Lesson 19. No other honour is claimed for any puppeteer here.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"Indian puppetry is a transmission mechanism for textual and mythological material, and not merely an entertainment." Examine. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The claim is well supported, provided it is stated as a function rather than as a eulogy.

**The textual base.** India's puppetry history is evidenced from the Harappa and Mohenjo-daro periods, and the Mahabharata, the Tamil *Silappadikaram* and marionette theatre from 500 BC all refer to it. The form also carries a philosophical dimension: the narrator is the *Sutradhar*, the holder of strings, and in the *Bhagavad Gita* God is described as a puppeteer.

**The repertoire.** Every named tradition performs inherited material - a named Ramayana recension in a Kerala shadow form; the epics and Puranas in an Andhra shadow theatre; the Ramayana and a Draupadi story in a Maharashtra leather form; Hindu mythology in an Odisha rod tradition; the *Raas Leela* in a Manipuri string tradition; the Ramayana and Mahabharata in a Kerala glove form. Each tradition also draws on local legends, mythology and folklore.

**The carriers.** Four of five shadow traditions have named communities, so the transmission has identifiable custodians.

Two qualifications. The Harappan statement is about evidence, not about a named tradition existing then. And the form incorporates dance, music, theatre, sculpture and painting, which makes it a multiple-skill transmission and therefore vulnerable when any one skill lapses.

Verdict: puppetry documents as it entertains, and the documentary claim is the stronger of the two in an answer.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the three textual references with the two-part philosophical framing 5 + six traditions matched to inherited repertoires 6 + named carrier communities identified as custodians 2 + the Harappan-evidence and multi-skill qualifications 2.**

### Examination use

For Prelims, the testable core is: the **four categories**; the **Harappa and Mohenjo-daro** evidence statement; the **three textual references** including **marionette theatre from 500 BC**; ***Sutradhar*** as **holder of strings**; the ***Bhagavad Gita*** puppeteer image; the **five arts incorporated**; the **present decline** with its **two causes**; and the **2020 award** with the **named organisation**. The likeliest trap is an item that asks which mechanism a mixed tradition belongs to.

For Mains, this lesson supplies the two analytical frames used for the rest of the puppetry block: **puppetry as archive** and **mechanism as the axis of classification**. Both are evidenced, both are portable and neither requires a single additional fact.

### Traps introduced here

- Classifying Indian puppetry by region rather than by mechanism.
- Treating the four categories as watertight, ignoring the mixed tradition and the derivational relation between two of them.
- Converting the Harappan evidence statement into a claim that a named tradition existed in that period.
- Describing puppetry as children's entertainment when the record supplies a philosophical framing.
- Reporting the decline without the dated response, or the response without the decline.
- Generalising a single named revival effort across all four mechanisms.

### Revision notes

1. Puppetry captivates **through the master's control over puppets**; it has **low cost** and **creative freedom in design, colour and movement**.
2. Indian history **evidenced by the Harappa and Mohenjo-daro periods**.
3. Textual references: the **Mahabharata**, the Tamil ***Silappadikaram***, and **marionette theatre from 500 BC**.
4. Philosophical side: the narrator is the ***Sutradhar***, the **'holder of strings'**; in the ***Bhagavad Gita*, God is described as a puppeteer**.
5. Each tradition has its **own distinct style**, drawing on **local legends, mythology and folklore**.
6. A **multifaceted art form incorporating dance, music, theatre, sculpture and painting** - a strength and a vulnerability at once.
7. **Four categories**: **string, shadow, glove, rod** - the record says **broadly** classified.
8. ⚠️ **Bommalattam mixes rod and string**; **rod puppets are larger variations of glove puppets**.
9. **Puppetry is currently on decline because of lack of funding and captive audience** - present tense, two named causes.
10. **Anupama Hoskere**, **Sangeet Natak Akademi Puraskar 2020**, for reviving puppetry through her organisation ***Dhaatu***.

### Concept check

**Question:** The record calls puppetry a low-cost art form and, a few lines later, says it is declining for lack of funding. Is that a contradiction? Answer precisely.

**Model answer:** No, and the reason is worth stating carefully because it converts an apparent inconsistency into an argument. The low-cost claim is about **production**: the figures are small, the materials are wood, cloth and leather, the design is unconstrained, and a show can be mounted without a built house, a large cast or expensive machinery. The funding claim is about **livelihood and continuity**: a practitioner needs an income, a troupe needs to travel, skills need to be taught to the next generation, and the multiple crafts the form incorporates - carving, painting, music, narration - each need someone who can afford to learn them. Those are different economies. An art can be cheap to stage and still be impossible to live by, and in fact the cheapness aggravates the problem, because a form with low production costs also commands low prices and has no capital base to fall back on. The second recorded cause makes this sharper still: the record names a lack not only of funding but of a **captive audience**, and an audience is exactly what converts low production cost into viable income. So the two statements together describe a form that is **easy to make and hard to sustain**, and the correct response follows from that diagnosis - audience-building and income support, rather than expensive production subsidy. That is why the record's own example of a response is an organised revival effort recognised by a dated award, rather than a construction grant.

**Misconception to avoid:** Resolving the tension by deciding that one statement must be wrong. Both are in the record and both are informative. The analytical move is to notice that they refer to different things - the cost of a performance and the viability of a practice - and to use the distinction, which also generalises to every other declining craft-based tradition an answer might have to discuss.

## Lesson 17 - String puppets: construction, control, and five traditions that share a mechanism and nothing else

Progress: 17/23 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the string-puppet baseline, and Kathputli, Kundhei, Gombeyatta, Bommalattam and Laithibi Jagoi

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried - the String Puppets sub-section with its four construction features, and the full example table covering Rajasthan, Odisha, Karnataka, Tamil Nadu and Manipur
CA search: "Not applicable - mechanism and tradition lesson"
CA found: None found; none claimed
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: one baseline, five departures

```text
   THE STRING-PUPPET BASELINE  (string puppets = MARIONETTES)

   [1] generally EIGHT TO NINE INCH miniature figures CHISELLED OUT OF WOOD
   [2] PAINTED WITH OIL COLOURS, detailed with FACIAL FEATURES, fitted with
       WOODEN-PIPE LIMBS
   [3] MINIATURE JEWELLERY and other accessories attached for a
       TRUE-TO-LIFE feel
   [4] STRINGS attached to SMALL HOLES in the HANDS, HEAD and BACK of the
       body, controlled by the puppeteer

   NOW THE FIVE TRADITIONS, EACH AS A DEPARTURE FROM THAT BASELINE

   KATHPUTLI (Rajasthan)       departure: NO LEGS
     wooden dolls in bright Rajasthani dress, performed with FOLK MUSIC
     unique for having NO LEGS; controlled by STRINGS TIED TO THE
     PUPPETEER'S FINGERS

   KUNDHEI / KANDHEI NACHA (Odisha)   departure: MORE JOINTS
     made of LIGHT WOOD, dressed in LONG SKIRTS
     MORE JOINTS -> MORE FLEXIBILITY for the puppeteer
     strings attached to a TRIANGULAR PROP
     marked influence of ODISSI DANCE

   GOMBEYATTA (Karnataka)      departure: MORE THAN ONE PUPPETEER
     the traditional puppet show of Karnataka
     puppets STYLED AND DESIGNED on the characters of the YAKSHAGANA
     theatres
     unique feature: the use of MORE THAN ONE PUPPETEER

   BOMMALATTAM (Tamil Nadu)    departure: SCALE, and a MIXED MECHANISM
     MIXES ROD AND STRING puppetry
     puppets tied to an IRON RING ON THE PUPPETEER'S HEAD
     the LARGEST IN INDIA - up to 4.5 FT, 10 KG
     shows have FOUR STAGES: VINAYAK PUJA | KOMALI | AMANATTAM |
     PUSENKANATTAM

   LAITHIBI JAGOI (Manipur)    departure: LIFE-SIZE, and MASKS
     based on the RAAS LEELA of SHRI KRISHNA
     dates back to the 19th CENTURY
     characters presented by LIFE-SIZE DOLLS of 2-3 METRES
     BLACK BACKDROP
     DEMON characters presented by COSTUME MASKS
```

### 1. The learner's actual problem

Five traditions share one mechanism, and a learner who knows only that they are all string puppets cannot answer a single discriminating question about them. The efficient structure is the one in the diagram: hold the **four baseline construction features** once, then hold **one departure per tradition**. No legs. More joints. More puppeteers. Larger, and mixed. Life-size, and masked. Five words carry five traditions.

### 2. The baseline, stated exactly

**String puppets or marionettes have a prominent presence in the Indian cultural traditions.** Their features are:

- the puppets are **generally eight to nine inch miniature figures chiselled out of wood**;
- **painted with oil colours, detailed with facial features, and fitted with wooden-pipe limbs**;
- **miniature jewellery and other accessories are attached to give them a true-to-life feel**;
- **strings are attached to small holes in the hands, head and back of the body, which are then controlled by the puppeteer**.

Read the four as a design logic rather than as four facts. A figure eight to nine inches tall carved from wood is a **sculptural object**; oil paint and facial detailing make it a **painted one**; jewellery and accessories aim at a **true-to-life feel**; and the three attachment points - hands, head and back - determine exactly what the figure can do. Three points of suspension permit gesture, head movement and torso carriage, which is a specific and limited expressive vocabulary. This is the concrete content of the claim in Lesson 16 that puppetry incorporates sculpture and painting: the claim is not metaphorical, it is a description of how the figure is made.

Note also that the baseline immediately explains the two most striking departures below. A tradition whose figures reach **4.5 feet and 10 kilograms** cannot be controlled from small holes by a hand alone, which is why it is tied to an iron ring on the puppeteer's head and why it needs rods as well as strings. A tradition whose figures are **two to three metres** is not a miniature art at all.

### 3. The five traditions

**Kathputli (Rajasthan).** ***Kathputli*, the traditional string puppets of Rajasthan, are wooden dolls in bright Rajasthani dress, performed with folk music.** **They are unique for having no legs, and are controlled by strings tied to the puppeteer's fingers.**

The two identifiers are the **absence of legs** and the **finger-tied strings**, and they are connected: a figure without legs is a figure whose movement is carried entirely by the upper body and the swing of the costume, which suits direct finger control. The **bright Rajasthani dress** and the **folk-music accompaniment** complete the identification.

**Kundhei / Kandhei Nacha (Odisha).** **The string puppets of Odisha are known as *Kundhei*.** They are **made of light wood and are dressed in long skirts**. **The puppets have more joints, thus giving the puppeteer more flexibility.** **The strings are attached to a triangular prop.** **There is a marked influence of Odissi dance in Kundhei puppet shows.**

Four identifiers, and a clean contrast with the Rajasthani tradition: **light wood** against carved wooden dolls, **long skirts** against bright regional dress, **more joints** against no legs, and a **triangular prop** against strings tied to the fingers. The **Odissi influence** is the entry's cross-art fact and belongs to Lesson 21; note that it is an influence recorded on the **movement** of the puppets, which is precisely what more joints make possible. Construction and stylistic influence are connected here, not two separate facts.

**Gombeyatta (Karnataka).** It is the **traditional puppet show of Karnataka**. **The puppets are styled and designed based on the various characters of the Yakshagana theatres.** **A unique feature of this puppetry is the use of more than one puppeteer to manipulate the puppets.**

Two identifiers. The **Yakshagana character design** is the fourth appearance of that tradition in this session and completes the cluster built in Lesson 13: a regional theatre supplies the visual design of a regional puppet tradition. The **multiple puppeteers** is the unique feature and the likeliest item component.

⚠️ **The collision to disarm:** *Gombeyatta* names this **string** tradition of Karnataka, while ***Togalu* Gombeyatta** names the **shadow** tradition of the same state, taught in Lesson 18. One word, two mechanisms, one state. The discriminator is the prefix ***Togalu***, and a stem that omits it is asking about the string tradition.

**Bommalattam (Tamil Nadu).** **Bommalattam of Tamil Nadu mixes rod and string puppetry.** **Puppets, tied to an iron ring on the puppeteer's head, are the largest in India - up to 4.5 ft, 10 kg.** **Shows have four stages: *Vinayak Puja*, *Komali*, *Amanattam* and *Pusenkanattam*.**

This is the densest puppetry entry in the record and every element is testable: the **mixed mechanism**, the **iron ring on the head**, the **largest in India** claim with **both numbers**, and the **four named stages**. The control method deserves a moment: tying the figure to a ring worn on the head transfers part of the load to the puppeteer's body and frees the hands for rods and strings. That is an engineering solution to the scale problem, and saying so converts a memorised detail into understanding.

The four named stages also make this the fifth instance in this session of a performance with formally named divisions, after the classical five-part shape, the Assamese three *Khandas*, the Konkan two-part drama and the Odia narrator structures.

**Laithibi Jagoi (Manipur).** **This form of puppetry is based on the *Raas Leela* of Shri Krishna.** It **dates back to the 19th century.** **Characters are presented by life-size dolls of 2-3 metres with a black backdrop.** **Demon characters are presented by costume masks.**

Four identifiers: the **repertoire**, the **century**, the **scale with its black backdrop**, and the **costume masks for demons**. Two of these connect outward. The **black backdrop** is a staging decision that belongs beside the shadow tradition's white screen in the next lesson - two puppetry traditions solving the figure-against-background problem in opposite ways. And the **costume masks** put this entry into the mask thread of Lesson 21, alongside the Assamese one-act form, the Garhwal ritual theatre and Chhau mask-making.

### 4. What the five have in common, and what they do not

They share a **mechanism**: a figure moved by strings under a manipulator's control. That is genuinely all. Their **scale** ranges from eight inches to three metres. Their **control** ranges from strings tied to the fingers, to a triangular prop, to multiple puppeteers, to an iron ring worn on the head. Their **material** ranges from carved wood to light wood to life-size dolls. Their **repertoire** ranges from a regional folk idiom to a dance-influenced style to designs taken from a regional theatre to the *Raas Leela*. And one of them is not purely a string tradition at all.

That range is the lesson's analytical point and it is worth one sentence in any answer on Indian puppetry: **a shared mechanism in this art guarantees a shared principle of control and nothing else** - not scale, not material, not repertoire, not the number of hands required. It is the reason a mechanism-based classification is informative rather than reductive: it tells you what to ask about each tradition, and the answers differ.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"A shared mechanism in Indian puppetry guarantees a shared principle of control and little else." Examine with reference to string puppetry. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model

The five string traditions share suspension and manipulation by strings, and diverge on everything measurable.

**Scale** runs from an eight-to-nine-inch baseline to figures of four and a half feet and ten kilograms, and to life-size dolls of two to three metres. **Control** runs from strings tied to the puppeteer's fingers, to a triangular prop, to more than one puppeteer, to an iron ring worn on the head. **Construction** runs from carved wooden dolls without legs to light wood with more joints. **Repertoire** runs from a bright regional folk idiom, through Yakshagana-styled characters, to the *Raas Leela*. One of the five is not purely a string tradition at all, mixing rod and string.

Verdict: the classification is informative because it tells you *what to ask* about a tradition; the answers differ, and reciting the mechanism is not identifying the form.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the shared principle stated once 1 + four axes of divergence, each with two contrasting instances 6 + the mixed tradition named as the internal exception 2 + a verdict on what a mechanism label does and does not deliver 1.**

### Examination use

For Prelims, the baseline construction set is testable in its own right - **eight to nine inches**, **chiselled out of wood**, **oil colours**, **wooden-pipe limbs**, **miniature jewellery**, **strings through small holes in the hands, head and back** - and so is each departure: **Kathputli's legless design and finger-tied strings**; **Kundhei's light wood, long skirts, more joints, triangular prop and Odissi influence**; **Gombeyatta's Yakshagana-styled characters and multiple puppeteers**; **Bommalattam's rod-and-string mixture, iron head ring, 4.5 ft and 10 kg, and four named stages**; **Laithibi Jagoi's *Raas Leela* base, 19th century, 2-3 metre dolls, black backdrop and costume masks for demons**.

For Mains, this lesson gives the concrete evidence for two claims made abstractly in Lesson 16 - that puppetry incorporates sculpture and painting, and that mechanism predicts construction, scale and stage relation - and it supplies the cross-art links that Lesson 21 uses.

### Traps introduced here

- Confusing *Gombeyatta* with *Togalu Gombeyatta*.
- Classifying Bommalattam as purely string when the record calls it a mixture of rod and string.
- Attaching the triangular prop to Kathputli or the finger-tied strings to Kundhei.
- Attributing the multiple-puppeteer feature to a tradition other than the Karnataka string show.
- Giving Laithibi Jagoi a white screen instead of a black backdrop.
- Applying the eight-to-nine-inch baseline to the two large-scale traditions.

### Revision notes

1. **String puppets = marionettes.** Baseline: **8-9 inch** figures **chiselled out of wood**; **oil colours**, **facial features**, **wooden-pipe limbs**; **miniature jewellery** for a **true-to-life feel**; **strings through small holes in the hands, head and back**.
2. **Kathputli (Rajasthan)** - wooden dolls in **bright Rajasthani dress**, **folk music**; **no legs**; **strings tied to the puppeteer's fingers**.
3. **Kundhei / Kandhei Nacha (Odisha)** - **light wood**, **long skirts**, **more joints → more flexibility**, strings on a **triangular prop**, **marked Odissi dance influence**.
4. **Gombeyatta (Karnataka)** - puppets **styled on Yakshagana theatre characters**; unique feature **more than one puppeteer**.
5. **Bommalattam (Tamil Nadu)** - **mixes rod and string**; tied to an **iron ring on the puppeteer's head**; **largest in India, up to 4.5 ft and 10 kg**; four stages - ***Vinayak Puja, Komali, Amanattam, Pusenkanattam***.
6. **Laithibi Jagoi (Manipur)** - based on the ***Raas Leela* of Shri Krishna**; **19th century**; **life-size dolls, 2-3 metres**; **black backdrop**; **demon characters by costume masks**.
7. The three suspension points - **hands, head, back** - define the expressive vocabulary of the baseline figure.
8. ⚠️ ***Gombeyatta*** = string, Karnataka; ***Togalu* Gombeyatta** = shadow, Karnataka. The prefix is the discriminator.
9. Scale across the category runs from **eight inches to three metres**; control runs from **fingers** to a **head-worn iron ring**.
10. A shared mechanism guarantees a shared **principle of control** and nothing else.

### Concept check

**Question:** Bommalattam figures are up to 4.5 feet and 10 kilograms, are tied to an iron ring on the puppeteer's head, and the tradition mixes rod and string. Show how those three facts explain one another rather than being three separate details.

**Model answer:** They form a single engineering chain. Start from the scale: a figure of four and a half feet and ten kilograms is far outside the string-puppet baseline of eight to nine inch wooden miniatures, and no manipulation system designed for a miniature can carry it. Strings through small holes in the hands, head and back work because a light figure can be suspended and articulated by the fingers alone; a ten-kilogram figure cannot, because the weight would consume the hands entirely and leave nothing free for articulation. The **iron ring worn on the puppeteer's head** solves exactly that problem: it transfers the load-bearing function from the hands to the body, so that the figure is supported by the head while the hands remain free to control movement. And once the hands are free but the figure is heavy, **rods** become the natural control for the limbs, because a rod transmits force directly and does not depend on gravity and slack the way a string does - which is why the tradition is recorded as mixing rod and string rather than belonging cleanly to either category. So scale forces the support method, and the support method plus scale forces the mixed control. Reading the entry this way also explains why the record places it among the string examples while explicitly calling it a mixture: it is a string tradition that has had to borrow from another mechanism in order to grow.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding that the four-category classification is therefore wrong or that Bommalattam should be reclassified as a rod tradition. The record's own wording is that puppetry is **broadly** classified into four categories, and it describes this tradition as a mixture while listing it among string examples. The correct answer in an item or an essay reproduces the mixture; it does not pick a side the record has declined to pick.

## Lesson 18 - Shadow puppets: what light, leather and a screen actually require

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried - the Shadow Puppets sub-section with its three defining features, and the full example table covering Kerala, Maharashtra, Karnataka, Odisha and Andhra Pradesh
CA search: "Not applicable - mechanism and tradition lesson"
CA found: None found; none claimed
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the optical system, and the five traditions inside it

```text
   THE SHADOW-PUPPET SYSTEM - AN OPTICAL, NOT A MECHANICAL, ART

        LIGHT SOURCE          FLAT LEATHER FIGURE        WHITE SCREEN
        (behind)        -->   (cut out; painted     -->  (audience in front)
                              IDENTICALLY ON BOTH
                              SIDES of the leather)
                                      |
                              MOVEMENT CREATES SHADOWS
                              THAT FORM VIVID IMAGERY

   THREE RECORDED FEATURES
   [1] shadow puppets are FLAT FIGURES CUT OUT OF LEATHER
   [2] the figures are PAINTED IDENTICALLY ON BOTH SIDES of the leather
   [3] placed on a WHITE SCREEN with LIGHT BEHIND; their MOVEMENT creates
       SHADOWS that form VIVID IMAGERY

   THE FIVE TRADITIONS, BY WHAT EACH ADDS

   TOLPAVA KUTHU (Kerala)       -> RITUAL SITE + NAMED STAGE + OIL LAMPS
     leather puppets; mainly in the BHADRAKALI TEMPLES of Kerala
     on a special stage called KOOTHUMADAM
     an OVERNIGHT leather doll dance; normally the KAMBA RAMAYANA
     OIL LAMPS lit behind the stage help create the shadows
     believed to have originated in the 9th or 10th CENTURY AD
     the NAIR community is mainly associated with it

   CHAMADYACHA BAHULYA (Maharashtra) -> A SECOND EPIC SOURCE
     a LEATHER puppetry form
     scenes from the RAMAYANA and from PANCHAVATI (Story of Draupadi)
     the THAKAR TRIBAL COMMUNITY is mainly associated with it

   TOGALU GOMBEYATTA (Karnataka) -> SIZE AS A SOCIAL CODE
     the SHADOW THEATRE of Karnataka
     uses puppets of DIFFERENT SIZES TO SHOW SOCIAL STATUS -
       LARGE for KINGS and DEITIES, SMALLER for COMMONERS
     mainly performed by the KILLEKYATA community

   RAVANCHHAYA (Odisha)         -> NO JOINTS, AND NON-HUMAN FIGURES
     the MOST THEATRICAL shadow puppetry of Odisha
     DEER-SKIN puppets with BOLD POSTURES and NO JOINTS - making it COMPLEX
     NON-HUMAN FIGURES like TREES and ANIMALS are also used
     mainly performed by the BHAT community
     known for its LYRICAL and SENSITIVE NARRATION

   THOLU BOMMALATA (Andhra Pradesh) -> SCALE AND COLOUR
     the SHADOW THEATRE of Andhra Pradesh
     accompanied by a CLASSICAL MUSIC background
     revolves around MYTHOLOGICAL and DEVOTIONAL tales of the EPICS and
       PURANAS
     the puppets are LARGER IN SIZE and COLOURED ON BOTH SIDES
```

### 1. The learner's actual problem

Shadow puppetry looks like the simplest of the four mechanisms and is in fact the most constrained, because the mechanism is **optical**. Every design decision in this category is downstream of one physical fact: the audience sees an interruption of light, not an object. A learner who grasps that fact can derive most of the baseline features instead of memorising them, and can then hold the five traditions by what each one adds on top.

### 2. The baseline, and why each feature follows from the optics

**India has a rich tradition in shadow puppetry, which has survived to date.** The features are:

- **shadow puppets are flat figures cut out of leather**;
- **the figures are painted identically on both sides of the leather**;
- **the puppets are placed on a white screen with light behind, and their movement creates shadows that form vivid imagery**.

Now derive them.

**Why flat?** Because a three-dimensional figure interrupting light produces an uninformative blob. Only a flat profile produces a legible outline, which is why the whole expressive burden in this category falls on **silhouette** - the cut of the figure - rather than on modelling.

**Why leather?** Because the material must be rigid enough to hold a cut profile, thin enough to be worked into fine detail, and capable of being made translucent so that colour and perforation register against the light. Leather satisfies all three.

**Why painted identically on both sides?** This is the feature most often reproduced without understanding, and it is the most revealing. A figure seen only in silhouette would not need paint at all. Painting it, and painting it **identically on both sides**, means the tradition intends colour and detail to be visible **through** the leather, and intends the figure to read the same whichever way it is turned. Both-side painting is therefore evidence that these are **translucent colour images**, not merely black shadows - which is exactly why one tradition below is described as using puppets **coloured on both sides** as a distinguishing feature.

**Why a white screen with light behind?** Because the screen is the image plane and the audience is on the far side of it. This is the most radical stage relation in the topic: the performer is **invisible by design**, and the audience never sees the object, only its projection.

### 3. The five traditions

**Tolpava Kuthu (Kerala).** It is **performed using leather puppets** and is **mainly performed in the Bhadrakali temples of Kerala** on a **special stage called *koothumadam***. It is an **overnight leather doll dance** where **normally the *Kamba Ramayana* is performed**. **The oil lamps lit behind the stage help to create shadows.** It is **believed to have originated in the 9th or 10th century AD.** **The Nair community is mainly associated with this form of puppetry.**

Six identifiers and not one of them is shared: the **temple type**, the **named stage**, the **overnight duration**, the **named Ramayana recension**, the **oil lamps as the light source**, the **century with its hedge**, and the **community**. Note two things. The **named stage** is unusual - this is the only puppetry entry in the record with a proper name for its performance structure, and it ties the tradition physically to the temple. And the **oil lamps** convert the optics of the baseline into a specific, datable technology: a light source of many small flames produces a soft, mobile, multiple-edged shadow, which is a different image from a single point source.

⚠️ The century carries the record's own hedge - **believed to have originated** - and the hedge should be reproduced.

**Chamadyacha Bahulya (Maharashtra).** It is a **leather puppetry form where scenes from the *Ramayana* as well as from *Panchavati* (Story of Draupadi) are performed**. The **Thakar tribal community of Maharashtra is mainly associated with this puppetry.**

Two identifiers: the **double repertoire** with its named second source, and the **tribal community carrier**. This is the only entry in the puppetry block with a **tribal community** named as the carrier, and it belongs in any answer that has to show how specifically the record names its performers.

**Togalu Gombeyatta (Karnataka).** ***Togalu Gombeyatta*, the shadow theatre of Karnataka, uses puppets of different sizes to show social status - large for kings and deities, smaller for commoners.** It is **mainly performed by the Killekyata community.**

The size-as-status device is the most analytically interesting single detail in the puppetry block. It is a pure instance of the *Natyadharmi* mode from Lesson 3: a conventional, symbolic code that the audience is trained to read, encoding a social fact in a physical property of the figure. It is also a solution available **only** in this mechanism, because a shadow image has no absolute scale - the audience has nothing to compare the figure against except the other figures - and so relative size can be made to carry meaning without looking like a mistake.

⚠️ **The collision, settled:** the prefix ***Togalu*** marks the **shadow** tradition; **Gombeyatta** without it is the **string** tradition of Lesson 17. Both are Karnataka; only one uses leather and light.

**Ravanchhaya (Odisha).** ***Ravanchhaya*, the most theatrical shadow puppetry of Odisha, uses deer-skin puppets with bold postures and no joints, making it complex.** **Non-human figures like trees and animals are also used.** **Performed mainly by the Bhat community, it is known for its lyrical and sensitive narration.**

Five identifiers, and one of them is a genuine paradox worth explaining. **No joints makes it complex.** That seems backwards until you think about the optics. A jointed figure can change its posture during the performance; an unjointed figure cannot. So every expressive requirement must be built into the **cut of the silhouette itself** - the bold posture is fixed at the moment of making - and the performance must generate variety through movement of the whole figure, its angle to the light, its distance from the screen and its relation to other figures. Difficulty is transferred from the manipulation to the design and the staging. That is why the record calls the tradition both **the most theatrical** and **complex**.

The **non-human figures** matter too: trees and animals as separate cut-outs mean the tradition builds **scenery** out of the same material as its characters, which no other puppetry entry in the record does.

**Tholu Bommalata (Andhra Pradesh).** It is the **shadow theatre of Andhra Pradesh**. The **show is accompanied by a classical music background** and **revolves around mythological and devotional tales of the Epics and Puranas**. **The puppets are larger in size and coloured on both sides.**

Three identifiers: the **classical music accompaniment**, which is unusual in this block and is the point at which puppetry touches the music owner; the **epic and Puranic repertoire**; and the **large, both-side-coloured figures**. The last confirms the reading of the baseline in section 2: colour on both sides is named here as a **distinguishing feature**, which is only meaningful if the colour is seen.

### 4. The comparison that does the most work

| | Tolpava Kuthu | Chamadyacha Bahulya | Togalu Gombeyatta | Ravanchhaya | Tholu Bommalata |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **State** | Kerala | Maharashtra | Karnataka | Odisha | Andhra Pradesh |
| **Material note** | leather | leather | - | **deer-skin** | - |
| **Community** | **Nair** | **Thakar tribal** | **Killekyata** | **Bhat** | - |
| **Signature device** | **koothumadam** stage; **oil lamps**; overnight | double repertoire incl. **Panchavati** | **size codes social status** | **no joints**; **non-human figures** | **large**, **coloured on both sides** |
| **Repertoire** | ***Kamba Ramayana*** | Ramayana + Draupadi story | - | - | **Epics and Puranas** |
| **Music** | - | - | - | **lyrical, sensitive narration** | **classical music background** |
| **Date** | **believed 9th or 10th century AD** | - | - | - | - |

Read the community row across: **four of five traditions in this mechanism have a named carrier community**, which is the highest density of named carriers anywhere in the puppetry block and among the highest in the session. If an answer needs to show that Indian performance traditions are community-held rather than generically popular, this row is the evidence.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Explain how the optics of shadow puppetry determine what its traditions are able to represent. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model

The audience sees an interruption of light, not an object, and every feature follows.

**Flatness** is forced, because a solid figure casts an unreadable shape; expression therefore lives in the cut silhouette. **Leather** is chosen because it holds a cut profile, takes fine detail and can be worked thin enough for light to pass. **Painting identically on both sides** makes sense only if colour is meant to be seen through the figure, which is why one tradition is distinguished by being larger and coloured on both sides.

Two representational consequences are unique to this mechanism. Because a projection has no absolute scale, **size can encode status** - large for kings and deities, smaller for commoners. And because posture is fixed at the cutting stage, a tradition with **no joints** transfers complexity from manipulation to design and staging.

Verdict: read the physics first, because the aesthetics are downstream of it.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the three baseline features each derived from the optics 5 + two representational consequences unique to this mechanism 4 + a verdict placing physics before aesthetics 1.**

### Examination use

For Prelims, the baseline is testable - **flat figures cut out of leather**, **painted identically on both sides**, **white screen with light behind**, **movement creates shadows forming vivid imagery** - and so is every signature device above. The likeliest traps are the *Gombeyatta* prefix, the community-to-tradition pairs, and the **deer-skin** material, which belongs to exactly one entry.

For Mains, this lesson supplies the clearest available demonstration that **mechanism determines expressive possibility**: flatness forces silhouette, translucency permits colour, absent scale permits size-coding, and absent joints transfers complexity to design. That is a real argument about form, and it is available from five recorded entries.

### Traps introduced here

- Confusing *Togalu Gombeyatta* with *Gombeyatta*.
- Attaching deer-skin to a tradition other than the Odisha shadow form.
- Swapping the four named communities between traditions.
- Dropping the hedge from the Kerala tradition's ninth- or tenth-century origin.
- Treating both-side painting as decorative when the record names both-side colouring as a distinguishing feature elsewhere.
- Reading "no joints" as simplicity when the record says it makes the form complex.

### Revision notes

1. **Shadow-puppet baseline**: **flat figures cut out of leather**; **painted identically on both sides**; **white screen with light behind**; **movement creates shadows that form vivid imagery**.
2. **Tolpava Kuthu (Kerala)** - **Bhadrakali temples**; special stage ***koothumadam***; **overnight leather doll dance**; normally the ***Kamba Ramayana***; **oil lamps behind the stage**; **believed to have originated in the 9th or 10th century AD**; **Nair** community.
3. **Chamadyacha Bahulya (Maharashtra)** - leather; **Ramayana** and ***Panchavati* (Story of Draupadi)**; **Thakar tribal community**.
4. **Togalu Gombeyatta (Karnataka)** - shadow theatre; **different sizes show social status**, **large for kings and deities**, **smaller for commoners**; **Killekyata** community.
5. **Ravanchhaya (Odisha)** - **most theatrical** shadow puppetry; **deer-skin**; **bold postures**, **no joints**, **complex**; **non-human figures** such as **trees and animals**; **Bhat** community; **lyrical and sensitive narration**.
6. **Tholu Bommalata (Andhra Pradesh)** - **classical music background**; **mythological and devotional tales of the Epics and Puranas**; puppets **larger in size** and **coloured on both sides**.
7. Flatness forces **silhouette**; leather permits **fine cutting and translucency**; both-side painting means colour is meant to be **seen through** the figure.
8. The white screen makes the performer **invisible by design** - the most radical stage relation in the topic.
9. **No joints → complexity moves from manipulation to design and staging.**
10. **Four of five** shadow traditions have a **named carrier community**.

### Concept check

**Question:** Togalu Gombeyatta uses large puppets for kings and deities and smaller ones for commoners. Why is that device available in shadow puppetry in a way it is not available in string puppetry, and what does the answer show about the relation between mechanism and meaning?

**Model answer:** It is available because a shadow image has **no absolute scale**. The audience sees a projection on a screen and has no independent reference - no visible stage floor, no performer's hand, no object of known size - against which to judge how big any figure is. Relative size therefore becomes a free variable that the tradition can assign meaning to, and the assignment reads as a **code** rather than as an inconsistency: a large figure for a king or a deity and a smaller one for a commoner is understood as status, not as a manufacturing error. In string puppetry the situation is reversed. The figures are physical objects on a visible stage, manipulated by visible or inferable hands, with a recorded baseline of eight to nine inches; a figure at twice that size would register as a different **object**, and the traditions that do go larger do so for reasons of spectacle and control, not code - a tradition whose figures reach four and a half feet is recorded as the largest in India, not as depicting greater status. What this shows is that **meaning in these arts is constrained by mechanism**. A symbolic device is not simply chosen by a tradition; it is made possible or impossible by the physics of how the image reaches the audience. That is a far stronger claim about Indian puppetry than any statement about its antiquity, and it is fully evidenced by two entries in the same state.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading the size code as a claim that the tradition's performers held a particular social ideology. The record states a representational convention - large for kings and deities, smaller for commoners - and that is a statement about how the form communicates status, not about what its practitioners believed status ought to be. An answer that converts a stage convention into a political position has gone beyond the evidence.

## Lesson 19 - Rod and glove puppets, and the mixed forms that break a clean four-way classification

Progress: 19/23 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the glove-puppet baseline and Pavakoothu; the rod-puppet baseline and Yampuri, Putul Nachh and Kathi Kandhe; and the recorded relations between categories

### Visual: two mechanisms, and the line the record itself draws between them

```text

                                                internal relation.

     began in the 18th CENTURY                 traditional ROD puppet of Bihar
```
