```text
       FACE PAINT                                WOOD, then PAINTED and DRESSED
     narrates the RAMAYANA and                   in BRIGHT COLOURS
       MAHABHARATA
                                             PUTUL NACHH (West Bengal, Odisha,
                                              Assam)
                                               rod puppet DANCE
                                               3 to 4 FT JATRA STYLE figures
                                               with NECK and SHOULDER JOINTS
                                               puppeteers HIDDEN BEHIND A
                                                 CURTAIN, control them with
                                                 WAIST RODS
                                               HARMONIUM, CYMBALS and TABLA

                                             KATHI KANDHE / KATHI KUNDHEI
                                              NACHA (Odisha)
                                               name from KATHI - WOODEN ROD
                                               themes from HINDU MYTHOLOGY
                                               60 CM JATRA STYLE puppets in
                                                 FULL DRESS
                                               2004: MAGUNI CHARAN KUANR
                                                 received the SANGEET NATAK
                                                 AKADEMI AWARD for his
                                                 contribution to Kathi Kundhei
                                                 Nacha and to Indian puppetry
```

### 1. The learner's actual problem

These two categories are the ones a learner is most likely to compress, because one has a single named example and the other has three short ones. That compression is a mistake for two reasons. First, the glove category contains the most precise control description in the entire puppetry block - which finger does what - and it is directly testable. Second, the rod category contains the record's own statement that **one category is derived from another**, which is the fact that settles how the four-way classification should be described.

### 2. Glove puppets: the most precise control description in the record

**Glove puppets (sleeve, hand or palm puppets) are small figures with a head and arms in a long skirt, usually made of cloth or wood.** **Worn like a glove, the puppeteer uses the index finger for the head and the thumb and middle finger for the hands, bringing the puppet to life.**

**Glove puppets are popular all across India, with the performance usually accompanied by rhythmic beats of drum or *dholak*.**

Four things deserve attention.

**The three names.** *Sleeve*, *hand* and *palm* puppets are all recorded as names for this category, and all three describe the same relation: the figure is **worn**, not held.

**The construction.** A **head and arms in a long skirt** is the whole figure - there is no body in the sculptural sense, because the puppeteer's hand is the body. That is the most economical construction in the four categories and it explains why the material can be **cloth or wood**: the rigid parts are only the head and the hands.

**The control.** **Index finger for the head; thumb and middle finger for the hands.** This is the only place in the record where a specific finger is assigned to a specific part of a puppet, and it is therefore the most likely control detail to appear in an objective item. It also explains the expressive character of the category: a head and two hands under independent finger control give **immediate, fast, gestural** movement, at the cost of any movement below the waist.

**The distribution.** Glove puppets are **popular all across India**. This is the only category described as pan-Indian rather than concentrated, and the contrast with the rod category - **mainly popular in the region of eastern India** - is a directly testable pair.

**Pavakoothu (Kerala).** **The traditional glove puppet show of Kerala began in the 18th century.** **Strongly influenced by Kathakali, its puppets have colourful headgear, feathers and face paint, and performances narrate the *Ramayana* and *Mahabharata*.**

Four identifiers: the **century**, the **Kathakali influence**, the **three appearance features**, and the **epic repertoire**. The Kathakali influence is the entry's cross-art fact and belongs beside the Odissi influence on the Odisha string tradition and the Yakshagana design of the Karnataka string tradition. Notice the pattern that has now appeared three times: **a regional performance tradition supplies the visual and stylistic vocabulary of a regional puppet tradition**. Lesson 21 develops this into a general claim.

⚠️ **The collision to disarm:** ***Pavakoothu*** is the Kerala **glove** tradition; ***Tolpava Kuthu*** is the Kerala **shadow** tradition. One state, two mechanisms, two similar names. The discriminators are **leather, temple, screen and overnight** for the shadow form, and **glove, headgear, feathers and face paint** for this one.

### 3. Rod puppets: a category defined by reference to another

**Rod puppets are larger variations of glove puppets and are controlled by the puppeteer with the help of rods from behind a screen.** **It is mainly popular in the region of eastern India.**

That first sentence is the most structurally important statement in the puppetry block, because it means the four categories are **not four independent types**. One of them is defined as a scaled-up version of another with a different control method. Combined with the Tamil Nadu tradition that mixes rod and string, the honest description of Indian puppetry's classification is: **four broad categories, with one derivational relation and at least one recorded hybrid**. Reproduce the record's own word - **broadly** classified - and the description is accurate.

**Yampuri (Bihar).** It is the **traditional rod puppet of Bihar**. **The puppets are generally made of wood and are without any joints.** **They are carved out of a single piece of wood and then painted and dressed in bright colours.**

Three identifiers, and one of them repeats a design principle already met: **no joints**, as in the Odisha shadow tradition of Lesson 18. The consequence is the same and worth restating, because the mechanism differs: an unjointed figure carved from a single piece of wood has a **fixed posture**, so all expressive variation must come from the movement of the whole figure and from the staging. A jointless rod puppet and a jointless shadow puppet solve the same problem in two different media.

**Putul Nachh.** **The rod puppet dance of West Bengal, Odisha and Assam uses 3 to 4 ft *Jatra* style figures with neck and shoulder joints.** **Puppeteers, hidden behind a curtain, control them with waist rods, accompanied by harmonium, cymbals and tabla.**

Five identifiers: the **three states**, the **scale**, the ***Jatra* style** description, the **two named joints**, the **waist rods with the puppeteer hidden behind a curtain**, and the **three instruments**. Two of these deserve comment. The **neck and shoulder joints** are a direct contrast with the Bihar tradition's jointlessness, within the same mechanism and the same region. And the ***Jatra* style** is the cross-reference flagged in Lesson 10: an eastern folk theatre form lends its name as a **style descriptor** for puppet figures. This is the fourth instance of a regional performance tradition shaping a puppet tradition, and the only one where the influence is registered in the figures' **style label** rather than in their design source.

**Kathi Kandhe / Kathi Kundhei Nacha (Odisha).** ***Kathi Kundhei Nacha* derives its name from *kathi* (wooden rod).** **Themes are based on Hindu mythology, with 60 cm *Jatra* style puppets in full dress.**

**In 2004, Maguni Charan Kuanr was the recipient of the Sangeet Natak Akademi Award for his contribution to *Kathi Kundhei Nacha* and to Indian puppetry.**

Four identifiers: the **etymology** from the wooden rod, the **theme**, the **60 cm scale** with the same ***Jatra* style** description, and the **dated award with its named recipient**.

⚠️ **The Odisha triple, settled once.** Odisha now carries three puppet traditions across three mechanisms, and their names are genuinely confusable: ***Kundhei* / *Kandhei Nacha*** is the **string** tradition (light wood, long skirts, more joints, triangular prop, Odissi influence); ***Ravanchhaya*** is the **shadow** tradition (deer-skin, no joints, non-human figures, Bhat community); and ***Kathi Kandhe* / *Kathi Kundhei Nacha*** is the **rod** tradition (named from *kathi*, the wooden rod; 60 cm; Hindu mythology; the 2004 award). The discriminating prefix is ***Kathi***, and it means the rod itself. That single etymology separates the two similarly named Odisha traditions permanently.

### 4. The complete mechanism map, assembled

| Mechanism | Baseline | Named traditions | Regional spread |
|---|---|---|---|
| **String** | 8-9 inch wooden figures; oil colours; wooden-pipe limbs; miniature jewellery; strings through holes in hands, head and back | **Kathputli** (Rajasthan), **Kundhei** (Odisha), **Gombeyatta** (Karnataka), **Bommalattam** (Tamil Nadu, **mixed rod and string**), **Laithibi Jagoi** (Manipur) | West, east, south and north-east |
| **Shadow** | Flat leather cut-outs; painted identically on both sides; white screen with light behind | **Tolpava Kuthu** (Kerala), **Chamadyacha Bahulya** (Maharashtra), **Togalu Gombeyatta** (Karnataka), **Ravanchhaya** (Odisha), **Tholu Bommalata** (Andhra Pradesh) | Peninsular and eastern |
| **Glove** | Head and arms in a long skirt, cloth or wood; worn like a glove; index finger head, thumb and middle finger hands; drum or *dholak* | **Pavakoothu** (Kerala) | **Popular all across India** |
| **Rod** | Larger variations of glove puppets; rods from behind a screen | **Yampuri** (Bihar), **Putul Nachh** (West Bengal, Odisha, Assam), **Kathi Kandhe** (Odisha) | **Mainly eastern India** |

Read the last column. **Glove is the only pan-Indian category; rod is the only regionally concentrated one**; string and shadow are distributed across several regions each. That distribution pattern is itself a useful answer component, and it follows from the mechanics: the cheapest and most portable mechanism spreads furthest, and the one requiring a screen, rods and a concealed operator concentrates.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Compare the glove and rod mechanisms as two solutions to the same design problem, and say what the comparison reveals about the four-fold classification. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The problem is identical: to animate a figure whose expressive content lies in a head and two arms, from a position the audience cannot see.

**The glove solution** keeps the figure small. It is a head and arms in a long skirt, of cloth or wood, worn like a glove, with the index finger at the head and the thumb and middle finger at the hands. The hand *is* the body, which is why no external screen is needed, why the register is fast and gestural, and why the category is recorded as popular all across India, accompanied by drum or *dholak*.

**The rod solution** scales the same anatomy up. Rod puppets are larger variations of glove puppets, controlled by rods from behind a screen, mainly in eastern India. Scale forces every other change: the hand can no longer be the body, so rods replace fingers - waist rods in one tradition; concealment must become external, so a screen or curtain appears; and material becomes wood. The anatomy persists, one tradition carrying neck and shoulder joints only and another none at all.

**What it reveals.** The four categories are not four independent types. One is defined as a scaled variant of another, and a Tamil Nadu tradition mixes rod and string. The record's own word is *broadly* classified.

Verdict: describe the four mechanisms, then name the derivation and the hybrid - that precision is what a classification demand rewards.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the shared design problem stated before either solution 2 + the glove mechanism with its exact finger assignment and pan-Indian spread 4 + the rod mechanism with the four changes scale forces 5 + the derivation and hybrid used to qualify the classification 3 + a verdict on what a classification demand rewards 1.**

### Examination use

For Prelims, the two baselines are testable in full, and the finger assignment is the single most distinctive control detail in the block. Named-tradition items will be built on: **Pavakoothu** (18th century, Kathakali influence, headgear, feathers, face paint, Ramayana and Mahabharata); **Yampuri** (Bihar, wood, no joints, single piece, bright colours); **Putul Nachh** (three states, 3-4 ft, *Jatra* style, neck and shoulder joints, waist rods, puppeteers behind a curtain, harmonium, cymbals, tabla); and **Kathi Kandhe** (*kathi* = wooden rod, Hindu mythology, 60 cm, *Jatra* style, **2004 Sangeet Natak Akademi Award to Maguni Charan Kuanr**).

For Mains, this lesson completes the mechanism map and supplies the structural observation that the four-way classification contains a derivation and a hybrid - which is exactly the kind of precision a *classify* or *discuss* directive rewards over a bare list of four names.

### Traps introduced here

- Confusing *Pavakoothu* with *Tolpava Kuthu*.
- Confusing the three Odisha puppet traditions, or missing that *Kathi* names the rod.
- Giving *Yampuri* joints, or denying them to *Putul Nachh*.
- Reversing the two distribution statements: glove is pan-Indian, rod is mainly eastern.
- Reading "*Jatra* style" as a reference to the Bengal folk theatre rather than as a description of the puppet figures.
- Stating the four categories as four independent types without the recorded derivation and hybrid.
- Inventing additional puppeteer awards; the record supplies exactly two, in 2004 and 2020.

### Revision notes

1. **Glove puppets** = **sleeve, hand or palm puppets**: **head and arms in a long skirt**, **cloth or wood**; **worn like a glove**; **index finger → head**, **thumb and middle finger → hands**; **popular all across India**; **drum or *dholak***.
2. **Pavakoothu (Kerala)** - glove puppet show; **began in the 18th century**; **strongly influenced by Kathakali**; **colourful headgear, feathers, face paint**; **Ramayana and Mahabharata**.
3. **Rod puppets** = **larger variations of glove puppets**, controlled with **rods from behind a screen**; **mainly popular in eastern India**.
4. **Yampuri (Bihar)** - traditional rod puppet; **wood**; **without any joints**; **carved out of a single piece**; **painted and dressed in bright colours**.
5. **Putul Nachh** - rod puppet dance of **West Bengal, Odisha and Assam**; **3 to 4 ft *Jatra* style** figures with **neck and shoulder joints**; puppeteers **hidden behind a curtain**, **waist rods**; **harmonium, cymbals and tabla**.
6. **Kathi Kandhe / Kathi Kundhei Nacha (Odisha)** - from ***kathi***, **wooden rod**; **Hindu mythology**; **60 cm *Jatra* style** puppets **in full dress**.
7. **2004** - **Maguni Charan Kuanr**, **Sangeet Natak Akademi Award**, for **Kathi Kundhei Nacha** and Indian puppetry.
8. ⚠️ Odisha triple: ***Kundhei*** string · ***Ravanchhaya*** shadow · ***Kathi* Kundhei Nacha** rod. The prefix ***Kathi*** is the discriminator.
9. ⚠️ Kerala pair: ***Pavakoothu*** glove · ***Tolpava Kuthu*** shadow.
10. The four-way classification contains **one derivation** (rod from glove) and **one recorded hybrid** (rod-and-string) - say **broadly** classified.

### Concept check

**Question:** The record says rod puppets are "larger variations of glove puppets". Take that seriously as a claim about design and say what changes, and what does not, when a glove puppet is scaled up into a rod puppet.

**Model answer:** What does **not** change is the basic anatomy and the basic problem. Both are figures whose expressive content sits in the **head and the arms**, with no articulated lower body - the glove figure is a head and arms in a long skirt, and the rod figure keeps the same essential shape, as the traditions confirm: one is recorded with neck and shoulder joints only, and another has no joints at all. Both are also operated from **below or behind**, out of the audience's sight. What **does** change is everything that follows from size. The puppeteer's hand can no longer be the body, because a three-to-four-foot figure is too large and too heavy to wear; so the hand is replaced by **rods**, and the record specifies waist rods for one tradition. The concealment changes with it: a worn figure hides the operator's hand inside itself, whereas a rod figure needs an external concealment, and the record duly specifies a **screen** in the general description and a **curtain** in one tradition. Material changes: cloth is adequate for a worn figure, while the rod traditions are recorded in **wood**. And the expressive register changes - the fast, immediate, finger-driven gesture of a glove figure gives way to the slower, broader, more sculptural movement of a large carried figure, which is consistent with one rod tradition being jointless and another carrying only neck and shoulder joints. So the derivation is real and productive: hold the glove baseline, apply scale, and the rod category's construction, control, concealment and material all follow.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding that rod puppetry is therefore a lesser or secondary category. A derivational description in a classification is a statement about design relation, not about artistic standing, and the rod traditions have their own repertoire, their own regional concentration, their own accompaniment and their own dated recognition. The category is derived in construction, not subordinate in practice.

## Lesson 20 - Martial and ritual performance: the continuum between combat training and choreographed display

Progress: 20/23 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: the named martial traditions as performance - weapon, movement, community and occasion - and the boundary at which sport recognition takes over

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: one continuum, and where each tradition sits on it

### 1. The learner's actual problem

The martial material sits at the edge of this topic and a learner needs to know two things at once: **which part of it this session owns**, and **what analytical claim it supports**. The answer to the first is that these traditions are owned here **as performance** - weapon, movement, community and occasion - while their modern recognition as sports, their federations and their inclusion in sporting programmes belong to another owner. The answer to the second is the continuum in the diagram: these practices sit between combat training and choreographed display, and at least two of them are recorded as having **moved** along it.

### 2. The evolution overview, stated exactly

The record supplies a five-stage chronology, and reproducing its brackets is worth more than any general statement about ancient martial traditions.

| Stage | Bracket | Content as recorded |
|---|---|---|
| **Vedic texts** | **1500 BCE - 600 BCE** | The earliest references to training and fighting techniques are found in the Vedas; the **Dhanurveda** and **Yajurveda** mention forms of combat known as ***dhanurvidya*** or ***angavidya***, encompassing archery, the art of warfare, swordsmanship, the uses of weapons and the training of an army |
| **Early epics** | **600 BCE - 400 CE** | The Ramayana and Mahabharata contain detailed accounts of martial skills and warfare; the **Arjuna-Karna battle**, showcasing bows, swords, fists and daggers, is recorded as having inspired Indian martial-arts practitioners |
| **Regional influence** | **200 BCE - 1200 CE** | Regional martial arts developed - **Kalaripayattu** (Kerala), **Silambam** (Tamil Nadu), **Thang-ta** (Manipur), **Gatka** (Punjab) |
| **Effects of foreign invasions** | **1200 CE - 1800 CE** | Mongol, Turk, Persian, Mughal and European contact influenced and shaped Indian martial techniques and strategies |
| **Modernisation** | **Late 19th century - present** | Traditional Indian martial arts changed; masters and organisations worked towards preserving and modernising ancient techniques |

Two observations for an answer. The **third stage is the one that names the four traditions**, and it is the stage that matters for this topic, because it is where regional, community-held practice appears. And the **fifth stage is where the boundary falls**: preservation and modernisation by organisations is where sport recognition, federations and programmes begin, and that is the other owner's material.

### 3. The traditions, read as performance

**Kalaripayattu (Kerala).** **One of the oldest martial arts in India**, **although practised in most parts of Southern India, it originated in the state of Kerala around the 3rd century BC**. ***Kalari***, a **Malayalam word**, refers to a **specific type of school, gymnasium or training hall where martial arts are practised or taught**. The art **includes mock duels - armed and unarmed combat - and physical exercises**. It is **generally not accompanied by music**. **Kalaripayattu's most important key is footwork.**

Its techniques and aspects include: ***Uzhichil***, massage with **Gingli oil**; fighting with ***Otta***, an **'S'-shaped stick**; ***Maipayattu***, body exercises; ***Koltharipayattu***, the use of **wooden weapons**; ***Angathari***, the use of **sharp metal weapons** and the sticks of *Kolthari*; and ***Verumkai***, **bare-handed fight**.

**In 2017, the Government of India awarded the Padma Shri to Shri Meenakshi Amma for her efforts in preserving Kalaripayattu.** **In 2021, the Kerala state government announced the establishment of a Kalaripayattu Academy in Thiruvananthapuram.** It is **mentioned in the *Vadakkan Pattukal*, a collection of Malayalam ballads of the medieval period**, **written about the *Chekavar* - a warrior surname - of the Malabar region of Kerala**.

Three features make this the richest entry. The **named training hall** gives the practice an institutional site, exactly as a monastery or a temple front does for other traditions in this session. The statement that it is **generally not accompanied by music** is unusually informative for a performance-boundary question: a practice without music sits further towards the combat end of the continuum than one with drumming and singing. And the ***Vadakkan Pattukal*** reference gives the tradition a **literary attestation attached to a named warrior group and region**, which is the martial equivalent of a textual anchor.

**Silambam (Tamil Nadu).** A **type of staff fencing**, described as a **modern and scientific martial art form of Tamil Nadu**. **The Pandyas, Cholas and Cheras promoted it during their reign.** **References in the Tamil epic *Silappadikaram* (2nd century AD) mention the trade of silambam staves, swords and armour with Rome, Greece and Egypt.** **The long staff was used for both mock fighting and self-defence.** Its techniques include **swift movements of the foot**, **use of both hands to wield the staff**, **thrust, cut, chop and sweep**, and the development of **force, momentum and precision at different levels of the body - head, shoulder, hip and leg level**. A player must be trained to **diffuse an uncontrollable mob** using strokes such as **snake hits, monkey hits and hawk hits**, and to **deflect stones**.

⚠️ Note the second appearance of ***Silappadikaram*** in this chapter. In Lesson 16 it is cited as a text that **refers to puppetry**; here it is cited for a reference to the **trade in staves, swords and armour**. One text, two distinct uses - and a reminder that a source citation must be read for what it actually supports.

**Thang-ta and Sarit Sarak (Manipur).** **Created by the Meitei people of Manipur, Thang-ta is an armed martial art that finds mention as one of the most lethal combat forms.** ***Sarit Sarak***, on the other hand, is an **unarmed art form that uses hand-to-hand combat**. ***Thang* refers to a sword, while *Ta* refers to a spear** - the two main elements. **The two components together are called *Huyen Langlon*.** **Gurumayum Gourakishor Sharma, a leading exponent of *Huyen Langlon*, was awarded the Padma Shri in 2009.**

Four identifiers: the **community**, the **etymology**, the **armed-and-unarmed pair** with the umbrella name, and the **dated honour with its named recipient**. The etymology is the most frequently tested element in the entire martial set.

**Chholia (Uttarakhand).** A **traditional martial dance and combat art**, **mainly in the Kumaon and Garhwal regions**. It **originated as a ritual performed by warriors before battle to invoke courage and train in combat skills**. **Over time, it became an important part of wedding ceremonies, symbolizing valour, protection and auspiciousness for the couple.** The dance involves **sword and shield techniques, agile footwork and synchronized group formations**, often **accompanied by singing and drumming that narrate local legends and heroic tales**. Dancers wear **traditional warrior attire, including turbans, dhotis and ornamental weapons**. It **preserves martial techniques, community bonding and cultural heritage**, making it a **living tradition passed down through generations**.

This is the topic's clearest recorded instance of **movement along the continuum**. A practice that began as a pre-battle ritual for warriors and became a wedding-ceremony performance has changed its occasion, its audience and its function while retaining its technique. Cite it whenever an answer needs to show that the combat-to-display line is a path, not a partition.

**Pari-khanda (Bihar).** **Created by the Rajputs**, it **involves fighting using sword and shield**. **Still practised in many parts of Bihar, its steps and techniques are widely used in Chhau dance.** The name comes from ***Pari***, meaning **shield**, and ***khanda***, meaning **sword**. It **involves the practice of *chaalis* or steps**, associated with movements like ***haathi chaali*** (elephant movement) and ***baagh chaali*** (tiger movement).

This is the topic's clearest recorded instance of **martial technique absorbed into a performance form**: the steps of a Bihar sword-and-shield practice are widely used in a masked dance tradition. Together with the previous entry it gives two distinct mechanisms by which combat becomes performance - **the same practice changing occasion**, and **technique migrating into a different form**.

**Gatka (Punjab).** A **weapon-based martial art form performed by the Sikhs of Punjab**. **The name refers to the one whose freedom belongs to grace.** It **features the skilful use of weapons, including stick, *Kirpan*, *Talwar* and *Kataar*.** **Attack and defence are determined by the various positions of hands and feet and the nature of the weapon used.** It is **displayed on a number of celebrations in the state including fairs.** **The Defendu system of martial art, devised by Captain William E. Fairbairn and Captain Eric Anthony Sykes prior to the Second World War, borrowed techniques from Gatka.**

The word **displayed** in the record is the reason this entry belongs in this lesson: the practice has a recorded public, festive presentation context alongside its combat content.

**Mardani Khel (Maharashtra).** A **traditional Maharashtrian armed martial art**, **practised widely in the district of Kolhapur**. It **focuses primarily on skills of weaponry, especially swords, swift movements and the use of low stances suited to its originating place, the hill ranges**. It is **known for the use of the unique Indian *Patta* (sword) and *Vita* (corded lance)**. **Famous practitioners included Shivaji and Tanaji Malusare.** **The martial culture of the Marathi people was mentioned by the Chinese monk Xuanzang in the 7th century.**

Note the **terrain-to-technique link** - low stances suited to hill ranges - which is the only entry in the set that explains a technique by geography, and is therefore worth citing in any answer about environment and cultural form.

**Paika Akhada (Odisha).** **A combination of dance and combat.** **Earlier used by warriors, now practised as a performing art.** The term ***Paika*** refers to a **warrior or soldier**, and ***Akhada*** to a **training ground or arena**. **Earlier, it was practised by the *Paikas*, who belonged to the warrior communities of Odisha and were known for their bravery and skills.** They **played an important role in defending the region and served as soldiers and bodyguards to the kings and local chiefs.**

This is the single most explicit statement of the continuum in the record: **earlier used by warriors, now practised as a performing art**. If an answer needs one sentence to establish that Indian martial traditions move between combat and display, this is that sentence.

### 4. The remaining named set, held as a ledger

The record names a further set of traditions. They are held here as a ledger so that nothing disappears, with the performance-relevant content retained.

| Tradition | Region | Performance-relevant content |
|---|---|---|
| **Mallakhamb** | Maharashtra and Madhya Pradesh | Uses **pole and rope**; **MP declared it the state sport in 2013**; references in the Ramayana, **Chandraketugarh pottery of the 2nd-1st century BC** and accounts of **Buddhist Chinese pilgrims**; the **earliest direct literary mention is in the 12th-century *Manasollasa* by the Chalukya king Someshvara-III**; four categories - ***Jarasandhi*** (breaking limbs and joints), ***Bhimaseni*** (sheer strength), ***Hanumanti*** (technical superiority), ***Jambuvanti*** (locks and holds); long called a **'poor man's sport'** |
| **Cheibi Gad-Ga** | Manipur | Sword and shield, now a **stick encased in soft leather** and a **leather shield**; contest in a **circle of 7 metres** with two lines **2 metres apart**; the ***Cheibi*** stick is **2 to 2.5 ft**, the shield about **1 metre**; victory by **points** for skill and force |
| **Thoda** | Himachal Pradesh | A **mixture of martial arts, sports and culture**, during **Baisakhi in April**; prayers to **Goddesses Mashoo and Durga**; relies on **archery**; dated by tradition to the Mahabharata in the **Kullu and Manali** valleys; two groups of roughly **500** people each, **most of them dancers who come to boost morale**; teams called ***Pashis*** and ***Saathis***, believed to descend from the **Pandavas and Kauravas**; archers **aim below the knee** |
| **Teri Chha** | Sikkim | Armed combat, unarmed technique and physical conditioning; historically practised by **local warriors to protect villages and maintain law and order**; includes **rituals, exercises and disciplined training** |
| **Lathi** | Majorly **Punjab and West Bengal** | ***Lathi*** = a **stick**, usually cane, **6 to 8 ft**, sometimes **metal-tipped**; still a popular sport form in villages |
| **Inbuan Wrestling** | Mizoram | Believed to have its genesis in **1750 AD**; strict rules **prohibiting stepping out of the circle, kicking and knee bending**; won by **lifting the opponent off their feet**; involves **catching the belt** worn at the waist |
| **Kuttu Varisai** | Tamil Nadu | **First mentioned in Sangam literature**; **'empty hand combat'**; an **unarmed Dravidian** art; **grappling, striking and locking**; **animal-based sets** - snake, eagle, tiger, elephant, monkey; considered the **unarmed component of Silambam** |
| **Musti Yuddha** | Varanasi | **Unarmed**, resembling boxing; **kicks, punches, knee and elbow strikes**; popular in the **1960s**; develops the **physical, mental and spiritual**; four categories named after deities - ***Jambuvanti, Hanumanti, Bhimaseni, Jarasandhi*** |
| **Sqay** | Kashmir | **Sword-fighting with graceful movements**; ***Sqay*** means **'knowledge of war'**; **blue official uniform**, head guard and chest guard |
| **Kathi Samu** | Andhra Pradesh | **Practised by the royal armies**; ***Kathi*** = **sword**; performed at a place called ***garidi***; begins with ***vairi***; ***gareja*** involves **holding four swords, two in each hand**; patronised by the kingdoms of **Vizianagaram and Karvetinagaram** |
| **Bandesh** | India | Ancient **unarmed** art using **lock holds against an armed opponent without killing them** |
| **Malla Yuddha** | South India | Traditional **combat wrestling**, related to other Southeast Asian styles including **Naban** |
| **Insuknawr** | Mizoram | Players use **round wooden rods** within a circle |
| **Mukna** | Manipur | Generally played on the **last day of the festival of *Lai Haraoba*** |
| **Kirip, Saldu** | Nicobar | Also known as **Nicobarese wrestling** |
| **Varma Adi** | Tamil Nadu | Attacks aimed at the **body's vital spots** |
| **Adimurai** | Tamil Nadu and Kerala | Believed to have been developed by Tamil sages called ***Siddhars***; practised widely by the **Nadar** community and by some **Ezhavas and Nairs of South Kerala** |

⚠️ **The boundary, stated once.** Several of these entries carry **sport recognition** facts - a state sport declaration, a federation, a ministry recognition, inclusion in a national youth games programme. Those belong to the **sport and youth-affairs owner**, not to this topic, and they are named here only where they are attached directly to a tradition's own description. This lesson does not build a sport-policy argument, and it does not take up the circus history and indigenous-sports material that sits beside this section but outside what this topic owns.

### 5. The two analytical claims this lesson licenses

**Claim one: the continuum is real and movement along it is recorded.** Two entries state it directly - a practice **earlier used by warriors, now practised as a performing art**, and a practice that **originated as a pre-battle ritual and became part of wedding ceremonies** - and a third states technique migration, with the **steps and techniques of a Bihar sword-and-shield art widely used in a masked dance form**. That is three distinct pieces of evidence for one claim.

**Claim two: these are not one "ancient Indian martial art".** They differ by **weapon** (staff, sword and spear, sword and shield, pole and rope, cane stick, bare hand), by **community** (a named people of Manipur, the Sikhs of Punjab, Rajput creators in Bihar, warrior communities of Odisha, a named Tamil Nadu community and Kerala groups), by **occasion** (a spring festival, state celebrations and fairs, weddings, the last day of a named Manipuri festival), by **accompaniment** (one generally without music, another with singing and drumming), and by **evidence type** (a Sangam mention, a medieval ballad collection, a twelfth-century text, a seventh-century traveller's notice). ⚠️ And antiquity claims carried in oral lineage should be distinguished from securely dated textual evidence: the record itself supplies the hedges - *believed to have its genesis*, *dated back to the Mahabharata*, *believed to have been developed by* - and they should be reproduced.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"Indian martial traditions are best described by weapon, movement, community and occasion rather than as one ancient art." Critically examine. (20 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The prescription is correct, and the record defeats the homogenising alternative on four axes.

**Weapon.** A staff in Tamil Nadu; sword and spear in Manipur; sword and shield in Bihar and Uttarakhand; pole and rope in Maharashtra and Madhya Pradesh; a cane stick of six to eight feet in Punjab and Bengal; and bare hands in more than one tradition.

**Movement.** Footwork named as the most important key in Kerala; low stances suited to hill ranges in Kolhapur; leaping attack and defence in Manipur; synchronized group formations in Uttarakhand; archery aimed below the knee in Himachal Pradesh.

**Community.** The Meitei people; the Sikhs of Punjab; Rajput creators in Bihar; the *Paikas* of Odisha; the Nadar community with some Ezhavas and Nairs.

**Occasion.** Baisakhi in April; fairs and state celebrations; wedding ceremonies; the last day of a named Manipuri festival; and one tradition recorded as generally not accompanied by music at all.

**The critical part.** Two entries record movement along the combat-to-display continuum - one earlier used by warriors and now practised as a performing art, another that began as a pre-battle ritual and became part of weddings - and a third records technique migrating into a masked dance. The four axes therefore describe positions that can change, not fixed essences. ⚠️ Antiquity claims are hedged in the sources themselves, and modern sport recognition belongs to another owner.

Verdict: describe by axis, then say whether the tradition has moved.

**Unique scoring note:** **20 marks = four axes, each with at least four named instances 10 + the continuum evidenced by two movements and one technique migration 5 + the axes reframed as changeable positions 3 + the hedged-antiquity and sport-boundary cautions 1 + a verdict adding movement to description 1.**

### Examination use

For Prelims, the highest-value items are the etymologies and the named particulars: ***Kalari*** as a training hall, ***Otta*** as an S-shaped stick, ***Uzhichil*** as massage with Gingli oil, ***Verumkai*** as bare-handed fight; ***Thang*** sword and ***Ta*** spear with ***Sarit Sarak*** and ***Huyen Langlon***; ***Pari*** shield and ***khanda*** sword, with the Chhau link and the *chaalis*; ***Patta*** and ***Vita***; ***Paika*** warrior and ***Akhada*** arena; ***Sqay*** as knowledge of war; ***Kathi*** as sword with *garidi*, *vairi* and *gareja*; the ***Pashis*** and ***Saathis*** of the Himachali archery contest; and the dated honours - **Padma Shri 2017** for the named Kalaripayattu exponent, **Padma Shri 2009** for the named *Huyen Langlon* exponent, and the **2021** state announcement of an academy at Thiruvananthapuram.

For Mains, the lesson supplies a disciplined treatment of martial traditions as performance and a strong general caution against homogenising them.

### Traps introduced here

- Calling these traditions collectively "the ancient Indian martial art".
- Reversing *Thang* and *Ta*, or *Pari* and *khanda*.
- Attaching the Chhau link to a tradition other than the Bihar sword-and-shield art.
- Reproducing an oral-lineage antiquity claim without its recorded hedge.
- Importing sport-policy material - federations, state-sport declarations, national programmes - into a performance-traditions answer as though this topic owned it.
- Treating the combat-and-display line as a fixed classification when two entries record movement along it.

### Revision notes

1. Five-stage evolution: **Vedic texts 1500-600 BCE** (*dhanurvidya*, *angavidya* in **Dhanurveda** and **Yajurveda**); **early epics 600 BCE-400 CE**; **regional influence 200 BCE-1200 CE** (**Kalaripayattu, Silambam, Thang-ta, Gatka**); **foreign invasions 1200-1800 CE**; **modernisation late 19th century-present**.
2. **Kalaripayattu** - Kerala, **around the 3rd century BC**; ***Kalari*** = school/gymnasium/training hall; **mock duels**; **generally not accompanied by music**; **footwork** is the key; ***Uzhichil*** (Gingli-oil massage), ***Otta*** (S-shaped stick), ***Maipayattu***, ***Koltharipayattu***, ***Angathari***, ***Verumkai***; ***Vadakkan Pattukal*** ballads about the ***Chekavar*** of Malabar; **Padma Shri 2017** to **Meenakshi Amma**; **2021** academy announced at **Thiruvananthapuram**.
3. **Silambam** - Tamil Nadu **staff fencing**; promoted by the **Pandyas, Cholas and Cheras**; ***Silappadikaram* (2nd century AD)** on the trade of staves, swords and armour with **Rome, Greece and Egypt**; **snake, monkey and hawk hits**.
4. **Thang-ta** - **Meitei** people of Manipur; ***Thang*** sword, ***Ta*** spear; with unarmed ***Sarit Sarak*** = ***Huyen Langlon***; **Padma Shri 2009** to **Gurumayum Gourakishor Sharma**.
5. **Chholia** - Uttarakhand, **Kumaon and Garhwal**; originated as a **pre-battle ritual**, became part of **wedding ceremonies**; sword and shield, **synchronized group formations**, **singing and drumming** narrating local legends.
6. **Pari-khanda** - Bihar, **created by the Rajputs**; ***Pari*** shield, ***khanda*** sword; its **steps and techniques are widely used in Chhau dance**; ***chaalis*** including ***haathi chaali*** and ***baagh chaali***.
7. **Gatka** - Sikhs of Punjab; **stick, *Kirpan*, *Talwar*, *Kataar***; **displayed at celebrations including fairs**; the **Defendu** system borrowed from it.
8. **Mardani Khel** - Maharashtra, **Kolhapur**; ***Patta*** and ***Vita***; **Shivaji and Tanaji Malusare**; Marathi martial culture noted by **Xuanzang in the 7th century**.
9. **Paika Akhada** - Odisha; **a combination of dance and combat**; **earlier used by warriors, now practised as a performing art**; ***Paika*** warrior, ***Akhada*** arena.
10. Ledger: **Mallakhamb** (pole and rope; *Manasollasa*, 12th century; four categories), **Cheibi Gad-Ga**, **Thoda** (Baisakhi; *Pashis* and *Saathis*), **Teri Chha**, **Lathi**, **Inbuan** (1750 AD), **Kuttu Varisai** (Sangam; unarmed component of Silambam), **Musti Yuddha**, **Sqay**, **Kathi Samu**, **Bandesh**, **Malla Yuddha**, **Insuknawr**, **Mukna** (*Lai Haraoba*), **Kirip/Saldu**, **Varma Adi**, **Adimurai**.

### Concept check

**Question:** Build the strongest available argument that Indian martial traditions belong inside a discussion of performance traditions, using only recorded evidence, and then state precisely where that argument stops.

**Model answer:** Three pieces of evidence carry the argument. First, one tradition is recorded as **a combination of dance and combat**, **earlier used by warriors** and **now practised as a performing art** - a direct statement that the same practice has changed category. Second, another is recorded as having **originated as a ritual performed by warriors before battle** and as having **become an important part of wedding ceremonies**, performed with synchronized group formations and accompanied by singing and drumming that narrate local legends and heroic tales; that is an occasion-change, an audience-change and a function-change while the technique persists. Third, a Bihar sword-and-shield art is recorded as one whose **steps and techniques are widely used in Chhau dance**, which shows technique migrating into an established performance form rather than the practice itself moving. Supporting these are the display contexts the record names: a Punjab practice **displayed on a number of celebrations including fairs**, a Himachali contest held during a named spring festival in which **most participants are dancers who come to boost morale**, and a Manipuri practice generally played on the last day of a named festival. Taken together, these establish that several of these traditions are performed before audiences, on occasions, with accompaniment, by identifiable communities - which is precisely what this topic means by a performance tradition. The argument **stops** at three points. It does not claim that all martial traditions are performances; one is explicitly recorded as **generally not accompanied by music**, and several are described purely in terms of technique, weapon and contest rules. It does not claim any of them is primarily an art rather than a combat practice. And it does not extend to their modern careers as recognised sports - state sport declarations, federations and national programmes - which belong to another owner and cannot be used to argue a point about performance.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the continuum as a historical direction in which combat always becomes display over time. The record does not support a one-way story. One entry moved from pre-battle ritual to wedding performance; another is described as **modern and scientific** while retaining mock fighting and self-defence; another was borrowed by a twentieth-century combat system devised for wartime use. Practices move along the line in both directions and sometimes occupy several points at once.

### Visual: three threads, and the boundary they run across

```text
   KURUVANJI            <- DANCE BALLET form with BHARATANATYAM principal
```

### 1. The learner's actual problem

By this point the session has accumulated a large number of facts that cross between forms and between topics. Left scattered, they are liabilities: they invite over-generalisation in a Mains answer and confusion in an objective item. Gathered into threads, they become the topic's strongest synthesis material. This lesson gathers them, and then draws the boundary that keeps them from spilling into other owners' territory.

### 2. The mask thread, and how to state it safely

The mask is the best single illustration of a discipline established in Lesson 5: a statement about one system is not a statement about a tradition.

The baseline is the classical one - **masks were not used** on the Sanskrit stage as recorded. Against that baseline the record supplies four positive mask facts and two adjacent ones:

- **Ankia Naat** (Assam): one of the **unique features** of this form of theatre is the **use of masks to depict special expressions**;
- **Ramman** (Uttarakhand): people of the **Bhandari caste** wear the **sacred mask symbolising Narasimha**, half-man and half-lion;
- **Laithibi Jagoi** (Manipur): **demon characters are presented by costume masks**;
- **Chhau**: **mask making** attached to this form is recognised as a field of activity in its own right;
- adjacent: **Theyyam** actors wear **elaborate headgear and colourful costumes**, and **Pavakoothu** puppets have **colourful headgear, feathers and face paint**.

Notice what each mask is **for**, because the four uses are different and that difference is the analytical content. The first marks **expression** - the mask carries a special emotional state. The second is **sacred and iconographic** - a specific deity form worn by a specific caste in a ritual theatre. The third is **categorial** - masks separate demon characters from other characters in a puppet tradition. The fourth is a **craft** - mask making as a transmissible skill distinct from performing.

⚠️ The two adjacent facts must not be silently converted into mask facts. **Elaborate headgear** and **face paint** are recorded as such, and the record distinguishes them from masks by using different words. Where a form's record says headgear, write headgear.

The safe general sentence, then, is: **the classical Sanskrit staging system as recorded did not use masks, while mask use elsewhere in Indian performance is widespread, form-specific and functionally varied - expressive, iconographic, categorial and craft-based.** That sentence is defensible, precise and immediately usable.

### 3. The narrator thread, and what it explains

The second thread is the most consistent structural feature in the whole session. Ten separate entries put a **figure or group outside the action** whose job is to carry the performance to the audience.

Read the thread as three distinct solutions to one problem.

**The single officiant.** The Sanskrit *Sutradhar* who opens with ritual and announces time and place is the first instance: one figure who frames the play from outside it.

**The narrator with a chorus or accompanying group.** This is the dominant eastern and southern solution - a lead narrator plus a group that supplies rhythm, chorus or argument. The record names five: an Assamese narrator with a named musician group playing two instruments; an Assamese narrator with a chorus; an Odia chief singer with a co-narrator; a Karnataka narrator aided by a group; and an Andhra main artist with two co-artists supplying rhythm and chorus.

**The narrator as the whole performance.** The extreme case, and the most striking entry in the session: a Karnataka and Kerala form performed **seated, without any costume, dance or acting**, in which the narrator and his group are not a frame around the drama but the drama itself.

Why does this thread matter? Because it explains the **stage relation** of Indian performance more generally. A stage without scenery, performed in a courtyard, a street, an open field or in front of a temple, cannot build its setting physically. The narrator is the device that supplies setting, links episodes, carries scripture and comments on the action - and once you see that, the Sanskrit announcement of time and place, the folk narrator with his chorus and the puppeteer called the holder of strings are three versions of the same solution.

### 4. The cross-form influence thread, and the general claim

Nine recorded influences cross between forms, and they fall into three patterns.

**Theatre supplies puppetry with its visual and stylistic vocabulary.** A Karnataka string tradition's puppets are **styled and designed based on the characters of the Yakshagana theatres**; a Kerala glove tradition is **strongly influenced by Kathakali**; two eastern rod traditions use figures described as ***Jatra* style**. Three different mechanisms, four traditions, one direction of influence.

**Dance supplies puppetry with its movement.** An Odisha string tradition shows a **marked influence of Odissi dance** - and, as Lesson 17 showed, its construction with **more joints** is what makes that influence physically possible.

**Forms within a region shape one another.** A coastal Karnataka ritual theatre is **influenced by the Yakshagana form**; a Karnataka open-air tradition **is a form of Yakshagana**; a seated narration tradition is its **predecessor**; a Tamil Nadu form is performed in a **dance ballet form with Bharatanatyam as the principal dance form**; and a Bihar martial art's **steps and techniques are widely used in Chhau dance**.

The general claim these license is precise and should be stated precisely: **in Indian performance, a regionally dominant form tends to supply the design, movement and stylistic vocabulary of neighbouring forms, including those using an entirely different mechanism.** That is evidenced nine times over and is far stronger than a general statement about the integration of the Indian arts.

The record's own summary claim supports it: puppetry **is a multifaceted art form that incorporates dance, music, theatre, sculpture and painting**. Lesson 17 gave that claim its concrete content - figures **chiselled out of wood**, **painted with oil colours**, **detailed with facial features**, fitted with **miniature jewellery** - which is sculpture and painting in the literal sense, not the metaphorical one.

### 5. The boundary, drawn exactly

Now the discipline. Cross-art facts are the most tempting material in this topic for an answer that wants to sound synthetic, and they are the easiest place to cross into another owner's territory. Four rules.

**Dance grammar belongs to the dance owner.** This session names a dance form only where the record makes it an influence on, a component of, or a classification question for a theatre or puppet tradition. It does not teach *nritta*, *nritya*, *natya*, *abhinaya*, the nine *rasas* or the recognised classical forms, and it does not adjudicate whether a named dance-drama is a dance or a theatre. Where a form sits on that frontier - and several southern entries do - this session says so and routes the classification question rather than settling it.

**Music theory belongs to the music owner.** This session names instruments and song genres only as **identifiers**: the *khol* and cymbals of an Assamese musician group, the *Nagada* of a north Indian form, the *kathia* of an Odia form, the *dhol* and *mohuri* of its variant, the *Burra* and the *Oggu* of two Andhra forms, the *Jhanjhar* and *dholak* of a Gujarat form, the *Tal* and *Maddale* that name a Karnataka form, the *Mizhavu* and *Edakka* of Kutiyattam, the *ektara*, harmonium, *sarangi*, *dholak* and *khartal* of a northern form, the harmonium, cymbals and tabla of a rod-puppet tradition, and the *Lavani* songs that accompany a Maharashtra form. It does not teach raga, tala or the classical systems, and where a chapter word collides with a music term - as one Rajasthani folk theatre's name does - it states the collision and routes the music sense out.

**Sculpture and painting belong to their owners.** Here a carved and painted puppet, a leather cut-out coloured on both sides and a set of painted narrative pictures are **performance objects with manipulation methods**. Their material history, iconographic analysis and pictorial technique are not claimed.

**Status and institutions belong to the heritage owner.** This session records the inscriptions that attach to its own forms and the establishment facts the record supplies for the academy and the drama school. It does not maintain totals, award lists or policy positions, and the single current record it claims is stated once, in the next lesson.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**State and evidence the strongest defensible claim about cross-art influence among Indian performance traditions, and explain what the weaker version costs. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The defensible claim is that a **regionally dominant performance form supplies the design, movement and stylistic vocabulary of neighbouring forms, including forms built on a different mechanism**.

**The evidence runs in several directions.** Theatre into puppetry: a Karnataka string tradition's puppets are styled and designed on the characters of a regional theatre; a Kerala glove tradition is strongly influenced by a Kerala dance-drama; rod-puppet figures in three eastern states are described by the style name of an eastern folk theatre. Dance into puppetry: an Odisha string tradition shows a marked influence of Odissi, and its extra joints are what make that influence physically possible. Within a region: a coastal ritual theatre is influenced by a regional theatre; an open-air form is recorded as a form of it; a seated narration tradition is its predecessor; a Tamil form is performed in a dance ballet with a named principal dance form; and a Bihar martial art's steps are widely used in a masked dance.

**The cost of the weaker version.** "The Indian arts are integrated" is unfalsifiable, so it demonstrates no reading; it is not actionable in an answer; and it conceals the interesting pattern, which is directional, mechanism-crossing and in one case registered in the receiving form's construction.

⚠️ The limit: influence is not lineage, and naming it does not settle whether a dance-drama belongs to dance or to theatre.

Verdict: cite nine influences, not one adjective.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the specific claim stated before any evidence 3 + influences in at least three directions with named receiving forms 7 + the construction-enabled case identified 2 + three costs of the generic version 2 + the lineage and classification limits 1.**

### Examination use

For Prelims, the three threads are themselves the exam material: the **four mask uses and the two adjacent headgear facts**; the **ten narrator conventions with their exact names**; and the **nine recorded cross-form influences**. Almost any matching item in this topic that pairs a form with a feature is drawing on one of these three lists.

For Mains, this lesson supplies the synthesis paragraph that a twenty-mark answer needs, and it supplies it in a defensible form: not "the Indian arts are integrated" but "a regionally dominant performance form supplies the design, movement and stylistic vocabulary of neighbouring forms across mechanisms, and the evidence is nine recorded influences."

### Traps introduced here

- Writing that Indian theatre did not use masks, or that it is characterised by masks. Both over-generalise.
- Converting recorded headgear and face paint into masks.
- Claiming a general integration of the Indian arts without the specific recorded influences that evidence it.
- Teaching dance grammar, raga or tala under this topic's name.
- Settling a dance-versus-theatre classification that the record leaves open.
- Reading a shared word - a Rajasthani folk theatre's name, a Konkan drama's name, a puppet style label - as a reference to another topic's material.

### Revision notes

1. Baseline: **masks were not used** in the classical Sanskrit system as recorded.
2. Four recorded mask uses: **expressive** (Ankia Naat, special expressions); **sacred and iconographic** (Ramman, Bhandari caste, Narasimha); **categorial** (Laithibi Jagoi, demon characters by costume masks); **craft** (Chhau mask making as its own field).
3. Adjacent but distinct: **elaborate headgear** (Theyyam) and **headgear, feathers and face paint** (Pavakoothu).
4. Narrator conventions: ***Sutradhar*** (Sanskrit stage; Ankia Naat with the ***Gayan-Bayan Mandali***; Bhaona singing holy verses; Bhavai as ***Nayaka***; puppetry as **holder of strings**); ***Ojha*** and ***Palis***; ***Gayaka*** and ***Palia***; three narrators in **Chaiti Ghoda**; ***Bhagavata*** and ***Arthadharis***; a main artist with **two co-artists**.
5. Extreme case: **Tal-Maddale** performed **seated, without costume, dance or acting** - narration as the whole performance.
6. Theatre → puppetry: **Yakshagana-styled Gombeyatta**, **Kathakali-influenced Pavakoothu**, ***Jatra*-style** rod figures.
7. Dance → puppetry: **Odissi influence on Kundhei**, made possible by its **extra joints**.
8. Within-region shaping: **Bhuta Kola influenced by Yakshagana**; **Bayalata is a form of Yakshagana**; **Tal-Maddale is its predecessor**; **Kuruvanji in a dance ballet form with Bharatanatyam**; **Pari-khanda steps used in Chhau**.
9. Puppetry **incorporates dance, music, theatre, sculpture and painting** - literally, in the construction of the figures.
10. Boundary: dance grammar, music theory, sculptural and pictorial analysis, and list or award administration are each owned elsewhere.

### Concept check

**Question:** State the strongest defensible general claim about cross-art influence in this topic, and explain why the weaker, more familiar version of that claim would cost marks.

**Model answer:** The strongest defensible claim is that **a regionally dominant performance form supplies the design, movement and stylistic vocabulary of neighbouring forms, including forms that use an entirely different mechanism**. It is defensible because it is evidenced nine times in one chapter and in several directions at once: a Karnataka theatre tradition supplies the character designs of a Karnataka string-puppet tradition, is named as the influence on a coastal ritual theatre, has an open-air form recorded as a form of itself, and has a seated narration tradition recorded as its predecessor; a Kerala dance-drama strongly influences a Kerala glove-puppet tradition; an Odisha dance form leaves a marked influence on an Odisha string-puppet tradition whose extra joints make that influence physically possible; an eastern folk theatre's name becomes a style label for rod-puppet figures in three states; a Tamil Nadu form is performed in a dance ballet with a named principal dance form; and a Bihar martial art's steps and techniques are widely used in a masked dance. The **weaker version** - "the Indian arts are integrated" or "Indian performance is holistic" - costs marks for three reasons. It is unfalsifiable, so it demonstrates nothing about whether the candidate has read anything. It is not actionable in an answer, because nothing follows from it. And it obscures the genuinely interesting pattern, which is directional and mechanism-crossing: the influence flows from the dominant local performance form outward, it reaches traditions built on completely different principles, and in at least one case it is registered in the physical construction of the receiving form rather than merely in its style. The specific claim also protects the answer from an examiner's obvious challenge, because every clause of it can be cited.

**Misconception to avoid:** Using the cross-influence claim to erase the boundaries between topics. The influences are recorded facts about how forms shaped one another; they are not a licence to teach dance grammar or musical theory under this topic's name, and they do not settle whether a dance-drama should be classified as dance or as theatre. Name the influence, cite it, route the classification question, and stop.

## Lesson 22 - Two kinds of decline, and what recognition can and cannot do

Progress: 22/23 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: the completed historical contraction against the current documented pressure, the recognition instruments available, their limits, and the one current status record this session claims

### Visual: two declines, side by side, with different responses

```text

   +---------------------------+  +---------------------------------------+

   +---------------------------+  +---------------------------------------+
```
