```text
   |         difficult Sanskrit|  |                                       |
   |         Pali/Prakrit common|  |                                      |
   |         patronage shifted |  |                                       |
   |         to dance and music|  |                                       |
   +---------------------------+  +---------------------------------------+
   | EVIDENCE textual and      |  | EVIDENCE living practice - named      |
   |         historical        |  |         practitioners, dated awards   |
   +---------------------------+  +---------------------------------------+
   | SURVIVES a living lineage |  | SURVIVES the skills and the named     |
   |         - KUTIYATTAM,     |  |         traditions; fourteen named    |
   |         since the 10th c  |  |         puppet traditions are recorded|
   |         AD, Sanskrit-     |  |         as existing forms             |
   |         adhering          |  |                                       |
   +---------------------------+  +---------------------------------------+
   | RESPONSE documentation,   |  | RESPONSE the two things named as      |
   |         transmission,     |  |         lacking: FUNDING and AUDIENCE |
   |         training,         |  |         - i.e. livelihood support and |
   |         performance space |  |         audience building             |
   +---------------------------+  +---------------------------------------+

   >> ONE DIAGNOSIS DOES NOT FIT BOTH. Writing "traditional arts are dying"
      collapses two different problems into one unusable sentence.
```

### 1. The learner's actual problem

Every candidate can write that Indian traditional arts face decline. Almost none can say **what kind of decline**, **for which form**, **with what evidence** and **requiring what response**. This lesson supplies exactly that, using only the record's own language, and it is the highest-value analytical block in the second half of this topic.

### 2. The distinction, stated exactly

**The classical case.** The record lists three reasons for the decline of Sanskrit theatre - writers moved from plays to poems and songs; strict rules and difficult Sanskrit reduced popularity while Pali and Prakrit became common; and under Muslim rulers dance and music were patronised more than theatre. These are given in the **past tense**, as a **historical episode**, and the record immediately places beside them the surviving lineage that **has survived since the 10th century AD**.

**The present case.** **Puppetry is currently on decline because of lack of funding and captive audience.** This is the **present tense**, with **two named, specific causes**, and it is followed immediately by a **named person, a named organisation and a dated award** for reviving the art.

⚠️ The distinction is not between a dead art and a living one. Both have living continuations. It is between a **completed process whose causes are historical** and a **current condition whose causes are operating now**. The practical consequence follows directly: you cannot fund a writer back into the fourteenth century, but you can fund a puppeteer this year.

### 3. Why the distinction changes the answer

Four consequences, each usable.

**It changes what counts as evidence.** For the classical case the evidence is textual and historical - the treatise, the recorded reasons, the surviving lineage with its castes, languages and instruments. For the present case it is **living-practice evidence**: named practitioners, named organisations, dated recognitions, fourteen named puppet traditions across four mechanisms that exist as described.

**It changes what counts as a response.** Documentation, transmission, training and performance space are the appropriate responses to a historical contraction with a surviving lineage. **Funding and audience** are the appropriate responses to a present shortage of funding and audience. The record names the problem in terms that imply its own remedy, which is exactly why a specific diagnosis is worth more than a general lament.

**It changes the qualification you must add.** For the classical case the necessary qualification is that the form is **not extinct**. For the present case the necessary qualification is that a **single named revival effort cannot be generalised across four mechanisms** whose construction, scale, skill requirements and carrier communities differ substantially - a shadow tradition needing leatherwork, cutting and lighting is not in the same position as a glove tradition needing a cloth figure and a drummer.

**It changes the shape of the verdict.** A verdict on the classical case is historical: the form contracted for identifiable reasons and one lineage survived because it was embedded in a regional community and a rule-governed repertoire rather than in a court. A verdict on the present case is prospective: the skills exist, the traditions are named and described, and the recorded deficits are economic - which makes this a solvable problem rather than a loss already suffered.

### 4. The recognition instruments, and their exact limits

Four kinds of instrument appear in this topic, and they do different things.

| Instrument | What it does | What it does **not** do |
|---|---|---|
| **A restrictive statute** - the **Dramatic Performances Act, 1876**, imposed to curb political themes | Demonstrates that a stage was politically consequential | Recognise, fund or protect anything |
| **An international inscription** - **Kutiyattam 2008**, **Ramman 2009**, **Mudiyettu 2010**, **Chhau 2010** | Records that a state party nominated an element and a committee inscribed it in a stated year | Certify antiquity, confer classical status, rank quality or guarantee continued practice |
| **A national academy** - the **Sangeet Natak Akademi**, created by resolution in **1952** and inaugurated on **28 January 1953**, for promoting the performing arts **including theatre** - and its awards, such as the **2004** award for a rod-puppet tradition and the **2020** award for puppetry revival through a named organisation | Confers recognition and a purse on named practitioners; signals which fields the state treats as living | Substitute for an audience, or by itself reverse a documented economic deficit |
| **A training institution** - the **National School of Drama**, recorded as producing great theatre personalities, set up under the academy in **1959** and autonomous from **1975** | Reproduces skills and practitioners | Preserve a form that no one is paying to watch |

⚠️ The column on the right is the part that earns marks. Almost every weak answer in this area treats recognition as though it were the solution; the disciplined answer treats it as **one instrument with a specific function and a specific limit**, and pairs it with the recorded deficit it cannot itself repair.

### 5. The one current record this session claims

📰 **Current record, stated once, file-wide.** On **10 June 2026**, the **Sangeet Natak Akademi, the National Academy of Music, Dance and Drama, New Delhi**, issued a press release announcing the **Sangeet Natak Akademi Fellowship (Akademi Ratna)** and the **Sangeet Natak Akademi Awards (Akademi Puraskar) for the years 2024 and 2025**, together with the **Ustad Bismillah Khan Yuva Puraskar** for the same two years. The **General Council**, at its meeting held from **22 to 26 March 2026** in New Delhi, **unanimously elected seven eminent personalities** in the field of the performing arts as **Akademi Fellows**, a **fellowship restricted to 40 at any given time**; **selected 108 artists** for the Akademi Puraskar from the fields of **Music, Dance, Theatre, Traditional/Folk/Tribal/Music/Dance/Theatre/Puppetry, and Overall Contribution/Scholarship and others in the performing arts**; and **selected 106 young artists** for the Ustad Bismillah Khan Yuva Puraskar. The release records that the **Akademi Awards have been conferred since 1952**, that the **Akademi Fellowship carries a purse of Rs. 3,00,000** and the **Akademi Award a purse of Rs. 1,00,000**, each **besides a Tamrapatra and Angavastram**, and that the **Yuva Puraskar carries Rs. 25,000** with the same two items. It also records that the awardees **cover the entire gamut of the performing art forms expressed in the form of music, dance, drama, folk and tribal art, puppetry and allied theatre art forms**.

**Why this is a genuine linkage rather than decoration.** Three reasons, each tied to something taught above.

First, it shows the **academy created by resolution in 1952** still performing the function the record attributes to it - promoting the performing arts including theatre - through a **dated institutional act** seventy-four years later.

Second, it shows that **puppetry and theatre are named categories** in the current award architecture, alongside music, dance and folk and tribal art. That matters directly to the decline argument: the record documents puppetry as declining for lack of funding and audience, and the current position shows a national instrument that still carries a puppetry category and a purse. The instrument exists; the limits identified in the table above still apply to it.

Third, it supplies a **current anchor for the two dated puppetry awards** already taught - the 2004 rod-puppetry award and the 2020 puppetry-revival award - by showing the same institution and the same award series operating now.

⚠️ **The boundary, observed exactly.** The administration of this award architecture, its complete list of recipients, its running totals and its policy history belong to the **heritage, conservation and institutions owner**, which holds its own verification date. This session records the announcement, its date, its stated categories, its counts and its purse figures because they are directly relevant to the topic's own safeguarding argument, and it claims nothing further. **No individual recipient of the 2024 or 2025 awards is named anywhere in this session**, and no award, honour or year is attributed to any practitioner beyond the two the core record itself supplies.

### 6. The synthesis: what safeguarding means in this topic

Pull the threads together and a defensible position emerges.

The strongest safeguarding claim available here is **not that these forms are old**. Antiquity is the weakest available argument, and in several cases it is contested - a cave label that is disputed, a treatise with a four-century bracket, an origin "believed" to lie in the ninth or tenth century, a tradition "perhaps" one of the oldest.

The strongest claim is that **these forms are still performed, by identifiable people, on identifiable occasions**. The record supports it comprehensively: a Sanskrit-adhering theatre held as the privilege of two named castes with a named living exponent; shadow traditions carried by four named communities; a Konkan drama performed by farmers; pastoral communities carrying their caste gods from place to place; two Manipuri institutions founded specifically to keep traditional theatre alive; fourteen named puppet traditions with their construction and control described in the present tense; and a national award architecture that currently names puppetry and theatre among its fields.

And the correct qualification is that **continued performance, not preservation in an archive, is what keeps these forms alive** - which is why the two recorded deficits are audience and funding, and why documentation alone answers neither.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"Two performance traditions in this topic are recorded as having declined, but not in the same tense." Examine what follows from that difference for analysis and for response. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The difference of tense is not a stylistic accident; it marks two structurally different situations.

Court Sanskrit theatre is described in the past. Its causes are historical and of three distinct kinds - writers moved from plays to poems and songs; strict rules and difficult Sanskrit reduced popularity while Pali and Prakrit became common; and under Muslim rulers dance and music were patronised more than theatre. None of these can now be acted upon, and the process they describe is finished.

Puppetry is described in the present: it is *currently* on decline, because of lack of funding and captive audience. Those causes are operating now, are only two, and are both economic.

Three consequences follow. **Evidence** differs: the first case rests on text and history, the second on living practice - named practitioners, a named organisation, dated awards and fourteen named traditions across four mechanisms described in the present tense. **Response** differs: documentation, transmission, training and performance space suit a historical contraction with a surviving lineage; livelihood support and audience building suit a present shortage of exactly those two things. **Qualification** differs: the first case must not be called extinct, because a Kerala lineage has continued since the tenth century; the second must not be generalised from one revival case across four dissimilar mechanisms.

Verdict: read the tense of the record before prescribing, because one diagnosis will not fit both.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the tense distinction named as the organising move 2 + three historical causes and two present causes stated exactly 5 + three consequences drawn separately for evidence, response and qualification 6 + the non-extinction and non-generalisation cautions 1 + a verdict that makes the tense a procedural instruction 1.**

### Examination use

For Prelims, the dated set is: **Sangeet Natak Akademi** - created by resolution in **1952**, inaugurated **28 January 1953**; awards **conferred since 1952**; the **2004** and **2020** puppetry-related awards with their named recipients; the **National School of Drama**, under the academy from **1959**, autonomous from **1975**; and the four inscriptions - **Kutiyattam 2008**, **Ramman 2009**, **Mudiyettu 2010**, **Chhau 2010**. 📰 The current linkage adds the announcement of **10 June 2026** with its **seven Fellows**, **108 Akademi Puraskar artists**, **106 Yuva Puraskar artists**, the **fellowship limit of 40**, and the purse figures of **Rs. 3,00,000**, **Rs. 1,00,000** and **Rs. 25,000**.

For Mains, this is the topic's best analytical block. The two-decline distinction, the instrument-and-limit table and the closing qualification together make a complete, evidenced and non-generic safeguarding answer.

### Traps introduced here

- Writing a generic "traditional arts are dying" conclusion.
- Treating the classical contraction and the present puppetry pressure as one phenomenon with one remedy.
- Reading an international inscription as a statement about antiquity, classical status or quality.
- Generalising one named revival effort across all four puppet mechanisms.
- Naming award recipients, totals or list positions that this topic does not own.
- Concluding that recognition alone solves a documented funding and audience deficit.

### Revision notes

1. **Two kinds of decline**: a **completed historical contraction** with historical causes, against a **current condition** described in the **present tense** with **two named causes**.
2. Classical causes: **writers moved to poems and songs**; **strict rules and difficult Sanskrit** while **Pali and Prakrit became common**; **patronage shifted to dance and music** under Muslim rulers.
3. Present causes: **lack of funding** and **lack of captive audience**.
4. Both have living continuations: **Kutiyattam since the 10th century AD**; **fourteen named puppet traditions** described in the present tense.
5. Recognition instruments and their limits: a **restrictive statute (1876)** proves consequence but protects nothing; an **inscription** records a dated listing but certifies neither antiquity nor quality; an **academy award** recognises a practitioner but does not create an audience; a **training school** reproduces skills but cannot sustain an unattended form.
6. Dated anchors: **SNA created by resolution 1952, inaugurated 28 January 1953**; awards **conferred since 1952**; **2004** rod-puppetry award to **Maguni Charan Kuanr**; **2020** award to **Anupama Hoskere** through ***Dhaatu***; **NSD** under the academy **1959**, autonomous **1975**.
7. Inscriptions attaching to this topic's forms: **Kutiyattam 2008**, **Ramman 2009**, **Mudiyettu 2010**, **Chhau 2010**.
8. 📰 Current record: the academy's **10 June 2026** announcement - General Council **22-26 March 2026**; **7 Fellows** (fellowship limited to **40** at any time); **108 artists** for the Akademi Puraskar for **2024 and 2025**, across fields including **Theatre** and **Traditional/Folk/Tribal/Music/Dance/Theatre/Puppetry**; **106 young artists** for the **Ustad Bismillah Khan Yuva Puraskar**; purses of **Rs. 3,00,000**, **Rs. 1,00,000** and **Rs. 25,000**, each with a **Tamrapatra and Angavastram**.
9. ⚠️ Award administration, complete recipient lists and running totals belong to the **heritage and institutions owner**. No 2024 or 2025 recipient is named here.
10. The strongest safeguarding claim is **not antiquity** but **continued performance by identifiable people on identifiable occasions**.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate writes: "India's performance traditions are dying and must be preserved before they disappear." Rewrite that sentence so that every clause is defensible from this topic, and explain what each change buys.

**Model answer:** A defensible rewriting is: "One named art form in this field - puppetry - is recorded as currently in decline for two specific reasons, a lack of funding and a lack of captive audience, while the court Sanskrit tradition underwent a historical contraction whose causes were authorial, linguistic and patronage-related and whose Kerala lineage continues; the appropriate responses therefore differ, with livelihood support and audience building answering the first and transmission, training and performance space answering the second." Four changes are doing the work. Replacing **"India's performance traditions"** with a **named form** buys precision and truth, because the record documents a present decline for puppetry and does not make that claim of the folk field, of the surviving Sanskrit lineage or of the modern stage. Replacing **"are dying"** with the recorded causal language buys an actionable diagnosis: a funding shortage and an audience shortage are problems with identifiable remedies, whereas dying is a condition with none. Adding the **second decline type** buys the analytical distinction the topic is actually built on and prevents the answer from prescribing one remedy for two different problems. And replacing **"preserved before they disappear"** with **differentiated responses** buys the closing qualification that matters most here - that continued performance, not archival preservation, is what sustains a living form, which is why the record's own example of a response is an organised revival effort recognised by a dated award rather than a documentation project.

**Misconception to avoid:** Over-correcting into the claim that nothing is at risk. The record is explicit and present-tense about one form's condition and names two causes for it. The discipline is to say exactly what is at risk, for which form, on what evidence, and what would answer it - not to replace an alarmist generalisation with a complacent one.

## Lesson 23 - Attribution, chronology, region, terminology and answer construction

CA found: None found; none claimed
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: five disciplines, and the planted confusions each one defuses

```text
   DISCIPLINE 1 : TERMINOLOGY - one word, several systems
     SUTRADHAR     Sanskrit stage manager | folk narrator | puppeteer
                   ("holder of strings"); in BHAVAI he is called NAYAKA
     NAYAKA        Sanskrit HERO | the Bhavai SUTRADHAR
     GOMBEYATTA    Karnataka STRING puppets
     TOGALU G.     Karnataka SHADOW puppets
     KUNDHEI       Odisha STRING puppets
     KATHI KUNDHEI Odisha ROD puppets (KATHI = wooden rod)
     PAVAKOOTHU    Kerala GLOVE puppets
     TOLPAVA KUTHU Kerala SHADOW puppets
     KHYAL         here a RAJASTHANI FOLK THEATRE (music topic owns the
                   vocal genre)
     JATRA         eastern folk THEATRE | a STYLE LABEL for rod puppets
     DASHAVATAR    Konkan folk DRAMA (temple architecture owns the
                   Deogarh temple)
     BHAND         UP mimicry form | part of the Kashmiri BHAND PATHER
     SILAPPADIKARAM cited for PUPPETRY and, separately, for the TRADE IN
                   STAVES, SWORDS AND ARMOUR

   DISCIPLINE 2 : CHRONOLOGY - three clocks, never merged
     TEXT        Natya Shastra 200 BC - 200 AD | Ain-i-Akbari mention |
                 Manasollasa 12th c | Silappadikaram 2nd c AD
     PRACTICE    Kutiyattam since 10th c AD | most folk theatre 15th-16th c
                 | Krishnanattam mid-17th c | Pavakoothu 18th c |
                 Laithibi Jagoi 19th c | Kuruvanji ~300 years ago |
                 Tolpava Kuthu believed 9th-10th c AD
     INSTITUTION 1775 Calcutta Theatre | 1876 Act | 1872 first Bengali
                 professional company | 1883 Star Theatre | 1887 Shimla
                 Gaiety | 1943 IPTA | 1944 Prithvi | 1952 SNA | 1959/1975
                 NSD | 1969 & 1976 Manipur | 1978 permanent Prithvi |
                 2004, 2008, 2009, 2010, 2020 recognitions

   DISCIPLINE 3 : REGION - the record's own dual attributions
     Yakshagana: Karnataka AND parts of Kerala
     Tal-Maddale: coastal Karnataka AND Kerala
     Villu Paatu: Kerala AND parts of Tamil Nadu
     Theyyam: Kerala, with a Karnataka counterpart (Bhuta Kola)
     Dashavatar: Sindhudurg (Maharashtra) AND North Goa
     Swang: table says Rajasthan/MP-Malwar; text says Rajasthan and Haryana
     Naqal/Bhand: Punjab (Naqal) AND Uttar Pradesh (Bhand)
     Nautanki: "North India"
     Therukoothu: Tamil Nadu AND Tamil-speaking regions of SRI LANKA
     Putul Nachh: West Bengal, Odisha AND Assam
     Glove puppets: "popular ALL ACROSS INDIA"

   DISCIPLINE 4 : FORM AND MECHANISM - never infer one from the other
     the STATE does not predict the mechanism (Odisha has all three;
     Karnataka and Kerala have two each)
     the MECHANISM does not predict scale (8 inches to 3 metres in one
     category), material, repertoire or number of puppeteers
     the CATEGORY set is BROAD: rod is derived from glove; Bommalattam
     mixes rod and string

   DISCIPLINE 5 : ATTRIBUTION - say only what is recorded
     NO invented origin dates, founders, awards or performer honours
     REPRODUCE the record's hedges: "often known as", "perhaps one of the
     oldest", "believed to have originated", "as per Bharata", "almost rare"
     DO NOT extend a one-system statement across a tradition (the mask
     sentence is the standing example)
```

### 1. The learner's actual problem

Everything in this session is now available; what remains is the discipline that keeps it defensible under pressure. Five failure modes account for almost all lost marks in this topic, and each has a corresponding discipline. This lesson states them, assembles the planted confusions in one place, and then gives the answer architecture.

### 2. Discipline one: terminology

Indian performance vocabulary is shared across systems while job descriptions, mechanisms and referents are not. The list in the diagram is not decorative; every entry in it is a real collision inside this session's own material, and each is the kind of pairing an item-setter reaches for first.

Three rules handle all of them.

**Read the qualifier, not the root.** *Togalu* marks the shadow tradition. *Kathi* marks the rod tradition. *Tol* marks the leather form. *Pather* marks the Kashmiri form. The qualifier is where the information is.

**Ask which system the stem is in.** If a stem mentions gharanas, a vocal genre or a musical structure, a familiar word belongs to the music topic. If it mentions a *shikhara* or a temple site, it belongs to architecture. If it mentions a mechanism, a community or a region, it belongs here.

**State the function, then the particular.** This is the rule from Lesson 1 and it holds for every shared word: say what the figure or the term does, then say what it does in this specific form.

### 3. Discipline two: chronology

Keep the three clocks separate and reproduce the record's own hedges. The clock list in the diagram is complete for this session; three cautions apply to it.

**The bracket is the answer.** Where the record gives a range - a four-century treatise bracket, a period from the 1850s to the 1920s, a fifteenth-to-sixteenth-century beginning - write the range, not a point.

**A century with a hedge keeps the hedge.** Two entries are explicitly hedged: a shadow tradition **believed to have originated** in the ninth or tenth century, and a theatre tradition described as **perhaps** one of the oldest.

**Institutional dates are the densest and most confusable set in the topic.** The pairs most often swapped are 1872 and 1883, 1943 and 1944, 1959 and 1975, and 1969 and 1976. Learn them as pairs and the confusion disappears: a company then a house; an association then a touring theatre; a founding then an autonomy; two Manipur institutions seven years apart.

### 4. Discipline three: region

The record gives dual and multiple attributions repeatedly, and reproducing them is not pedantry - it is the difference between a correct and an incorrect matching answer. Two further points.

**One recorded internal discrepancy is preserved rather than resolved.** A northern musical drama is placed in the table under Rajasthan and Madhya Pradesh's Malwar region while its own description says the forms are popular in Rajasthan and Haryana. This session reproduces both and settles neither.

**One attribution is transnational.** A Tamil street theatre is recorded in Tamil Nadu **and Tamil-speaking regions of Sri Lanka**, the only cross-border attribution in the folk table. Dropping the second half is a common and avoidable loss.

### 5. Discipline four: form and mechanism

Two inferences are invalid and both are tempting.

**State to mechanism.** Odisha carries string, shadow and rod traditions; Karnataka carries string and shadow; Kerala carries shadow and glove. Naming a state tells you nothing about how a figure moves.

**Mechanism to everything else.** Within the string category alone, scale runs from eight inches to three metres, material from carved wood to light wood to life-size dolls, control from finger-tied strings to a triangular prop to an iron head-ring to multiple puppeteers, and repertoire from a regional folk idiom to the *Raas Leela*. A shared mechanism guarantees a shared principle of control and nothing more.

And the category set itself is **broad** rather than exhaustive: rod is defined as a larger variation of glove, and one named tradition mixes rod and string.

### 6. Discipline five: attribution

Four prohibitions, stated plainly.

**Do not invent an origin date.** Several forms in this session have none recorded, and inventing one is the most easily detected error in the topic.

**Do not invent or transfer an honour.** This session records exactly five dated personal or institutional honours from the core record - a 2004 puppetry award, a 2017 Padma Shri for a martial tradition, a 2009 Padma Shri for another, a 2020 puppetry-revival award, and a Ramon Magsaysay award for a named theatre-group founder without a year - plus the academy's current announcement, whose individual recipients this session deliberately does not name. Do not multiply this set.

**Do not universalise a one-system statement.** The mask sentence is the standing example and the principle is general: a statement recorded about the classical staging system, or about one tradition, is not a statement about Indian performance.

**Reproduce the hedges.** *Often known as*, *perhaps one of the oldest*, *believed to have originated*, *as per Bharata*, *almost rare*, *generally*, *broadly classified*, *mainly*. Every one of these appears in the record for a reason, and reproducing them is the cheapest available demonstration of source discipline.

### 7. The answer architecture

**Directive and demand map.**

| If the question says | It is really testing | Do not write |
|---|---|---|
| *Discuss the conventions of classical Sanskrit theatre* | Act structure, closure convention, protagonist rule, the five-part shape, the ritual opening, the staging prescription and the character categories - plus what a convention proves | A plot summary of one play, or a claim about Indian drama in general |
| *Why did Sanskrit theatre decline?* | Three differently-typed causes, and the survival of a living lineage | "It died out in ancient India" |
| *Folk theatre as a vehicle of social communication* | The three-stage arc, evidenced by named forms, plus the infrastructure-of-attention argument | "Folk theatre entertains villagers" |
| *Classify Indian puppetry* | Four mechanisms, each with construction, control and named traditions, plus the derivation and the hybrid | Four category names with no mechanism |
| *Compare puppetry traditions* | Material, mechanism, scale, region, community and repertoire on one axis | Four separate descriptions in sequence |
| *Martial and ritual performance traditions* | Weapon, movement, community and occasion, region by region, and the combat-to-display continuum | "India has many ancient martial arts" |
| *Modern Indian theatre* | The blended source base, the proscenium, commerce, subject matter, the 1876 Act, and the sequence of answers to who pays and who authorises | A list of playwrights |
| *Safeguarding performance traditions* | The difference between a completed historical contraction and a current documented deficit, and what each instrument can and cannot do | "Our heritage must be preserved" |

**Qualified thesis options.** Four are available and each is evidenced in this session.

- **The convention thesis.** Sanskrit theatre is best explained through the rules it accepted - act range, a closure convention, a male protagonist who attains his desire, a ritual opening led by a white-clad officiant, a cosmologically divided stage and a language register assigned by character - because those rules, not individual plots, are what made it a distinct performance system.
- **The mechanism thesis.** Indian puppetry is classified by mechanism rather than by region because mechanism predicts construction, scale, control, stage relation and expressive possibility, while region predicts none of them - and the one device unique to shadow puppetry, size as a social code, proves the point.
- **The two-decline thesis.** Court Sanskrit theatre's decline is a completed historical process with authorial, linguistic and patronage causes, whereas puppetry's is a current documented deficit of funding and audience; the two require different responses, and collapsing them produces an unusable prescription.
- **The occasion thesis.** Indian folk theatre is best read by occasion, carrier, means and theme rather than by state, because the record consistently supplies all four and because occasion - a Dussehra window, a serpent-goddess festival, a spring festival under its regional name, a harvest, a votive fulfilment, an eight-night temple cycle - is what sustains the form materially.

**Mark-scaled structures.**

| Marks | Structure |
|---|---|
| **10** | Thesis → the form or system defined with its precise vocabulary → two named examples with their discriminating features → one caution reproduced from the record → verdict |
| **15** | Thesis → the classification or convention set in full → a comparison built on one axis rather than four descriptions → chronology or decline handled with the three clocks kept apart → one dated anchor → a two-part verdict that accepts and qualifies |
| **20** | All of the above → **plus** the complete arc from treatise to folk field to modern stage → the cross-art influence claim in its specific form → the recognition instruments with their limits → the performance-not-preservation qualification → a verdict answering the exact wording |

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"India's performance traditions are best understood through their mechanisms of transmission rather than through their antiquity." Critically examine with reference to theatre and puppetry. (20 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The claim is largely correct: antiquity here is either contested or uninformative, while transmission is documented and explanatory.

Antiquity fails on both counts. The earliest performance spaces carry a contested label - the world's oldest amphitheatres. The foundational treatise is bracketed across four centuries. A Karnataka tradition is only *perhaps* one of the oldest, and a Kerala shadow form is *believed* to date from the ninth or tenth century. Even a firm date explains nothing: that a Kerala theatre has survived since the tenth century does not tell you how.

Transmission does. That theatre survives as the privilege of two named castes, performing a repertoire that completely adheres to a codified text, in three languages, with two named instruments and a convention by which every character opens with a recollection of past events. Each element is a mechanism: a community holds the right, a text fixes the standard, a convention fixes the form. The field repeats it: shadow traditions carried by four named communities, a Konkan drama by farmers, itinerant pastoral communities carrying their caste gods, puppet figures whose designs and movement come from a regional theatre and dance, and institutions founded in 1969 and 1976 to keep traditional theatre alive.

The qualification: transmission also explains failure. Puppetry's decline is a transmission problem - a shortage of funding and of a captive audience - not an age problem, so the fitting responses are economic.

Verdict: antiquity dates a tradition; transmission explains why it survives, and what would end it.

**Unique scoring note:** **20 marks = four antiquity claims shown to be hedged or uninformative 4 + the surviving Sanskrit lineage decomposed into its transmission mechanisms 5 + four further transmission instances drawn from folk, puppetry and modern institutions 5 + the critical turn showing that transmission also explains decline 4 + a verdict that assigns each concept its distinct function rather than dismissing antiquity outright 2.**

### Examination use

For Prelims, this lesson is the planted-confusion table. Work through it once before the examination and the most expensive objective errors in this topic become unavailable.

For Mains, it is the answer architecture: directive map, thesis options, mark-scaled structures and the five disciplines that keep every claim defensible.

### Traps introduced here

- Answering a mechanism question with a regional fact.
- Merging the text, practice and institution clocks.
- Dropping a dual regional attribution or the single transnational one.
- Resolving a recorded internal discrepancy instead of stating it.
- Inventing a date, a founder or an honour.
- Deleting a hedge because the unhedged sentence reads better.

### Revision notes

1. **Terminology**: read the qualifier - ***Togalu***, ***Kathi***, ***Tol***, ***Pather*** - and ask which system the stem is in.
2. Shared words with different referents: ***Sutradhar***, ***Nayaka***, ***Gombeyatta***, ***Kundhei***, ***Khyal***, ***Jatra***, ***Dashavatar***, ***Bhand***.
3. **Three clocks**: **text** (200 BC-200 AD; *Ain-i-Akbari*; 12th-century *Manasollasa*; 2nd-century *Silappadikaram*), **practice** (10th c; 15th-16th c; mid-17th c; 18th c; 19th c; ~300 years ago), **institution** (1775, 1872, 1876, 1883, 1887, 1943, 1944, 1952, 1959, 1969, 1975, 1976, 1978, 2004, 2008, 2009, 2010, 2020).
4. Institutional pairs most often swapped: **1872/1883**, **1943/1944**, **1959/1975**, **1969/1976**.
5. **Dual attributions** to reproduce: Yakshagana, Tal-Maddale, Villu Paatu, Theyyam, Dashavatar, Swang, Naqal/Bhand, Nautanki, Therukoothu, Putul Nachh; glove puppets **all across India**; rod puppets **mainly eastern India**.
6. The **Swang** attribution is an internal discrepancy: **preserve both**, resolve neither.
7. **State does not predict mechanism**; **mechanism does not predict** scale, material, repertoire or puppeteer count.
8. The four puppetry categories are **broad**: **rod derives from glove**; **Bommalattam mixes rod and string**.
9. **Five recorded honours** from the core record, and no more: **2004**, **2009**, **2017**, **2020**, and an undated **Ramon Magsaysay** award. No 2024 or 2025 recipient is named in this session.
10. **Hedges to reproduce**: *often known as*, *perhaps one of the oldest*, *believed to have originated*, *as per Bharata*, *almost rare*, *generally*, *broadly classified*, *mainly*.

### Concept check

**Question:** A stem reads: "With reference to Indian puppetry, consider the following: the tradition is performed in Karnataka; its figures are cut from leather; the size of a figure indicates the social status of the character it represents." What is the reasoning route to an answer, and which of the five disciplines does the stem exploit?

**Model answer:** The route runs through mechanism first, then the qualifier, then the state. The **second clause settles the mechanism**: figures cut from leather belong to shadow puppetry, whose recorded baseline is flat figures cut out of leather, painted identically on both sides and placed on a white screen with light behind. The **third clause settles the tradition uniquely**: only one recorded tradition uses puppets of different sizes to show social status - large for kings and deities, smaller for commoners - and it is the shadow theatre of Karnataka. The **first clause is the trap**, because Karnataka carries two puppetry traditions, one string and one shadow, whose names differ only by a qualifier. Reading the state first would leave the answer ambiguous; reading the mechanism first resolves it, and the qualifier confirms it. The disciplines the stem exploits are the **first**, terminology, because the two Karnataka traditions share a name and are separated only by a prefix meaning leather, and the **fourth**, form and mechanism, because a candidate who reasons from state to mechanism will find two candidate traditions and no way to choose. The general lesson is the one this session has repeated: in puppetry, **read the mechanism clause first and the state clause last**, because the mechanism is informative and the state is not.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming that because the size device is memorable it must be widespread. It is recorded for exactly one tradition and it is available at all only because a shadow image has no absolute scale. Treating it as a general feature of Indian puppetry would lead a candidate to accept it for a string or rod tradition, where the figures are physical objects on a visible stage and relative size reads as a difference of object, not of status.

**Answer-neutrality, observed absolutely.** No answer letter, truth-value marking, option elimination or corrective mapping is recorded anywhere in this session. The **official 2024 Set-A key and the provisional 2026 Set-A key held locally were not consulted**. The reproducing source prints its own two-item key for its objective set; **that key was read and deliberately not carried into this session**.

**Reproduction policy for objective items, stated once.** Where this session does **not** own all the discriminating material in an item, the item is reproduced exactly, with its stem and response choices. Where this session **does** own all the discriminating material, reproducing the response choices alongside the teaching would effectively disclose the answer; those items are therefore recorded as an **exact demand description** with the lessons that supply the material, and their response choices are withheld. Main Examination items carry no response choices and are reproduced in full.

## Owned objective items, reproduced from the core art source's chapter list

Two entries are reproduced in that chapter's objective list. **No answer letter is recorded for either of them.**

### Central Armed Police Forces examination, 2018 - Binodini Dasi

**Recorded as a demand description; response choices withheld under the reproduction policy above.**

**Exact stem.** "Which of the following statements about Binodini Dasi, a pioneering figure in Bengali theatre, in the late 19th and early 20th century is/are correct?" - the two numbered statements concern, in order, whether she was one of the prime movers behind the setting up of the Star Theatre (1883) in Calcutta, and whether she serialised her autobiography *Amar Katha* (My story) between 1910 and 1913. "Select the correct answer using the code given below."

**Why the response choices are withheld.** Lesson 15 records the material bearing on both statements directly, so printing the option set beside that teaching would disclose the answer.

**Ownership.** Owned by this topic. The item is attributed in the reproducing source to a **Central Armed Police Forces examination**, not to the Civil Services Examination, and that attribution is preserved rather than upgraded.

**Demand.** A two-statement item on a single named practitioner, testing an **institutional contribution** and a **publication fact** independently. It is the clearest instance in this topic of an item built on a person rather than on a form.

**What this session supplies.** Lesson 15 records that **Binodini Dasi (1863-1941)**, popularly known as ***Notee Binodini***, was the **prima donna of Bengali theatre** and the **first woman in India to become a notable stage performer from the Bengal Presidency in the 1870s**; that she **began her career in acting at the age of 12 and ended by the time she was 23**; that she **also made contributions in founding the Star Theatre in Kolkata in 1883**; and that her **autobiography, *Amar Katha* (The Story of My Life), was published in 1913**.

⚠️ **A discrepancy, recorded and not resolved.** The item's second statement describes the autobiography as **serialised between 1910 and 1913**, while the chapter's prose records it as **published in 1913**. Serialisation across a period and publication in a year are not mutually exclusive descriptions, but they are different claims. This session prints both and settles neither. Lesson 15 states the safe formulation for an answer.

### Civil Services Examination, 2014 - Kalaripayattu

**Recorded as a demand description; response choices withheld under the reproduction policy above.**

**Exact stem.** "With reference to India's culture and tradition, what is 'Kalaripayattu'?"

**Why the response choices are withheld.** Lesson 20 records the tradition's category, region and character in full, so printing the four descriptions beside that teaching would disclose the answer.

**Ownership.** Owned by this topic.

**Demand.** A single-definition item requiring the candidate to place a named tradition in the right **category** - distinguishing a martial practice from a devotional cult, a metalworking tradition and a dance-drama tradition - and to attach the right **region**. It is a category-and-region item, not a technique item.

**What this session supplies.** Lesson 20 records that **Kalaripayattu** is **one of the oldest martial arts in India**, **practised in most parts of Southern India** and **originating in the state of Kerala around the 3rd century BC**; that ***Kalari*** is a **Malayalam word** for the **school, gymnasium or training hall** where the art is practised or taught; that it **includes mock duels, armed and unarmed combat, and physical exercises**; that it is **generally not accompanied by music**; and that **footwork is its most important key**. Lesson 23's terminology and form disciplines supply the general rule the item is built on: **place the tradition in its category before attaching a region, because a category error cannot be repaired by a correct region.**

## Owned Main Examination items, reproduced in full

Five entries are reproduced in that chapter's Main Examination list. **No official paper is held locally for any of them**, and they are reproduced from that list with its year attributions. Main Examination items carry no response choices, so each is reproduced in full with its directive, its demand and a concise answer approach. **No solved model answer is given for any of them**, in line with this session's practice.

### 2011 - five traditional theatre forms

> **Write a sentence each on any five of the following traditional theatre forms:**
>
> (a) Bhand Pather
> (b) Swang
> (c) Maach
> (d) Bhaona
> (e) Mudiyettu
> (f) Dhashavatar

*(Reproduced from the core art source's theatre-chapter Main Examination list, attributed there to CSE, 2011. The sixth item is printed in that list as **Dhashavatar**; this session teaches the form under the spelling **Dashavatar** used in the same chapter's classification table, and records the variant rather than resolving it.)*

**Directive.** *Write a sentence each* - a compressed identification demand across six named forms, of which five must be attempted.

**Demand.** One **discriminating** sentence per form. The demand is explicitly for brevity, which means a generic sentence about folk theatre scores nothing: each sentence must contain the feature that separates that form from the other five.

**Concise answer approach, and where the material sits.** Lesson 11 supplies the first three and the sixth is in Lesson 12; Lesson 10 supplies the fourth; Lesson 8 and Lesson 13 supply the fifth. For each, the discriminating element is: for the Kashmiri form, **contemporary social satire or mythical stories, secular in outlook although performed by Muslims, with music, dance and drama** - and the caution from Lesson 11 that it must not be fused with the Uttar Pradesh mimicry form of a similar name; for the Rajasthan and Madhya Pradesh form, **musical dramas sung through verses with five named instruments and mimicry accompanied by dialogue**, together with its recorded popularity in Rajasthan and Haryana; for the Malwa form, **dialogues delivered as couplets called *Rangat Dohas***, with a shift from mythological to romantic themes; for the Assamese form, that it is a **presentation of Ankia Naat**, introduced by **Sankardeva in the early 16th century**, with the **Sutradhar narrating and singing verses from holy texts**; for the Kerala form, that it is a **ritual theatre and dance drama** inscribed on the intangible-heritage list in **2010** - and ⚠️ that this session holds only those three facts for it, so the sentence must not be padded; and for the Konkan form, that it is performed by the **farmers of the Konkan region, notably Sindhudurg and North Goa**, to honour the **ten incarnations of Vishnu**, in **two parts** of which the **second is the main act**.

### 1995, 2005 and 2007 - Yakshagana

> **Write about Yakshagana**

*(Reproduced from the same list, attributed there to CSE, 1995, 2005 and 2007 - the only item in this topic's reproduced list recorded as having appeared in three separate years.)*

**Directive.** *Write about* - an open identification demand on a single named form, with no analytical hinge supplied. The absence of a directive verb is itself informative: the examiner wants coverage, ordered.

**Demand.** A complete account of one tradition: antiquity, origin, carrier, development, influence and current status, plus whatever distinguishes it from its neighbours.

**Concise answer approach, and where the material sits.** Lesson 13 supplies the complete record and, decisively, the **cluster**. Open with the recorded hedged antiquity - **perhaps one of the oldest theatre traditions**, **prevalent till date in Karnataka and parts of Kerala**. Then the origin: **the royal courts of the Vijayanagara empire**, performed by the community known as ***Jakkula Varu***. Then the development: **originally a descriptive dance-drama enacted by a single artist**, with **later forms adopting further variations and becoming a typical dance drama**. Then the influence on it: **strongly influenced by the Vaishnava Bhakti Movement**. Then - and this is what separates a strong answer from a competent one - the form's **reach**: its **predecessor** is the seated, costume-free narration tradition with its *Bhagavata* and *Arthadharis*; an **open-air Karnataka tradition performed during local-deity worship is a form of it**; a **coastal Karnataka ritual theatre is influenced by it**; and a **Karnataka string-puppet tradition's figures are styled and designed on its characters**. Close with the qualification from Lesson 13 that a court-origin entry inside a folk classification complicates the urban-rural contrast rather than breaking it.

### 1987 - origins of five traditional folk theatres

> **Where did the following traditional folk theatres originate?**
>
> (a) Jatra
> (b) Tamasha
> (c) Koodiyattam
> (d) Bhavai
> (e) Nautanki

*(Reproduced from the same list, attributed there to CSE, 1987.)*

**Directive.** *Where did ... originate* - a pure region-and-origin demand across five named forms.

**Demand.** Regional attribution, with enough supporting detail to show the attribution is known rather than guessed. Note that the five forms are deliberately drawn from five different parts of India and from **three different categories** - a folk theatre of eastern India, a Maharashtra folk form, a Sanskrit-adhering classical lineage, a Gujarat form and a north Indian form - so the item is also testing whether the candidate notices that one of the five is not a folk theatre at all.

**Concise answer approach, and where the material sits.** Lesson 10 supplies the first as a **popular folk theatre of eastern India**, **generally an open-air performance initiated by the Vaishnava saint Chaitanya Mahaprabhu** during his travels through **rural Bengal**. Lesson 12 supplies the second as a **Maharashtra** form known for humour and erotic content, with **female actors who even play male roles** and ***Lavani*** accompaniment. Lesson 8 supplies the third as **Kerala**'s Sanskrit-adhering theatre, **surviving since the 10th century AD**, the traditional **privilege of the Chakyar and Nambiar castes** - and ⚠️ the answer should say plainly that this one is not a folk theatre but India's oldest continuing theatre, because noticing the category is the sharpest thing available in a short answer. Lesson 11 supplies the fourth as **Gujarat**, with its **series of small plays**, **balanced earthen pots or brass pitchers** and its ***Sutradhar* known as *Nayaka***. Lesson 11 supplies the fifth as **North India**, an **offshoot of Swang**, mentioned in **Abul Fazl's *Ain-i-Akbari***, with the ***Nagada*** drum and later **schools around Kanpur and Lucknow**.

### 1985 - efforts to develop theatre in India

> **What efforts have been made to develop theatre in India? Name any two important persons associated with the development of this art.**

*(Reproduced from the same list, attributed there to CSE, 1985.)*

**Directive.** *What efforts have been made* plus an explicit instruction to **name any two persons** - a two-part demand in which the second part is mandatory and specific.

**Demand.** An account of **organised, institutional and individual effort**, not a history of forms. The word *efforts* directs the answer towards deliberate action: associations formed, theatres established, institutions founded, movements developed.

**Concise answer approach, and where the material sits.** Lesson 15 supplies the complete sequence and Lesson 14 supplies its precondition. Structure it as the six answers to the question of who sustains a performance: the **first Bengali professional theatre company in 1872**; the **literary repertoire** of a celebrated playwright whose first play came at twenty; the **Indian People's Theatre Association of 1943**, cultural wing of the Communist Party, writing on social themes such as the Bengal famines, disbanded in 1947 and now existing in three named states; **Prithvi Theatre in 1944**, a moving theatre with 150 artists and more than 2,000 plays, with a permanent house from 1978; the **Sangeet Natak Akademi in 1952** and the **National School of Drama**; and the deliberate regional return of **Kalakshetra Manipur in 1969** and the **Chorus Repertory Theatre in 1976**, together with the **Third Theatre** movement's small, experimental, socially conscious plays with minimalist staging and direct actor-audience interaction. For the mandatory two persons, any two named in Lesson 15 will serve, and the strongest pairing gives one institution-builder and one movement-founder with their recorded contributions attached. ⚠️ Name only what the record supplies; do not attach an award, a date or a founding to a person for whom none is recorded.

### 1982 - the author of a named play

> **Who wrote the play Ghasiram Kotwal?**

*(Reproduced from the same list, attributed there to CSE, 1982.)*

**Directive.** *Who wrote* - a single-fact attribution demand.

**Demand.** One name, correctly attributed. There is no analytical content to supply and no structure to build; the item either is or is not known.

**Concise answer approach, and where the material sits.** Lesson 15 records the attribution: the play was written by **Vijay Tendulkar**, who appears in the core record's own list of noted modern theatre personalities. ⚠️ The attribution itself is carried from a bounded verification recorded in the source ledger, because the core chapter names the playwright among modern personalities without attaching this title to him. **Play authorship and literary analysis belong to the language-and-literature owner**; this session carries the single attribution the item requires and claims nothing further about the text.

The festival and the ballad in the other two pairs are **not owned here** and are not taught in this session. Response choices withheld, since the pair this session owns is fully taught here |

This session owns the play-type vocabulary and **not** the attribution of plays to playwrights, which the core record itself routes to the literature owner. Response choices withheld |
| **2024, Preliminary GS-I** | The latest Indian inclusion on the intangible-heritage list, routed to the **dance** owner | **Adjacent; not owned.** This session records only the four inscriptions that attach to forms it teaches - 2008, 2009 and two in 2010 - and Lesson 22 states the reason: totals, current positions and list administration belong to the heritage and institutions owner |
| **2026, Preliminary GS-I** | A Bagh Caves painting whose subject is described as a folk dance, routed to the **painting-traditions** owner | **Adjacent; not owned.** This session holds no record of that painting and supplies nothing towards the item |
