## The honest nil, stated plainly

**No Main Examination question and no Preliminary item in the audited 2018-2026 window is owned by this topic.** That is the complete and accurate position, and it should be read correctly rather than as a reason to neglect the topic.

Three things follow from it. First, the topic's examinable weight in the recent window sits in **vocabulary and identification** that can appear inside another owner's item - a martial tradition inside a pairs item, a play-type name inside a literature item, an inscription year inside a heritage item. Second, the reproduced lists show that this material **has been examined repeatedly at Main Examination level in earlier decades**, across five entries between 1982 and 2011, one of which recurs in three separate years. Third, every original Mains question in the next-but-one section is **explicitly original practice** and is not represented as a past question; none of them reproduces or closely paraphrases any of the five reproduced items, and the two directive shapes those items use - *write about* a single named form, and *where did these originate* - are deliberately not reused.

# CUMULATIVE CONCEPT CHECKS

## Cumulative check 1 - after the classical block, Lessons 2 to 8

**Question:** Using only the classical block, construct the shortest defensible account of what happened to Sanskrit theatre, and mark every place where a hedge is required.

**Model answer:** A codified dramatic theory was written down in a treatise bracketed *200 BC to 200 AD* - hedge one, the bracket is the answer - which classified drama by two modes and ten play-types while developing only two of the latter, and prescribed a convention set: four to seven acts, a closure in which the hero wins or does not die with tragedy *almost* rare - hedge two - a male protagonist attaining his desire, and a five-part shape. It prescribed a staging system: a ritual opening behind the curtain, a white-clad stage manager offering prayers and announcing time and place, a house holding around four hundred persons *as per Bharata* - hedge three - two storeys assigning the celestial above and the terrestrial below, expressive curtains, and no masks *in this system* - hedge four. Its characters were classified in three kinds with a comic figure who questioned social norms through satire and spoke Prakrit while others spoke Sanskrit. The tradition then contracted for three recorded reasons - authorial migration, formal and linguistic difficulty as Pali and Prakrit became common, and a comparative patronage shift under Muslim rulers - and it did *not* become extinct, because a Kerala lineage completely adhering to the treatise's rules has survived since the tenth century AD as the traditional privilege of two named castes. The earliest claimed performance spaces are *often known as* the world's oldest amphitheatres - hedge five - and the label is contested.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing the hedges weaken the account. They are what make it defensible, and four of the five are the record's own words.

## Cumulative check 2 - after the folk field, Lessons 9 to 13

**Question:** A stem gives you a form and one feature. Rank the feature types in order of how reliably each identifies a form in this field, and justify the ranking from the record.

**Model answer:** Most reliable first. **A named community or caste** is close to decisive, because the record attaches a specific carrier to relatively few forms and almost never repeats one - a Brahmin community that narrates with painted pictures, a Muslim community of impersonators, two Kerala castes with a traditional theatre privilege, a caste that wears a Narasimha mask in Garhwal, a named community that performed a Karnataka court-origin form, and two pastoral communities of the Deccan plateau. **A named instrument** is next, because instruments are rarely shared across forms in this record and several forms are named after theirs. **A named occasion** is third and very strong where present - a serpent-goddess festival, the period before Dussehra, Holi celebrated under its regional name, a harvest season, an eight-night temple cycle, a votive performance on agricultural prosperity. **A named narrator convention** is fourth, strong but with a trap, because several forms have narrator pairs or trios and the counts must be right. **A language or dialect** is fifth and very strong where present but present for only three forms. **A theme** is weakest, because epic and Vaishnava material is shared across the entire field and identifies almost nothing. **A state** is weakest of all, because the record repeatedly gives two.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding that theme never matters. It is decisive where it is unusual - a biblical repertoire, a named Krishna text, a named Ramayana recension, a Draupadi story, a form honouring ten incarnations. The rule is that a *shared* theme identifies nothing while a *singular* one identifies immediately.

## Cumulative check 3 - after the modern stage, Lessons 14 and 15

**Question:** What did the colonial-era stage add to Indian performance that the field of Lessons 9 to 13 did not already have?

**Model answer:** Four things, and not one of them is theatre itself. **A framed, enclosed site**: urban centres led to proscenium-style theatre, which is a stage relation absent from a field performed in open air, open courtyards, streets, temple fronts and seated narration. **A commercial economy**: theatres became commercial, so an audience paying to enter replaced occasion and community as the funding basis - with the single recorded exception of a Maharashtra harvest-season form described as a blend of commercial and folk theatre. **Directly named contemporary subject matter**: dowry, caste, religious hypocrisy and politics, rather than social comment encoded inside a character type or a satirical convention. And **a blended source base**, drawing on Sanskrit *and* Western texts to produce a style the record calls blended rather than borrowed. The state's response - a statute of 1876 imposed to curb political themes - is the evidence that the third of these had become consequential. What the colonial stage did **not** add is performance, dramatic skill, social commentary or institutional organisation, all of which the preceding field already had.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the four additions as improvements. They are changes in site, economy, subject and source, and one of them - the enclosed ticketed house - arguably narrows access relative to an open courtyard, which is precisely the problem a later minimalist movement set out to solve.

## Cumulative check 4 - after the puppetry block, Lessons 16 to 19

**Question:** Explain, using three traditions from three different mechanisms, why the record classifies Indian puppetry by mechanism rather than by region.

**Model answer:** Because mechanism predicts the things that matter and region predicts nothing. Take a **shadow** tradition of Karnataka: because the image is a flat leather cut-out interrupting light on a white screen, the figure has no absolute scale, and the tradition can therefore use size to encode social status - large for kings and deities, smaller for commoners - a device unavailable to any other mechanism. Take a **string** tradition of Tamil Nadu: because the figures reach four and a half feet and ten kilograms, far beyond the eight-to-nine-inch baseline, the load is transferred to an iron ring worn on the puppeteer's head and rods are added to the strings, producing a recorded hybrid. Take a **glove** tradition of Kerala: because the figure is a head and arms in a long skirt worn on the hand, with the index finger controlling the head and the thumb and middle finger the hands, the expressive register is fast and gestural and there is no lower body at all. Now try the same exercise by region. Odisha carries string, shadow and rod traditions; Karnataka carries string and shadow, with near-identical names separated by one prefix; Kerala carries shadow and glove, also with near-identical names. The state tells you which traditions exist there and nothing about how any of them works.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding that region is irrelevant. It is decisive for the **carrier community**, which the record names for four of five shadow traditions, and for the cross-form influences in which a regionally dominant theatre or dance supplies a puppet tradition's design or movement. Region explains the tradition's social and artistic neighbourhood; mechanism explains the object and its possibilities.

## Cumulative check 5 - after the advanced block, Lessons 20 to 22

**Question:** Three things in this session are often described as "dying traditions": court Sanskrit theatre, puppetry and the martial traditions. Assess each description against the record.

**Model answer:** **Court Sanskrit theatre** is the only one for which a decline is recorded as a completed historical process, with three named causes - authorial migration to poems and songs, formal and linguistic difficulty as Pali and Prakrit became common, and a patronage shift towards dance and music. But "dying" is still wrong, because a Kerala lineage completely adhering to the treatise's rules has survived since the tenth century as the privilege of two named castes, with a named living exponent. The accurate description is a historical contraction with a surviving lineage. **Puppetry** is the only one for which the record uses the present tense: it is *currently* on decline, for two specific reasons, lack of funding and lack of a captive audience - and the same passage names a practitioner, an organisation and a dated award for reviving it, while fourteen named traditions are described in the present tense across four mechanisms. The accurate description is a current, documented, economically-caused pressure on a living art. **The martial traditions** are not described as dying at all. They are described as having moved: one is *earlier used by warriors, now practised as a performing art*; another originated as a pre-battle ritual and became part of wedding ceremonies; a third's steps and techniques are widely used in a masked dance form; and several are displayed at named festivals and celebrations. The accurate description is transformation of occasion and function, not decline.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reaching for a single narrative because all three are old. The record gives three different situations, uses three different tenses, and names different kinds of cause in each. A candidate who notices the tense of the source's own sentences has most of this answer already.

## Cumulative check 6 - across the whole session

**Question:** If you were allowed one sentence about Indian theatre and puppetry, and it had to be both true and useful, what would it be - and what would you have had to reject to get there?

**Model answer:** The sentence: **"Indian performance traditions are best read through six evidence questions - what text is performed, where the performer stands, what mechanism produces the action, which community carries the skill, on what occasion it happens, and what a named body has formally said about it - because every one of those is recorded, discriminating and actionable, while the questions of antiquity, authenticity and artistic rank are either contested or unanswerable from the evidence."** To reach it I would have had to reject five tempting alternatives. A sentence about **antiquity**, because the earliest space carries a contested label, the foundational treatise carries a four-century bracket, and three of the topic's oldest traditions are recorded with *perhaps*, *believed* and *around*. A sentence about **continuity**, because the record shows contraction, transformation, colonial contact, reconstruction and present pressure rather than an unbroken line. A sentence about **integration of the arts**, because the useful claim is far more specific - a regionally dominant form supplies the design, movement and style of neighbouring forms across mechanisms, evidenced nine times. A sentence ranking **classical above folk**, because the record's contrast is sociological and its own folk classification contains a court-origin tradition and a colonial-contact Christian form. And a sentence about **dying traditions**, because the record describes three different situations in three different tenses with different causes.

**Misconception to avoid:** Thinking that the six-question frame is a substitute for the facts. It is the opposite: it is a way of organising a very large number of specific, named, dated facts so that the right one can be retrieved under examination pressure. A frame with nothing in it scores as poorly as a list with no frame.

# ORIGINAL 10-, 15- AND 20-MARK MAINS MODEL PRACTICE

Every question below is **original practice written for this session**. None reproduces or closely paraphrases any reproduced past question, and the two directive shapes used by the reproduced Main Examination items - *write about* a single named form, and *where did these originate* - are deliberately not reused. Word ceilings are stated and observed.

## Ten marks

### Original 1

**"A prescriptive treatise tells you what a tradition required, not what it performed." Examine this statement with reference to classical Sanskrit theatre. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model

The statement is sound, and the record proves it twice over.

The treatise supplies a requirement set: four to seven acts; a closure in which the hero wins or does not die; a male protagonist who attains his desire; a shape of opening, progression, development, pause and conclusion; a ritual opening led by a white-clad stage manager who prays and announces time and place; a house of around four hundred persons, two-storeyed for celestial and terrestrial spheres; curtains; no masks.

Two internal signals mark these as requirements: the capacity and storeys are attributed to Bharata, and the absolute that plays always ended happily is at once qualified by tragedy being *almost* rare.

The limit: a prescription is still evidence of what a tradition codified; a scheme naming ten play-types, one built on loss, was not naive.

Verdict: read the treatise as a code, never as a survey.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the requirement set reproduced with its exact numbers 3 + the two internal signals of prescription, the attribution and the hedge 3 + the counter-limit that a prescription is still evidence of what was codified 2 + a verdict that gives the correct citation practice 2.**

### Original 2

**Explain why Indian puppetry is classified by mechanism, and show what that classification predicts. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model

Mechanism is the only variable that predicts the rest.

Each category carries a construction and control description: string figures of eight to nine inches in wood, with strings through holes in hands, head and back; shadow figures cut flat from leather, painted identically on both sides against a lit white screen; glove figures of head and arms in a long skirt, worn on the hand, index finger at the head; rod figures, larger variations of glove puppets, moved by rods behind a screen.

These predict expressive possibility: only a shadow image, lacking absolute scale, can make size encode status - large for kings and deities, smaller for commoners; only a worn figure gives fast finger-driven gesture.

Region predicts none of this: one eastern state carries string, shadow and rod alike.

Qualification: the record says *broadly* classified - rod derives from glove, and one Tamil Nadu tradition mixes rod and string.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = all four mechanisms with their construction and control 4 + two predictive consequences demonstrated from the mechanism itself 3 + the region counter-example 1 + the derivation-and-hybrid qualification reproduced with the record's own hedge 2.**

### Original 3

**Assess the role of the narrator who stands outside the action in Indian performance traditions. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model

The outside narrator solves the problem of a stage without scenery, and recurs across the field.

Its classical form is the stage manager who enters in white, prays, summons the leading lady and announces time and place - the device by which an unscened stage acquires a setting. Folk forms multiply the figure: an Assamese narrator with a named musician group on *khol* and cymbals; another singing verses from holy texts; a main narrator with a chorus; an Odia chief singer with a co-narrator; a Karnataka narrator aided by a group; an Andhra main artist with two co-artists giving rhythm and chorus.

Its extreme case is a Karnataka and Kerala tradition performed seated, without costume, dance or acting, narration being the drama.

In puppetry the figure returns as the manipulator-narrator, the holder of strings.

Assessment: the narrator supplies setting, continuity, scripture and comment - functions a scened stage distributes elsewhere.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the classical instance with its four recorded actions 2 + at least five folk instances named with their exact narrator vocabulary 4 + the extreme seated case identified as the limiting form 2 + the puppetry return and a four-function assessment 2.**

## Fifteen marks

### Original 4

**"Occasion, not state, is the organising principle of Indian folk theatre." Discuss. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The claim is largely right and its strength lies in what it prevents.

Occasion is recorded specifically and repeatedly. A north Indian epic enactment happens mainly before Dussehra. A Garhwal ritual theatre is dedicated to a named local deity and performed with a sacred mask by a named caste. A Himachali form is performed when agricultural prosperity is achieved or wishes fulfilled - a votive trigger, not a calendar date. An Assamese narrative form belongs to the serpent-goddess festival. A Goan form is performed during Holi, celebrated regionally as the spring festival. A Maharashtra form belongs to the rice-cultivating tract at harvest. A Kerala dance-drama runs eight days on eight consecutive nights at temples. A Kerala shadow tradition performs overnight at temples of a named goddess. Occasion is the material basis of the form: it supplies audience, calendar and funding.

State, by contrast, is recorded unreliably. Several forms carry two attributions - a Karnataka tradition also in parts of Kerala, a seated narration form in coastal Karnataka and Kerala, a bow-song form in Kerala and parts of Tamil Nadu, a Konkan drama across a modern border, a rod-puppet tradition in three states, a Tamil street theatre extending to Tamil-speaking Sri Lanka - and one entry carries an internal discrepancy between its table attribution and its own description.

The qualification: occasion is not recorded for every form, and where it is absent, carrier and means take over as identifiers.

Verdict: read by occasion first, carrier second, means third and state last.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = eight distinct occasion instances, each named with its trigger 6 + the material argument that occasion supplies audience, calendar and funding 3 + six dual or multiple attributions plus the recorded internal discrepancy 4 + the qualification that occasion is not universal 1 + a ranked reading order as the verdict 1.**

### Original 5

**Examine the proposition that Indian folk theatre cannot be reduced to a single type. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The proposition holds, and the record refutes the single-type assumption on four separate axes.

**Origin.** Most folk theatres began in the fifteenth and sixteenth centuries with devotional themes. But one Karnataka tradition originated in the royal courts of an empire and was performed by a named community; a Kerala maritime form's origin is traced to the advent of the Portuguese; and a Maharashtra ballad tradition arose from a play lauding a ruler's deeds after a specific killing.

**Religion.** The field is predominantly Hindu in repertoire, yet contains a Christian art form with biblical themes and legendary Christian warriors, a Kashmiri form described as secular in outlook although performed by Muslims, and a mimicry tradition performed by a named Muslim community.

**Gender convention.** Three contrary conventions coexist: a north Indian epic enactment performed by male actors who also perform the heroine; a Manipuri form in which women's roles are played by men under a specific name; and a Maharashtra form whose unique feature is female actors who even play male roles.

**Economy.** One Maharashtra form is recorded as a blend of commercial and folk theatre, breaking the assumption that commerce belongs only to the urban stage.

The qualification: these are exceptions within a real category, not proof that the category fails. Rural siting, community transmission, occasion-bound performance and regional idiom describe the great majority.

Verdict: state the general pattern, name the counter-instances precisely, and do not smooth them away.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = four named axes of variation 4 + at least two evidenced counter-instances on each axis 6 + the qualification that the category still does real work 3 + a verdict that instructs how to write the pattern and the exceptions together 2.**

### Original 6

**"Modern Indian theatre is best understood as a sequence of answers to the question of who sustains a performance." Discuss. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The framing orders a crowded century without reducing it to a list of names.

**The market answers first.** The first Bengali professional theatre company was cofounded in 1872 by a figure largely responsible for the golden age of Bengali theatre; theatres had become commercial, and within that structure the first woman in India to become a notable stage performer from the Bengal Presidency emerged in the 1870s, contributing to a theatre house founded in 1883.

**Reputation answers next.** A celebrated playwright's repertoire, begun at twenty, carried themes of nationalism, spirituality and the socio-political situation.

**A party answers in 1943.** A theatre association formed as the cultural wing of a political party wrote on social themes including the Bengal famines, was disbanded in 1947 and now exists in three named states.

**Mobility answers in 1944.** A moving theatre of a hundred and fifty artists held more than two thousand plays before acquiring a permanent house in 1978.

**The state answers in 1952**, with a national academy for promoting the performing arts including theatre, and a national drama school producing practitioners.

**The region answers in 1969 and 1976**, with two Manipuri institutions founded to keep traditional theatre alive.

**Minimalism changes the question**, through a movement of small, experimental, socially conscious plays with minimalist staging and direct actor-audience interaction, making theatre accessible to all.

The qualification: these coexist rather than succeed one another, and one founder belongs to two of them.

Verdict: the sequence explains the century; it does not date anyone's disappearance.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = seven distinct answers, each with its date and defining feature 7 + the reading of the last as changing rather than answering the question 3 + the coexistence qualification with the overlapping-founder evidence 3 + a verdict that resists a replacement narrative 2.**

## Twenty marks

### Original 7

**"In Indian performance, the community that carries a tradition is more informative than the region in which it is found." Critically examine with reference to theatre and puppetry. (20 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The claim is strong for identification and weaker for explanation, and the distinction is worth drawing.

**For identification it is decisively right.** The record names carriers with precision and rarely repeats one: a Sanskrit-adhering Kerala theatre held as the privilege of two named castes; a Garhwal ritual theatre whose sacred Narasimha mask is worn by a named caste; a Gujarat picture-narration form of a named Brahmin community; a mimicry tradition of a named Muslim community; a Karnataka court-origin theatre performed by a named community; two pastoral communities of the Deccan carrying their caste gods; a Konkan drama performed by farmers; and, in puppetry, four of five shadow traditions with named communities in Kerala, Maharashtra, Karnataka and Odisha. Regions, by contrast, are frequently doubled and never unique: one eastern state alone carries string, shadow and rod traditions whose names differ by a single prefix.

**For explanation the claim needs qualification.** Region explains what the carrier cannot: why a Karnataka puppet tradition's figures are styled on a Karnataka theatre's characters, why a Kerala glove tradition is strongly influenced by a Kerala dance-drama, why an Odisha puppet tradition's movement shows a marked influence of an Odisha dance, and why a coastal Kerala form bears European elements.

**And both are silent on mechanism**, which alone predicts construction, scale, control and expressive possibility.

Verdict: carrier identifies, region explains the neighbourhood, mechanism explains the object - and a complete answer uses all three rather than ranking two.

**Unique scoring note:** **20 marks = eight named carriers across theatre and puppetry 6 + the region-doubling counter-evidence with the single-state triple 3 + four cross-form influences shown to be regional rather than communal 5 + the third axis introduced to show both claims incomplete 4 + a three-part verdict that assigns each variable a distinct function 2.**

### Original 8

**Critically examine the claim that recognition, documentation and training are sufficient responses to the condition of India's traditional performance forms. (20 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

They are necessary and jointly insufficient, and the record shows where each stops.

**Recognition** has three instruments here. An international list carries four forms taught in this session, inscribed in 2008, 2009 and twice in 2010. A national academy created in 1952 confers dated awards, including one in 2004 for a rod-puppet tradition and one in 2020 for puppetry revival through a named organisation. And a colonial statute of 1876, imposed to curb political themes, shows that official attention can restrict as readily as honour. Recognition confers standing; it does not create income or attention.

**Documentation** suits a completed contraction. Court Sanskrit theatre declined for three recorded reasons and survives as texts, theory and one living lineage; archives are proportionate there.

**Training** reproduces practitioners: a national drama school produced great theatre personalities, and two Manipuri institutions of 1969 and 1976 were founded to keep traditional theatre alive.

**Where all three stop** is stated plainly. Puppetry is *currently* on decline because of lack of funding and captive audience. Neither an inscription, nor a single award, nor a school addresses an audience shortage; and the four puppet mechanisms differ in construction, scale, skill and carrier, so one revival case cannot be generalised across them.

Two further qualifications: several traditions are transforming rather than declining, from warrior practice to performing art or pre-battle ritual to wedding ceremony; and antiquity claims here are frequently hedged in the sources.

Verdict: the three responses secure standing, record and skill; only funded, attended performance secures the form.

**Unique scoring note:** **20 marks = three recognition instruments with dated instances, including the restrictive one 5 + documentation matched to the completed contraction and training matched to skill reproduction 4 + the recorded deficit quoted as the test of sufficiency 4 + the non-generalisation caution across four mechanisms 3 + two further qualifications on transformation and hedged antiquity 2 + a verdict distinguishing standing, record and skill from sustenance 2.**

# REMEDIATION

## The twelve errors that cost most, and the correction for each

| # | Error | Correction |
|---:|---|---|
| 1 | "Indian theatre did not use masks." | The **classical Sanskrit staging system as recorded** used no masks. Masks appear in an Assamese one-act form for special expressions, in a Garhwal ritual theatre as a sacred Narasimha mask worn by a named caste, in a Manipuri puppet tradition for demon characters, and as a recognised mask-making craft attached to Chhau |
| 2 | "Sanskrit theatre died out." | It **contracted** for three recorded reasons. A Kerala lineage completely adhering to the treatise's rules has **survived since the 10th century AD** |
| 3 | "The *Natya Shastra* describes ten types of plays." | It **names ten and describes two** - *Nataka* and *Prakarana* |
| 4 | "Sitabena is the world's oldest amphitheatre." | It is **often known as** such; the label is **disputed** and must be reproduced with its hedge |
| 5 | "Folk theatre is a simplified form of classical theatre." | The contrast recorded is **sociological** - urban and sophisticated against rural and rustic. Most of the folk field begins on its own clock in the **15th-16th century**, and the classification contains a court-origin tradition and a colonial-contact Christian form |
| 6 | Reversing *Lokadharmi* and *Natyadharmi*. | ***Lokadharmi*** = **realistic depictions of daily life**; ***Natyadharmi*** = **conventional, more stylised narration with overt symbolism** |
| 7 | Treating the four puppetry categories as four independent types. | The record says **broadly** classified. **Rod puppets are larger variations of glove puppets**, and **Bommalattam mixes rod and string** |
| 8 | Confusing *Gombeyatta* with *Togalu Gombeyatta*, or *Kundhei* with *Kathi Kundhei Nacha*, or *Pavakoothu* with *Tolpava Kuthu*. | Read the **qualifier**: ***Togalu*** marks leather and shadow; ***Kathi*** means the **wooden rod**; ***Tol*** marks the leather form. The qualifier carries the information |
| 9 | "Traditional arts are dying." | Name the **form**, the **tense**, the **cause** and the **response**. Puppetry is recorded as **currently** in decline for **lack of funding and captive audience**; the court Sanskrit form underwent a **completed historical contraction**; several martial traditions are recorded as **transforming**, not declining |
| 10 | Reading an international inscription as a statement about antiquity or classical status. | An inscription records that a state party nominated an element and a committee inscribed it in a **stated year**. Totals, current positions and list administration belong to the heritage owner |
| 11 | Inventing an origin date, a founder or a performer honour. | This session records **five** dated or named honours from the core record and **no others**, and it names **no recipient** of the current award announcement. Where no date is recorded, none is supplied |
| 12 | Forcing every form into one state. | Reproduce the record's own **dual and multiple attributions**, including the one **internal discrepancy** it contains and the one **transnational** attribution |

## Drill set A - one-line discriminators

Give the single feature that separates each pair. Answers follow each line.

1. **Ankia Naat and Bhaona.** - The second is a **presentation of** the first.
2. **Daskathia and Chaiti Ghoda.** - **Two narrators and one instrument** against **three narrators and two instruments**.
3. **Ojhapali and Daskathia.** - ***Ojha* and *Palis*** against ***Gayaka* and *Palia***; a serpent-goddess festival against a Shiva theme.
4. **Naqal/Bhand and Bhand Pather.** - **Mimicry with a *behrupiya*** against **contemporary social satire, secular in outlook**.
5. **Swang and Nautanki.** - The second is an **offshoot of** the first, and carries the ***Nagada*** and the *Ain-i-Akbari* mention.
6. **Burra Katha and Oggukatha.** - **Percussion instrument plus two co-artists** against **a small hand drum associated with Shiva, pastoral communities and caste gods**.
7. **Tal-Maddale and Yakshagana.** - The first is the **predecessor**, performed **seated without costume, dance or acting**.
8. **Bayalata and Yakshagana.** - The first **is a form of** the second, performed **open-air during local-deity worship**.
9. **Theyyam and Bhuta Kola.** - Recorded as **similar practices** in **Kerala** and **Karnataka** respectively.
10. **Krishnanattam and Chavittu Natakam.** - **Mid-17th century, *Krishna Geethi*, eight nights at temples** against **maritime, Christian, Portuguese advent, biblical themes**.
11. **Kathputli and Kundhei.** - **No legs, finger-tied strings** against **more joints, triangular prop, Odissi influence**.
12. **Gombeyatta and Togalu Gombeyatta.** - **String, Yakshagana-styled, multiple puppeteers** against **shadow, size-coded status, Killekyata community**.
13. **Ravanchhaya and Tholu Bommalata.** - **Deer-skin, no joints, non-human figures, Bhat community** against **classical music background, larger, coloured on both sides**.
14. **Yampuri and Putul Nachh.** - **No joints, single piece of wood** against **3-4 ft, neck and shoulder joints, waist rods, three instruments**.
15. **Pavakoothu and Tolpava Kuthu.** - **Glove, 18th century, Kathakali-influenced** against **shadow, *koothumadam*, oil lamps, overnight, Nair community**.
16. **Thang-ta and Sarit Sarak.** - **Armed** against **unarmed**; together ***Huyen Langlon***.
17. **Pari-khanda and Chholia.** - **Steps used in Chhau dance** against **pre-battle ritual that became a wedding performance**.
18. **Kalaripayattu and Silambam.** - **Generally not accompanied by music, footwork key, *Kalari* training hall** against **staff fencing, *Silappadikaram* trade reference, animal-named strokes**.

## Drill set B - carrier and community

Name the community or carrier the record attaches to each.

1. Kutiyattam - **Chakyar and Nambiar castes**.
2. Ramman's sacred mask - **Bhandari caste**.
3. Naqal and Bhand - **Muslim Bhand community**.
4. Garoda - the ***Garoda* Brahmin community**.
5. Yakshagana - ***Jakkula Varu***.
6. Bhuta Kola - mainly the **Tulu-speaking population** of coastal Karnataka.
7. Oggukatha - ***Kuruma*** and ***Golla* (Yadavs)** of the Deccan plateau.
8. Powada - ***Gondhalis*** and ***Shahirs***.
9. Dashavatar - **the farmers of the Konkan region**.
10. Nacha - **the rural communities** of Chhattisgarh.
11. Tolpava Kuthu - the **Nair community**.
12. Chamadyacha Bahulya - the **Thakar tribal community**.
13. Togalu Gombeyatta - the **Killekyata community**.
14. Ravanchhaya - the **Bhat community**.
15. Shumang Leela's women's roles - men called ***Nupi Shabis***.
16. Thang-ta - the **Meitei people** of Manipur.
17. Pari-khanda - **created by the Rajputs**.
18. Paika Akhada - the ***Paikas***, warrior communities of Odisha.
19. Adimurai - the **Nadar** community, and some **Ezhavas and Nairs** of South Kerala.
20. Gatka - the **Sikhs of Punjab**.

## Drill set C - chronology, stated safely

Give the date or bracket exactly as recorded, with its hedge where one exists.

1. *Natya Shastra* - **200 BC - 200 AD**.
2. Kutiyattam's survival - **since the 10th century AD**.
3. Most folk theatres began - **15th-16th century**.
4. Ankia Naat - **15th-16th century AD**; Bhaona - **early 16th century**.
5. Krishnanattam - **mid-17th century**.
6. Kuruvanji - **originated around 300 years ago**.
7. Tolpava Kuthu - **believed to have originated in the 9th or 10th century AD**.
8. Pavakoothu - **began in the 18th century**.
9. Laithibi Jagoi - **dates back to the 19th century**.
10. The Calcutta Theatre / New Playhouse - **founded 1775, active until 1808**.
11. Great National Theatre - **1872**; Star Theatre, Kolkata - **1883**.
12. Dramatic Performances Act - **1876**; Gaiety theatre, Shimla - **since 1887**.
13. Parsi theatre - **1850s-1920s**; into film-making **from the 1930s**.
14. IPTA - **1943**, disbanded **1947**; Prithvi Theatre - **1944**, permanent house **1978**.
15. Sangeet Natak Akademi - created by resolution **1952**, inaugurated **28 January 1953**; National School of Drama under it **1959**, autonomous **1975**.
16. Kalakshetra Manipur - **1969**; Chorus Repertory Theatre - **1976**.
17. Puppetry awards - **2004** and **2020**.
18. Inscriptions attaching to this topic's forms - **2008**, **2009**, **2010**, **2010**.
19. Martial honours recorded - **Padma Shri 2009** and **Padma Shri 2017**; a state academy announced **2021**.
20. Martial evolution brackets - **1500-600 BCE**, **600 BCE-400 CE**, **200 BCE-1200 CE**, **1200-1800 CE**, **late 19th century-present**.

## Drill set D - the "do not supply" list

Ten things a candidate may expect this session to contain, which it deliberately does not.

1. **No answer letter, key or option elimination** for any reproduced question.
2. **No recipient name** from the current award announcement of 2024 and 2025.
3. **No running total** of Indian intangible-heritage inscriptions, and no current list position.
4. **No list of ancient Sanskrit dramas or their authors**, which the core record itself routes to the literature owner - beyond the single attribution required by one owned item.
5. **No circus history and no indigenous-sports table**, which sit in the same chapter but outside this topic's ownership.
6. **No sport-policy material** - federations, state-sport declarations, ministry recognitions or national games programmes - beyond the bare mentions the record attaches to a tradition's own description.
7. **No dance grammar**: no *nritta*, *nritya*, *natya*, *abhinaya*, nine *rasas*, *hasta* or *mudra* teaching, and no adjudication of whether a named dance-drama is a dance or a theatre.
8. **No raga, tala or classical-system teaching**; instruments and song genres appear only as identifiers.
9. **No origin date, founder or honour** for any form or person where the record supplies none.
10. **No claim that any tradition is unchanged, pure, authentic or superior** to any other.

# COMPLETE CONSOLIDATED REGISTER NOTES

## Evidence base and dating control

1. **Sitabena and Jogimara caves** - **often known as the world's oldest amphitheatres**; cited as showing India's ancient theatre tradition. ⚠️ The label is **disputed**; reproduce the hedge.
2. ***Natya Shastra***, **Bharat Muni**, **200 BC - 200 AD** - the **first treatise on dramaturgy**. Write the **bracket**, never a single year.
3. It records that **Brahma created the *Natya Veda* from the four Vedas** and **details ten types of Sanskrit plays**.
4. Theatre in India **began as a narrative art form blending music, dance and acting**.
5. Four words for drama: ***nataka*** (from ***nata***, dancer), ***rupaka***, ***drishyakavya*** (poetry that is seen), ***preksakavya*** (poetry for spectators).
6. **Three clocks**: **text** · **practice** · **institution**. Never merged.
7. A **prescription is not a survey**. Where the record attributes a feature to Bharata, keep the attribution.

## The codified Sanskrit system

1. **Two modes**: ***Lokadharmi*** = **realistic depictions of daily life**; ***Natyadharmi*** = **conventional plays, more stylised narration, overt symbolism**.
2. **Ten play-types**: **Nataka, Prakarana, Bhana, Prahasana, Dima, Vyayoga, Samavakara, Vithi, Ihamrga, Utsrishtikanka (Anka)**. ⚠️ Corrupted forms in circulation: *Ihamgra*, *Vyayog*, *Svakarna*, bare *Anka*.
3. The treatise **describes only *Nataka* and *Prakarana***.
4. **Conventions**: generally **four to seven acts**; **always happy endings - unlike the Greek tragedies - where the hero wins or does not die**, with **portrayal of tragedy almost rare**; a **male protagonist** who **always achieves the object of his desire**; and a defined **opening, progression, development, pause and conclusion**.
5. The ending convention constrains **closure**, not content - one play-type is built on **loss or abandonment**.
6. **Ritual progression**: ***Purva-raga*** behind the curtain → ***Sutradhar*** in **white** with **assistants** → **prayers** and **blessings** → **leading lady summoned** → **time and place announced**.
7. **Stage as per Bharata**: **around 400 persons**; **two-storeyed**, **upper celestial**, **lower terrestrial**; **curtains used to intensify impact**; **masks not used**.
8. **Characters, three kinds**: ***Nayaka*** (males; **Lalita** kind-hearted, **Shanta** calm and composed, **Uddhata** agitated or arrogant; may be a ***Pratinayaka*** such as **Ravana** or **Duryodhana**); ***Nayika*** (females; **queens, friends, courtesans (*Ganika*), divine lady (*Divya*)**); ***Vidusaka*** (comic, **noble and good-hearted**, **often a friend of the hero**, **questions prevailing social norms through satire**).
9. **Register rule**: the ***Vidusaka* spoke in Prakrit while others spoke in Sanskrit** - social position marked by language on stage.
10. The Sanskrit play became an **amalgamation of entertainment and religious traditions**. Its list of ancient dramas is routed to the **literature owner**.

## Contraction and the surviving lineage

1. **Three recorded causes**: writers **moved from plays to poems and songs**; **strict rules and difficult Sanskrit** reduced popularity while **Pali and Prakrit became common**; under **Muslim rulers**, **dance and music were patronised more than theatre**.
2. The causes are of **three types** - **production**, **reception**, **patronage** - and are **not mutually exclusive**.
3. ⚠️ Sanskrit theatre is **not extinct**.
4. **Kutiyattam / Koodiyattam** - **India's oldest continuing form of theatre**; a **living tradition since the 10th century AD**; **Kerala**.
5. **Completely adheres** to the rules of the ***Natya Shastra***; the traditional **privilege of the Chakyar and Nambiar castes**.
6. Performed in **Sanskrit, Prakrit and Malayalam**, with ***Mizhavu*** and ***Edakka***.
7. All characters begin with ***Nirvahana***, a **recollection of past events**; the story then unfolds **leisurely**, with **commentaries on social, philosophical and political behaviour**.
8. Leading exponent: **Margi Madhu Chakyar**. 📰 Inscribed **2008**.
9. Survival mechanism: **text-adhering repertoire + caste-held transmission + regional base**, not court patronage.
10. Kerala neighbours to keep apart: **Krishnanattam**, **Chavittu Natakam**, **Theyyam**, **Mudiyettu**.

## The folk field - definition and arc

1. Folk theatre **reflects local lifestyle, social norms, beliefs and customs**.
2. **Unlike the sophisticated urban Sanskrit theatre**, it has **rural roots and a rustic style** - a **sociological** contrast, not a ranking.
3. **Most folk theatres began in the 15th-16th century with devotional themes**, **later added love stories and local heroes**, and **post-Independence spread social lessons as well as entertainment**.
4. Read every form on four axes: **occasion · carrier · means · theme**.
5. Recurring devices: the **outside narrator**, the **mask**, the **named instrument**, the **painted picture**, **impersonation**, the **gendered convention**, the **dialect or purpose-built language**.
6. Gender conventions run in **three contrary directions** within this one field.
7. The category contains a **court-origin** tradition and a **colonial-contact Christian** form, and one entry recorded as a **blend of commercial and folk theatre**.

## The folk field - east and north-east

1. **Ankia Naat** (Assam) - **one-act play**; **Sankardev and Madhavdev**, **15th-16th century AD**; **opera style**; **life of Lord Krishna**; ***Sutradhar*** with the ***Gayan-Bayan Mandali*** playing ***khol*** and **cymbals**; **masks for special expressions**; written in ***Brajavali***, an **Assamese-Maithili mixed language**.
2. **Ojhapali** (Assam) - narrative theatre of the **Manasha / Serpent Goddess** festival; ***Baniya Khanda, Bhatiyali Khanda, Deva Khanda***; ***Ojha*** narrator, ***Palis*** chorus.
3. **Bhaona** (Assam) - a **presentation of Ankia Naat**; **Sankardeva, early 16th century**; **Vaishnavite themes**; *Sutradhar* **narrates and sings verses from holy texts**.
4. **Jatra** (WB / eastern India) - **open-air**; initiated by **Chaitanya Mahaprabhu** to propagate **Krishna's teachings**; later ***Rama Jatra, Shiv Jatra, Chandi Jatra***; modern **secular, historical, patriotic** themes. ***Sahi Jatra*** is Odisha's street-theatre form.
5. **Daskathia** (Odisha) - ***Gayaka*** chief singer and ***Palia*** co-narrator; ***kathia***; **Lord Shiva**. Variant ***Chaiti Ghoda*** - ***dhol*** and ***mohuri***, **three narrators**.
6. **Thok Lila** (Manipur) - **satire, wit, comedy**.
7. **Shumang Leela** (Manipur) - **play of the open courtyard**; women's roles by men called ***Nupi Shabis***; promotes **humanism, brotherhood, tolerance, confidence, devotion, truth and justice**.
8. Regional threads: **Vaishnava propagation** and the **named narrator with a chorus**.

## The folk field - north and west

1. **Ramlila** (UP) - **Ramayana** in songs, dances and dialogues, **mainly before Dussehra**; **male actors**, who also perform **Sita**.
2. **Naqal / Bhand** (Punjab / UP) - **mimicry**; ***behrupiya*** or ***naqalchi***; **social or political message** through comedy, absurdities, wit and humour; **Muslim Bhand community**.
3. **Nautanki** (North India) - **offshoot of Swang**; mentioned in **Abul Fazl's *Ain-i-Akbari***; historical, social and folk tales; **lyrical dialogue**; drum ***Nagada***; later schools around **Kanpur** and **Lucknow**.
4. **Bhand Pather** (J&K) - **contemporary social satire** or **mythical stories**; **secular in outlook**, performed by **Muslims**; music, dance and drama.
5. **Kariyala** (HP) - local deity ***Bijeshwar***; **Solan, Shimla, Sirmour**; performed on **agricultural prosperity** or **fulfilled personal wishes**.
6. **Ramman** (Uttarakhand) - **Garhwal**; ***Bhumiyal Devta***; on the **UNESCO Representative List** (📰 **2009**); **Bhandari caste** wears the **sacred Narasimha mask**; stories of Rama narrated.
7. **Swang / Saang** - table: **Rajasthan / MP-Malwar**; text: popular in **Rajasthan and Haryana**; **musical dramas sung through verses**; ***ektara, harmonium, sarangi, dholak, khartal***; **mimicry with dialogue**. ⚠️ Preserve both attributions.
8. **Khyal** (Rajasthan) - folk theatre of **storytelling, music and dance** at **festivals and fairs**. ⚠️ Not the Hindustani vocal genre.
9. **Maach** (MP) - **Malwa**; mythological first, **romantic folk tales** later; **couplets - *Rangat Dohas***.
10. **Raslila** (Gujarat) - **dance drama** of the **adolescent love stories of Krishna and Radha**. **Garoda** (Gujarat) - **Garoda Brahmin** community; **painted pictures**; romance and valour. **Bhavai** (Gujarat) - **series of small plays**; romantic; **earthen pots or brass pitchers balanced**; **semi-classical music in folk style** with ***Jhanjhar*** and ***dholak***; ***Sutradhar* known as *Nayaka***.

## The folk field - Deccan and west coast

1. **Tamasha** (Maharashtra) - **humour and erotic content**; **female actors who even play male roles**; ***Lavani*** songs.
2. **Powada** (Maharashtra) - written after **Shivaji killed Afzal Khan**; **operatic ballads of valour**; ***Gondhalis*** and ***Shahirs***.
3. **Zadipatti** (Maharashtra) - ***zadi*** = rice; **rice-cultivating region**, **harvest season**; **blend of commercial and folk theatre**.
4. **Dashavatar** (Maharashtra / Goa) - **farmers of the Konkan**, notably **Sindhudurg** and **North Goa**; **ten incarnations of Vishnu**; ***poorva-ranga*** and ***uttara-ranga***, the **second the main act**.
5. **Ranmale** (Goa) - **ritualistic and folk**; Ramayana and Mahabharata; during **Holi, celebrated as *Shigmo***.
6. **Tiatr** (Goa) - **Roman Konkani dialects**; **social, religious and political themes**; **'Mirror of Goan Culture'**.
7. **Nacha** (Chhattisgarh) - **rural communities**; ***Gandawa Nacha, Khare Saaj Nacha, Dewar Nacha, Baithe Saaj Nacha***; **night**; **comedy essential**.
8. **Pagati Veshaalu** (Telangana / Krishna district, AP) - **role-playing act** around a principal ***vesham***.
9. **Burra Katha** (AP) - from ***Burra***; **main artist/narrator + two co-artists** for **rhythm and chorus**.
10. **Oggukatha** (AP) - from ***Oggu***, a **small hand drum associated with Shiva**; **Kuruma** and **Golla (Yadavs)** of the **Deccan plateau**; **itinerant**; **caste gods**.

## The folk field - south, and the Yakshagana cluster

1. **Tal-Maddale** (Karnataka / Kerala) - ***Tal*** cymbal, ***Maddale*** drum; **predecessor of Yakshagana**; **seated, without costume, dance or acting**; ***Bhagavata*** with ***Arthadharis***.
2. **Yakshagana** (Karnataka + parts of Kerala) - **perhaps one of the oldest**, **prevalent till date**; **royal courts of the Vijayanagara empire**; community ***Jakkula Varu***; **originally a single-artist descriptive dance-drama**; **Vaishnava Bhakti** influence.
3. **Bayalata** (Karnataka) - **open-air**; **worship of the local deity**; **Radha-Krishna**; **a form of Yakshagana**.
4. Yakshagana's reach: **Gombeyatta** puppets styled on its characters; **Bhuta Kola** influenced by it.
5. **Bhuta Aradhana / Bhuta Kola** (Karnataka) - ***Bhuta*** = **spirit**; **ancestors, avatars of Hindu gods, local folklore heroes**; **Tulu-speaking** population; **coastal Karnataka**; ***Kola*** the chief form; **ritualistic theatre**.
6. **Chavittu Natakam** (Kerala) - **maritime**, **Christian**; **European + Kerala** elements; **advent of the Portuguese**; **Bible** and **legendary Christian warriors**.
7. **Krishnanattam** (Kerala) - **mid-17th century**; ***Krishna Geethi***; **eight days, eight consecutive nights at temples**.
8. **Theyyam** (Kerala / Karnataka) - **open theatre** before **local temples**; **spirit of the ancestors** besides gods; **Vaishnavism, Shaktism, Shaivism now common**; **elaborate headgear**; similar to **Bhuta Kola**. **Mudiyettu** (Kerala) - ritual theatre and dance drama; 📰 **2010**.
9. **Villu Paatu / Villadi Champaatu** (Kerala / TN) - **bow-song**; **Ramayana**; **bow-shaped instruments**.
10. **Bhagavata Mela** (TN) - villages; ***Bhagavata Purana***; religious festivals; **stylized acting**. **Kuruvanji** (TN) - **around 300 years ago**; **Tirikooda Rasappa Kavirayar**; **'fortune-teller'**; **dance ballet with Bharatanatyam**. **Therukoothu** (TN + **Tamil-speaking Sri Lanka**) - **street theatre**; **Mahabharata**, sometimes Ramayana; **'clever stage tricks'**.

## The modern stage

1. Grew during the **colonial era**, influenced by **Sanskrit *and* Western texts**; **Calcutta and Madras** led to **proscenium-style** theatre; a style **blended with Indian and Western elements**.
2. **Theatres became commercial**; plays addressed **dowry, caste, religious hypocrisy and politics**.
3. **Dramatic Performances Act, 1876** - **imposed to curb political themes**. Legislation is **evidence of political consequence**.
4. **The Calcutta Theatre / The New Playhouse** - **1775 to 1808**. **Hira Lala Sen's** first motion pictures in Bengal at **The Star, Minerva and Classic** theatres. **Gaiety Theatre, Madras** - first **permanent cinema theatre**, **Raghupathy Venkaiah**. **Gaiety theatre, Shimla - since 1887**.
5. **Parsi theatres** - **western India, 1850s-1920s**; **Gujarati and Marathi**; **colourful backdrops and music essential**; **romance, humour, melodrama**; **from the 1930s** into **film-making**.
6. **Girish Chandra Ghosh (1844-1912)** - **golden age of Bengali theatre**; cofounded the **Great National Theatre**, the **first Bengali professional theatre company, 1872**; **nearly 40 plays**.
7. **Binodini Dasi (1863-1941)**, ***Notee Binodini*** - **prima donna**; **first woman in India to become a notable stage performer from the Bengal Presidency in the 1870s**; career from **12 to 23**; ***Amar Katha*** **published 1913**; contributed to founding the **Star Theatre, Kolkata, 1883**.
8. **Tagore** - first play at **20**, ***Valmiki Pratibha***; ***Raktakarabi*, *Chitrangada*, *Post-Office***; **nationalism, spirituality, socio-political situation**. **Dinabandhu Mitra (*Nildarpan*)**; **Prasanna Kumar Thakur**.
9. **IPTA 1943** - cultural wing of the **Communist Party**; **disbanded 1947**; **Bengal famines**; **Balraj Sahni, Prithviraj Kapoor, Bijon Bhattacharya, Ritwik Ghatak, Utpal Dutt**; **now in Chhattisgarh, Punjab and West Bengal**. **Prithvi Theatre 1944** - **moving**, **150 artists**, **2000+ plays**; **permanent 1978**.
10. **SNA** created by resolution **1952**, inaugurated **28 January 1953**; **NSD** (under the academy **1959**, autonomous **1975**); **Kalakshetra Manipur 1969**, **Heisnam Kanhailal**; **Chorus Repertory Theatre 1976**, **Ratan Thiyam**; **Badal Sircar (1925-2011)** and the **Third Theatre** - small, experimental, socially conscious; **minimalist staging**, **direct actor-audience interaction**, **accessible to all**. **K.V. Subbanna** - **NINASAM**, **Ramon Magsaysay**. ***Ghashiram Kotwal*** by **Vijay Tendulkar**.

## Puppetry - framing and mechanism

1. Puppetry captivates **through the master's control over puppets**; **low cost**; **creative freedom in design, colour and movement**.
2. Indian history **evidenced by the Harappa and Mohenjo-daro periods**; references in the **Mahabharata**, the Tamil ***Silappadikaram***, and **marionette theatre from 500 BC**.
3. **Philosophical side**: the narrator is the ***Sutradhar***, **'holder of strings'**; in the ***Bhagavad Gita*, God is described as a puppeteer**.
4. It **incorporates dance, music, theatre, sculpture and painting** - a strength and a vulnerability.
5. **Four categories, broadly**: **string, shadow, glove, rod**. ⚠️ **Rod derives from glove**; **Bommalattam mixes rod and string**.
6. **String baseline**: **8-9 inch** wooden figures; **oil colours**; **wooden-pipe limbs**; **miniature jewellery**; **strings through holes in hands, head and back**.
7. **Shadow baseline**: **flat leather cut-outs**; **painted identically on both sides**; **white screen with light behind**.
8. **Glove baseline**: **head and arms in a long skirt**, cloth or wood; **index finger head**, **thumb and middle finger hands**; **popular all across India**; **drum or *dholak***.
9. **Rod baseline**: **larger variations of glove puppets**; **rods from behind a screen**; **mainly eastern India**.
10. **Currently on decline because of lack of funding and captive audience**; **Anupama Hoskere**, **SNA Puraskar 2020**, through ***Dhaatu***.

## Puppetry - the named traditions

1. **Kathputli** (Rajasthan) - **no legs**; **strings tied to the puppeteer's fingers**; bright Rajasthani dress; folk music.
2. **Kundhei / Kandhei Nacha** (Odisha) - **light wood**; **long skirts**; **more joints**; **triangular prop**; **marked Odissi influence**.
3. **Gombeyatta** (Karnataka) - **styled on Yakshagana theatre characters**; **more than one puppeteer**.
4. **Bommalattam** (Tamil Nadu) - **mixes rod and string**; **iron ring on the puppeteer's head**; **largest in India, up to 4.5 ft and 10 kg**; four stages ***Vinayak Puja, Komali, Amanattam, Pusenkanattam***.
5. **Laithibi Jagoi** (Manipur) - ***Raas Leela*** of Krishna; **19th century**; **2-3 metre life-size dolls**; **black backdrop**; **demon characters by costume masks**.
6. **Tolpava Kuthu** (Kerala) - **Bhadrakali temples**; ***koothumadam*** stage; **overnight**; ***Kamba Ramayana***; **oil lamps behind the stage**; **believed 9th or 10th century AD**; **Nair** community.
7. **Chamadyacha Bahulya** (Maharashtra) - **Ramayana** and ***Panchavati* (Story of Draupadi)**; **Thakar tribal community**.
8. **Togalu Gombeyatta** (Karnataka) - **size shows social status**, **large for kings and deities**, **smaller for commoners**; **Killekyata** community.
9. **Ravanchhaya** (Odisha) - **most theatrical**; **deer-skin**; **bold postures**, **no joints**, **complex**; **trees and animals**; **Bhat** community; **lyrical and sensitive narration**. **Tholu Bommalata** (AP) - **classical music background**; **Epics and Puranas**; **larger**, **coloured on both sides**.
10. **Pavakoothu** (Kerala) - **18th century**; **strongly influenced by Kathakali**; **headgear, feathers, face paint**; **Ramayana and Mahabharata**. **Yampuri** (Bihar) - **no joints**, **single piece of wood**, **bright colours**. **Putul Nachh** (WB, Odisha, Assam) - **3-4 ft *Jatra* style**; **neck and shoulder joints**; **waist rods**, puppeteers **behind a curtain**; **harmonium, cymbals, tabla**. **Kathi Kandhe** (Odisha) - from ***kathi***; **Hindu mythology**; **60 cm**; **2004 SNA Award to Maguni Charan Kuanr**.

## Martial and ritual performance

1. **Five-stage evolution**: **Vedic 1500-600 BCE** (***dhanurvidya*** / ***angavidya*** in the **Dhanurveda** and **Yajurveda**); **epics 600 BCE-400 CE**; **regional 200 BCE-1200 CE**; **invasions 1200-1800 CE**; **modernisation late 19th century-present**.
2. **Kalaripayattu** - Kerala, **around the 3rd century BC**; ***Kalari*** = training hall; **mock duels**, **generally not accompanied by music**, **footwork** key; ***Uzhichil, Otta, Maipayattu, Koltharipayattu, Angathari, Verumkai***; ***Vadakkan Pattukal*** on the ***Chekavar***; **Padma Shri 2017** to **Meenakshi Amma**; **2021** academy announced at **Thiruvananthapuram**.
3. **Silambam** - Tamil Nadu **staff fencing**; **Pandyas, Cholas, Cheras**; ***Silappadikaram* (2nd century AD)** on trade with **Rome, Greece and Egypt**; **snake, monkey, hawk hits**.
4. **Thang-ta** - **Meitei**; ***Thang*** sword, ***Ta*** spear; with unarmed ***Sarit Sarak*** = ***Huyen Langlon***; **Padma Shri 2009** to **Gurumayum Gourakishor Sharma**.
5. **Gatka** - Sikhs of Punjab; **stick, *Kirpan*, *Talwar*, *Kataar***; **displayed at celebrations including fairs**; borrowed by the **Defendu** system.
6. **Mardani Khel** - Maharashtra, **Kolhapur**; ***Patta*** and ***Vita***; **Shivaji and Tanaji Malusare**; noted by **Xuanzang, 7th century**.
7. **Pari-khanda** - Bihar, **created by the Rajputs**; ***Pari*** shield, ***khanda*** sword; **steps and techniques widely used in Chhau dance**; ***haathi chaali***, ***baagh chaali***.
8. **Chholia** - Uttarakhand; **pre-battle ritual → wedding ceremony**; sword and shield, **synchronized group formations**, **singing and drumming**.
9. **Paika Akhada** - Odisha; **a combination of dance and combat**; **earlier used by warriors, now practised as a performing art**.
10. Ledger: **Mallakhamb** (*Manasollasa*, 12th century; four categories), **Cheibi Gad-Ga**, **Thoda** (**Baisakhi**; ***Pashis*** and ***Saathis***), **Teri Chha**, **Lathi**, **Inbuan** (**1750 AD**), **Kuttu Varisai** (Sangam; unarmed component of Silambam), **Musti Yuddha**, **Sqay**, **Kathi Samu**, **Bandesh**, **Malla Yuddha**, **Insuknawr**, **Mukna** (***Lai Haraoba***), **Kirip/Saldu**, **Varma Adi**, **Adimurai**.

## Masks, narration and cross-art relations

1. **Baseline**: **masks were not used** in the classical Sanskrit system as recorded.
2. **Four mask uses**: **expressive** (Ankia Naat), **sacred and iconographic** (Ramman), **categorial** (Laithibi Jagoi), **craft** (Chhau mask making).
3. **Adjacent but distinct**: **elaborate headgear** (Theyyam); **headgear, feathers and face paint** (Pavakoothu).
4. **Narrator conventions**: ***Sutradhar*** across three systems; ***Ojha*** and ***Palis***; ***Gayaka*** and ***Palia***; three narrators in **Chaiti Ghoda**; ***Bhagavata*** and ***Arthadharis***; a main artist with **two co-artists**; ***Nayaka*** in Bhavai.
5. **Extreme case**: **Tal-Maddale** - narration as the whole performance.
6. **Theatre → puppetry**: **Yakshagana → Gombeyatta**; **Kathakali → Pavakoothu**; ***Jatra*** style → rod-puppet figures.
7. **Dance → puppetry**: **Odissi → Kundhei**, enabled by its **extra joints**.
8. **Within-region shaping**: **Bhuta Kola ← Yakshagana**; **Bayalata is a form of Yakshagana**; **Tal-Maddale is its predecessor**; **Kuruvanji ← Bharatanatyam**; **Chhau ← Pari-khanda**.
9. **The defensible general claim**: a **regionally dominant form supplies the design, movement and stylistic vocabulary of neighbouring forms across mechanisms**.
10. **Boundaries**: dance grammar, music theory, pictorial and sculptural analysis, play authorship, list and award administration, sport policy and the circus are each owned elsewhere.

## Status, decline and safeguarding

1. **Two kinds of decline**: a **completed historical contraction** against a **current documented deficit**.
2. **Classical causes** are historical; **puppetry's causes** are **lack of funding** and **lack of captive audience**, in the **present tense**.
3. Both have **living continuations**: **Kutiyattam since the 10th century AD**; **fourteen named puppet traditions** described in the present tense.
4. **Recognition instruments and limits**: a **restrictive statute (1876)** proves consequence but protects nothing; an **inscription** records a dated listing, not antiquity or rank; an **academy award** recognises a practitioner but creates no audience; a **training school** reproduces skills but cannot sustain an unattended form.
5. Dated anchors: **SNA created by resolution 1952, inaugurated 28 January 1953**; awards **conferred since 1952**; **2004** and **2020** puppetry awards; **NSD 1959 / 1975**.
6. Inscriptions attaching to this topic's forms: **Kutiyattam 2008**, **Ramman 2009**, **Mudiyettu 2010**, **Chhau 2010**.
7. 📰 **Current record, once only**: the academy's press release of **10 June 2026** - General Council **22-26 March 2026**; **7 Akademi Fellows** (fellowship limited to **40** at any time); **108 artists** for the **Akademi Puraskar for 2024 and 2025** across fields including **Theatre** and **Traditional/Folk/Tribal/Music/Dance/Theatre/Puppetry**; **106 young artists** for the **Ustad Bismillah Khan Yuva Puraskar**; purses of **Rs. 3,00,000**, **Rs. 1,00,000** and **Rs. 25,000**, each with a **Tamrapatra and Angavastram**.
8. ⚠️ **No recipient of the 2024 or 2025 awards is named in this session**, and list totals belong to the heritage owner.
9. **Martial traditions are recorded as transforming, not declining** - warrior practice to performing art; pre-battle ritual to wedding ceremony; technique absorbed into a masked dance.
10. The strongest safeguarding claim is **not antiquity** but **continued performance by identifiable people on identifiable occasions**.

## Answer-writing spine

1. Open with the **six evidence questions**, never with a value claim.
2. Define the form or system with **its own vocabulary** before describing it.
3. Use **discriminating** features, not shared ones.
4. Keep the **three clocks** apart and reproduce every **hedge**.
5. Reproduce **dual regional attributions** and the one recorded **internal discrepancy**.
6. In puppetry, read **mechanism first**, **state last**.
7. Name the **carrier community** wherever the record supplies one.
8. Use the **two-decline distinction** in any safeguarding demand.
9. State **cross-art influence in its specific form**, not as a general claim about Indian holism.
10. Close with a **qualified verdict** that answers the exact wording - and never with a generic appeal to heritage.

**Controls observed across the 31 models.** Every model carries its own **unique scoring note**, worded distinctly and summing exactly to its stated marks. Every model body sits **within its stated word ceiling**. No stem reproduces or closely paraphrases any reproduced past question, and the two directive shapes used by the reproduced Main Examination items - *write about* a single named form, and *where did these originate* - are not reused anywhere. Each lesson-local demand is built on material that its own lesson teaches and that no other lesson owns: scope and boundaries; the four words for drama; mode against genre; the closure convention; ritual, architecture and speech as staging; character and register; the three causes of contraction; the surviving lineage and its limits; the folk arc; eastern propagation and narrator grammar; northern satire; Deccan naming logics; the Karnataka cluster; the 1876 statute as behavioural evidence; the professional performer; puppetry as transmission; divergence within one mechanism; shadow optics; glove against rod; martial description by axis; cross-art influence stated precisely; the two tenses of decline; and transmission against antiquity.

## Bounded-research declaration

This session was generated under the bounded-research rule. The substantive base is the complete core owner, the complete enrichment owner and one core OCR art and performance source. External work was confined to three declared purposes.

**(a) PYQ verification and routing.** The routing ledgers and the locally held official papers named in the manifest above, together with the two reproduced question lists in the core art source's own chapter.

**(b) One bounded material-fact pass.** The list of items was fixed before searching and is reproduced here in full, with the outcome of each.

| # | Item | Why it was on the list | Outcome |
|---:|---|---|---|
| 1 | **Mudiyettu** - form-type, region and inscription year | The form is named in an owned Main Examination item reproduced in this chapter, but does not appear in the chapter's own folk-theatre classification table; omitting it would leave a real gap in an owned question | **Resolved.** Recorded as the **ritual theatre and dance drama of Kerala**, inscribed on the **UNESCO Representative List in 2010**. Nothing beyond those three facts is claimed |
| 2 | ***Ghashiram Kotwal*** - authorship | An owned Main Examination item asks who wrote the play; the chapter names the playwright among modern personalities without attaching this title to him | **Resolved.** Written by **Vijay Tendulkar**, who appears in the core record's own list of noted modern theatre personalities. No further claim about the text is made, and literary analysis remains routed to the literature owner |
| 3 | **Kutiyattam** - inscription year and status | The core owner records 2008 and instructs verification against the official element page; the topic's recognition control depends on the year being right | **Resolved.** Inscribed **2008** |
| 4 | **Ramman** - inscription year | The chapter records that the form is listed on the Representative List but supplies no year | **Resolved.** Inscribed **2009** |
| 5 | **Chhau** - inscription year | The chapter records the inscription in an adjacent chapter without a year, and Lesson 22 needs the date for the recognition sequence | **Resolved.** Inscribed **2010**. The form's dance classification remains routed to the dance owner |
| 6 | **Sitabena and Jogimara** - the status of the "world's oldest amphitheatre" label | The core owner flags the label as disputed and instructs that it not be stated as settled | **Resolved as a dispute, not as a fact.** The reading of the site as a performance space is contested, with alternative functions proposed and the neighbouring inscription read in more than one way. This session reproduces the label with its hedge and imports none of the contested detail |
| 7 | **National School of Drama** - its relation to the academy and its dates | The core owner states that the school was parented by the academy and independent from 1975; the chapter names the school without dates, and the parent-to-independence relation is examinable | **Resolved.** Set up under the **Sangeet Natak Akademi in 1959**; became an **autonomous body in 1975** |
| 8 | **The ten rupakas** - correct names and spellings | The chapter's printed list contains forms that do not match the standard set, and the core owner warns against reconstructing spellings from faulty text | **Resolved as a terminology correction.** The standard set is **Nataka, Prakarana, Bhana, Prahasana, Dima, Vyayoga, Samavakara, Vithi, Ihamrga and Utsrishtikanka (Anka)**. The chapter's *Ihamgra*, *Vyayog*, *Svakarna* and bare *Anka* are recorded in Lesson 3 as variants and are not silently corrected away |
| 9 | **Sangeet Natak Akademi** - the relation between its 1952 date and the 1953 date in circulation | The core source, the enrichment owner and the official 2026 release all use 1952, while 1953 is widely given as the academy's founding year; the topic's institutional clock depends on the relation being stated correctly | **Resolved as two distinct acts, not as a competing pair.** The academy was **created by a Government of India resolution in 1952** and **formally inaugurated on 28 January 1953**. Both are recorded in Lesson 15, Lesson 22 and the register notes, and the official release's own phrase - awards **conferred since 1952** - is reproduced unchanged |

**(c) One bounded current linkage.** Exactly one is claimed, file-wide, in Lesson 22: the **Sangeet Natak Akademi press release of 10 June 2026** announcing the Akademi Fellowship and Akademi Puraskar for 2024 and 2025 and the Ustad Bismillah Khan Yuva Puraskar for the same years. Only its **date, the General Council meeting dates of 22-26 March 2026, the counts of seven Fellows, 108 Akademi Puraskar artists and 106 Yuva Puraskar artists, the fellowship limit of forty, the field names including Theatre and Traditional/Folk/Tribal/Music/Dance/Theatre/Puppetry, the statement that awards have been conferred since 1952, and the purse figures with the Tamrapatra and Angavastram** are used. **No individual recipient is named anywhere in this session.** Every other pre-teach checklist in this file records the absence of a genuine linkage truthfully, and no current claim appears anywhere else.

## Discrepancies recorded rather than resolved

| # | Discrepancy | How this session handles it |
|---:|---|---|
| 1 | The core art source's printed list of the ten play-types contains **Ihamgra**, **Vyayog**, **Svakarna** and a bare **Anka**, which do not match the standard set | Lesson 3 prints the standard set, records the variants explicitly, and instructs that an unfamiliar name should not be reconstructed from a half-remembered list. Neither form of the list is silently preferred |
| 2 | The core art source states both that Sanskrit plays **always had happy endings** and that the **portrayal of tragedy was almost rare** | Lesson 4 reproduces both, names the difference in force, and supplies the formulation that preserves it. The absolute is not written alone |
| 3 | The **Swang** entry is placed in the classification table under **Rajasthan / Madhya Pradesh (Malwar region)** while its own description says the forms are popular in **Rajasthan and Haryana** | Lesson 11 prints both attributions and Lesson 23 lists the entry among the region-discipline cases. Neither attribution is deleted |
| 4 | The reproduced objective item describes **Amar Katha** as **serialised between 1910 and 1913**, while the chapter's prose records it as **published in 1913** | Lesson 15 and the PYQ section record both, note that serialisation and publication are different claims, and supply the safe formulation. Neither is resolved |
| 5 | The reproduced Main Examination list prints **Dhashavatar**, while the classification table prints **Dashavatar** | Both spellings are recorded in the PYQ section; the session teaches the form under the table's spelling and does not multiply forms out of a variant |
| 6 | **Kutiyattam** appears as **Kuttiyatam** and **Koodiyattam** in the same passage | All three spellings are recorded in Lesson 8; the session uses one consistently and states the variants once |
| 7 | **Kathi Kandhe** appears as **Kathi Kundhei Nacha** within the same entry, and the award note uses the second form | Both are recorded in Lesson 19 with the ***kathi*** etymology as the discriminator against the Odisha string tradition |
| 8 | The enrichment owner records an open caution that its account of the reasons for the decline of Sanskrit theatre rests on the sub-section heading without a line-by-line re-extraction | **Discharged.** Lesson 7 reproduces the three causes directly from the core art source's own sub-section text |
| 9 | The core owner's four-mechanism table gives **Putul Nach (West Bengal)** and **Tholu Bommalata (Andhra Pradesh)** in abbreviated form, while the core art source gives **Putul Nachh (West Bengal, Odisha and Assam)** and fuller descriptions | Lessons 18 and 19 use the fuller source record and state the three-state attribution explicitly |
| 10 | The core owner's martial map includes **Pari-khanda / Chhau-linked (eastern India)** while the core art source locates **Pari-khanda** in **Bihar** with the Chhau link stated as a use of its steps | Lesson 20 uses the source's precise formulation - Bihar, created by the Rajputs, with steps and techniques widely used in Chhau dance |
| 11 | The core art source and the enrichment owner both date the **Sangeet Natak Akademi** to **1952**, and the official 2026 release states that its awards have been **conferred since 1952**; an official institutional account of the academy dates its **creating Government of India resolution to 1952** but its **formal inauguration to 28 January 1953**, and 1953 is therefore also current in circulation | **Disclosed, not adjudicated.** Lesson 15 states both acts explicitly - created by resolution in 1952, inaugurated on 28 January 1953 - and instructs that neither year be asserted alone as *the* founding date. Lesson 22, the register notes and the coverage material carry the same double formulation, and the official release's own wording, awards **conferred since 1952**, is reproduced exactly and not rewritten to 1953 |

## Declared gaps - material a candidate may expect that this session does not supply

1. **No answer letter, key, option elimination or corrective mapping** for any reproduced question. The official 2024 key, the provisional 2026 key and the reproducing source's own two-item key were all available and were deliberately not carried.
2. **No recipient name** from the 2024 or 2025 Akademi Fellowship, Akademi Puraskar or Yuva Puraskar lists, although the announcing document was read.
3. **No running total** of Indian intangible-heritage inscriptions and no current list position. Routed to the heritage, conservation and institutions owner, which holds its own verification date.
4. **No list of ancient Sanskrit dramas, their authors or their texts**, beyond the single play attribution required by an owned item. The core record itself routes that list to the literature owner.
5. **No circus history and no indigenous-sports table.** Both sit in the same source chapter and are outside this topic's recorded ownership; they are routed out rather than improvised into the teaching.
6. **No sport-policy material** - federations, state-sport declarations, ministry recognitions or national youth-games inclusion - developed as an argument. Where the source attaches such a fact directly to a tradition's own description it is recorded in the ledger table of Lesson 20 and nowhere else.
7. **No dance grammar**: no *nritta*, *nritya*, *natya*, *abhinaya*, nine-*rasa*, *hasta* or *mudra* teaching, and no adjudication of whether a named dance-drama belongs to dance or to theatre.
8. **No raga, tala or classical-system teaching.** Instruments and song genres appear only as identifiers of a performance form.
9. **No origin date, founder, community or honour** for any form or person where the source supplies none. Several folk entries carry no century and none is supplied.
10. **No mapping of Mudiyettu beyond three facts** - its form-type, its region and its inscription year. The core chapter's classification table does not contain the form, and nothing further is claimed for it.
11. **No treatment of the 2026 Preliminary item on a Bagh Caves painting**, and no material supplied towards it.
12. **No claim that any tradition is unchanged, pure, authentic or artistically superior** to any other.

## Source-use statement

Canonical Markdown supplied the topic's ownership boundaries, the essential-vocabulary set, the trap set, the directive and demand map, the qualified thesis options, the mark-scaled structures and the factual-risk and dynamic-status controls. The enrichment owner supplied the completed-versus-ongoing decline distinction, the transformation-and-vulnerability vocabulary, the performance-as-archive framing, the multi-art integration argument and the safeguarding-response comparison. The core OCR art and performance source supplied the complete classical treatment, the full folk-theatre classification table entry by entry, the complete modern-theatre section with its venue boxes and personalities table, the complete puppetry section with all four mechanisms and every named tradition, the martial-arts section read for its performance content, and the two reproduced question lists. The PYQ routing ledgers and the locally held official papers supplied ownership, exact wording and key status. The single bounded current linkage supplied one dated institutional record.

No artifact under the permanently excluded package directory was read, searched, cited or relied upon at any stage of this generation, and no learner-v2 artifact was used. Qdrant was not required and did not block or delay this session.
